POST HEARING BRIEF OF GEORGIA POWER COMPANY 
I.INTRODUCTION
Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power” or the “Company”) submits this brief in support of its proposed Alternate Rate Plan (“ARP”) as amended by the Company’s August 22, 2022 Supplemental Filing, September 28, 2022 Errata, and November 21, 2022 Errata filed in this proceeding. The Company’s 2022 Rate Case was filed pursuant to the terms and conditions of the Georgia Public Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) February 6, 2020 Order Adopting Settlement Agreement in Georgia Power’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516 (“2019 Rate Case Final Order”). As explained herein, based on the evidence before it, the Commission should approve the Company’s application for a rate increase. 
II.SUMMARY OF THE ARGUMENT
Georgia Power recommends that the Commission maintain the constructive regulatory environment, effectuated through the Company’s ARP, that has benefitted Georgia Power’s customers for decades. This Commission’s constructive regulatory policies have created a sustained period of high customer satisfaction, high reliability, and rates below the national average, and have contributed to Georgia consistently being ranked as the best state in the nation in which to do business. This Commission has a well-deserved national reputation for maintaining a consistent and balanced regulatory environment that benefits all stakeholders. 
Many of the recommendations and positions taken by the Commission’s Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff and Intervenors appear to ignore what has changed over the past three years, as well as what has remained constant since the 2019 rate case order. Changes over the past three years include: (1) the emergence of a global pandemic; (2) rampant and rapid inflation that has increased costs for businesses and consumers alike; (3) growing pressure on supply chain and labor markets; (4) significant increases in the federal funds rate and cost of borrowing; and (5) evolving customer needs, demands and requests for increased distributed energy resources and carbon-free energy. Notwithstanding these changes, several things have remained constant: (a) this Commission’s continued constructive and balanced approach that ensures customers continue to receive clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electricity, even during a global pandemic; (b) the importance of financial integrity and credit quality, which is never more evident than during times of global uncertainty and financial market volatility, such as that being experienced today; (c) customers remain at the center of everything the Company does, as evidenced by Georgia Power’s continued excellent customer service and reliability performance with rates below the national average; and (d) that the state of Georgia continues to be ranked as one of the best places for business, with Georgia’s economy continuing to grow and with economic development reaching all-time highs.
When considering the past three years, many of the recommendations made by parties in this case would harm customers by upsetting the well-designed and balanced regulatory structure currently in place, and ultimately undermine the financial health and integrity of Georgia Power. These recommendations are unacceptable to the Company. 
PIA Staff and Intervenors have also proposed modifications to the ARP that are fundamentally inconsistent with the concept of an ARP that incentivizes the Company to innovate and achieve operational efficiencies that benefit customers. Therefore, Georgia Power does not support the recommendations to narrow the earnings band, set the top end of the earnings band at an artificially low level, or eliminate the sharing mechanism for earnings above the top end of the band. The recommended alterations to the Company’s proposed authorized earnings band would remove revenue sharing incentives and effectively create an earnings cap, potential rate instability, and be counterproductive to the goal of incentivizing the Company to drive efficiencies in the business for the benefit of customers. In addition, the recommendations by Staff and other intervenors to revise the content and estimates supporting the Company’s annual compliance filings edges ever closer to having abbreviated rate cases every year, which encourages less predictability in rates. 
The Company opposes the return on equity (“ROE”) setpoint and capital structure recommendations put forth by PIA Staff and several Intervenors. If adopted, PIA Staff’s recommended 105 basis point ROE reduction from the Company’s current setpoint would represent the largest single ROE reduction for Georgia Power in decades. Many of these same parties also propose to reduce the equity portion of the Company’s capital structure, the most drastic of which would reduce the Company’s equity ratio from 56% to 51% in the second and third years of the ARP. The Company’s current capital structure was approved by this Commission in response to the 2017 Tax Cut and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) and modified in the Company’s last rate case. Passage of the TCJA was the impetus for the initial increase in equity several years ago, and the impact of the TCJA remains unchanged. With this in mind, PIA Staff’s suggested 500-basis point equity ratio reduction from the current Commission-approved 56% based on the anticipated completion of Plant Vogtle Units 3 and 4 has no merit. Such severe changes to the Company’s ROE and capital structure would significantly impair the Company’s financial integrity and its ability to raise capital at reasonable cost and upon reasonable terms for the benefit of customers. In contrast, the ARP, together with the Company’s proposed 11.00% ROE and 56% equity level, will support the Company’s financial integrity and ensure the Company continues to access capital markets at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. At these levels, the Company’s Funds from Operations (“FFO”) to Debt ratio, a key metric used by the ratings agencies to analyze a company’s financial risk, will meet the thresholds identified by the credit rating agencies as necessary to maintain the Company’s current credit quality. 
PIA Staff wrongly asserts that the Company can arbitrarily reduce its Operations and Maintenance (“O&M”) expense based on the past three years, as if major outage or maintenance work occurs at the same level every year and based on the erroneous assumption that operating through the recent pandemic was indicative of Georgia Power’s cost to serve customers during a normal operating environment. The Company’s O&M requests in this case were thoughtfully designed to support the next three years of critical function and operation of Georgia Power to ensure that the Company can continue to provide clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electric service with exceptional customer service. 
PIA Staff has also proposed to substantially reduce or eliminate transmission and distribution investments within the Grid Investment Plan, which are required to support the long-term reliability and resiliency of our electric system, seemingly without considering the long-term adverse impacts such reductions would cause to the electric system. The Company cannot delay or take shortcuts in implementing these grid investments as they are necessary to address aging transmission infrastructure and improve reliability performance for nearly 1.4 million customers throughout the state who are served by lower performing feeders. 
In the aggregate, the modifications proposed by PIA Staff and several Intervenors undercut core aspects of the ARP and are unacceptable. The Company cannot agree to an ARP that would impair its financial integrity, threaten its current credit ratings, fundamentally alter the framework of the ARP and strip it of its core benefits, or jeopardize the Company’s ability to provide customers with safe and reliable electric service. The Commission should approve the Company’s proposed ARP, without modification, as a fair and reasonable resolution of this proceeding that appropriately balances the interests of customers, the Company, and investors. Approval of the Company’s three-year ARP will continue the Commission’s long-standing tradition of constructive regulation and provide stability and certainty for customers and the Company. 
The Company understands this Commission must consider the cost of electric service when evaluating Georgia Power’s cost to serve its 2.7 million customers. Georgia Power has thoughtfully and appropriately balanced the request in this proceeding with both the financial impacts to, and needs of, our customers. Georgia Power operates a capital-intensive business and has the privilege and obligation to serve its customers in this rapidly changing world. Adopting the Company’s proposed ARP will ensure the Company remains financially healthy and able to provide clean, safe, reliable, and affordable service to its customers now and into the future. Therefore, and for the reasons discussed further herein, the Commission should approve the Company’s application to adjust rates as filed, including the Company’s proposed ARP. 
III.PROCEDURAL BACKGROUND
The Commission issued its Procedural and Scheduling Order for the Company’s 2022 Rate Case on April 7, 2022, as subsequently amended by the July 26, 2022 Amended PSO, August 5, 2022 Second Amended PSO, and November 8, 2022 Third Amended PSO (collectively as amended, the “PSO”). In accordance with the PSO, the Company’s June 24, 2022 filing included the minimum filing requirements specified by Commission Rule 515-2-1-.04, along with the direct testimony and exhibits of Christopher C. Womack, the panel of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson, James M. Coyne, Lee Evans, Steven M. Fetter, and Larry T. Legg. Hearings regarding the Company’s direct case were held September 27, 2022, through September 29, 2022. 
On October 20, 2022, the Commission’s Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff filed the direct testimony and exhibits of the following witnesses: the panel of Ralph C. Smith and Robert L. Trokey; Michael P. Gorman; the panel of Jamie Barber, Benjamin Deitchman, and Glenn A. Watkins; the panel of Stephen J. Baron and Leah Wellborn; Rajendrasinh (Raj) Rana; Kevin O’Donnell; Lance Kaufman; the panel of Paul J. Alvarez and Dennis Stephens; and Dante Mugrace. Also on October 20, 2022, Americans for Affordable Clean Energy (“AACE”) filed the direct testimony and exhibits of the panel consisting of Joseph Akers, Ron Nelson, and Barry Selvidge; ChargePoint, Inc., filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Justin D. Wilson; the Commercial Group filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Steve W. Chriss; EVgo Services, LLC, filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Colin Murchie; the Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”) filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Jeffry Pollock, Billie S. LaConte, and Roger C. Tutterow, Ph.D; the Georgia Coalition of Local Governments (“Coalition”), filed the direct testimony and exhibits of C. Nicholas Deffley and David A. Nifong; Georgia Interfaith Power & Light (“GIPL”) filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Mark E. Ellis and Justin Barnes; Georgia Solar Energy Industries Association (“GSEIA”), Solar Energy Industries Association (“SEIA”), and Vote Solar filed the direct testimony of Kevin Lucas and Thatcher Young; Metropolitan Atlanta Rapid Transit Authority (“MARTA”) filed the direct testimony of Kevin Hurley; Southern Alliance for Clean Energy (“SACE”) and Southface Energy Institute, Inc. (“Southface”) filed the direct testimony of Ronald Lehr; the U.S. Department of Defense and all other Federal Executive Agencies (“DOD”) filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Maureen L. Also on October 20, 2022, and in accordance with the PSO, Georgia Power filed the supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of Dr. David Gattie and the panel of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Watkins; the Coalition filed the direct testimony David G. Brown and Alden M. Hathaway. On November 23, 2022, Georgia Power filed the supplemental rebuttal testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Hearings regarding the Company’s rebuttal testimony, PIA Staff and Intervenor supplemental direct, and the Company’s supplemental rebuttal testimony were held November 29, 2022 through 30, 2022. 
IV.ARGUMENT 
A.Approval of the Company’s Application as Filed Will Continue the Constructive Regulatory Environment that Customers, the Commission, the Company, and Investors Rely Upon. 

Approval of the Company’s Application, including the Company’s recommended ARP, supports the constructive regulation in Georgia that has benefitted customers for decades. The Company’s filing in this case is consistent with the Commission’s policies that have cultivated a sustained period of high customer satisfaction, high reliability, rates below the national average, and contributed to Georgia consistently being ranked as the best state in the nation in which to do business. The Commission’s regulatory structure has created an environment in which: (i) the Company can provide the clean, safe, and reliable service that our customers depend on to meet their evolving energy needs; (ii) the Company can access capital on favorable terms to meet the current and future needs of our customers; and (iii) the Company’s investors have an opportunity to receive the returns appropriate for a well-run and efficient utility that meets the needs of its customers. This Commission’s ability to balance strong and consistent policy while also adapting to rapidly evolving technology and implementing gradual changes when appropriate, has facilitated a consistent regulatory environment viewed favorably by customers and investors alike. Approval of the Company’s filing will continue this trend.
The ARP filed by the Company satisfies the core responsibilities of the Commission as stated by the Supreme Court of Georgia:

It is the responsibility of the commission to require a regulated utility to provide a level of service within its service area this year, next year, and in the foreseeable future; consonant with this responsibility the commission must approve utility rates that will produce enough revenue not only for operating expenses but also for the capital costs of the business. Capital for the business includes that required for current and future construction, and its costs include interest and dividends that must be paid to reasonably attract such capital. 

Georgia Power Co. v. Georgia Public Service Commission, 231 Ga. 339, 344 (1973).

Given the Company’s performance under the Commission’s consistent regulation within the context of a global pandemic, rampant inflation, supply chain and labor market pressures, increasing cost of debt, and evolving customer needs, the modifications and changes proposed by PIA Staff and Intervenors are inappropriate and punitive. There is no support under Georgia law, the Commission’s regulatory precedent, or the record in this proceeding, for the proposition implicit in PIA Staff and certain Intervenor’s recommendations that rates should be set as low as possible according to calculations that clearly threaten the Company’s ability to maintain, let alone improve, its credit metrics. Moreover, the parties advocating for these modifications disregard the impact their proposals would have on the Company’s ability to access capital markets on reasonable terms and attract the substantial capital necessary to continue providing clean, safe, and reliable service at the industry-leading level of customer service that the Company’s customers deserve and expect. 
Rating agencies look for consistent application of sound economic regulatory principles by utility regulators. (See Tr. 795-96.) In fact, the most recent opinions from each of the three credit rating agencies reference their assessment that Georgia Power’s multiyear rate plan has been “supportive of GPC’s credit quality,” that “the overall regulatory framework in Georgia will remain credit supportive,” and that they “expect a constructive resolution to Georgia Power’s pending rate case filing, which proposes a three-year rate plan.” (Tr. 3949.) In contrast, if a regulatory body allowed a utility the opportunity to earn a fair return on utility assets and subsequently applied cost recovery principles in a manner inconsistent with the regulatory compact, investors’ interest in providing funds to that utility would be altered, the utility’s risk profile would deteriorate, and the utility’s access to capital at reasonable terms would suffer – all to the detriment of customers. (See Tr. 794.)
The combined effect of the recommendations by PIA Staff and some Intervenors to alter the Company’s ARP proposal would result in a negative reaction from investors and could lead to negative rating actions to the detriment of the Company and customers. (Tr. 3950, 3957, 4167, 4171, 4184.) The Company encourages this Commission to continue fostering regulatory consistency and avoid drastic action that could disturb the balance this Commission has created, which has been proven to benefit customers.
B.The Commission Should Adopt the Company’s Proposed ROE, Cost of Capital, and Capital Structure, and Reject PIA Staff’s ROE, Cost of Capital, and Capital Structure to Avoid Jeopardizing Georgia Power’s Financial Integrity.
1.The Commission should adopt the Company’s proposed capital structure and ROE as calculated by Mr. Coyne. 
The Commission should adopt Mr. Coyne’s 11% ROE recommendation and the Company’s proposed 56% equity capital structure to ensure the Company can continue to access capital markets at reasonable rates and terms. Georgia Power proposes that its capital structure containing approximately 56% common equity and 44% debt be used for ratemaking purposes in this proceeding. (Tr. 72, 202, 631, 672.) Mr. Coyne analyzed the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model, Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”), Bond Yield Risk Premium model, and the Expected Earnings models for a comparable utility group and estimated the cost of equity to run from 8.99% to 13.55%, with a four model average of 10.92%. (Tr. 631, 652, 667, 673.) Based on these analyses and including an adjustment for flotation costs, Mr. Coyne recommends an allowed ROE equal to 11.00% for Georgia Power in this proceeding. (Tr. 631, 673.) Mr. Coyne’s recommendation also considers the general economic and capital market environment and the influence capital market conditions exert over the results of the DCF, CAPM, and Risk Premium models. (Tr. 632.) An 11.00% ROE is a fair, forward-looking rate of return on equity that provides the Company with an opportunity to earn a return on its investment over the period in which rates are in effect commensurate with returns that investors expect to earn on other investments of similar risk. (Tr. 678.)
The Commission should also consider that, relative to the 2019 rate case, the three key quantitative components impacting the Company’s weighted cost of capital (approved capital structure, projected cost of debt, and allowed cost of equity) in this request results in a net decrease of $18 million to our requested revenue requirement. (Tr. 214.) Over the past three years the Company has taken advantage of a historically low interest rate environment and relatively flat yield curve and is passing those savings to customers with the projected embedded cost of debt. (Tr. 215.) Although presently in a rising interest rate environment, the Company will continue to seek, as it has in the past, financing structures that minimize the impact of cost to our customers.
2.PIA Staff and Intervenors’ recommended ROE reductions are drastic, unprecedented, and punitive.
The positions taken by PIA Staff and several Intervenors would undermine Georgia’s well-established and balanced regulatory structure by drastically reducing the Company’s ROE. PIA Staff’s recommended 105 basis point ROE reduction would represent the largest single ROE reduction for Georgia Power in decades and would be particularly egregious in light of the Company’s superior performance over the last three years, including during the COVID-19 pandemic. (Tr. 3946.) A 105-basis point reduction in ROE is not consistent with utility performance that exceeds customer and investor expectations. (See Tr. 4009.) Since 2019, the Company has performed well, delivering clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electricity to customers while striving to find efficiencies and foster innovation. The ROE reductions proposed by Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis would signify a dramatic departure from the gradual approach employed by this Commission over the past several rate cases and would undo the steps the Commission took in 2018 to address the Tax Reform Act and protect the Company’s credit quality from the stresses placed on it by tax reform. (Tr. 3957, 4025, 4108, 4122, 4178-79.) The capital markets would surely view such a reduction negatively as it would represent a radical and dramatic departure from the consistent and historically constructive regulation for which this Commission has a reputation – one where excellence is recognized and rewarded, not punished. (Id.)
PIA Staff and several Intervenors reference the industry average authorized ROEs over various time frames, whether to note that they have been declining since 2009 (Tr. 3480) or have averaged within the range of 9.35% to 10.42% for years 2010 through 2022 to date. (Tr. 2460-62, 2709-11, 2849, 3517-18.) However, Georgia Power is not an average utility and Georgia is not an average state. (Tr. 574.) The Commission has never approved Georgia Power’s ROEs based upon industry average and has, in fact, historically established Georgia Power’s ROE set point above the national average. (Tr. 575.) Indeed, the Commission has historically approved, through the three-year ARPs, ROEs approximately 100 basis points above the industry average, taking into consideration the Company’s superior performance, ability to manage operating risk and agreement not to seek rate relief for three years except in limited circumstances. 
The Company’s proposed ROE of 11.00% is thoroughly supported by Mr. Coyne. While PIA Staff Witness Gorman, GIPL Witness Ellis, GAM Witness LaConte, and DOD Witness Reno questioned Mr. Coyne’s results by taking positions that can differ among professional experts, Mr. Coyne appropriately defended his positions based on his considerable and extensive knowledge and expertise. (Tr. 2752, 3495-96, 3652-53.) For example, several parties questioned Mr. Coyne’s use of market capital structure adjustment to correct for financial risk differences between the proxy group’s market capital structure and the book regulatory capital structure proposed for Georgia Power. Mr. Coyne defended this adjustment as correct and necessary to provide an overall cost of capital commensurate with market expectations. 
PIA Staff and Intervenor recommendations do not represent current market conditions and would not result in full recovery of the Company’s cost of equity. (Tr. 3957, 4116, 4120-21, 4179.) As shown in Table 1 below, three of the parties who recommended ROE adjustments in this case who also made ROE recommendations in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case, recommended higher adjustments in the 2022 Rate Case than they did in the 2019 Rate Case. (Tr. 1295, 1471, 2716, 3441, 3264.) Comparing these parties’ prior ROE recommendations with current recommendations inherently acknowledges that the cost of capital has increased in the last three years.

Despite implicitly acknowledging that the cost of capital has increased since 2019, each party recommending an ROE setpoint proposed a value lower than the Company’s current ROE. These parties provide no justification for recommending such severe reductions in the Company’s cost of equity since the last base rate case. Mr. Ellis’ 5.54% ROE recommendation is almost 500 basis points below Georgia Power’s current ROE, nearly 400 basis points below the 9.53% average authorized ROE for vertically integrated electric utilities in 2021 (Tr. 4122, 4178), and 300 basis points below any return authorized for any electric utility in at least 40 years. (Tr. 4117, 4178.) In fact, Mr. Ellis’ recommendation is so low as to be confiscatory and severely beyond the zone of reasonableness for a vertically integrated investor-owned utility. 
For these reasons, Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis’ recommendations are not only unsupported but also are unduly punitive in light of Georgia Power’s record of strong performance. (Tr. 3957.) The ROE reductions proposed by PIA Staff and Intervenors are a dramatic departure from the Commission’s constructive and supportive regulatory environment over the past several rate cases (Tr. 3957, 4025, 4108, 4122, 4178-79), and would surely be viewed negatively by the capital markets, as Mr. Fetter unequivocally testified. (Tr. 4184.) 
3.PIA Staff’s and Intervenors’ proposed equity reductions would destroy the necessary balance agreed to following the Tax Reform Settlement and affirmed in the 2019 Rate Case and would threaten the Company’s financial integrity. 
Many of the same parties proposing drastic ROE cuts also propose to reduce the equity portion of the Company’s capital structure, including PIA Staff’s recommended 500 basis point reduction from the level deemed appropriate by this Commission just three years ago. Such unprecedented and severe changes would significantly impair the Company’s financial integrity, which is necessary to ensure its ability to raise capital at reasonable cost and upon reasonable terms for the benefit of customers.
Although Mr. Gorman acknowledges that the Company’s authorized equity ratio was previously increased due to the negative effects of the 2017 TCJA, he completely ignores that these negative effects continue today. (Tr. 3538.) Instead, he proposes that the Company’s equity ratio should be reduced to 51% once Vogtle Unit 4 is placed in service (Tr. 3515), wrongly assuming that the construction of Vogtle Units 3 and 4, rather than the TCJA, was the catalyst to increase the Company’s equity ratio to 56%. (Tr. 3540, 3542, 3545.) As such, the placement of Vogtle Units 3 and 4 in service should not be an advance trigger for a reduction in the Company’s equity ratio. The Commission should adhere to its prior policy findings and maintain the Company’s equity ratio due to the continued risk and negative impacts from the TCJA. 
The Settlement Agreement approved in the 2019 Rate Case left it to the Commission to determine the Company’s appropriate capital structure. Based on the evidence presented in that case, the Commission found the appropriate capital structure to be 56% equity and 44% debt. (Tr. 3440-41.) As noted in the Commission’s Short Order Adopting Settlement as Modified in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516, the Company’s equity ratio was adjusted from 51% to 55% as a part of the TCJA Settlement in Docket No. 36989 (“Tax Reform Settlement”) to address the negative implications of tax reform and provide support for maintaining the Company’s credit profile. (Tr. 3537-38.) The Commission further determined that the “proposed capital structure of 56% common equity level is just and reasonable considering all of the evidence presented.” There have not been, nor are there anticipated to be, any changes to the TCJA or its negative impacts on the Company since the Commission approved the 56% equity, 44% debt capital structure in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case that would justify lowering the Company’s equity ratio. (Tr. 3956.)
GAM Witness LaConte recommends the Company’s equity ratio should be more in line with the average equity ratio for the companies included in Witness Coyne’s proxy group that have similar credit ratings as Georgia Power or should not exceed 53%. (See Tr. 2713.) Ms. LaConte’s proposal of 53% equity is not based on a careful and accurate analysis of the credit rating implications of her recommendation. If a 53% equity level were adopted by the Commission, that decrease would cause over 200 basis points of deterioration in FFO to debt, harming the Company’s financial integrity. (Tr. 3956-57.) Additionally, as mentioned earlier, the 56% equity level set in the 2019 rate case appropriately mitigates the negative credit impacts of the TCJA. (Tr. 3957.) The impacts of the TCJA still exist; therefore, the Company must maintain its 56% equity ratio as carefully reviewed and approved by the Commission in the 2019 rate case. None of the capital structure recommendations proposed by PIA Staff or Intervenors adequately reflect Georgia Power’s need for continued access to capital on reasonable terms and the importance of maintaining its credit quality. (Tr. 4182.) Therefore, the Commission should reject the proposed capital structure changes proffered by PIA Staff and Intervenors. 
4.Errors in PIA Staff Witness Gorman’s FFO to debt calculations result in an incorrect conclusion that the Company’s future cash flows are sufficient to yield an appropriate credit rating going forward.
The Company’s request in this case carefully sought to ensure the Company could continue to access capital markets to meet the future needs of customers, continue to provide high levels of service and preserve the opportunity to earn a fair return on investments necessary to support a well-run and efficient utility. To evaluate how its recommendations would impact Georgia Power’s credit ratings, the Company calculated its FFO to debt ratio, which is a financial metric used to assess risk based on the Company’s ability to pay off debt using net operating income alone. (Tr. 799-800.) Mr. Gorman acknowledges the importance of maintaining the Company’s FFO to debt (financial risk metrics) above the credit rating agencies’ general benchmarks and tries to present his analysis with that in mind. (Tr. 3531-33.) However, due to an error in calculating the FFO to debt ratio, his resulting proposition would undermine the Company’s ability to meet the rating agencies’ expectations of restored credit metrics to levels supported by Witness Fetter. 
Debt imputation is the act of adding debt-like fixed obligations to a utility’s “traditional” debt balance when calculating FFO to debt. (Tr. 3953.) For many years, S&P has viewed certain components on the balance sheet, including pension obligations, lease liabilities and asset retirement obligations, as similar to debt and therefore adds them to the debt balance when calculating credit metrics. (Id.) S&P uses the information included on the Company’s publicly available balance sheet and accompanying financial disclosures to obtain the necessary information for the proper debt imputation calculation. (Id.) Georgia Power has no off-balance sheet debt, or any other off-balance sheet components, that the rating agencies use when calculating adjusted debt. (Tr. 3954.)
Mr. Gorman and the Company agree that these debt-like obligations are the type of debt imputations that are appropriate to include in estimating S&P’s FFO to debt calculation. (Tr. 3669, 3954.) However, due to the confusion around where these debt-like components are identified, these components were omitted from Mr. Gorman’s calculations. (Tr. 3671-72; 3678.) Mr. Gorman should have included the debt imputations related to these debt-like components in his estimated recalculation of S&P credit metrics, as he did in the 2019 rate case. (Tr. 3954.) By excluding more than $5.5 billion of debt imputations, Mr. Gorman’s credit metric expectation is significantly overstated and fails to illustrate the harmful effects of his recommended ROE and equity ratio. (Tr. 3952.) The magnitude of this error casts substantial doubt on the reliability of Witness Gorman’s statements supporting how his recommendations affect the Company’s credit quality. 
In his recalculation of one of S&P’s most important credit metrics, FFO to debt, Mr. Gorman omitted the important debt imputation methodology S&P has directed in previously issued authoritative publications. (See Tr. 3678, 3952.) These debt imputations are essential adjustments that must be made to arrive at a properly recalculated S&P credit metric. (Tr. 3676-78, 3953.) Had Mr. Gorman’s recalculation included the proper S&P debt imputations, it would have shown that the Company’s metrics, assuming Staff’s adjustments were accepted by the Commission, are insufficient to maintain sufficient access to capital at reasonable rates. Due to this error and the assumptions serving as the basis for Mr. Gorman’s recommendations, the Commission should not adopt PIA Staff’s proposed ROE and capital structure, which will be more deleterious to Georgia Power’s financial integrity than even PIA Staff intended. 
5.The cumulative effect of PIA Staff’s recommendations is extremely negative to the Company’s financial integrity and the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s ROE and capital structure recommendations.
The cumulative negative impact of Mr. Gorman’s recommended 9.45% ROE set point and capital structure of 51% equity, beginning in year 2024, coupled with the recommendations of Messrs. Smith and Trokey, would be dramatic and harmful to the Company’s financial integrity. (Tr. 3950.) As stated above, if one were to recalculate Georgia Power’s FFO to debt assuming Mr. Gorman’s recommended ROE of 9.45% and an equity ratio of 51% in 2024-2025, the results are extremely negative to the financial integrity of Georgia Power. (Tr. 3950.) Further, incorporating the detrimental effects of Messrs. Smith and Trokey’s recommendations, the 2023-2025 average FFO to debt would deteriorate by roughly 500 basis points. (Tr. 3951.) The resulting Georgia Power credit profile, assuming the collective recommendations by PIA Staff, would place the Company’s financial integrity at or below the lowest acceptable level recommended by Mr. Fetter. (Id.) A key component of this rate case is to maintain the credit quality Georgia Power has today, not diminish it.
Based on the latest Moody’s Credit Opinion issued October 26, 2022, Moody’s states:
Georgia Power operates within a credit supportive Georgia regulatory framework which generally provides timely recovery of prudent costs and investments and allows for the company to maintain a typically stable financial profile.
Moody’s goes on to state:
Georgia Power is currently in a pending rate case and will look to address the deferred fuel costs in a filing next February. Depending on resolution of these regulatory filings, we expect Georgia Power’s financial profile to improve including a ratio of CFO pre-W/C to debt of about 20% in 2023. Once the new nuclear units are in service and depending on the ultimate investment recovery allowed by regulators as result of the cost prudency review, the majority of which will be completed when Unit 4 is in service, we expect a substantial increase in Georgia Power’s cash flow which should translate into considerable improvement in credit metrics.
(Tr. 3951-52.)
The punitive adjustments that Mr. Gorman and Messrs. Smith and Trokey recommend would limit the FFO to debt metric to approximately 20% for the foreseeable future without the opportunity for improvement. Such limitation runs counter to the rating agencies’ expectation that, beginning in 2024, Georgia Power will remedy its credit profile such that it assumes a position of improved cash flow and adequate credit quality. (Tr. 118, 125, 132, 3952.) PIA Staff’s unreasonable recommendations would harm the Company’s credit profile, impair the Company’s ability to improve its credit metrics, disturb the constructive regulatory environment that has benefitted Georgia Power’s customers, and harm customers by restricting the Company’s access to debt capital at reasonable terms and costs. (Tr. 3951.)
6.The Company’s ability to manage its business to meet investor expectations ensures that Georgia Power is in a financially healthy position to meet customer expectations as it delivers clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electric service.
The changes PIA Staff and many of the intervening parties propose are ill-advised and fail to consider the impact of their proposals on the Company’s ability to access capital markets on reasonable terms to attract the substantial capital necessary to continue providing customers with the clean, safe, and reliable electric service they deserve and expect. (Tr. 4012.) Customers benefit when the Company balances the interests of all stakeholders because a financially healthy utility can access and attract the necessary capital (both debt and equity) to meet the needs of its customers and is more likely to achieve high levels of reliability and customer satisfaction. (Tr. 3950, 3977.) Customers also benefit from the Company’s operating efficiencies in its efforts to meet investor expectations. (Tr. 3950.) The efficiencies gained and the corresponding cost reductions that result from those efforts are provided to customers through savings in rates. (Tr. 264-65, 575.)
In addition to accessing capital markets on a timely basis at reasonable rates, a utility with strong credit ratings shares the benefit of those attractive interest rate levels with customers since the cost of capital is factored into utility rates. (Tr. 797-98, 806.) Conversely, the lower a regulated utility’s credit rating, the more that utility must pay to raise funds from debt and equity investors to carry out its capital-intensive operations. (Tr. 798.) In turn, the ratemaking process factors the cost of capital for both debt and equity into customer rates. (Tr.790-91.) This is especially true for a company like Georgia Power, which needs to attract significant levels of capital in the near term for continued transmission and distribution investments, environmental compliance, and the construction of new nuclear units, all while continuing to safely and reliably serve customers. (Tr. 794, 799, 806.) Therefore, the Commission should approve the rate adjustment and capital structure Georgia Power proposes in this case so that the Company remains positioned to continue meeting the energy needs of our customers and the communities we serve.
C.The Company Cannot Accept an ARP as Proposed by PIA Staff and Some Intervenors. 

Under Georgia law, the Company’s agreement is required to enter into an ARP. Without an agreement, this case must be resolved under traditional ratemaking standards, which involve a single test year analysis and would eliminate the positive attributes of a three-year predictable plan such as the institution of earnings ranges and earnings sharing mechanisms. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-25; 46-2-26.1. Without an ARP, more frequent, successive rate cases would be required over the next three years to accomplish what a three-year ARP can accomplish now. (Tr. 3958.) However, successive annual cases do not possess the positive attributes of rate certainty, rate stability, or the earnings sharing mechanism present in an ARP. (Id.) PIA Staff and several Intervenors have proposed radical and harmful modifications to the ARP that should be rejected. PIA Staff goes so far as to suggest that the Commission impose an earnings cap (see Tr. 1379), which is fundamentally inconsistent with the concept of the ARP sharing mechanism that benefits both customers and the Company. (Tr. 3945-46.) These proposed modifications undercut core aspects of the ARP, are unacceptable, and the Company would not agree to such an ARP. 
1.Continuation of the Company’s current earnings band will retain many of the positive structural features of the ARP for customers’ benefit. 
The Company requested to maintain the current approved earnings band of 9.50% to 12.00% within an ARP construct that provides for the Company to share 80% of any earnings above the band with customers. (Tr. 208.) Under this arrangement, all stakeholders win as demonstrated by the Company’s consistent delivery of exceptional service while keeping rates low in relation to other utilities. Maintaining the earnings band in its current form affords the Company the opportunity to continue the innovation and efficiencies in operations that the Company was able to identify since the last rate case. Customers have benefitted under this structure and will continue to benefit under the Company’s proposed ARP and earnings band if approved by the Commission.
2.PIA Staff and Intervenor-proposed changes to the earnings band fail to incentivize the Company to innovate and seek operational efficiencies that provide customer benefits.
a.The earnings sharing mechanism should not be modified and PIA Staff’s proposed earnings cap should be rejected. 
PIA Staff has proposed an earnings band ranging from 8.45% to 10.45% and recommended that 100% of earnings above the band be applied to deferred costs rather than split between customers and the Company. (Tr. 1379.) PIA Staff’s proposal amounts to an earnings cap by eliminating any retained revenues earned above the earnings band. (Id.) If an earnings cap was approved in this manner, the Company would not be appropriately incentivized to innovate and seek operational efficiencies during the ARP period, which is not in the best interests of customers. In addition, the top end of the earnings band proposed by PIA Staff is set artificially low, below even the Company’s cost of equity. PIA Staff’s elimination of the sharing mechanism and imposition of the earnings cap is unreasonable and should be rejected.
b.The Company will not accept a narrowed earnings band as recommended by DOD Witness Blank.
It appears that Mr. Blank is only recommending an ROE earnings band of 100 basis points above the ROE if the Commission authorizes an ROE below the Company’s requested 11%. (Tr. 3413.) This change would result in the top of the earnings band being lower than the 12% proposed by the Company. Otherwise, Mr. Blank reiterates his recommendation from the 2019 rate case that the earnings band be narrowed to +/- 50 basis points above and below the target ROE based on the assumption that it would reduce the risk to the Company. (Tr. 3412.) However, narrowing the earnings band may increase risks and uncertainty to customers, altering the fundamental nature of the ARPs that have been in place for the last 25 years. (Tr. 3962.) Georgia Power has rightly proposed to maintain the current approved earnings band of 9.50% to12.00%, consistent with the structure of the earnings bands previously approved by the Commission for the last several decades, and recommendations to the contrary should be rejected. (Tr. 208, 3962.)
c.The Commission should reject Commercial Group’s recommendation to adjust the earnings band commensurate with any adjustment to the requested 11% ROE set point. 
The Commission should reject the Commercial Group Witness Chriss’ recommendation to adjust the earnings band commensurate with any adjustment to the requested 11% ROE set point. (Tr. 2453.) If the Commission were to authorize a set point ROE less than the Company’s requested 11%, the bottom end of the earnings band should remain at 9.50% with the top end of the earnings band set at 12.00%, which is consistent with the structure of the earnings bands previously approved by the Commission.
3.Customers benefit from the Commission’s adoption of an ARP.
As a result of the ARP currently in place, the Company returned approximately $297 million to customers through the earnings sharing arrangement approved by the Commission in the 2013 base rate case, without jeopardizing superior customer satisfaction or reliability. (Tr. 3203, 3961.) In the absence of an ARP, there would be no earnings sharing mechanism or incentive for the Company to share earnings with customers. (Tr. 3945.) In addition, under the ARP, the amount customers pay through rates does not change when the Company’s earnings are within, or exceed, the band because rates are established at the ROE set point. (Tr. 3961.) As previously mentioned, the sharing mechanism is a critical component of the ARP and appropriately encourages the Company to look for efficiencies and process improvements. (Tr. 3961.) This incentive provides the opportunity for increased earnings that benefit both customers and the Company.
Three-year ARPs also avoid the potential for more frequent base rate cases, provide rate stability and predictability to customers, and signal the type of regulatory consistency viewed favorably by credit rating agencies. Further, because the Company surrenders its right to file a full rate case during the three-year term when the ROE is within the authorized earnings band, the Company assumes the risks of changing conditions during the term of the ARP. (Tr. 3958.) For example, the level of inflation may increase more than projected, the cost of debt or equity might increase, sales may be less than forecasted, or the Company’s overall cost of service may be more than forecasted. (Id.) A three-year rate plan also holds the Company accountable for inefficiencies or cost overruns due to rates being set at a predetermined level for the duration of the three-year period. (Id.) The Commission’s history of adopting three-year ARPs benefits customers and the Company alike. As such, the Commission should adopt the structure of the Company’s proposed ARP and reject proposals that would alter that structure.
4.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommended data revisions to the annual compliance filing process as an unnecessary erosion of the ARP process. 
The data elements currently updated in the annual Compliance Filings established in the Settlement Agreement approved in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case provide benefits to customers and equity to all parties. The additional elements that Staff proposes to incorporate into the annual Compliance Filings risk transforming this established, efficient, and effective process into a burdensome, mini rate case proceeding. (See Tr. 1373.) More importantly, as the number of data elements to be re-estimated and readdressed on an annual basis via the Compliance Filings increases, the more the benefits and value of the three-year ARP are undermined. (Tr. 3980; 4008.)
D.The Commission Should Reject Most of PIA Staff and Intervenors’ Revenue Requirement Adjustments. 
1.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to use the test period revenue requirement to set rates in the first year of the three-year ARP.
The Company has included in its filing both the test period revenue requirement and the revenue requirements for each plan year 2023 through 2025. (Tr. 226.) However, the Company supports setting the rates in the first year of the proposed ARP based on the revenue requirement for 2023. The revenue deficiency calculated for the calendar year ending December 31, 2023, rather than the deficiency for the split test year, reflects a more accurate depiction of the costs the Company will incur in its first year under a new ARP when the new rates are effective. Determining the revenue deficiency using the calendar year reduces regulatory lag. In the Company’s Errata Filing, the projected revenue deficiency based on the traditional test period and the 2023 calendar year is $459.8 million and $612.3 million, respectively, resulting in a difference of $152.5 million. This difference illustrates that basing the year one rate increase on the split test period’s revenue deficiency would create a revenue shortfall of $152.5 million in the very first year of the three-year period. Setting rates based on the test period would not permit the Company to timely recover its prudently incurred costs in serving customers. Therefore, the Commission should reject PIA Staff recommendations to use the test period revenue requirement to set first year rates in 2023.
2.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to use any revenue sufficiency to accelerate recognition of deferred costs. 
Georgia Power’s proposed case does not have a projected revenue sufficiency in 2023 to apply to deferred costs as proposed by PIA Staff. (Tr. 3959.) As stated previously, the Company’s revenue deficiency should be based on the calendar year 2023 revenue requirement rather than the test period, if the Company’s ARP is adopted. As such, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendations to apply any revenue sufficiency to accelerate recognition of deferred costs or to amortize a portion of CCR ARO compliance costs. Nevertheless, as stated in the Company’s rebuttal testimony, the Company generally agrees with PIA Staff’s concept of applying revenue sufficiency, if any, to certain regulatory assets to maintain rate stability for the customers. (Id.)
3.In the interests of greater rate stability, the Commission should adopt the Company’s proposed levelized rate increase rather than the step increase proposed by PIA Staff.
As part of its recommended ARP, the Company proposed a levelized approach to balance the benefit to customers from three years of stable and predictable rates with the Company’s need to timely recover its projected revenue deficiency. (Tr. 73.) Rate stability provides customers with a beneficial tool when budgeting for their utility costs and provides a predictable revenue stream for the Company. A levelized approach to rate increases shifts economic risks to the Company by insulating customers from the impacts of a possible downturn in the sales forecast or cost increases. (Id.) This approach makes practical sense as the Company is better equipped to manage negative economic factors that impact the cost of running the business, as opposed to asking customers to shoulder the impacts, which would occur with increased rates under step increases. In addition, customers are fairly compensated for the projected over-collection in the first two years of the levelization. For the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company proposes to defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving customers credit on the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted average cost of capital. This proposed deferral reduces the levelized revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully amortizes the advancement by the end of 2025. (Tr. 3960-61.)
In contrast, the step increase approach would increase customer rates by an increasing amount during each of the next three years. Although an annual increase would result in a lower than requested projected revenue requirement for years 2023 and 2024, the increase for 2025 would be greater than the levelized amount, and rates would increase more overall during the ARP. The levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to customers than a step increase approach over the three-year ARP. (Tr. 3960, 4017.) The fluctuation in projected costs year-to-year under a step approach creates uncertainty for customers. (Id.) Although the Company shares PIA Staff’s interest in rate stability, levelizing the proposed increases over the three-year ARP is the more appropriate approach for customers. 
4.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s O&M reductions that are based solely on prior historical levels of O&M expense. 
The Company’s O&M expenses are complex and driven by many factors, such as timing and scope of outages, scheduled maintenance cycles, repair work, medical claims, the labor market, and contractor rates. The conditions in the historical period are not identical to the test period and the Company and its business units consider all these factors in the development of their respective budgets for the upcoming three-year period. (Tr. 3967.) In addition, the historical years used by PIA Staff Witness Mugrace includes years 2020 and 2021, which were greatly impacted by the COVID pandemic and are not representative of a “normal” year that should be relied upon as reflective of costs to be incurred in the forecasted periods. (Id.) Simply put, while historical expenses may be an input into the Company’s budgeting process and considered in forecasting O&M expenses, it would be inappropriate to rely only on historical levels of O&M expense as the sole indicator of future expenses in the coming three years.
Annually, the Company utilizes input from all areas of the business to plan and budget the resources needed in support of expected workload for the upcoming five-year forecasted period. The Company considers planned outage schedules, expected maintenance activities, asset and vegetation management programs, customer service needs, technology, fleet, materials, staffing levels, contractor rates, and expected inflation when developing the budget. Budget assumptions are vetted and approved to ensure consistency across business units. During this process, Georgia Power reviews expenses and adjusts budgeted amounts based on known increases or decreases, accordingly. (Id.) Even with known cost increases, budgets may fluctuate over a given period of time due to multiple factors, including unit outages, changes in workforce needs for both Company labor and contract labor, Company benefit costs, and Commission-approved items such as storm recovery and demand side management (“DSM”) expenses. The Company does not create its budget by simply applying an inflation factor to all the actual or historic year expenses, in part because the Company’s O&M costs are not static over time. (Id.)
Moreover, the Company is constantly focused on managing its O&M expenditures, emphasizing cost containment as part of its corporate culture, as reasonable and necessary cost containment is in the best interest of both the Company and its customers. (Tr. 3968.) The Company has identified efficiencies in nearly every part of its business while still providing superior customer satisfaction and reliable service to customers. If historical expenses alone were considered in setting the appropriate O&M expenses for the ARP period, they would not adequately capture these efficiencies and cost savings for customers. The Commission should reject each of PIA Staff’s recommendations to replace the Company’s carefully budgeted O&M expense estimates with simple historical averages.
5.The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs associated with the innovation center in retail rates. 
PIA Staff Witness Mugrace argues that the Company’s costs incurred related to the Energy Innovation Center (“EIC”) should be removed from retail rate base because such costs are “not known and measurable” and “may not achieve any objective with respect to reliability, affordability, sustainability or customer engagement.” (Tr. 2027.) Mr. Mugrace likens these expenses to research and development costs and claims that there is no evidence of benefit to customers. (Id.) The Company disagrees with PIA Staff’s characterization of the EIC and the allegations that it provides no known or measurable benefit to customers. The EIC is related to but distinct from Research and Development in that the EIC is focused on education and exchanging ideas and information with all customer segments and stakeholders. (Tr. 3969.) The Company engages directly with customers through the EIC platform to share with customers general educational materials on innovative technologies, discuss end-use applications that will be beneficial for customers, bring stakeholders together, and establish a foundation for future partnerships between the utility and customers. Establishing a forum to circulate these ideas around technology innovation, and their market milestones, is fundamentally beneficial to Georgia Power’s customers because it has led to project activities that deliver cost saving efficiency and sustainability outcomes, with the potential to deliver grid-level benefits that improve reliability. (Id.)
For example, as explained by Mr. Robinson in the Company’s rebuttal hearing, the Company’s transition to using unmanned aerial systems to inspect transmission lines was an idea brought forth through the EIC. (Tr. 4039.) These types of efforts lead to direct customer engagement and benefits that are tangible, known, and measurable. The EIC is a resource for customers and stakeholders to learn about new energy technologies and how they may participate in their deployment, where applicable and appropriate. The Commission has previously approved the recovery of these costs in retail rates and should do so again here. 
6.The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs associated with service awards and recreation costs in retail rates. 
Contrary to the recommendations of Mr. Mugrace, the Company’s costs incurred related to service awards and recreation costs are not premature and are known and measurable. The Company’s service award program is a part of the overall compensation package offered to all full-time and part-time active employees, which helps motivate and retain the talent needed to efficiently operate the Company’s business. It demonstrates recognition and appreciation to employees for Company service at specific service intervals, which are estimable and measurable. Further, recognizing valuable and dedicated employees is important to the Company and valuable in providing consistent and top-notch service to Georgia Power’s customers. These costs are appropriately recovered in retail rates. The Commission has previously approved the recovery of these costs in retail rates and should do so again here.
7.The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs associated with association dues, community service, and experimental costs in retail rates. 
Witness Mugrace argues that costs associated with association dues, community service, and experimental costs do not benefit ratepayers in the provision of safe and adequate service. (Tr. 2038.) However, these costs are necessary to operating the business and our customers benefit from such activities. Through these efforts, the Company develops technology efficiencies that reduce O&M expenses and equipment improvements that improve the reliability of the grid. As a member of certain associations, the Company and its employees learn, grow, and contribute to the sustainability of the electric industry. For example, as members of the Institute of Electronic and Electronics Engineers, the Company has subject matter experts who sit on standards committees, which directly benefits the Company’s customers. (Tr. 4042.) Costs associated with community organizations, community development, economic development, and other business issues also benefit the communities we serve. Investments in these areas help the Company engage with customers and communities, which allows for growth and sustainability across the state. Again, the Commission has previously approved the recovery of these costs in retail rates and should do so again here. 
8.To appropriately account for the impact of rising inflation on the Company’s cost of service, the Commission should approve the Company’s calculation of incremental inflation in its revenue requirement. 
The Commission should reject Mr. Mugrace’s proposed inflation reductions as unnecessary and inappropriate. Mr. Mugrace argues that because inflation may not stay elevated during the three-year rate plan, the Company’s inflation adjustment forecast is too high resulting in rates being set too high for utility service. (Tr. 2043.) As stated in the Company’s rebuttal hearing, inflation has continued to increase since the Company’s filing in June 2022 (Tr. 4014) and is likely already underestimated rather than overestimated in the Company’s test year and each ARP year. Therefore, no reduction to the Company’s estimated incremental inflation is justified. 
In addition, Mr. Mugrace errs in calculating his inflation adjustment. As explained in the Company’s rebuttal testimony, the Company began to see significant price increases across all aspects of its business consistent with the rising inflation factors found in the Employment Cost Index (“ECI”) and Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) deflator. (Tr. 3971.) In response to these signals, the Company adjusted its projected O&M expenses to account for these cost increases in this filing by identifying labor and non-labor costs that typically would fluctuate based on inflation factors and applying the incremental increase in ECI and GDP inflation rates to the appropriate cost types. Although Mr. Mugrace states that the ECI is an appropriate source of inflation rates for labor costs (Tr. 2042), he ultimately used only the lower GDP index to escalate total non-Production O&M expenses, including labor and non-labor costs, to calculate his incremental inflation adjustment. (Tr. 2042-43.) However, to appropriately calculate an inflation adjustment, the O&M expenses need to be separated into labor and non-labor charges and the ECI and GDP rates need to be applied to the respective costs, as the Company did in performing its calculation of the incremental inflation adjustment. (Tr. 3972.) Mr. Mugrace’s adjustments underestimate and fail to capture the impact of inflation on the Company’s revenue requirement. Based on the foregoing error and the lack of need to adjust the incremental inflation in the Company’s proposal, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s inflation adjustment.
9.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposals that eliminate cost recovery for costs associated with the Company’s employee compensation programs.
To provide its award-winning service to customers, Georgia Power must attract and retain a high level of skilled and professional employees, who are paid competitively and appropriately based on their role. Georgia Power’s employee compensation program is designed to provide competitive compensation at levels targeting the 50th percentile of the appropriate markets. The Company’s well-balanced and market competitive compensation program provides different types of compensation that are commonly known and recognized by compensation professionals to be market appropriate to retain and attract talent, which includes (i) fixed based compensation, (ii) variable short-term compensation, and (iii) long-term stock-based compensation. The goal of providing different types of compensation is to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees are providing the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently serve its customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational and financial performance, thus aligning the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce.
a.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposed disallowances related to the structure of the Company’s total compensation plan.
According to the testimony of PIA Staff Witnesses Smith, Trokey, and Mugrace, certain portions of the Company’s total compensation program – the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to financial goals – do not benefit customers and should therefore be borne by shareholders instead of customers. (Tr. 1364-65, 2047-48.) The Company strongly disagrees with this opinion. The long-term incentive-based at-risk compensation program incentivizes employees to make decisions that are in the long-term best interests of customers and the Company. (Tr. 3977.) Customers benefit from a financially healthy company, which should be measured over the short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions made by the Company are optimized for both short- and long-term benefits. This is especially true in the utility industry, where decisions related to infrastructure and other major capital projects may have long-lasting financial consequences to all stakeholders, including customers. (Id.) Customers would be harmed if the Company were to drive its employees to sacrifice long-term financial health for short-term benefits. By designing the at-risk portion of the total compensation plan to include longer-term goals, the Company achieves an appropriate incentive balance that motivates employees to deliver safe, clean, and reliable electric service to our customers in a manner that is economically efficient both now and in the years that follow. (Id.)
The Company’s existing compensation policy is necessary to attract, motivate, and retain a highly skilled, dedicated workforce. If “at-risk” components of the employee compensation program, which includes the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to financial goals, were eliminated or significantly reduced, the Company would have to commensurately increase other components of its total compensation package – such as direct salary costs – to maintain its competitiveness as an employer within the industry and to continue to attract a skilled workforce. (Tr. 3975-76.) It would result in higher fixed costs and poor alignment of all stakeholders’ interests, including customers. (Tr. 3976)
PIA Staff’s proposed disallowance of these portions of the employee compensation program are not based upon any supporting data or market analysis that the Company’s expenses are excessive or that the programs are not competitive with the external market. There is also no basis for Mr. Mugrace’s assertion that, “[t]hese types of incentive costs benefit the Shareholders of the Company, and of SCS and SNC, and do not directly benefit the ratepayers in the area of safe and reliable utility service.” (Tr. 2332.) In fact, the opposite is true. Georgia Power could not efficiently operate its business or provide customers with clean, safe, and reliable utility service without its dedicated workforce. The budgeted costs to attract, retain, and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates. Moreover, the payroll tax expenses associated with stock-based compensation are a prudently incurred cost of doing business and, therefore, should also be recovered from customers. The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendations to remove the “at risk” portions and stock-based compensation from rates.
b.Georgia Power’s SERP, non-SERP, and retention expenses are necessary to retain highly skilled professional employees and are appropriately recovered in retail rates.
The Company’s costs related to Supplemental Executive Retirement Plan (“SERP”) and non-SERP benefit costs as well as employee retention are necessary expenses to ensure the attraction and retention of our highly skilled management team of leaders. The SERP and non-SERP programs are used to attract and retain senior management level employees across the various business units of the Company who have key roles, experiences, and skills in managing employees to ensure Georgia Power continues to provide reliable customer service and maintain our standards of safety. (Tr. 3973.) These types of programs are common for other companies who provide qualified pension plan benefits and are regularly evaluated to ensure that the total compensation for our employees, including these pension benefit programs, is market competitive. (Tr. 3973-74.) Furthermore, these non-qualified pension programs are not funded and therefore, are not covered by corporate-owned life insurance benefits as stated by Mr. Mugrace in his testimony. (Tr. 2036.) 
In addition, employee retention agreements are utilized as targeted, strategic compensation tools to retain high-performing employees in critical roles throughout the Company who offer deep experience, knowledge, and expertise that is highly sought after and crucial to retain. These agreements are entered into in direct response to specifically identified market pressures. Such pressures often include recruitment by Company competitors and peers, within and outside of the Company’s industry, as well as within and outside of the Company’s geographic footprint. Employee retention costs allow the Company to continue providing excellent service for our customer base and further maintain the resiliency of our infrastructure. (Tr. 3974.) Further, these costs offset the potential future expenses that would be incurred to recruit, develop, and train substitute employees for these key roles. As noted above, the Company could not offer superior customer service and clean, safe, and reliable service in the absence of a strong internal team. Therefore, these costs are operating expenses of the business and appropriate for inclusion in retail rates. 
c.Limiting the Company’s Performance Pay Program (“PPP”) compensation to just operational goals under incentivizes the Company’s employees to innovate and find better ways to serve customers. 
PIA Staff Witness Mugrace recommends disallowing two thirds of the Company’s PPP from recovery in retail rates. (Tr. 2047-48.) This recommendation undermines the strength of the Company’s compensation structure and will negatively impact every single employee of Georgia Power. Mr. Mugrace bases his recommendation on the assumption that compensation related to financial goals and earnings per share only benefit shareholders and exclude benefits for customers. (Id.) However, Mr. Mugrace offers no evidence or supporting testimony to validate such an assumption. In contrast, Company Witnesses clearly explained that without the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to the Company’s financial goals, the Company would have to commensurately increase other components of its compensation package, resulting in higher fixed costs. (Tr. 3950, 3977-78.) Goals based on financial metrics – when included in an incentive compensation plan designed to balance financial and operational goals – provide benefits to customers by reducing expenses, creating greater efficiencies in operations, ensuring a financially strong company, and allowing access to the capital markets at a low cost. (Tr. 3978.)
Georgia Power seeks to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees provide the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently serving its customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational and financial performance and allows for the alignment of the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce. A well-designed total compensation program using sound compensation practices and principles provides a balance between operational focus and financial focus for both the short term and longer term to drive employee behavior in ways that balance the interests of customers and shareholders alike. A compensation plan that contains only operational goals might inappropriately drive employees to use more financial resources than necessary to achieve operational success, while a plan that contains only financial goals might inappropriately drive employees to make decisions that adversely impact operational success. (Tr. 3978.) A balanced program of incentive compensation is a powerful tool that drives employees to put the customer at the center of all we do while sustaining the financial integrity of the Company.
Disallowing recovery of a financial component for a level of standard market pay forces a utility to consider modifying the entire incentive design to be based on operational measures alone (which are recoverable) and eliminate financial components of the incentive program design. However, making this change to an operational-based incentive program design would not be in the best interests of customers, as it would encourage focusing solely on operational performance without regard to or accountability for operational costs. (Id.) This imbalance is not in the best interests of customers, as the failure to appropriately consider operational cost impacts could lead to higher rate increases for customers in the future. As such, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposed disallowance and limitation of PPP compensation to only operational goals.
10.The Commission should approve the Company’s proposed treatment of the remaining net book value for units retired in the 2022 IRP as well as Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3. 
The Company has proposed to amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Wansley Units 1-2, Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1, which were retired by August 31, 2022 in accordance with the Company’s 2022 IRP, over their remaining useful lives as approved in Docket No. 42516. These proposed amortization periods are consistent with similar regulatory assets and PIA Staff did not object to the Company’s proposal. As such, the Commission should approve the Company’s proposed recovery and regulatory treatment for these assets. In addition, the Commission should approve the Company’s requested depreciation deferral and proposed nine- year amortization for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 beginning January 1, 2023. Based on the depreciation study filed in this case, Plant Bowen Units 1-2 are scheduled to be depreciated through 2027 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 are scheduled to be depreciated through 2028 in accordance with the retirement dates proposed in the 2022 IRP. For these units, the Company requests the Commission to defer the difference each year between the amount of depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are retired, the deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at the retirement date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending December 31, 2031. The Company’s request is reasonable and was unchallenged in this case. The Commission should approve the deferral and amortization of Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3 as requested by the Company without modification.
11.The Commission should reject GAM’s proposal to prohibit the Company from earning an ROE on unrecovered CCR ARO compliance costs until securitization is authorized.
As Witness Pollock admitted upon cross examination, securitization is not currently an option in Georgia and would require a change in state law to implement. (Tr. 2606.) As such, denying the Company the opportunity to recover all of its financing costs on any unrecovered CCR ARO compliance costs based on an option that is currently unlawful amounts to a disallowance and inappropriately and prematurely assumes that such costs were not prudently incurred. It would be inappropriate to deny the Company recovery of currently incurred financing costs based solely on the potential for future securitization legislation, over which neither the Company nor the Commission have control, and which might never come to fruition. Securitization is not needed as this Commission already has all the tools necessary to appropriately balance the needs of our customers and the Company. (Tr. 2740.)
The Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs are required to comply with federal and state environmental regulations and are appropriately included in rate base. The Commission has approved the Company’s Environmental Compliance Strategy, which included the Company’s ash pond closure plans and CCR strategy, as part of the 2022 IRP in Docket No. 44160. (Tr. 1285.) No party in this case filed testimony or presented evidence challenging the appropriateness of the Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs sought to be recovered in this case. Further, there have been no changes in the CCR permits issued to Georgia Power or in federal or state CCR regulations since the 2022 IRP that change the Company’s compliance strategy. As such, the Company should be able to timely recover these prudently incurred compliance costs and have the opportunity to recover all of its financing costs, including equity. Witness Pollock’s recommendation prevents the Company’s ability to recover its actual costs of financing these required expenditures. In addition, Witness Pollock’s proposed ratemaking treatment contradicts past treatment of ARO compliance costs and such departure from past precedent is unjustified. The Commission should not unduly restrict the Company’s ability to timely recover its prudently incurred CCR ARO compliance costs pursuant to the current and unchallenged recovery methodology and reject Witness Pollock’s deferral for securitization proposal. 
12.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s alternative revenue forecast. 
PIA Staff Witnesses Baron and Wellborn offered testimony proposing nothing more than a higher, alternative revenue forecast for the Commission’s consideration to lower the Company’s revenue requirement in this case. While the Company appreciates that differences of opinion may exist in the intricacies of forecast modeling, the Company believes the projected revenue forecast included in its filing continues to be the most reasonable and appropriate method to determine the Company’s revenue requirement at present rates. Further, PIA Staff agreed that the Company’s method was reasonable and did not identify any errors or corrections in the Company’s filing. (Tr. 1535.) With no shortcomings in the Company’s revenue forecast, there is no action for the Commission to take and no weight should be placed on the recommendations of Witnesses Baron and Wellborn. 
13.Other than the proposed extension of the depreciable life of Plant Hatch, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s depreciation recommendations.
PIA Staff Witness Kaufman removes contingency from plant dismantlement cost estimates for coal and gas production facilities, concluding that contingency represents “unexpected outcomes” and therefore, should be removed from the depreciation expense calculations for the Company’s coal and gas plants. However, when developing cost estimates, contingency is a fundamental element of a comprehensive and transparent estimate. Contingency is necessary to acknowledge and mitigate the risk of inherent cost uncertainties that accompany capital-intensive projects such as dismantlement of a coal or gas fired electric generation facility.
Mr. Kaufman’s depreciation adjustments fail to consider factors that may influence current and future plant dismantlements that have not impacted historic plant dismantlements. For example, prior to the retirement of Plant Hammond in 2019, historic retired facilities did not have air environmental controls (e.g., baghouses, scrubbers, Selective Catalytic Reduction Systems). These environmental controls significantly increase the footprint and dismantlement cost of the remaining coal facilities. The Company’s historic dismantlement cost estimates, including contingency, have been included in retail rates and the Commission should continue to approve such cost recovery here.
