In addition, PIA Staff Witness Kaufman presented depreciation rates that included calculation errors for Production, Transmission, Distribution, and General functions. When adjusting the lives for these functions, Mr. Kaufman simply extended the useful lives. He failed to consider the impact to other fundamental inputs such as interim retirements and theoretical reserves. By omitting the full impact of the proposed life extensions, Mr. Kauffman proposed depreciation rates that do not properly allocate the capital cost of the Company’s assets over their average service lives. 
Further, when applying Mr. Kauffman’s proposed adjustments to easements, PIA Staff failed to apply the proposed rates to the proper plant in service balances. Rather than applying the proposed rate for easements to the plant in service balance for easements, PIA Staff applied the functional composite rate, which includes easements, to the functional plant in service balance that does not include easements. The combination of these errors, along with the omission of contingency as described above, resulted in understated depreciation expense. Therefore, the Commission should reject all of Mr. Kaufman’s depreciation adjustments apart from extending the assumed life of Plant Hatch, with which the Company agrees. 
E.Electric Vehicle (“EV”) Charging Facilities Benefit Georgia Power Customers and the State and the Commission Should Reject PIA Staff’s Recommendation to Remove Georgia Power’s Electric Transportation Initiatives from Retail Rates. 
1.The Company plays a critical role in supporting the growth of the electric transportation ecosystem in Georgia and its investment provides benefits to Georgia Power’s customers.
The Company’s continued investments in a statewide EV charging infrastructure have enhanced Georgia’s electric transportation capabilities, realized economic savings, and provided environmental benefits while fostering the growth of statewide EV usage. As customer demand for EVs and access to fast charging continues to grow, Georgia Power proposes corresponding expansion of its investments in its customers. (Tr. 462, 464.)
Georgia Power seeks to expand its investment following the successes of those approved in the previous rate case. The 2019 Rate Case approved an $18 million capital investment in the Make Ready program, allowing the Company to partner with communities to deploy EV chargers to a variety of customers, ranging from local businesses to schools and churches. (Tr. 425-26.) The program has since been fully subscribed and now has a waiting list for future participants. (Tr. 426.) Similarly, multimillion dollar investments in the Community Charging program have allowed the Company to expand statewide access to EVs. The Company seeks to transition the program to offer and serve charging stations of last resort in underserved communities that lack a competitive charging market. (Tr. 370, 429, 3964.) 
With two major EV manufacturing announcements this year alone, the demand for EVs continues to increase, requiring further expansion of the state’s charging infrastructure to meet the energy needs of Georgia Power’s customers. (Tr. 78, 461-62; 3964.) The Company’s proposed investments will continue to benefit all Georgians through environmental impacts and downward pressure on energy rates. (Tr. 377.)
2.The Commission should approve the Company’s requested investment in Make Ready infrastructure, which appropriately fosters greater adoption of EV charging infrastructure by commercial and industrial customers. 
In light of the Make Ready program’s benefits and the success of the program since its approval in the 2019 Rate Case, the Commission should approve the Company’s request to continue investing in Georgia’s EV infrastructure by expanding the Make Ready program. (Tr. 141.) To satisfy its obligation of meeting the growing energy needs of its customers, the Company proposes investments of approximately $27 million in each of 2023, 2024, and 2025 in the Make Ready program. (Tr. 386, 3965.) Through the program, the Company will work with customers to build out the infrastructure behind the meter for qualifying commercial and industrial customers to facilitate the installation of EV charging stations. (Tr. 140, 373, 3964-65.) 
All ratepayers will experience benefits from the expansion of the Make Ready program. (Tr. 462-63.) EV owners and commercial partners will experience the firsthand benefits of electric transportation infrastructure upgrades. In addition, the Company’s Rate Impact Measure (“RIM”) positive analyses evidence downward pressure on rates for all ratepayers. (Tr. 369-70, 377, 388; 3966.) 
3.The Commission should approve the Company’s requested Community Charging funding in support of Georgia Power’s service as a provider of last resort in rural and underserved communities.
The Commission should also approve the Company’s requested Community Charging funding because it supports expansion of the state’s EV charging infrastructure, helping make EV ownership and usage more accessible to Georgians. With only approximately 3,000 EV charging plugs across Georgia, large swaths of the state’s rural and economically challenged communities do not have sufficient access to EV charging infrastructure, leaving countless Georgians without the ability to adopt EVs as a primary means of transportation. (Tr. 950.) The Company’s proposed Community Charging investment will allow it to reach underserved communities by installing chargers of last resort along statewide travel corridors without impacting existing competitive charging markets. (Tr. 370, 428-29, 460; 3965.) Presently, Georgia Power owns 59 EV charging locations through the Community Charging program, representing only 3% of Georgia’s EV charging infrastructure. (Tr. 951.) The Company’s proposed investments in the Community Charging program will support areas of the state without sufficient access to EV chargers by deploying an additional 33 locations over the next three years. (Tr. 967; 4088.)
F. The Commission Should Reject PIA Staff’s Proposal to Gut the Grid Investment Plan by Removing Capital Dollars for the Transmission Plan and Removing 85% of the Investments for the Distribution Plan.
Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan, approved by the Commission in the 2019 base rate case, is a 12-year program that focuses on improving distribution reliability and replacing aging transmission assets. (Tr. 244.) The Commission previously approved the Company’s request to invest $1.3 billion for the 2020-2022 period. (Tr. 244.) In the current case, the Company requested approval of dollars associated with the next three-year period of the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.) The Grid Investment Plan continues to focus on distribution investments that will improve reliability for customers and transmission investments to replace aging system assets. (Tr. 245.) Ignoring the need for the critical investments addressed by the Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff asks that this Commission reverse course and (1) reject all transmission investments planned for the next three years, and (2) remove 85% of the planned distribution investments. (Tr. 1558, 1582, 1584.) The Commission should reject this extreme recommendation, which is unreasonable, unsupported by the record in this case, and contrary to the best interests of customers.
1. The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendations concerning the transmission portion of Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan.
As outlined in the 2019 Rate Case and again in the current docket, the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan focuses on replacing aging assets that have or will exceed their Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. (Tr. 337, 3983.) Company Witness Robinson provided robust testimony explaining that Georgia Power’s transmission equipment installed during the 1950s, 1960s, and 1970s, is now approaching 50, 60, and 70 year lives. (Tr. 342-43, 4069-70.) Due to the large percentage of transmission equipment that is beyond or nearing its Company expected life, Georgia Power carefully designed the Grid Investment Plan to target the replacement of these assets to prevent transmission system reliability deterioration across the electric system. (Tr. 3983.) Based on the Company’s extensive operational experience, the likelihood of failure for the transmission assets included in the plan will exponentially increase as the assets age beyond their expected life. (Id.) If Georgia Power does not continue making these necessary investments in its transmission system, the reliability of the electric system will be negatively impacted due to increased failures related to aged transmission equipment. (Id.) Such failures can be catastrophic and simultaneous in nature. (Id.) Replacing these aged assets will (1) reduce the number of emergency failures, (2) mitigate system operation reliability concerns, and (3) avoid higher maintenance and replacement costs. Further, these investments will help ensure the safety of the general public and the Company’s employees. (Tr. 3983.)
a.The Company is not “prematurely replacing” transmission equipment in the Grid Investment Plan.
PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens mischaracterize the Company’s Grid Investment Plan strategy for the replacement of aged transmission assets as “premature equipment replacement.” (Tr. 1558.) Importantly, the Company relied on its extensive operational and maintenance experience, outage and failure history, and ability to extend the expected life of its transmission assets to determine the age at which it should replace the transmission assets within the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3986.) The Company also reviewed, analyzed, and compared its replacement assumptions against industry expected life ages for the transmission equipment identified for replacement in the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.)
As the Company testified during direct hearings, the Company used the bathtub curve to explain equipment failure rate variation over time. (Tr. 333-34.) Witness Alvarez and Stephens’ testimony presented a bathtub curve based only upon the estimated useful life of a hypothetical piece of equipment. (Tr. 1572.) In contrast, Georgia Power based its bathtub curve on the Company’s expected life for specific Georgia Power transmission assets. (Tr. 3982.) The life expectancy of Company equipment is supported by internal maintenance procedures, past experiences, equipment specifications, and equipment monitoring. (Tr. 3986.) Importantly, estimated useful life and expected life are two very different terms. (Id.)
Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens misrepresent the point at which the Company is replacing transmission equipment as part of the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3987.) They assume the Company is replacing equipment in the middle of its estimated useful life bathtub curve, whereas the Company is actually replacing equipment in the “end-of-life period” where failures occur at an exponential rate due to age-related degradation of equipment. (Id.) To be clear, the Company is strategically targeting transmission projects within this “end-of-life period” to prevent customer outages, avoid unplanned and catastrophic equipment failure, and better manage replacement costs for the benefit of customers. (Id.) The result, from both a reliability and cost perspective, would be worse if the Company waited until this aging equipment failed to replace it. Replacing transmission equipment after catastrophic failure costs 2 to 13 times more than strategically replacing this equipment through the Company’s Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 338, 3987.) The Company has presented the next three years of transmission projects within the Grid Investment Plan to strategically replace equipment that will have exceeded its Company expected life by the end of 2031 in the most coordinated and cost-effective manner, which is in the best interests of customers. (Tr. 3983.) Therefore, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s mischaracterization of the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan and approve the Company’s plan as presented.
b.The Company employed objective approaches for identifying aging transmission assets that warrant replacement as part of the Grid Investment Plan as recommended by PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana.
PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens assert that the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan should be based on objective approaches and diagnostic testing rather than simply on asset age. (Tr. 1558.) However, they ignore that the Company used objective measures and relied on its long history of robust maintenance practices, internal experience, and extensive operational knowledge to identify which transmission equipment to replace as part of the Grid Investment Plan, including preventive and diagnostic testing. (Tr. 3982.) To that end, Georgia Power incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace transmission equipment that will be beyond the Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. (Id.) Notably, through its comprehensive maintenance practices, the Company has already extended the life of this transmission equipment beyond its industry expected life. (Id.) For example, while the industry expected life for transformers and breakers is 35 years and 25 years, respectively, the Company’s expected life for transformers and breakers is 50 years and 35 years. (Id.) Accordingly, the Company has already adopted and implemented the objective approaches that PIA Staff recommends in identifying the proposed transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan. 
Further, the Commission should ignore Witness Stephens’ isolated example of a single 99-year-old transformer on Georgia Power’s system. (Tr. 1735, 3984.) This isolated example should not be used as a basis for assuming that the Company’s entire transformer fleet can or should operate that far beyond Company expected life. As shown in the Company’s rebuttal testimony, the percentage of transmission and distribution substation equipment within 10 years of the oldest vintages for such equipment on Georgia Power’s system represents only approximately 2% of each equipment category, with power transformers being less than 0.2%. (Tr. 3985.) Georgia Power acknowledges that a small portion of substation equipment can operate well past Company expected life. (Id.) However, this is the exception to the rule and the overwhelming majority of Georgia Power’s transmission and distribution assets should not be expected to operate safely and reliably for this long. Over the last 10 years, Georgia Power substation transformers have failed at an average age of 43 years, which is already past the industry expected life of 35 years, but earlier than the age of 50 years at which this equipment will be replaced within the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3983.) Given the large number of transmission assets that will exceed their Company expected life in the near future, replacement of these assets through the Grid Investment Plan is the appropriate solution, and better for customers and the transmission system than simply operating these aged assets for an unknown period of time until they fail. (Tr. 3985-86.)
Finally, contrary to Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana’s overreliance on inspection and performance testing for determining when equipment should be replaced, routine diagnostic testing and inspections cannot always accurately predict when equipment will fail. (Tr. 3982-83.) As Witness Robinson testified, of the 196 substation transformers that failed in-service over the last ten years, 108 (55% of these transformers) failed after passing such diagnostic tests and inspections. (Tr. 3983, 4058, 4060.) The Company’s extensive operational experience informed its recommendation to replace transmission assets that have or will exceed their Company expected life by 2031 through the Grid Investment Plan rather than solely relying on the results of the Company’s routine diagnostic tests and inspections. Therefore, the Commission should give little weight to the arguments of PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens in their attempt to discredit the Company’s strategic and thoughtful plan for transmission asset replacement.
c.A cost benefit analysis for the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan is not practical or necessary for the Commission to approve the Company’s request in this case.
Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens also argue that the Commission should not approve any transmission Grid Investment Plan funding in the absence of a cost benefit analysis. (Tr. 1587, 1746.) As explained repeatedly by the Company in this case (and in the 2019 Rate Case), it is impractical to perform a cost-benefit analysis for transmission investments included in the Grid Investment Plan due to the dynamic and networked nature of the transmission system. (Tr. 3984, 4067-68.) Several factors impact transmission system operation and could change suddenly in any such cost benefit study, including system loading and flows, facility and generation outages, and weather. (Tr. 3984.) The ability to conduct a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit analysis of transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan is further complicated by the type of failure that could occur (controlled or catastrophic), impacts to other capital projects, lead times for replacement equipment, and the cost of generation redispatch due to loss of transmission elements. (Id.) The constant change in these variables would produce a cost-benefit analysis with unreliable conclusions that could be rendered obsolete or invalid at any given time. The results of such analysis would be questionable, at best. (Id.) For these reasons, the Company used asset age to determine capital investment in each transmission facility to mitigate the risk of outages associated with a significant and growing number of assets that are approaching or have exceeded their Company expected lives. (Id.) Thus, even if the Company were to prepare a cost benefit analysis, it would not provide the Commission meaningful information on which to rely. Therefore, yet again, the Commission should dismiss PIA Staff’s attempt to undermine the Company’s Transmission Grid Investment Plan request.

2. The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to gut the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan by removing the undergrounding and basic insulation level investment packages.
The Company’s proposed undergrounding and Basic Insulation Level (“BIL”) investment packages should not be removed from the Grid Investment Plan. If it were to eliminate these investment packages, the Commission would eliminate 85% of the planned distribution investments included in the Grid Investment Plan—essentially forestalling the purpose of the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, which focuses on ensuring lasting reliability improvements while minimizing the total cost to customers. (Tr. 3989.) In addition, the Company disagrees with PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ assertion that “there is no pending reliability emergency.” (Tr. 1565, 1567, 3989.) Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue to worsen every year that investments are not made. Between 2013 and 2021, the Company’s system average interruption duration index (“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately 44% and system average interruption frequency index (“SAIFI”) worsened by approximately 10%. (Tr. 3989.) For a subset of feeders (also referred to as circuits) in the system, and the customers on those feeders, the decline in reliability is even more pronounced. This worsening reliability has significant negative impacts on customer experience due to more frequent and longer outages, as well as increases in the economic cost incurred by customers. (Tr. 3989-90.)
Removing the undergrounding and BIL investment packages from the Grid Investment Plan would result in significantly longer and more frequent outages across the system, as these two packages are the most effective way to permanently address the root causes of outages on the nearly 400 feeders selected for these investment packages. (Tr. 3990.) Without these investments, the Company would achieve an end-state SAIDI of approximately 127 by 2031, essentially equal to the program starting point SAIDI in 2019, compared to the Grid Investment Plan’s current projected end-state of approximately 80. (Id.) The results of PIA Staff’s recommendations would maintain the system-level SAIDI at third quartile levels and stall reliability improvement opportunities. (Id.) As such, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposal to remove the undergrounding and BIL packages from the Company’s Distribution Grid Investment Plan.
a.The Grid Investment Plan is a dynamic multiyear effort that the Company should appropriately revise as lessons are learned, not abandon simply because circumstances change and early results reveal more efficient future options.
Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens criticize the Company’s revised SAIDI target from 65 as filed in the 2019 Rate Case to 80 in the 2022 Rate Case. (Tr. 1563.) They claim that 23.1% deterioration in target system SAIDI after only three years “calls into question the Company’s projection of the actual performance improvement customers will receive for very large capital investment made by Georgia Power.” (Id.) In reality, the revision to the end-state SAIDI targeted in the Company’s Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan is a result of lessons learned from the first three years of implementation and not a reflection of changed reliability expectations. (Tr. 3990.) In fact, the Company has seen better than expected reliability improvements from the set of investments that have completed construction. (Id.) Initial results indicate an improvement of approximately 65% in both SAIDI and SAIFI for Grid Investment Plan projects that have been completed for over a year and cycled through both a winter and summer peak since completion. (Tr. 3990-91.) The investments are working as intended and are delivering improved reliability for Georgia Power’s customers. (Tr. 3991.)
Based on insights and lessons learned in the first three years of the program, Georgia Power confirmed the following: (i) that the overall strategy and economic rationale supporting the Grid Investment Plan is optimal for customers; (ii) the total cost required to deliver individual investments is higher than originally projected because of increases in scope and cost of work (e.g., inflation, COVID shortages); and (iii) while undergrounding remains the most effective solution for improving customer reliability, the use of undergrounding should be carefully evaluated to ensure it is used only where it is the most cost efficient solution. (Tr. 3991.) With these learnings, the Company appropriately reassessed and refreshed the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, employing the same methodology used in the 2019 Rate Case, to ensure that the investments being made continue to result in the lowest total cost to customers. (Id.) As a result of this evaluation, the Company determined that the set of distribution investments in the best interests of customers are projected to deliver an 80 end-state SAIDI as compared to the 65 end-state SAIDI included in the 2019 Rate Case plan, which are still first quartile results compared to industry peers. (Id.) The Company considers this intermittent refinement of the 10-year Grid Investment Plan essential for ensuring that customer capital is managed responsibly. (Id.) The outcome of these refinements is a distribution plan that continues to deliver, where it is economically beneficial, strong reliability benefits for customers. 
b.The distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan appropriately addresses feeder level issues through system level optimization. 
The Company’s distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan optimizes benefits at the system level rather than the feeder level because the cost of system reliability is borne by all customers served by Georgia Power. (Tr. 3992.) In other words, the cost for capital investment and ongoing O&M expense for the grid is paid for by all customers, regardless of each customer’s reliability experience and regardless of whether the customer’s individual feeder is included within the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.) The Company intentionally chose to optimize benefits across the system to ensure the investments made were in the best interest of all customers instead of just the subset of customers served by an individual feeder. (Id.)
Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend that the Company conduct a root cause analysis for every feeder on the Company’s system (Tr. 1608), which implies that the Company has not done such analysis. That is a misconception. Each and every distribution investment within the Grid Investment Plan is evaluated on a feeder-by-feeder basis with a root cause analysis. (Tr. 3992.) The Company does exactly the level of analysis suggested by Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens. The initial ‘hurdle’ for any feeder to qualify for investment is that the feeder has had sustained poor performance over the past five years. (Tr. 3993.) The Company then undertakes a second evaluation to determine the root causes of outages on each feeder to determine if the outages were a result of vegetation, vehicles, animals, or weather and how that compares to the rest of the system. (Id.) An evaluated feeder qualifies for a distribution investment package that would permanently resolve the outage cause(s) only if it experiences a higher percentage of outages driven by the particular cause(s) than the system average. (Id.) In this way, the Company deploys standard, repeatable criteria systematically across all feeders to identify outliers in each category of outage cause.
Therefore, prior to optimizing the plan, the Company has completed a feeder-by-feeder, root cause analysis on the entire distribution system. (Tr. 3993.) Through this analysis, the Company determines whether a feeder should be considered for any investment and, if so, which of the six possible investment options specifically would address the root causes of reliability issues on that feeder. (Id.) For each of the possible investment packages identified during the root cause analysis, the Company projects the costs and the reliability benefits specific to deploying qualified investment package(s) on each feeder. (Id.) The Company then evaluates the impact of all possible combinations of feeder level investments to determine the final investment plan that provides the best reliability and the lowest total cost to customers. (Tr. 3994.) As a result, the Company considers the direct cost of investments compared to the costs customers bear from increased outages if no investment was made. (Id.) Contrary to the allegations raised by PIA Staff, the Company appropriately considers feeder level costs and benefits prior to optimizing its proposed distribution investments.
c. The Company’s undergrounding and BIL investment packages offer cost effective and permanent solutions to systemic feeder level problems that cannot be addressed by greater vegetation management as proposed by PIA Staff. 
PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens wrongly claim that increased vegetation management is an effective alternative to the undergrounding and BIL Grid Investment Plan packages. (Tr. 1602.) PIA Staff offered no independent analysis to validate their assertion. (Tr. 3995.) Though vegetation management has an important place in managing overall system reliability, it is a temporary solution that cannot address a variety of other outage drivers, such as vehicles, wildlife, and lightning. (Id.)
PIA Staff could not explain how vegetation management could generate the same expected level of reliability improvement as the dedicated investment packages included in the Distribution Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3995.) During cross examination, Mr. Stephens confirmed that no analysis had been done to determine how much it would cost to actually improve reliability through vegetation management. (Tr. 1874.) Mr. Stephens himself also acknowledged that he had merely made an assumption about vegetation management as a cost-effective alternative. (Tr. 1872-73.) Many activities fall within the category of ‘vegetation management,’ and Mr. Stephens did not complete any analysis to determine which activities specifically would need to be added or increased to obtain reliability improvements through vegetation management. (Tr. 1875.) Finally, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens proposed a legislated expansion of utility rights of way as a potential approach to improving reliability. (Tr. 1605, 1877.) Mr. Alvarez confirmed at the hearing that he had not undertaken any analysis of how long it would take or how much it would cost to pass such legislation. (Tr. 1877-78.) As such, PIA Staff failed to demonstrate how its proposed alternatives to the Grid Investment Plan were either less costly or more beneficial than the Company’s proposal.
In addition, Staff’s assertions that the undergrounding and BIL packages are not cost-effective are unfounded. PIA Staff systematically underestimated investment benefits by not accurately representing the cost to customers of foregoing undergrounding and BIL. (Tr. 3994.) First, PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens did not account for the continued reliability degradation that customers would experience if feeders selected for undergrounding or BIL are denied investment, underestimating the economic cost to customers of the increased outages. (Id.) Second, PIA Staff did not quantify the outage repair-related and vegetation-related O&M expenses that will be incurred if these feeders are removed from the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.) In contrast, the Company has taken extreme care to account for all costs and benefits that will be experienced by customers during the formulation of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company is confident that the distribution investments that are part of the Grid Investment Plan are in the best interests of customers, resulting in the greatest reliability benefits for the lowest total cost to customers.
d.The Company’s Grid Investment Plan already encompasses the qualities of a Worst Performing Feeder Program as recommended by PIA Staff. 
In searching for an alternative to the Company’s Distribution Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff proposes that the Company simply invest in a more robust “Worst Performing Feeder” Program. (Tr. 1620.) Such a program would include (1) clear and repeatable criteria to identify poor-performing feeders, (2) a dedicated budget and associated personnel to administer the program, and (3) standard tracking and reporting on the program’s progress. (See Tr. 1608.) To be clear, the distribution portion of the Company’s Grid Investment Plan already encompasses the key qualities that Staff recommends as part of its proposed ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program. 
First, as is presented in the Customer Benefit Analysis, the Company has clear and specific criteria for each distribution investment package in the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3997.) This includes reliability-specific criteria (e.g., SAIDI, SAIFI), physical attribute-related criteria (e.g., overhead vs. underground), and criteria that identify the root causes of outages (e.g., outages due to vegetation or vehicles, feeder load). Where the root cause is unclear, not addressable, or cost prohibitive, an investment is not made. (Id.)
Second, the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan has a designated budget and designated personnel similar to what PIA Staff recommends. (Tr. 3998.) In the 2019 Rate Case, the budget for the first three years of the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan was approved by the Commission. Moreover, since 2019, the Company has established a team solely devoted to the Grid Investment Plan – from program and project managers, to engineers, supply chain personnel, and construction workers on the ground.
Third, Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend “standards for tracking spending, projects, and results.” (Tr. 1608.) The Company rigorously tracks all spending for the Grid Investment Plan (at both the project and program level), as well as the reliability performance of each Distribution feeder – before, during, and after investment. (Tr. 3998.) This fact is evidenced by the adjustments made by the Company in the overall plan and optimal range for investment. The Company also reports on the Grid Investment Plan performance during the Company’s rate case and makes itself available to respond to any data requests on the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.)
Finally, Staff failed to conduct the analysis required to validate its recommendation that the Company should focus only on feeders that are performing 2.5 times worse than average as part of its ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program. (Tr. 3996.) This lack of analysis is inconsistent with Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ own assertions that grid investments should only be made if they are cost effective at the feeder level. PIA Staff did not consider the type of investment that would be required to address the root cause of outages on feeders that are performing 2.5 times worse than average. (Id.) For many of these feeders, undergrounding and BIL would be the best solution to address root causes – yet both are investment packages within the Grid Investment Plan that Staff recommends removing. The distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan encompasses the very qualities that PIA Staff recommends should be part of the Company’s ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program and will provide key reliability benefits in a cost-effective manner for the benefit of customers. Therefore, the Commission should approve the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan as proposed by the Company, without modification.
G.To Appropriately Integrate Additional Distributed Energy Resources (“DER”) to the System, the Commission Should Approve the Company’s Requests for DERMS and Improved Communication and Visibility Requirements.

1.The Company must invest in DERMS to optimize the benefits from increased DER penetration on the system. 
PIA Staff proposes to reject DERMS spending until the level of DER on the grid increases and claims that existing systems and processes can manage DER. (Tr. 1625-26.) While current software systems allow for the monitoring of power flows and equipment status, they lack the enhanced monitoring and operational capabilities of DERMS that are necessary to allow for the optimization of an electric system that takes full advantage of the proliferation of DER. (Tr. 212, 259-60, 3999.) The systems currently in place cannot manage the substantial number of DER expected to connect to the electric grid in the coming years. As more DER are added to the system, it is essential that the Company have visibility, predictability, and control (where applicable) of these resources. Failure to do so could compromise the Company’s ability to accurately forecast and optimize the full array of generation resources and could negatively impact power quality and reliability. (Tr. 3999.) Further, current software systems do not allow a system operator to process the significant amount of data coming in from DER or make informed decisions quickly enough to optimize grid operation. (Id.)
In contrast, a DERMS that is fully integrated with the Company’s other grid control systems will allow the Company to continually optimize larger portions of the grid and address a variety of constraints, such as economic dispatch, Volt/VAR optimization, and reliability. (Id.) Additionally, DERMS will allow the Company to consider demand side resources alongside supply side resources in future IRPs, as ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. The Company’s implementation of DERMS at this point will also allow for maturation of the DERMS solution as thousands or tens of thousands of additional DER are connected to the grid. If DER growth continues unrestricted without the installation of DERMS, the Company risks a loss of grid control, which would compromise grid reliability and stability. (Id.) A DERMS solution is a natural, logical, and necessary step for the Company to prepare for the growth and integration of variable and distributed energy resources.
To reiterate, there are significant risks to grid stability if the Company were to delay implementing DERMS. (Tr. 4000.) Beginning implementation of a DERMS solution now allows for the full DERMS capabilities to be developed in parallel with increasing DER penetration across the electric grid. Further, the complexity of integrating DERMS with the Company’s existing critical software systems will require a significant amount of time. Undertaking DERMS over the next three years reduces the risk of restricting DER interconnections in the future because of a lack of DER forecasting, control, and optimization capability. (Id.) The Commission should approve the Company’s requested funding for DERMS in this case to optimize the integration of DER on the Georgia Power system. 
2. Improved communications interfaces with customer generation systems will enable the Company to safely and reliably integrate DER operating in parallel with the system. 
Contrary to the arguments posed by PIA Staff Witnesses Barber, Deitchman, and Watkins (Tr. 2195), the Company did not propose edits to Section G of its Rules and Regulations to require unconditional access and control of customer generators. Rather, the Company proposed requiring that the customer generation facilities interconnecting to the Company’s electric system include a communications interface, installed and maintained by the customer, that supports information exchange with, and control by, Georgia Power so that the Company may continue to operate the electric system in a safe, reliable, and efficient manner. (Tr. 4000.)
The proposed requirement to allow for communication and increased DER visibility is critical to maintaining the system’s safety and reliability. Without visibility, it is impossible for a utility to ascertain the status, availability, real time actual output, and forecast availability of a resource. Without control, it is impossible for a utility to update functional settings such as active power setpoint, frequency response, and reactive power modes in response to real time grid conditions. (Tr. 4001.) These functions enable real time economic optimization of the electric grid. The Company is requesting that all existing and future customer generation facilities be equipped with a local communications interface so that these facilities will be available to the Company’s eventual DERMS solution. Without visibility and control capability on existing customer generation facilities, the Company cannot be fully aware of the interaction of those facilities with other generation facilities and the rest of the electric grid. (Id.) As the number of distributed generators grows, the ability to communicate and potentially interact with customer generation will allow for additional growth that might not otherwise be possible. Therefore, the Commission should approve the Company’s proposed modifications to Section G of its rules and regulations. 
H. The Company’s Residential Rate Design Recommendations are in the Best Interests of Customers and Should be Approved by the Commission. 
1.PIA Staff’s proposal to eliminate the declining winter rate structure is unnecessary.
The Company originally designed the residential rate to reflect the seasonality of costs to serve residential customers. (Tr. 4236.) The increasing price blocks in June through September reflect the fact that the Company’s costs are higher in the summer months. (Id.) The increasing price structure provides incentives for energy efficiency and conservation. The Company is projected to remain a summer peaking utility and the inclining rate in the summer months and the declining block structure in the non-summer months supports that. (Id.) Further, the declining block structure in the non-summer months recognizes that off-peak costs are lower and provides price signals to reflect this reality. (Id.) The declining block structure provides an economical option for customers choosing to use electric-based heating. The prices are set to more than cover the Company’s marginal costs and resulting revenues place downward pressure on rates for all customers. (Id.) The Commission should continue to reject requests to change the declining block rate structure as it has in the last two rate cases.
2.Closing the ‘R’ Rate to new residential premises is an appropriate and gradual first step toward modernizing the Company’s rate offerings.
The Company’s proposal to close the R rate for new residential premises effective January 1, 2023, should be approved. This proposal is a logical step in the transition to more modern rate structures with price signals that more accurately reflect the cost of electric service for customers. (Tr. 4237.) To be clear, if the R rate is closed to new premises, customers will still be able to choose from among any of the Company’s residential rate options for which they qualify, including Time of Use – Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”); Time of Use – Plug-in Electric Vehicle (“TOU-PEV”); or PrePay. (Tr. Id.) After the initial year of service at those new premises, customers will be eligible for two additional rates, FlatBill and Pay by Day. (Id.)
3.TOU-RD is an appropriate default rate for new premises.
a.Customers can understand the TOU-RD rate.
PIA Staff wrongly asserts that customers cannot understand the TOU-RD rate. (See Tr. 2179.) Customers can understand that if pricing is higher during certain hours, they can shift their energy usage to hours during which prices are lower to save money. (Tr. 2893.) Customers are also fully capable of understanding that under a demand rate they can save money by not running major appliances at the same time. As analogized by GIPL Witness Barnes, rooftop solar customers can understand that to save money they can run their higher demand appliances during daylight hours when their behind the meter (“BTM”) systems are generating power in order to offset their own usage demand rather than purchasing that energy from the Company at a higher price. (Tr. 4647.) Similarly, rooftop solar and non-rooftop solar customers alike can understand the savings recognized by shifting their energy use to times of the day when energy costs less and avoiding running major appliances at the same time. 
Additionally, customers who have enrolled on the TOU-RD rate have remained on the rate for several years, which indicates that they understand and are satisfied with the rate. In fact, customer surveys reflect that TOU-RD customers are more satisfied and feel more knowledgeable about behavioral changes they can make to reduce their energy bills than customers on the R rate. (Tr. 4237-38.) Further, if customers have questions about the Company’s rates, Georgia Power’s team at the customer care center is available to help them understand how each rate option works, and the Company provides information directly to TOU-RD customers, in addition to making available helpful videos on the Company’s website. (Tr. 4261-62.)
b.The TOU-RD Rate is not more costly for customers than the R rate. 
The TOU-RD rate is not designed to cost customers more than the R rate; rather, the TOU-RD rate is designed to be revenue neutral for the average customer – meaning that the average customer would pay approximately the same annual bill amount on the TOU-RD rate as they would on the R rate. (Tr. 4238.) In fact, because TOU-RD provides better price signals to customers, they can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric bills by shifting usage to off peak periods and staggering the use of key appliances. (Id.) As a result, customers receive more efficient economic signals regarding their usage and energy choices. (Id.)
As new premises are added to Georgia Power’s electric system, it can take anywhere from one to two years for the premises’ monthly usage to normalize. (Id.) This ramp-up period could be due to numerous factors, including the timing of home construction, installation of appliances, or occupancy. At least one intervening witness commented during their direct testimony that the TOU-RD rate is an effective rate for customers that demonstrate a “good load factor.” (Tr. 4788.) By allowing new premises to mature towards a normal usage level, customers at these new premises will greatly improve their load factors and have a better bill experience when comparing TOU-RD to the R rate. Because the bill comparisons that PIA Staff highlights in testimony represent premises that are new to our system, the average monthly usage for those premises is far below the level at which the usage will ultimately mature. (Tr. 4238.) Therefore, the bill comparisons used by PIA Staff are heavily skewed by extremely low usage and fail to tell a complete story.
c. TOU-RD is less volatile than the TOU-REO rate. 
As a default rate for residential customers, the Company recommends using the comparatively less volatile rate, TOU-RD. The TOU-RD rate is much more stable than either TOU-REO or the ‘R’ Rate when comparing the difference between minimum and maximum bills over a given year, as shown in Figure 2 of the rebuttal testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. (Tr. 4240.) Recognizing customer interest in bill stability, the Commission should adopt the Company’s recommendation to use TOU-RD as the new default residential rate if closing the ‘R’ Rate to new premises is adopted. 
d.TOU-RD is more cost reflective than the R rate. 
TOU-RD is a modern, three-part rate that sends price signals that more accurately and appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. Costs to serve customers are divided into three components: (1) customer-related costs; (2) demand-related costs; and (3) energy-related costs. To ensure our rates most accurately reflect the cost to serve our customers, all three cost components should be accounted for within the design of a rate. (Tr. 4239.) As such, three-part rates, which include customer, demand, and energy-related components, are the most cost-reflective rates because they appropriately account for each of the three cost categories. (Id.) Conversely, when a rate, such as the R rate, lacks a demand component, the demand-related costs of electric service must be recovered through the rate’s energy component, which does not appropriately match the collection of revenues with how the Company incurs costs. The result is a less efficient price signal that leads to less efficient use of the electric system by customers and higher prices overall. (Tr. 4240.) Based on this reasoning, the Company supports customer movement to three-part rates such as TOU-RD. Because the TOU-RD rate provides better price signals than the R rate, customers can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric bills by shifting usage or staggering the use of key appliances. (Tr. 4233.)
I.The Company’s Commercial Rate Design Recommendations are in the Best Interests of Customers and Should be Approved by the Commission. 
The Company’s commercial and industrial rates continue to be appropriately structured, and the Company does not agree with PIA Staff and Intervenor proposed changes to these rates.
1. The Commission should deny PIA Staff’s recommendation that customers on marginal rates be assessed the fully embedded cost of the ECCR tariff because such a change is contrary with how marginal rates like RTP are designed to operate. 
Fully embedded costs are recovered from the Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the rates. (Tr. 4233.) To include embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of rates like RTP, as suggested by PIA Staff (Tr. 2148), would undermine the rate structure and significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers.
PIA Staff’s recommendation is fundamentally inconsistent with how marginal rates like RTP are designed to operate. (Tr. 4241.) For RTP as designed, the Company’s fully embedded costs are recovered from the CBL portion of the rate, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the rate. (Id.) To include fully embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of the RTP rate would not only corrupt the rate structure, but also prevent these rates from functioning as marginal rates because the hourly prices would no longer reflect marginal costs or real-time prices. (Tr. 4241-42.) Moreover, the ECCR costs include coal combustion residual asset retirement obligations (“CCR ARO”), which are fully embedded costs incurred by the Company independent of a customer’s kWh usage. (Tr. 4241.) As such, counter to recommendations by GIPL Witness Barnes (Tr. 2976) and PIA Staff (Tr. 2148), ECCR costs are appropriately recovered through the CBL portion of the rate.
If ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to the marginal portion of RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million overall. (Tr. 4242.) A cost shift of this magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers. The RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that are considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia. (Id.) PIA Staff’s proposal undermines the principles of marginal rate design by dramatically altering the allocation of ECCR costs within the RTP rate and would have a significant negative impact on the Company’s ability to help attract economic development opportunities to the state. (Id.) PIA Staff acknowledges the drastic nature of its proposal and offers to mitigate its own proposal by only shifting 50% of embedded costs to the marginal portion of the RTP rate. (Tr. 2148.) However, even an incremental move of 50% would have a dramatic and negative impact on RTP customers by fundamentally changing the rate and undermining the core principles of a marginal rate structure.
2.The Commission should deny DOD Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify the Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install behind the meter generation.
The Commission should reject DOD Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify the Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install BTM generation because customer generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does not justify the reduction of a customer’s CBL. CBLs may be reduced when load originally represented in the CBL has been permanently removed. (Tr. 4242.) Generation does not permanently remove load, and therefore does not justify a reduction of the customer’s CBL. (Tr. 4242-43.) Further, Mr. Blank misunderstands how the Company’s back up service rate and RTP rates work. He incorrectly assumes that an RTP customer can also take service under the Company’s Back Up Service rate simply because it has BTM generation on site. (Tr. 3404.) They cannot. (Tr. 4243.) This misunderstanding should not be used as a basis to make changes to how CBL reductions for RTP customers are calculated. The Commission should reject this recommendation. 
Second, Mr. Blank criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction assessment based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under the RTP tariff for two years. (Tr. 3404-5.) The Company supports the requirement that a customer remain off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because short term changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of embedded load into marginal load on the RTP rate. (Tr. 4243.) The RTP tariffs include provisions to revise the CBL as the result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy efficiency improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to temporary factors such as economic downturns. (Id.) Requiring customers who have not removed load to stay off RTP for 24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the integrity of the RTP program and protects other customers from potential cost shifts that might result from premature revision of the CBL. (Id.)
RTP customers can still benefit from installing onsite generation even in the absence of Witness Blank’s recommendations. RTP customers that install and use generation receive the benefit of the Company’s marginal price for that hour. (Tr. 4243.) This is true when the customer avoids paying the marginal price while operating above their CBL level, or when the customer receives a credit at the marginal price when operating below their CBL level. (Id.) 
3.The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to modify the terms and conditions of the Fixed Price Alternative (“FPA”) and Price Protection Product (“PPP”) offerings to remove Company discretion over when to offer these rates.
The Company considers multiple factors when deciding whether to offer the FPA rate and PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not limited to, uncertainty around environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors that could impact the pricing of these offerings. Additionally, it is important for the Company to maintain flexibility to act quickly and update PPP offerings to best reflect market conditions, especially during times of fuel market volatility as have been experienced for the last year. As a result, removing the Company’s discretion regarding when to offer these products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by other customers, since delays in acting during volatile markets could result in the development of product offerings that do not properly reflect pricing impacts.
J.The Commission Should Approve the Company’s Requested Block Prices for the Community Solar Program. 
The Community Solar tariff offers qualifying customers the opportunity to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing to a portion of the output from local solar facilities. (Tr. 888, 2184.) Every month, participating customers pay a fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities, and in return receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production from those facilities. (Id.) Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are retired on the customer’s behalf. (Tr. 888, 2184-85.)
The Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block to ensure that participants are not subsidized by non-participants. (Tr. 888, 928-29, 2185, 2299.) In addition, following the Commission’s approval to expand the opportunity to participate in Community Solar to customers on the General Service (“GS”) tariff, the Company has proposed that the pricing for GS Community Solar participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt block. (Tr. 888, 929.) The Community Solar subscription price is designed to ensure that participating customers do not shift costs to other customers when kilowatt hours reduce the monthly bills of participating customers. (Tr. 939.) As such, the Company has requested an increase in its subscription rates for residential and commercial customers participating in Community Solar. (Tr. 914, 929.) Based on the approved structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per kW block of energy as proposed by several parties would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift cost recovery to non-participants. (Tr. 914.)
 PIA Staff’s recommendation regarding the appropriate pricing for the Community Solar Program offers two alternatives and is premised on a Commission policy determination as to the purpose of the program: (i) if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy and realize bill savings, Staff recommends its $20 and $22 pricing for residential and commercial customers, or (ii) if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy but not the opportunity for bill savings, Staff supports the Company’s proposed $27.99 and 29.99 pricing for residential and commercial customers, respectively. (Tr.2449-50.) PIA Staff’s recommendations assume that this decision has not yet been determined. However, in the Commission’s June 7, 2017 Order approving the Community Solar Program tariff, the Commission clearly stated that the Community Solar Program “is not designed to produce credits in excess of a customer’s usage and a customer should anticipate the [Community Solar] program charges will exceed the value of any bill credits received.” Although PIA Staff may believe the purpose of the program is an open policy decision for the Commission, the Commission has already determined that the purpose of the program is not for bill savings. Rather, the Community Solar Program is targeted toward customers who are interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. As such, the Commission should approve the Company’s requested block price increase for the Community Solar program. 
K.The Charge It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider is an Appropriately Structured, Time Limited Offering that Will Encourage EV Adoption and Should be Approved by the Commission Without Modification. 
Georgia Power is supporting the rapidly expanding EV market with a rider that is tailored to provide affordably priced electric service and to encourage the growth of EVs in our state. (Tr. 407, 462, 2418.) The CIEV rider will modify how the Power & Light tariffs price commercial EV charging loads. (Tr. 886.) Specifically, the new CIEV rider will modify the monthly billing demands by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors as specified in the tariff. CIEV is a temporary rider that will expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load factor for the account reaches 15% or greater – whichever comes first. Participation is available to new, separately metered charging loads that are served on Georgia Power’s system after January 1, 2023. (Tr. 930.)
The Commission should not open the CIEV rider to existing locations as recommended by AACE, ChargePoint, and EVgo. (Tr. 2310-11; Tr. 2418-19; Tr. 2521-22.) First, the CIEV rider was designed to incentivize the installation of new EV charging stations and opening this rate to existing customers would defeat the purpose of incentivizing new installations. Second, by limiting this tariff to new, separately metered premises, the Company designed the CIEV rider to avoid a cost shift among existing customers. If existing customers were permitted to participate on the CIEV rider, the reduced billing demand allocation in each year would necessarily shift demand costs to other customers. The Company does not support this cost shift and advocates that the CIEV rider should be limited to new, separately metered premises in order to appropriately incentivize new EV charging installations without doing harm to existing customers.
L. The Company’s Cost of Service Study Reasonably Allocates the Company’s Revenue Requirement Across Retail Rates Based on Principles of Cost Causation and Should be Approved Without Modification. 
1. The 12-CP method is the most appropriate way to allocate the Company’s production costs. 
A 12-CP demand is defined as the sum of the highest kW load for the Company in each month of the 12-month period divided by twelve. (Tr. 892.) At the jurisdictional, class, or rate group level, 12-CP is the respective coincident demands at the time of the monthly system peaks. (Id.) Using this allocator ensures that the coincident load burden placed on the system by a jurisdiction, class, or rate group is allocated an appropriate share of the costs of the system. (Id.)
The Company’s adoption of seasonal planning reinforces the appropriateness of the 12-CP approach. (Id.) The Company must plan to have sufficient generation to serve its customers in all twelve months of the year and the 12-CP method appropriately captures the summer and winter peaks targeted through seasonal planning. (Id.) The summer and winter reliability planning of the Company further underscores that no one month or season is adequate at representing the coincident impact of customers on system capacity. (Id.) Georgia Power forecasts to remain a summer peaking utility. (Tr. 1061.) While the IRP identified winter reliability concerns, the Company’s rate designs remain appropriate to address the forecasted peak loads and higher marginal costs that occur in summer months. (Id.)
In addition, as stated in Mr. Evans’ Direct Testimony, the 12-CP allocation methodology has been used in every COSS study filed by the Company with this Commission since 1989. (Tr. 893.) The 12-CP method has been one of FERC’s preferred allocation techniques for determining wholesale jurisdictional obligations, and it has been used by the Company in its filings before FERC. (Id.) It has proven to be a common and popular allocation technique employed in numerous retail filings before many state commissions throughout the United States. (Id.) It is a straightforward methodology, has a strong cost causative relationship, and has a history of providing stable and sound results. (Id.) The Commission should approve the Company’s use of the 12-CP methodology for allocating production costs. 
2. The Commission should reject PIA Staff and Intervenor proposals to adopt alternative production cost allocators.
PIA Staff argues that the shortcoming of the 12-CP method is that accurate load data is required by class throughout the year. (Tr 2109.) PIA Staff Witness Watkins admitted at the hearing, however, that the Company has an abundance of necessary data. (Tr. 2248.) Thus, the only shortcoming identified by PIA Staff to the Company’s method does not hold true for Georgia Power. 
In lieu of the 12-CP methodology, PIA Staff proposes to use the Base Intermediate Peak and Probability of Dispatch methodologies. (Tr. 2243.) However, adoption of these methodologies not only impacts the allocation of costs in base rate cases, but also may impact the allocation of fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. The trade-off between the level of Production fixed investment and the variable cost of producing a unit of output is one primarily made between base capital costs and fuel costs. Altering the allocation of Production capital costs with such a complex calculation would necessitate a similar alteration to the allocation in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. Recognizing the current, approved allocation of fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket, the Company continues to support the 12-CP methodology as the most appropriate Production allocator. It has been employed by Georgia Power for allocating production capacity-related costs since 1989, and as such, has therefore influenced present rates. (Tr. 893.)
GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar and GAM propose the Company use either a 4-CP or 6-CP production cost allocation methodology, arguing that as a summer peaking utility, the four summer months June through September drive the Company’s peak demand and should be the basis for allocating the Company’s production costs. (Tr. 3215; Tr. 3217; Tr. 3230.) In reality, the generation planning for Georgia Power’s system is driven and influenced by non-summer periods in addition to summer peaks. Georgia Power utilizes Expected Unserved Energy (“EUE”) in its determination of the optimal reserve margin for both summer and winter seasons. A pure summer peak or 4-CP allocator is an oversimplification of the Company’s generation planning process. Using a pure summer peak allocator could create unstable, unbalanced, and inequitable cost allocation in which some rate groups receive little if any Production demand cost allocation whatsoever. An example of this could be if the pure summer peak allocator for that year occurred in daytime hours such that there was no subsequent cost allocation to outdoor lighting. The ongoing capacity needs of Georgia Power are heavily driven by winter needs and to not recognize or allocate costs based on winter loads would, therefore, be inappropriate. As such, the Commission should reject Intervenor proposals to modify the production cost allocation methodology and approve the Company’s continued use of a 12-CP methodology.
M.The Commission Should Not Expand the Monthly Netting Pilot and Should Revise the RNR Tariff to Address the Negative Impacts to Non-Participating Customers.
The Commission should reject Intervenor arguments in favor of expanding the monthly netting pilot, even as a short-term interim measure to increase BTM solar penetration rates. As explained by Company Witnesses Evans and Legg, the Company’s analysis identifies that the monthly netting pilot results in a substantial additional cost shift to non-participating customers above the baseline cost shift incurred by instantaneous netting under the RNR tariff. (Tr. 3719.) This additional cost-shift—while currently contained to only a subset of 5,000 pilot customers—will increase in both scope and severity were the Commission to adopt Intervenor recommendations to expand the monthly netting pilot. Further, PIA Staff and Intervenor proposals for valuing excess energy “pushed back” onto the grid by BTM resources significantly exceed the value established by the Commission in the Renewable Cost Benefit (“RCB”) Framework, which is appropriately based upon the Company’s avoided cost. 
In considering whether to expand the monthly netting pilot, the Commission should note that many other legislatures and commissions have found monthly netting to be a deeply flawed accounting construct that excessively compensates BTM solar resources. This excess compensation—while a boon to the solar industry—results in real and quantifiable harm to other customers. This harm increases exponentially at scale because as the amount of monthly netting customers grow, all other customers must shoulder an ever-increasing burden of the costs avoided by monthly netting customers. Conversely, instantaneous netting coupled with appropriate rate structures, such as three-part rates, will ensure that RNR customers receive appropriate price signals and compensation levels that encourage BTM solar development, while also preventing an inevitable and progressively significant cost shift that occurs under monthly netting. 
While the Company’s data suggests that the monthly netting pilot increased the rate of BTM solar adoption on the Company’s system, this increase in BTM adoption resulted from an excessive subsidy that creates considerable additional expense for non-participating customers. Furthermore, since the closure of the monthly netting pilot to new customers, the Company has continued to receive new BTM applications at a rate close to that under the monthly netting pilot. With this trajectory in mind, and given the boost to the BTM solar market provided by the recently-enacted federal Inflation Reduction Act (“IRA”) and other market trends decreasing the costs to install BTM resources, further subsidization of the BTM market through expansion of the monthly netting pilot is unnecessary and inappropriate. 
1.The Company’s analysis confirms that monthly netting creates a disproportionate cost shift that increases rates for non-participating customers.
Monthly netting does not adhere to fundamental rate design and cost-causation principles and presents two substantial flaws: (1) it compensates BTM customers at the Company’s retail rate – which is much higher than the price the Company otherwise would pay for energy; and (2) it enables BTM customers to avoid paying their share of the costs to construct, operate and maintain the Company’s electric system. This excess payment structure results in significant cross-subsidies that shift costs to non-participating customers. 
As demonstrated by Company Witnesses Evans and Legg, a customer participating in the monthly netting pilot shifts an additional $287 of costs onto non-participating customers on an annual basis. (Tr. 5031.) When limited to the current subset of 5,000 pilot customers, the incremental aggregated cost shift associated with monthly netting equals approximately $1.4 million annually. If expanded to between 30,000-75,000 customers, as recommended by Intervenors, this cost shift would increase to approximately $8.6 million to $21.5 million per year. Moreover, including the $826 annual cost shift that already results from RNR customers receiving service under the Company’s volumetric-based R tariff, this aggregate cost shift increases even further to approximately $24.8 million to $61.9 million per year. As the number of monthly netting customers grow, the costs that non-participating customers must pay to subsidize them will also grow. This increase compounds over time and has caused many states with aggressive monthly netting policies to reconsider and rebalance the interests of participating and non-participating customers in the interests of fairness underlying fundamental rate design and cost causation principles. 
a.Monthly netting customers rely upon and utilize the Company’s system while avoiding cost responsibility. 
By netting an RNR customer’s consumption of energy against the excess energy they export to the grid, monthly netting enables an RNR customer to receive a zero kilowatt-hour (“kWh”) bill during a billing period. On a simple volumetric rate such as the Company’s R rate, an RNR customer with a net zero kWh bill pays only the Company’s basic service charge and applicable riders. This is notwithstanding that a substantial portion of an RNR customer’s energy was supplied and delivered exclusively by the Company’s system during the billing period. In this way, monthly netting allows RNR customers to fully use and rely upon the Company’s electric system while avoiding contributing to the costs of the system.
As conceded by PIA Staff Witness Watkins during the hearing, RNR customers do not actually reduce their electricity consumption. (Tr. 4413.) RNR customers merely supplement Company-supplied energy with their own self-supplied generation during certain times of the day. An RNR customer’s peak demand remains the same, with or without the BTM solar generation. Because the Company is obligated to serve the peak demand of all of its customers, including RNR customers, the Company must plan for and be prepared to serve the RNR customer at all times, including when their BTM generation is intermittent. 
Despite PIA Staff and Intervenor attempts to downplay this issue, the prevalence of net zero bills for RNR customers is material. Indeed, PIA Staff’s Supplemental Direct Testimony Table 3 reveals that nearly 11% of monthly netting bills in 2021 resulted in net zero bills. (Tr. 4362, 4419.) Likewise, Table 4 of PIA Staff’s Supplemental Direct Testimony similarly demonstrates that nearly 40% of RNR customers experience at least one month with a net zero bill. (Tr. 4363, 4419-20.) PIA Staff Witness Watkins counters that the excess energy contributed by the BTM solar generation fully compensates for the energy consumed by the RNR customer during the billing period. (Tr. 4415.) PIA Staff’s argument, however, relies upon the assumption that the value of the customer’s excess energy and the value of energy delivered by the Company’s system are equal, which they are not. As testified by Company Witnesses Evans, Legg and Gattie (Tr. 3727-28, 3845), these values are not equal, and this false equivalence between imported and exported energy under monthly netting creates the cost shift revealed in the Company’s analysis and acknowledged in other jurisdictions. 
b.The Company’s retail rate includes components that are not related to the cost of energy.
By netting their consumption against excess generation, RNR customers receive compensation for their excess energy at the full volumetric portion of the Company’s retail rate. The Company’s Commission-approved retail rate, however, recovers costs in addition to energy costs, including costs for transmission, distribution and other services included in the Company’s cost of service. (Tr. 3726.) As evidenced by the multiple witnesses testifying on all of the cost of service issues in this proceeding, the Company’s retail rates are inclusive of its total cost of service. This includes costs associated with the Company’s other reasonable and cost-eligible expenses and investments required to provide safe and reliable electric service. 
By contrast, the excess energy pushed back by RNR customers does not provide all these services, yet under monthly netting these customers receive compensation at the full volumetric portion of the retail rate. Unlike the Company’s electric service, which provides all the services required to generate, transmit, and distribute energy to customers, BTM solar generators provide only marginal energy, which is why under the Commission-approved RCB Framework, this energy is appropriately valued at the utility’s avoided costs—the monetary value the Company saves by not having to generate this energy. Monthly netting is not part of the Company’s revenue requirement calculation but is rather part of cost allocation and rate design. As such, it must be evaluated in accordance with cost causation and rate design principles, which inform how the Company’s full revenue requirement should be recovered from customers. Multi-part rate designs are intended to ensure that RNR customers take service on a rate that is more reflective of their actual cost of service, thereby mitigating the cost shift to non-participants. Further, although PIA Staff argues that the Commission should ignore any cost shift because current rate design is not at parity, this does not provide a reasonable basis for further subsidizing BTM customers at the expense of all other customers. (Tr. 4358.) The goal of rate design should always be to reflect actual cost causation as closely as possible.
In lieu of recommending expanding the monthly netting pilot, PIA Staff lays out a range of possible compensation rates to be applied to the Company’s current instantaneous netting customers. This range varies from approximately 6.99 cents to over 13 cents. Compensating BTM solar resources at such high levels, which are well beyond the Company’s avoided costs, will further increase the cost shift to non-participating customers under the RNR tariff. As far as the Company is aware, these values provided by PIA Staff are not credibly tied to any quantification of actual benefit provided by the BTM resource, but rather represent a range of figures adopted from policy decisions made in other jurisdictions. This range of compensation rates is too arbitrary a set of variables to appropriately inform the level of compensation for BTM resources. The Company, PIA Staff, and several Intervenors have already undergone this analysis, in integrating BTM resources into the Company’s RCB Framework. Rather than selecting an arbitrary compensation rate with the primary goal of incentivizing BTM resources, the Commission should adopt a default RNR tariff rate design structure which better reflects the cost to serve customers, such as the Company’s TOU-RD rate. This rate is justifiable, cost-based, and is an appropriate step toward addressing the disproportionate cost shift associated with net metering and adopting a BTM solar program that fairly compensates BTM customers while protecting non-participating customers.
2.The Commission should not deviate from its deliberative and measured approach towards incentivizing renewable energy resources.
PIA Staff and Intervenors point narrowly to Georgia’s BTM solar penetration rate as an indictment of Georgia’s renewable energy policy, asserting that Georgia’s BTM solar penetration ranking alone infers a need for this Commission to act and expand the monthly netting pilot. (Tr. 3876-3877, 4349-4350, 4383.) Relying on BTM penetration rates as the primary measure of success, however, discounts Georgia’s considerable achievement in cost-effectively expanding renewable energy within Georgia without jeopardizing affordability for customers. As testified by Company Witness Gattie, this Commission’s deliberate and measured renewable energy policy has enabled the state to deploy considerable amounts of solar generation while simultaneously retaining residential rates below the national average. (Tr. 3836-3839, 3847.) Through its thoughtful energy policy, this Commission has produced meaningful results for customers. The Commission should consider this record of accomplishment and continue the successful path it has created here in Georgia. 
By holding BTM penetration rates as the sole measure of success, PIA Staff and Intervenors fail to consider that many states with higher BTM solar penetration rates have prioritized the development of BTM resources to the detriment of important considerations such as customer cost and system reliability. As explained by Company Witness Gattie, many states with high penetration rates of BTM solar have experienced significant negative impacts such as reduced system reliability, increased cost, and ratepayer fairness issues. Likewise, as noted previously, given overall market trends, Georgia is likely to see expanded BTM solar penetration, without additional subsidies or Commission action. 
First, notwithstanding that the monthly netting pilot reaching its full 5,000 customer limit, the Company continues to receive only slightly fewer BTM solar applications each month in 2022 than received each month in 2020 before the pilot was launched. (Tr. 3716.) This level of growth post-pilot, without expanded monthly netting incentives, supports the Company’s recommendations that this Commission need not introduce an additional subsidy for BTM resources. PIA Staff and Interveners have not provided credible evidence that the lack of monthly netting will significantly hamper growth rates. Such arguments also falsely cite to the existence of monthly netting as the sole variable limiting prior growth in BTM solar under the Company’s instantaneous netting program. These arguments ignore the significant reduction in the availability and cost for BTM solar facilities in the last few years and the growth of a robust solar installation industry—all of which have increased the availability and cost-competitiveness of BTM solar. 
According to public information made available by SEIA, “[t]he cost to install solar has dropped by more than 60% over the last decade . . . [and] . . . has experienced an average annual growth rate of 33% . . .[t]hanks to strong federal policies like the solar Investment Tax Credit, rapidly declining costs, and increasing demand across the private and public sector for clean electricity . . .” Also according to SEIA, and as noted in the below chart that identifies solar penetrations by state, Georgia’s level of solar deployment is on pace with other states such as New York, Florida and Arizona:

Second, the recently passed federal IRA will significantly increase development in Georgia’s BTM solar marketplace. GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar go to great lengths to purposely downplay the benefits of the IRA. (Tr. 4403-4404, 4848-4853.)
