Despite characterizing as speculative and negligible Company Witness Gattie’s conclusions regarding the wide-ranging benefits of the IRA and the IRA’s likely impact on the solar market, GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar have called the IRA “the most transformational clean energy policy in history” and have stated that “[t]he passage of this historic legislation has had an immediate impact [on the] long-term outlook for the U.S. solar industry.” (Georgia Power Company Exhibit No. 44.) According to GSEIA “[t]he passage of the Inflation Reduction Act has drastically improved baseline projections for the solar industry over the next five years.” As projected on SEIA’s publicly available webpage, the growth from the IRA will only build over time: 

Notably, GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar conditioned none of these statements on the availability of monthly netting. Instead, outside of their advocacy in this proceeding, these Intervenors have trumpeted the “transformative” impact on the installation of new solar capacity resulting from the IRA. Their attempts to downplay these same impacts on new solar capacity in this proceeding can only be described as self-serving. 
By GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar’s own account, the IRA will have a monumental impact on the growth of solar in the United States and indeed in Georgia. GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar’s arguments that the IRA will not be enough incentive for Georgia’s BTM solar market to grow is speculative and inconsistent with what they’ve said about the IRA elsewhere. All indications suggest that the IRA will significantly increase BTM solar development in Georgia. Expansion of the monthly netting pilot risks providing a too generous and unneeded subsidy to BTM solar at the expense of non-participating customers. Therefore, especially when considering the significant federal incentives that will come to Georgia from the IRA, the Commission should reject calls to expand the monthly netting pilot which provides an unnecessary subsidy for the BTM lobby at the expense of other customers. 
Finally, SACE Witness Hill proposed the strawman argument that Georgia could miss out on IRA tax benefits if the Commission did not expand monthly netting. (Tr. 4448, 4484-85.) This is a baseless claim and is merely a transparent attempt to divert attention from the benefits already provided under the IRA. Nothing in the IRA requires that rooftop solar customers have access to monthly netting before availing themselves of the tax breaks under the IRA, and SACE has provided no evidence supporting this statement. Not a single Georgia customer will miss out on any tax credits under the IRA if they choose to install BTM solar. Georgia will have the same access to funds under the IRA as every other state. The IRA exists to provide financial incentives to private investment to achieve a specific policy goal and exists without regard to the availability of monthly netting. 
3.The Commission should reject PIA Staff and Intervenor recommendations to recognize benefits not included in the Company’s Renewable Cost Benefit Framework. 
The Company has provided an exhaustive cost of service study in this case that includes the costs to serve all existing customers, including customers receiving service on the RNR tariff. Contrary to PIA Staff and Intervenor assertions, the Company considered all relevant, credible and quantifiable benefits provided by BTM solar in its analysis.
The Company has determined that approximately $21 in system benefits per customer accrue per year because of a customer’s BTM solar generating facility. Despite the Company’s quantification of these benefits, PIA Staff and Intervenors recommend the Commission recognize and include additional benefits not included in the Company’s analysis, such as economic development, environmental and other externalities that are not included in the Company’s cost of service. For instance, SACE Witness Brown testifies that the Company should compensate RNR customers not only for excess energy, but for things such as lowering reliance on fossil fuels and expanded employment opportunities. (Tr. 4892.) As this Commission has recognized many times before, compensation for export energy should not include externalities that are not otherwise included in the Company’s costs of service.
 From as early as 1923 with the Bluefield decision, the United States Supreme Court defined the guidelines by which a regulator sets rates for a private utility. Bluefield Water Works & Improvement Co. v. Public Service Commission of West Virginia, 262 U.S. 679 (1923). At its most basic, rates must be set to recover the utility’s reasonable costs and earn a reasonable return. In other words, establishing rates relies on the utility’s quantifiable amount of prudent investment and reasonable costs. Consistent with 100 years of law governing cost of service ratemaking, the Commission sets rates based on quantifiable costs associated with electric service and not indirect externalities.
Lawmakers and regulators can and do make policy decisions based on potential public benefits. But these policy decisions should not undermine the fundamental cost of service framework underpinning the way rates are established. Nor should these policy decisions create negative impacts for the Company’s other customers. If the Commission chooses to further incentivize the penetration of BTM solar because of its intangible environmental and economic benefits, that decision must be grounded in a knowledge of the costs such a choice imposes on the Company and on the non-participating customers that will ultimately bear those costs.
4.Other states are rejecting monthly netting due to significant cost shifts and non-participating customer impacts. 
Many legislatures and commissions that have recognized the negative externalities associated with monthly netting are now returning to avoided cost based methodologies as a replacement for monthly netting. As detailed by Company Witness Gattie, over the past several years, many states have reconsidered their net metering policies, primarily because monthly retail net metering overcompensates BTM solar resources at the expense of non-participating customers. (Tr. 3822-46.) Whether on the legislative or regulatory level, these states have targeted the very cost shift that the Company has highlighted in this case and changed their policies to mitigate the negative impacts caused by monthly netting. During the hearing, PIA Staff Witness Watkins acknowledged this point, agreeing that the “. . . cost shift was certainly an element in each of those discussions . . .” (Tr. 4407.) While these states have employed different ways of describing this cost shift, they generally agree that it arises directly from compensating monthly netting customers at a rate equal to the retail rate for electricity, which overstates the actual value these resources provide to the grid and pushes fixed cost recovery onto other customers. 
A poignant recent example can be found in California, where the California Public Service Commission (“CPUC”) is reconsidering its existing net metering tariff in Docket No. R.20-08-020 (“NEM 3.0 Proceeding”). Much like the monthly netting pilot in Georgia, California’s existing net metering policy provides customer-generators full retail rate credits for energy exported to the grid and requires them to pay a few charges that align NEM customer costs more closely with non-NEM customer costs. On November 10, 2022, the presiding administrative law judge issued a proposed decision recommending a move away from retail net metering. (Georgia Power Exhibit 43.) In doing so, the decision relies heavily on evidence that full retail net metering imposes a markedly unjust cost shift that requires remediation. As the CPUC notes, “[t]his cost shift is created by the ability of distributed generation customers to avoid fixed costs, including grid costs and public purpose program costs, which then become the responsibility of non-participating ratepayers, including low-income customers.” (Id. at 4.) Moreover, the decision notes that this cost shift ultimately undermines the clean energy goals, including electrification: “[b]ut a cost shift exists, and continuation of the cost shift feeds into higher electricity rates, which discourage electrification.” (Id. at 90.) It thus concludes, “[a]ccordingly, the successor tariff should address the cost shift not only to ensure equity but also to encourage electrification to ensure California can meet its climate and clean energy objectives.” (Id.)
In its decision, the CPUC was especially concerned with the effect that such cost shifts had on low-income non-participating customers:
The Commission agrees that the look-back study indicates that NEM 2.0 disproportionately harms low-income customers not participating in the net energy metering tariff. Findings in the look-back study show that NEM 2.0 and, thus, NEM 1.0 disproportionally benefited non-CARE residential net energy metering customers while all customers, including those with lower incomes, must bear the addition of the 82 to 91 percent of the cost of service bypass by net energy metering customers. The Commission finds the look-back study indicates that NEM 2.0 disproportionately harms low-income customers not for participating in the net energy meeting tariff.
Indeed, when presented with this evidence from California during his November 29, 2022 hearing testimony, Intervenor Georgia Coalition for Local Governments Witness Hill, while distinguishing the level of solar penetration in California from Georgia, conceded that cost-shifting was an issue which Georgia would eventually have to address concerning its net metering program. (Tr. 4508.) (“This -- the level of cost shifting and dealing with monthly net metering at some point, I don’t dispute that that’s a good idea.”). Moreover, he sensibly admitted that Georgia could and should draw lessons from the policy decisions of other jurisdictions, such as California:
Q And I assume, Dr. Hill, that you don’t oppose learning from lessons learned in some other jurisdictions; correct?
A No. Absolutely.
(Tr. 4511.)
While California is perhaps the most notable and recent state to reverse course on monthly netting, California is not the only state coming to this conclusion about the cost shifts inherent in retail net metering. In January 2017, in Decision No. 75859, the Arizona Corporation Commission (“ACC”) eliminated retail net metering within the state. In so doing, it similarly relied on demonstrable evidence that retail net metering inflicted costs on non-participating customers. The Commission noted, “[w]hile we refrain from commenting on the appropriateness of any particular rate design as part of this proceeding, the Commission is committed to modifying residential rate design in a manner that mitigates the recognized cost shift caused by rooftop solar customers’ self-consumption.” Similarly, the Louisiana Public Service Commission, in its Order dispensing with retail net metering, endorsed its staff’s analysis that, “[t]o the extent that the solar customers are paid any more than avoided costs for the energy they produce and sell to the utility, all of the non-solar customers must make up the difference.” 
To remedy this cost shift, many of these states have elected to replace net metering with a compensation regime based on avoided-cost methodologies. In Indiana, Gov. Eric Holcomb signed a bill (SA309) in 2017 that put the state on a path to phasing out retail net metering by 2047. Before that deadline, the bill allowed utilities to pay solar customers a new, lower rate – known as the “excess distribution generation” (“EDG”) rate – when solar penetration reached 1.5% of their summer peak load, or by July 2022. 
CenterPoint (previously known as Vectren), one of Indiana’s utilities, was the first to reach this 1.5% benchmark and in 2021 filed a request to institute the EDG rate. CenterPoint also proposed switching from monthly net metering to instantaneous netting, in which customers pay the full retail rate for all power used from the grid, and all solar power sent back to the grid is paid at the much lower EDG rate. The Indiana Utility Regulatory Commission (“IURC”) approved CenterPoint’s full request in the spring of 2021. Indiana’s other predominant utilities, including NIPSCO, AES, Indiana Michigan (“I&M”) Power, and Duke Energy, have followed suit in requesting the same instantaneous netting arrangement, and the IURC has approved their requests. 
In the 2020 South Carolina Public Service Commission (“South Carolina PSC”) proceeding in which Duke Energy Carolinas proposed a successor tariff to its monthly netting program, Duke estimated a cost shift of $35-$40 per month per monthly netting solar customer. The customer participation rate for monthly netting in South Carolina was 1.4% in 2021 when the South Carolina PSC approved a settlement agreement (to which Vote Solar was a signatory) to reform the NEM tariff and to greatly reduce the cost shift.
Arizona has adopted a similar path. Since the aforementioned ACC decision eliminating the state’s retail net metering program, the state has implemented net billing, under which utilities purchase excess solar at an “excess generation credit rate” that is worth less than the retail value of electricity. As with Indiana, this program employs instantaneous netting as well. Further, the ACC determined that rooftop solar customers are partial requirements customers who export power to the grid and may be separated into their own rate class for the purposes of assessing additional customer charges for net metering.
Intervenors have offered testimony noting that many states have not eliminated retail net metering, or they have adopted a variety of export compensation structures that retain elements of monthly netting. (Tr. 4350-4351, 4383-4384, 4397-4398.) But this testimony misses the point. Other states may have other programs, but all have acknowledged monthly netting is an inappropriate structure and the least-optimal option. Monthly netting was primarily enacted decades ago as a crude and easily administrable accounting method to compensate distributed solar resources. Notably, monthly netting was not based on any economic foundation as to how these resources should be fairly compensated. This has resulted in many states re-evaluating long standing net metering policies and attempting to quantify the “value” such resources provide. In considering whether to expand monthly netting, the Commission should take the lessons learned in other states and prevent similar problems from occurring here in Georgia. 
5.The Cogen Act prohibits a compensation rate for BTM resources in excess of avoided cost. 
Notwithstanding the cost shift and customer fairness issues associated with monthly netting described above, Georgia law prohibits the Commission from adopting a compensation rate for RNR customers that exceeds the Company’s avoided cost. Unlike other jurisdictions referenced above, Georgia has not legislatively mandated or approved monthly netting. The Georgia Cogeneration and Distributed Generation Act of 2001 (“Cogen Act”), which governs the rates and structure to be adopted under the RNR tariff, plainly states that the Company is not required to purchase BTM generation at a rate that exceeds the Company’s avoided cost. Indeed, while the Cogen Act clearly establishes an avoided cost cap to BTM compensation, the Cogen Act does not mandate or require the Company to credit BTM generation at the Company’s retail rate for electricity. 
The Cogen Act requires that the Company purchase excess energy from customers with BTM generation, like those served under the RNR tariff, until the aggregate demand of customer-owned generation exceeds 0.2 percent of the Company’s annual peak demand. The Cogen Act provides that—for instances where a customer’s generation exceeds the customer’s demand—the Company must pay for excess kilowatt-hours at an “agreed to rate” and may levy certain fixed customer charges to recover costs associated with serving BTM generation. As stated in the Cogen Act: 
(1)(C) When electricity generated by the customer’s distributed generation system exceeds the electricity supplied by the electric service provider, the customer generator: 

Shall be billed for the appropriate customer charges for that billing period; and 

(ii) Shall be credited for the excess kilowatt-hours generated during the billing period at an agreed to rate as filed with the commission, with this kilowatt-hour credit appearing on the bill for the billing period; or 

O.C.G.A. § 46-3-55(1)(C) (emphasis added.) 
First, the Cogen Act’s reference to an “agreed to rate approved by the Commission” makes clear the legislature intended for the Commission to establish an RNR-specific rate different from the Company’s retail rate. Had the Cogen Act simply intended for the Company to compensate BTM generation at the retail rate, the Cogen Act would not refer to this additional “agreed to rate” for BTM resources. Second, the Cogen Act further states that the “agreed to rate” the Company must pay to BTM generation shall not exceed the avoided cost rate, unless such generation is subscribed under a renewable energy program. Even in this limited exception, however, the Cogen Act only requires the Company to pay a rate equal to the cost of energy: 

(a) An electric service provider will only be required to purchase energy as specified in Code Section 46-3-55 from an eligible customer generator on a first-come, first-served basis until the cumulative generating capacity of all renewable energy sources equals 0.2 percent of the utility’s annual peak demand in the previous year; provided, however, that no electric service provider will be required to purchase such energy at a price above avoided energy cost unless that amount of energy has been subscribed under any renewable energy program.
(b) Once the capacity is subscribed, an electric service provider may purchase energy from an eligible customer generator at a cost of energy as defined for a utility by the commission, in the case of an electric utility, or by the appropriate governing body, in the case of any other electric service provider or electric supplier.
O.C.G.A. § 46-3-56 (emphasis added.)
The Company’s “cost of energy” for BTM solar is equal to the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy Cost rate—the rate currently provided to BTM instantaneous netting customers under the RNR tariff. Neither the Cogen Act, nor Georgia public utility law generally, define a “renewable energy program” nor do they lay out the necessary steps for such energy to be “subscribed.” A reasonable interpretation is that the phrase “unless that amount of energy has been subscribed under any renewable energy program” means that the Commission can establish a program whereby one or more customers who desire to purchase BTM-generated energy at a higher rate can do so—“subscribe”—with the purpose of increasing compensation to BTM generators. Such a program, properly designed, would further comport with the text of the Cogen Act because it would not shift the costs of the higher compensation rate to non-participating customers. But even with this generous interpretation, the Company still would not be required under the Cogen Act to purchase such energy from BTM sources at a rate that exceeds the cost of energy. 
The Cogen Act’s deliberate use of the term “energy” makes clear an intent to capture only the energy portion of the customer’s electric bill and exclude compensation for transmission and distribution and other services—all of which are components of the Company’s retail electric rate. 
As the testimony of Company Witnesses Legg and Evans demonstrates, the Company’s limited implementation of monthly netting results in significant cost shifts between monthly netting customers and non-participants. Rather than expanding monthly netting, the Cogen Act requires the Company to compensate customer generation at the avoided cost rate (absent Company agreement or a subscription program). To be clear, this is precisely the Company’s existing instantaneous netting structure under the RNR tariff, first established by the Commission shortly after the legislature first enacted the Cogen Act over twenty years ago, which should remain the structure moving forward. 
6.The Commission should adopt the Company’s RNR tariff recommendations to address the cost shift and consumer protection concerns.
a.The Commission should implement a default three part rate for RNR customers to mitigate the ongoing cost shift and incorrect price signals.
Due to the disproportionate cost shift presented under the Company’s current RNR tariff, the Company proposes requiring all customers who enroll in RNR on or after January 1, 2023 to take service under a three-part rate, such as TOU-RD. Three part rates, connect cost drivers with cost recovery and ensure that RNR customers take service on a rate that is closer to their actual cost of service, thereby lowering the disproportionate cost shift to non-participating customers. Adopting a rate design structure that better reflects the cost to serve RNR customers is the first step on the path toward addressing the cost shift and adopting a BTM solar program protective of non-participating customers.
Notably, monthly netting undermines fundamental principles of cost causation and rate design by artificially elevating the value of excess energy to the level of the Company’s total cost of service to generate electricity, deliver electricity across its system, and provide the infrastructure for a safe and reliable system. These are sunk costs, which are recovered over time and reflect substantial investments that provide a real value to customers. The Company cannot simply reduce its distribution and transmission costs that are necessary to serve its customers and they are appropriately recovered in rates as a component of the cost of electric service. If a rate structure requires the Company to over-compensate RNR customers for their excess energy, this subsidy necessarily shifts the responsibility for paying these customers’ share of system costs to non-participating customers. In summary, the current RNR tariff’s cost shift, which all parties agree exists, , is caused by RNR customers’ ability to bypass virtually all volumetric rates. This cost shift is mitigated with a three-part rate that is more reflective of the Company’s actual cost to serve RNR customers. 
PIA Staff and Intervenors primary concerns with the TOU-RD rate is that the rate lowers compensation for BTM solar. PIA Staff and Intervenors concerns are related to what compensation levels BTM solar customers receive and not whether the rate is fair, cost-based and non-harmful to non-participating customers. As pointed out during the hearing, Commissioner Shaw identified that without a time varying or demand-based rate structure, a monthly netting customer is under no incentive to consume self-generated power at peak times, or to install energy storage or modify behavior to maximize the alleged benefits of self-generated power. (Tr. 4854.) Under the Company’s proposal to adopt the TOU-RD rate for RNR customers, this issue would be addressed, as the TOU-RD rate sends the appropriate price signals to customers. With monthly netting, this price signal is not sent, because excess energy if fully compensated at the retail rate. 
b.Continued growth in the solar BTM market requires additional consumer protections.
Another unfortunate externality of expanding BTM solar resources has been an increasing number of unethical business practices employed by some solar marketers, lead generators, solar installers, and solar loan providers offering services to Georgia Power’s BTM customers. Increasing the incentives for BTM resources without additional oversight of BTM service providers invites potentially more unqualified actors and additional customer complaints. Therefore, in addition to its recommendations that the Commission reject expansion of the monthly netting pilot, the Company proposes the development of a formal framework for the Commission’s referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading business practices in the marketplace for BTM generation resources.
V.CONCLUSION
For all the above reasons, the Commission should approve Georgia Power Company’s 2022 Rate Case as filed by the Company.

Respectfully submitted, this 8th day of December 2022.

BY THE COMMISSION:

I. GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S 2022 RATE CASE

On June 24, 2022, Georgia Power Company (the “Company” or “Georgia Power”) filed this rate case pursuant to the terms and conditions of the Georgia Public Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) February 6, 2020 Order Adopting Settlement Agreement in Georgia Power’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516 (“2019 Rate Case Final Order”). In the 2019 Rate Case Final Order, the Commission provided Georgia Power with specific instructions for its next filing as follows: 

The Commission finds that a three-year term for the Settlement Agreement as Modified ending December 31, 2022 is reasonable. By July 1, 2022, the Company shall file testimony and exhibits required in a general rate case along with supporting schedules required by the Commission to support a “traditional” rate case. The test period utilized by the Company in its rate case filing shall be from August 1, 2022 to July 31, 2023. The Company may propose to continue, modify or discontinue this Alternate Rate Plan. The Company shall also file projected revenue requirements for calendar years 2023, 2024, and 2025.


Docket No. 42516, Order Adopting Settlement Agreement.

The Commission has general ratemaking jurisdiction over Georgia Power Company under O.C.G.A. Ch. 2, T. 46. The Georgia Public Service Commission has general supervision over electric light and power companies. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-20(a) and 46-2-21. The Commission has “exclusive power to determine what are just and reasonable rates and charges to be made by any person, firm, or corporation subject to its jurisdiction.” O.C.G.A. § 26-2-23; see also O.C.G.A. §§ 46-1-1(5), 46-2-24, 46-2-25, 46-2-26.1, and 46-2-26.2.

The Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing was made in compliance with the April 7, 2022 Procedural and Scheduling Order, as subsequently amended by the Commission’s July 26, 2022 Amended PSO, August 5, 2022 Second Amended PSO, and November 8, 2022 Third Amended PSO (collectively as amended, the “PSO”). The PSO for the Company’s 2022 Rate Case identified the procedures to be followed in this docket along with corresponding dates on which designated events were set to occur with respect to the Company’s filing. In the body of the PSO, the Commission, pursuant to O.C.G.A. § 46-2-25, suspended the subject matter of Georgia Power’s filing for a period of five months ending January 1, 2023. In addition, the Commission determined that the proceedings on the Company’s filing constituted complex litigation, as that term is defined in O.C.G.A. § 9-11-33. The Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing included information responsive to the Commission’s rule regarding Minimum Filing Requirements (“MFRs”); exhibits reflecting Georgia Power’s cost of service study, sales and revenue forecast, depreciation rates, and cash working capital; and the testimony and exhibits of Christopher C. Womack, the panel of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson, James M. Coyne, Lee Evans, Steven M. Fetter, and Larry T. Legg. On August 22, 2022, Georgia Power filed a supplemental filing to reflect the revenue requirement impacts from various items included in the Final Order approved by the Commission in the Company’s 2022 Integrated Resource Plan (“IRP”) and demand side management (“DSM”) Certification proceedings in Docket Nos. 44160 and 44161 (“Supplemental Filing”). On September 28, 2022, Georgia Power filed an errata to its filing correcting the projected test period revenues for the Fixed Price Alternative (“FPA”) rate, and on November 21, 2022, Georgia Power filed an errata to its supplemental direct testimony correcting a formulaic error in its analysis of the cost shift associated with monthly netting (together, the “Errata”). 
Georgia Power’s 2022 Rate Case filing, as amended by the Supplemental Filing and the Errata, requests approval to continue the three-year alternate rate plan (“ARP”) structure and requests rate increases of $852, $107 and $453 million to be effective January 1, 2023, 2024 and 2025, respectively. These proposed rate adjustment increases include a levelized adjustment for traditional base rates, a levelized decrease of $1 million for the traditional costs of the ECCR tariff, and annual increases to recover compliance costs associated with coal ash pond closure requirements as well as the DSM costs approved in Docket No. 44161 and municipal franchise fee (“MFF”) tariffs. Hearings on Georgia Power’s direct case in support of its filing were held September 37, 2022, through September 29, 2022.

In addition to the Commission’s Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff, which has a statutory right to participate in this proceeding, interventions were filed by a number of interested parties. These interested parties included the Americans for Affordable Clean Energy (“AACE”); ChargePoint, Inc.; the Commercial Group; Concerned Ratepayers of Georgia; Cypress Creek Renewables, LLC; EVgo Services, LLC; Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”); Georgia Coalition of Local Governments (the “Coalition”); Georgia Conservation Voters; Georgia Interfaith Power & Light, Inc. (“GIPL”), the Georgia Solar Energy Association, Inc. (“GA Solar”); Georgia Solar Energy Industries Association (“GSEIA”), Solar Energy Industries Association (“SEIA”), and Vote Solar; Georgia Watch; Interstate Gas Supply, Inc.; The Kroger Company (“Kroger”); Metropolitan Atlanta Rapid Transit Authority (“MARTA”); Resource Supply Management (“RSM”); the Sierra Club; Southern Alliance for Clean Energy (“SACE”) and Southface Energy Institute, Inc. (“Southface”); Southern Renewable Energy Association (“SREA”); the U.S. Department of Defense (on behalf of all Other Federal Executive Agencies) (“DOD”); and Utility Management Services. 
Thereafter, on October 20, 2022, PIA Staff and Intervenors filed testimony and exhibits presenting their respective direct cases. With the exception of Concerned Ratepayers of Georgia, Cypress Creek Renewables, LLC, Georgia Conservation Voters, GA Solar, Georgia Watch, Interstate Gas Supply, Inc., Kroger, RSM, Sierra Club, and SREA, all other parties to this case filed direct testimony in this proceeding. Also on October 20, 2022, and in accordance with the PSO, Georgia Power filed the supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of Dr. David Gattie and the panel of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Hearings on PIA Staff and Intervenors’ direct cases were held November 8, 2022 through November 10, 2022, and hearings regarding the Company’s supplemental direct testimony were held on November 10, 2022.
On November 18, 2022, the Company filed the rebuttal testimony of the panel of Aaron P. Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson, James M. Coyne, Steven M. Fetter, and the panel of Lee Evans and Larry T. Legg in response to the positions advocated by PIA Staff and various Intervenors. Also on November 18, 2022, PIA Staff filed the supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of the panel of Jamie Barber, Timothy Cook, and Glenn A. Watkins; the Coalition filed the direct testimony David G. Hill, Ph.D.; GIPL filed supplemental direct testimony of Justin Barnes; GA Solar filed the supplemental direct testimony of Kenneth Sercy; GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar filed supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of the panel of Kevin Lucas, Thatcher Young, and Will Giese; and SACE and Southface filed the direct testimony and exhibits of Dr. Marilyn A. Brown and Alden M. Hathaway. On November 23, 2022, Georgia Power filed the supplemental rebuttal testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Hearings regarding the Company’s rebuttal testimony, PIA Staff and Intervenor supplemental direct, and the Company’s supplemental rebuttal testimony were held November 29, 2022 through November 30, 2022. 
At each phase of the evidentiary hearings in this case, the Commission also heard from numerous public witnesses who expressed their views on the Company’s application, either individually or on behalf of specific groups.

II.FINDINGS OF FACT AND CONCLUSIONS OF LAW

A.ROE, Capital Structure, Cost of Debt, and Financial Integrity 
To ensure that the competing interests of all parties were properly considered, the Commission has carefully analyzed all evidence of record including the testimony given and the various exhibits entered by all the parties. As set forth hereinafter, the Commission makes findings of fact and conclusions of law based on the evidentiary record created.  
Commission Determination Regarding Return on Equity (“ROE”). 
The difference between the respective ROE recommendations of Georgia Power and PIA Staff were significant. Georgia Power requested an ROE of 11.0%; whereas PIA Staff recommended an ROE of 9.45%, GAM recommended an ROE of no higher than 9.83%, GIPL recommended an ROE of 5.54%, and DOD recommended an ROE of 9.47%.

Georgia Power Witness Coyne relies on the following widely accepted models to develop his ROE determination: the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model, Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”), Bond Yield Risk Premium model, and the Expected Earnings models for a comparable utility group. Witness Coyne also considers the general economic and capital market environments, and the influence that capital market conditions exert over the results of the DCF, CAPM, and Risk Premium models in arriving at his recommended ROE. Based on that analysis. Witness Coyne estimates the cost of equity to run from 8.99% to 13.55%, with a four-model average of 10.92%. After including an adjustment for flotation costs, Witness Coyne arrives at his recommended allowed ROE of 11.00% in this proceeding. 

DOD Witness Reno recommends an ROE of 9.47% with a rate of return of 6.85%, an embedded cost of long-term debt of 3.53%, and an assumed capital structure comprised of 44.18% long-term debt and 53.00% common equity. Ms. Reno bases her ROE recommendation on the higher end of an 8.81-9.47% range derived from her mean estimates of market-based cost of equity methodologies using a proxy group of comparable risk utilities. Witness Reno develops her ROE recommendation using the variants of the Constant-Growth DCF model, the CAPM and Empirical CAPM, and the Comparable Earnings Model (“CEM”). 

PIA Staff Witness Gorman uses the DCF Model with constant growth, sustainable growth, and multi-stage growth rate estimates, the Risk-Premium Model, and the CAPM to estimate the Company’s recommended ROE at 9.45%. Witness Gorman recommends that the Commission reject Witness Coyne’s recommended ROE of 11.0% as excessive. 

GAM Witness LaConte recommends a ROE of 9.83%. Witness LaConte bases her recommendation on Witness Coyne’s analysis, adjusted to remove flotation costs, include the 180-day DCF analysis, and to consider historical market risk premiums in CAPM. 

GIPL Witness Ellis recommends a ROE of 5.54%. Witness Ellis uses the DCF and CAPM models, however, he uses drastically different input models that have not been adopted by this Commission and to do so now would be unprecedented.

As in every rate case, the Commission understands its obligation to provide a fair rate of return to the regulated utility. A fair rate of return is normally interpreted to mean that an efficient and economically managed utility will be able to maintain its financial integrity, attract capital, and have an opportunity to earn comparable returns to those of similar risk investments. These concepts are derived from economic and financial theory and are generally implemented using financial models and economic concepts. In determining a fair rate of return in the present case, the Commission is mindful of the standards established by the United States Supreme Court in Bluefield Water Works and Improvement Co. v. Public Serv. Comm’n of West Virginia, 262 U.S. 679 (1923), and Federal Power Comm’n v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591 (1942). 

Consistent with the Bluefield and Hope decisions, the Commission has considered comparable earnings, financial integrity, and capital attraction in reaching the decision that the 11.0% ROE recommended by Witness Coyne is just and reasonable. An 11.0 % ROE is a fair, forward looking rate of return on equity that provides the Company with an opportunity to earn a return on its investment over the period in which rates are in effect commensurate with returns that investors expect to earn on other investments of similar risk. Accordingly, the Commission finds and concludes that the ROE proposed by Witness Coyne’s recommendation will allow the Company to continue accessing capital markets at competitive rates and on reasonable terms. 

Witness Coyne’s testimony in this case supported his quantification and application of equity flotation costs and a financial risk adjustment. PIA Staff Witness Gorman, DOD Witness Reno, and GAM Witness LaConte presented no credible contrary analysis. Notwithstanding arguments against including flotation costs, Witness Coyne calculates flotation costs based upon economic evidence. 

Witness Coyne’s use of market capital structure adjusted to correct for financial risk differences between the proxy group’s market capital structure and the book regulatory capital structure proposed for Georgia Power is appropriate. Witness Coyne’s adjustment is correct and necessary to provide an overall cost of capital commensurate with market expectations. Neither Witness Gorman nor Witness Reno contested his testimony that his financial risk adjustment reflected universally accepted financial economic theory underlying estimations of the of the cost of capital. 

The recommendations of Witnesses Reno, Ellis, LaConte, and Gorman are unreasonably low under the governing Hope and Bluefield standards, as was shown by the prospective comparable earnings analysis presented by Witness Reno and Witness Coyne. Although every methodology for estimating the cost of equity has its limitations, no witness presented a sufficient justification for the Commission to depart from its long history of affording due weight to a prospective comparable earnings assessment using the expected performance of a reasonable and representative proxy group of utilities. The Commission considered the fact that PIA Staff Witness Gorman ignored the comparable earnings analysis, which has been one of the traditional cost of equity analyses employed by prior PIA Staff witnesses and accepted by this Commission. Although Witness Reno conducted a comparable earnings analysis, she provided no justification for ignoring the results. 

After careful consideration of the evidence presented by the parties, this Commission concludes that a 9.45% or 9.83% ROE is unjustifiably low and could impede the Company’s ability to attract and access capital at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. The corrected calculation of the cash flows available to the Company after debt service further confirm that a ROE below 9.83% on the existing capital structure is unreasonably low and likely would result in financial harm detrimental to the interests of customers and the public. Through the ROE it awards, the Commission has historically acknowledged the Company’s long-standing history of excellent performance and customer service as being above industry average. Witnesses Gorman, Ellis, LaConte, and Reno disregard this Commission’s well-established precedent and policy without explanation. In light of the increased risk the Company assumes by agreeing to be bound by an ARP, this Commission has included additional basis points in its awarded ROE. PIA Staff’s recommendations would result in at least a 105 basis point reduction in ROE from the Company’s current authorized ROE, which ignores and contradicts this Commission’s policy of gradualism. Therefore, the Commission approves a 11.0% ROE, as supported by Company Witness Coyne.
Commission Determination Regarding Capital Structure. 
Georgia Power proposed that a capital structure containing approximately 56% common equity and 44% debt be used for ratemaking purposes in this proceeding. The Company maintains that the proposed 11.0% ROE and 56% equity ratio will ensure that Georgia Power has access to capital whenever necessary, provides the opportunity to earn a fair return for its investors, and does so at rates favorable to customers. Witness Reno accepted the Company’s proposed capital structure. 

Staff Witness Gorman agrees with the request 56% common equity structure for 2023. Witness Gorman recommends that the Commission lower the Company’s equity ratio to 51% common equity for 2024 and 2025 after Vogtle Units 3 and 4 are placed in-service, consistent with the ratio approved in the 2013 Rate Case. According to Witness Gorman, his recommendations are consistent with ratemaking capital structures for other similarly situated regulated utility companies and are adequate to support the Company’s bond rating, financial integrity, and credit standing, but at much lower cost to customers. Witness Gorman contends that Witness Coyne’s 56% common equity ratio is excessive for a low-risk electric regulated utility and compared to the electric utility industry average. 

GAM Witness LaConte testifies that the Company’s 56% common equity ratio is excessive and unnecessary to maintain financial integrity. Witness LaConte testified that the average book common equity ratio of Witness Coyne’s proxy group, adjusted to remove utilities credit ratings lower than the Company’s, is 53% and recommends the Company’s common equity ratio be authorized at a level no greater 53% to be more in line with the average of other vertically integrated electric investor-owned utilities. 

GIPL Witness Ellis recommends an increase in the Company’s proposed common equity ratio to 61.7%, which he justifies due to his drastically low recommended ROE of 5.54%. Witness Ellis does not provide any examples of investor-owned utilities operating under a similar recommended ROE and common-equity ratio structures. 

The Commission finds and concludes that adoption of PIA Staff and Intervenors’ capital structure positions would contradict this Commission’s history of consistent and predictable regulation and harm the Company’s financial integrity. The Commission understands that its constructive regulation is a component of the credit rating agencies’ assessment of Georgia Power’s credit profile because it determines the Company’s rate levels necessary to recover expenses such as depreciation, O&M, storm damage cost recovery, and return on investment as well as the terms and conditions of electric service. This Commission reviewed the Company’s equity ratio in light of the Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) less than five years ago and determined that an increase in equity capitalization was necessary to counterbalance the negative effects of tax reform on the Company’s cash flows. The impacts of the TCJA still exist; therefore, the Company argues it must maintain its 56% equity ratio as carefully reviewed and approved by the Commission in the 2019 Rate Case. None of the capital structure recommendations proposed by PIA Staff or Intervenors adequately reflect Georgia Power’s need for continued access to capital on reasonable terms and the importance of maintaining its credit quality. Based on the evidence presented, the Commission finds and concludes that the recommended capital structure of 56% common equity level is just and reasonable to all parties considering all the evidence presented and is necessary to avoid a credit rating downgrade. 
Commission Determination Regarding Cost of Debt. 
Georgia Power recommended a 3.53% cost of debt be used for ratemaking purposes in this proceeding. Only DOD Witness Reno challenges the Company’s proposed cost of long-term debt. Witness Reno testifies that market and economic indicators do not support the increasing cost of debt in the ARP. Instead, Witness Reno recommends that for purposes of the ARP, the Commission require that the Company use the cost of long-term debt for the test year (3.53 %). 

The Commission finds that the Company’s proposed cost of debt is a fair and reasonable approximation of the cost of debt during the three-year ARP period and should be approved. 
Commission Determination Regarding Georgia Power Financial Integrity. 
In addition to setting rates that permit the Company to continue to provide clean, safe, reliable and affordable electric service, the Commission is tasked with ensuring the financial integrity of the Company in order for it to access capital at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. The Company presented testimony on the impacts of its proposed cost of capital and capital structure as well as the impacts of PIA Staff’s proposals on the Company’s financial risk metrics used by the credit rating agencies to determine credit quality. Although PIA Staff Witness Gorman calculated the FFO to Debt for S&P, he erred in his calculations and underestimated the effect of his recommendations on the Company’s financial health. When corrected, these errors reveal that PIA Staff’s proposals would reduce the Company’s credit metrics to levels well below the very clearly communicated expectations of the credit rating agencies, and that would likely trigger negative ratings actions (Tr. 3950, 4184.) This degradation in financial health and credit quality would harm the Company’s customers. Notably, PIA Staff counsel did not question the Company’s calculations or resulting impact of Witness Gorman’s errors during the hearings on the Company’s rebuttal testimony. 

To evaluate how its recommendations would impact Georgia Power’s credit ratings, the Company calculated its FFO to debt ratio, which is a financial metric used to assess risk based on the Company’s ability to pay off debt using net operating income alone. (Tr. 799-800.) Mr. Gorman acknowledges the importance of maintaining the Company’s FFO to debt (financial risk metrics) above the credit rating agencies’ general benchmarks, and attempted to present his analysis with that in mind. (Tr. 3531-33.) However, as the Company pointed out on rebuttal, Mr. Gorman’s FFO to debt calculation did not impute debt in accordance with S&P’s credit metric methodology.  This error stemmed from confusion between the PIA Staff and the Company as to whether Georgia Power had debt-like obligations including pension obligations, lease liabilities, and asset retirement obligations and where such obligations are recorded. By excluding more than $5.5 billion of debt imputations, Mr. Gorman’s credit metric expectation is overstated and fails to illustrate the harmful effects of his recommended ROE and equity ratio. Had Mr. Gorman’s recalculation included the proper S&P debt imputations, it would have shown that the Company’s metrics, assuming Staff’s adjustments were accepted by the Commission, are insufficient to maintain sufficient access to capital at reasonable rates. Due to this error and the assumptions serving as the basis for Mr. Gorman’s recommendations, the Commission does not adopt PIA Staff’s proposed ROE and capital structure. The cumulative adjustments recommended by PIA Staff would limit the FFO to debt metric to approximately 20% for the foreseeable future without the opportunity for improvement. 
The Commission’s action in this proceeding should not impair or worsen the Company’s credit quality or restrict the Company’s access to capital at reasonable terms and costs. The Commission agrees with the Company that by setting rates based on a 11.0% ROE, 56% common equity ratio, and 3.53% cost of debt is fair and reasonable and will not threaten the Company with a credit rating downgrade. 
B. Alternate Rate Plan and Alternate Rate Plan Earnings Band
Under Georgia law, the Commission may not “order” the Company to enter into an ARP unless Georgia Power agrees to such an ARP. Without an agreement, the Commission must resolve this case under traditional ratemaking standards, which involve a single test year analysis and would eliminate the positive attributes of a three-year predictable plan such as the institution of earnings ranges and earnings sharing mechanisms. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-25; 46-2-26.1. However, for several rate case cycles, this Commission has approved, and the Company has agreed to, three-year ARPs in lieu of traditional test year ratemaking. The Company recommends continuing the current ARP structure approved by the Commission in the 2019 Rate Case. This would continue the earnings sharing mechanism for earnings above the band and maintain the Interim Cost Recovery mechanism for earnings projected to fall below the band. The Company proposed to keep the current earnings band of 9.50% to 12.00% with the approved ROE as an approximate midpoint. 

PIA Staff proposed an earnings band ranging from 8.45-10.45% with the 9.45% ROE set point centered in the middle of the 200-point band. PIA Staff also proposed modifications to the Company’s ARP to apply all earnings above the band to deferred costs rather than shared between customers and the Company.

Regarding the earnings band, DOD Witness Larry Blank recommends an ROE earnings band of 100 basis points above the ROE if the Commission authorizes an ROE below the Company’s requested 11%. In the alternative, Witness Blank reiterates his recommendation from the 2019 Rate Case that the earnings band be narrowed to +/- 50 basis points above and below the target ROE based on the assumption that it would reduce the risk to the Company. GSEIA Witnesses Lucas and Young recommended the Commission reduce the approved band to a 100-point band (+/- 50 basis points), with the ROE at the center, but testify that at minimum it should be set at a 200-point band. Commercial Group Witness Chriss supports the Company’s band and recommends that the Commission maintain the basis point spread around the band of the final approved ROE. 

The Commission has resolved prior Georgia Power rate case proceedings through three-year accounting orders as opposed to traditional rate case proceedings. Under this arrangement, all stakeholders win as demonstrated by the Company’s consistent delivery of exceptional service while keeping rates low in relation to other utilities. Maintaining the earnings band incentivizes the Company to continue to innovate and find efficiencies in operations beyond those the Company was able to identify since the last rate case. Customers have benefitted under this structure and the Commission approves the continuation of these benefits for customers under the Company’s proposed ARP. PIA Staff’s recommendation is effectively an earnings cap with the top end of the band set artificially low at a level below even the Company’s cost of equity. 

Witness Blank’s recommendation to narrow the ROE earnings band to +/- 50 basis points fails to recognize that narrowing the earnings band necessarily increases risk to customers. Increasing the risk to customers alters the fundamental nature of the ARPs that have been in place for the last 25 years. Georgia Power has proposed an earnings band around the proposed ROE that is consistent with the structure of the earnings band previously approved by the Commission. 

Therefore, the Commission finds and concludes that since customers benefit under this ARP structure, the Company’s three-year proposed ARP is fair and reasonable in light of all the evidence presented. The Commission finds that it is reasonable to adopt the Company’s proposed ARP and approve the current earnings band of 9.50% to 12.00%. 
C. Commission Determination on Recovery of CCR ARO Compliance Costs 
The Company and PIA Staff both agree there should be a continuation of the CCR ARO cost recovery methodology approved in the 2019 Rate Case. GAM Witness Pollock recommends the Company not be allowed to earn a return on the CCR ARO regulatory asset balance until securitization is authorized. 
The Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs are required to comply with federal and state environmental regulations and are appropriately included in rate base. Further, this Commission approved the Company’s Environmental Compliance Strategy, which included the Company’s ash pond closure plans and CCR strategy, as part of the 2022 IRP in Docket No. 44160. No party in this case filed testimony or presented evidence challenging the appropriateness of the Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs sought to be recovered in this case. Witness Pollock’s recommendation is not currently possible and would require a change in state law to implement. It would be inappropriate for this Commission to deny the Company recovery of its currently incurred financing costs based solely on the potential for future securitization legislation. 

This Commission has carefully considered the evidence and testimony presented on these issues and finds that it is just and reasonable for Georgia Power to continue to recover CCR ARO compliance costs over a three-year period in base rates as proposed by the Company because this provides the best balance between the impact on customer’s rates and the total cost. No Commission action on securitization of CCR ARO compliance costs is warranted at this time. GAM Witness Pollock’s recommendation is denied.
D.Revenue Requirement Adjustments 
Commission Determination Regarding Levelized or Step Rate Adjustment. 
Within its recommended ARP, the Company proposed to levelized its requested rate increase to balance the benefit to customers from three years of stable and predictable rates with the Company’s need to timely recover its projected revenue deficiency. The Company argues that customers are fairly compensated for the projected over-collection in the first two years of the levelization. For the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company would defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving customers credit on the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted average cost of capital. This proposed deferral reduces the levelized revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully amortizes the advancement by the end of 2025. The Company argues a levelized approach to rate increases shifts economic risks to the Company by insulating customers from the impacts of a possible downturn in the sales forecast or cost increases. The Company states that it is better equipped to manage negative economic factors that impact the cost of running the business, as opposed to asking customers to shoulder the impacts, which would occur with increased rates under step increases. The Company claims a levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to customers than a step increase approach over the three-year ARP. 
In contrast, PIA Staff advocates for a step increase approach, which would increase customer rates by an increasing amount during each of the next three years and better align with the Company’s demonstrated revenue deficiencies in 2024 and 2025. Under PIA Staff’s approach, current rates would be maintained through 2023 and no rate increase would take place until 2024. This results in lower than projected revenue requirements for years 2023 and 2024. 
The Commission finds and concludes that in light of all the facts of the case that levelizing the proposed increases over the three-year ARP is the more appropriate approach for customers. All parties support rate stability and the Commission holds that the fluctuation in projected costs year-to-year under a step approach creates greater uncertainty for customers than under a levelized approach. Therefore, PIA Staff’s recommended rate adjustment structure with a step increase across the three-year ARP is denied. 
Commission Determination Regarding Electric Vehicle Charging Facilities. 
Since 2014, the Company has deployed electric vehicle (“EV”) charging infrastructure throughout the state to bring greater awareness to the benefits of driving electric vehicles, including both economic savings and environmental impact, as well as enabling the growth of EVs in Georgia. The EV chargers were installed to help the Company respond to the evolving needs and expectations of its customers, including expanded access points for customers to charge electric vehicles. Georgia Power’s 2022 Rate Case filing reflects (i) $27 million per year for each year of the ARP for infrastructure upgrades to implement the Make Ready program and (ii) $3 million per year to maintain the development and deployment of the Community Charging program, both of which connect communities across the state through corridor chargers and serves the infrastructure needs of existing drivers in high-adoption regions. The Company proposed to use the Community Charging program to expand critical charging infrastructure to alleviate charging deserts that are found in rural and underserved communities and would not impact existing competitive charging markets.

The Company argued that because the Commission has previously approved the recovery of certain EV-related costs from both the Make Ready and Community Charging programs and because of their proven success, the Commission can and should approve the Company’s full EV-related request in this proceeding. Nevertheless, Georgia Power acknowledged that the actual amount to be recovered is a policy decision by the Commission. 

PIA Staff Witnesses Trokey and Smith excluded in full both Make Ready and Community Charing program EV costs and revenues from rate base in their recommended adjustments to the Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing. According to PIA Staff, Make Ready program costs should not be included in the Company’s base rates because the program obligates all customers to subsidize an unregulated service, from which they will not directly benefit. Staff argues that the Company’s implementation of Make Ready to date has exceeded the scope of uses specified in the 2019 rate case stipulation. Staff further argues that the Make Ready Program creates an uneven playing field for EV Charging Service providers. Regarding the Community Charging program, Staff argues the program does not generate enough revenue to cover its costs and that it is inappropriate to allow the Company to expand its market share of retail chargers at the expense of customers in light of market competition and increased state and federal fundings.

 The AACE Witness panel of Witnesses Akers, Nelson, and Selvidge support the concept of the Make Ready program but recommend that it include targeted goals, objectives, guardrails, and be subject to Commission review. The panel argued that the Company’s current community chargers are not serving charging deserts. The AACE panel recommended the Community Charging program be limited to only provide level 2 charging, and proposed that the Company be directed to develop public guidelines through which the need for community charger investment can be identified. 

EVgo Witness Murchie recommended the Commission approve the Company’s proposed expansion of the Make Ready program. Witness Murchie recommended the Commission direct the Company to develop and publish its program rules and requirements and expand eligibility to include third-party charging station owner-operators. Witness Murchie also recommended the Commission approve the Community Charging program with additional specifications. He recommended the Company use objective and publicly available siting criteria to determine the location of its Community Charging stations and that it file a pricing proposal with the Commission based on a market analysis to allow stakeholder and additional Commission review. 

ChargePoint Witness Wilson testified in support of both the Make Ready and Community Charging programs, and Coalition Witnesses Deffley and Nifong provided testimony in support of the Make Ready program and recommended its eligibility expand to include sites with less than five charging stations. 

The Commission finds and concludes that the $30 million annual capital investment for projected capital and O&M costs associated with the Company’s two electric transportation programs should be included in the Company’s retail rates. As previously stated by this Commission, whether EV charging facility costs warrant inclusion in the Company’s cost of service is a matter of Commission policy. This Commission finds that EV charging facility costs must not be examined in isolation: EV charging stations serve as much more than an additional source of revenue for the Company or any other business for that matter. Electric transportation investments also help attract economic growth, as evidenced by the major EV manufacturing announcements, and EV charging stations provide public benefits above and beyond dollars and cents. For example, the overall grid benefits from EV drivers who routinely shift their load and place a positive benefit onto the grid. In addition, evidence presented by the Company indicates that the Make Ready program has positive Rate Impact Measure values, which benefits all customers by putting downward pressure on rates. Accordingly, the Commission finds that allowing EV charging station costs into rate base strikes the right balance for the monetary and non-monetary benefits associated with EV infrastructure deployment. 
Commission Determination Regarding the Company’s Grid Investment Plan 
Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan is a 12-year strategic effort that focuses on (1) improving distribution reliability and (2) replacing aging transmission assets. The Commission approved the first three years of projected capital expenditures for this program in the 2019 Rate Case. As part of its current filing, the Company requests that the Commission approve dollars associated with the next three-year period of the Grid Investment Plan (2022-2025). Company Witness Robinson testified regarding the Company’s Grid Investment Plan request in this docket. He explained that while the Grid Investment Plan objectives remain unchanged, the Company refined certain program components and assumptions based on the positive insights, learnings, and data gathered during the initial three-year implementation period of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company has requested $2.2 billion for the transmission and distribution portions of the Grid Investment Plan for projects to be initiated during the three year ARP window from 2022 through 2025.
PIA Staff witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana oppose the Grid Investment Plan and recommend that the Commission: (1) reject all transmission investments planned for the next three years, and (2) remove 85% of the planned distribution investments. Through these recommendations, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens ask that the Commission reverse course and disregard the evidentiary record from the 2019 Rate Case and the present docket supporting the Company’s investment in the Grid Investment Plan. Regarding the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff contends that the Company is prematurely replacing equipment based solely on asset age and recommends that Georgia Power be required to employ more objective testing and inspection practices for identifying appropriate transmission investments. Regarding the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff recommends that this Commission eliminate 85% of the planned distribution investments included in the Grid Investment Plan through denial of the undergrounding and Basic Insulation Level investments. In addition, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend that all Grid Investment Plan costs be accompanied by corresponding reliability improvements outweighing such cost. Given that the Company’s revised program components based on learnings from initial implementation, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens question all estimate and benefit projections underpinning the Grid Investment Plan. PIA Staff suggests that no pending “reliability emergency” that justifies dramatic increases transmission and distribution expenditures as proposed in the Grid Investment Plan.
The Company filed rebuttal testimony responding to the issues raised by Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana. The Company fundamentally disagrees with Staff’s Grid Investment Plan-related recommendations and overall characterization of the rationale and justification for these investments. The Company rebutted the notion that the Grid Investment Plan involves the “premature replacement” of aging transmission assets. The Company explained in the 2019 Rate Case and in the current docket that asset age drives the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan. According to the Company, Georgia Power employed objective approaches to determine which equipment should be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan as proposed by PIA Staff. Witness Robinson pointed out that the Company relied on its long history of robust maintenance practices, internal experience, and historical operational knowledge to determine which transmission equipment will be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace transmission equipment that will be greater than the Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. Georgia Power explained that transmission equipment installed during the 1950s, 1960s, and 1970s, is now approaching 50, 60, and 70 year lives. The transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan reasonably targets the replacement of aging assets to preserve and improve system reliability and help ensure the safety of the general public and Company employees.
The Company also responded to arguments that the Commission should require cost-benefit analyses for each transmission investment included in the Grid investment Plan. The Company maintains that such analyses are impractical and not useful given the dynamic and networked nature of the transmission system. Witness Robinson identifies several factors that complicate the development a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit analysis of these investments, including failure type (controlled or catastrophic), impacts on other capital projects, lead times for replacement equipment, and redispatch generation-related costs. 
Georgia Power also countered PIA Staff’s distribution-related recommendations. Witness Robinson explained that eliminating 85% of the planned distribution investments as proposed by Witness Alvarez and Stephens, eliminates the overarching purpose and rationale supporting the Grid Investment Plan. The Company disagreed with PIA Staff regarding the existence of a pending reliability emergency and maintains that Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue worsening each year that the Company does not make these grid investments. The Company presented data showing that from 2013—2016, its system average interruption duration index (“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately 44% and system average interruption frequency index (“SAIFI”) worsened by approximately 10%. The Company highlights that it carefully and strategically developed the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan to ensure lasting reliability improvements while minimizing the total cost to the customer. Witness Robinson also rebutted arguments that it reduced reliability expectations by revising the end-state SAIDI for the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan. Witness Robinson highlights that Georgia Power appropriately acted in the best interest of customers by reassessing and refreshing the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan—these efforts helped ensure that the investments will continue producing positive results in the lowest total cost to customers. The Company explains that is intermittent refinement a multi-year investment plan is essential to ensure that customer capital is managed responsibly. The Company maintains that the Grid Investment Plan encompasses the qualities of the alternative programs recommended by PIA Staff—it accomplishes these goals strategically and appropriately addresses feeder level issues through system level optimization. 
Considering all the evidence, the Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable and appropriate for the Company to continue pursuing investments in the Grid Investment Plan as proposed. Specifically, the Commission approves dollars associated with the next three-year period of the Grid Investment Plan: $2.2B for the 2023-2025 period. The Commission notes that Company witness Robinson presented detailed and persuasive testimony in support of the Grid Investment Plan. In light of the Commission’s assessment of the credibility of witnesses opining on the facts and legal considerations at issue with the Grid Investment Plan, the Commission relies heavily on Witness Robinson’s testimony. Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana did not present sufficient evidence challenging the reasonableness and justification for the Grid Investment Plan-related work. The recommendations and testimony presented by these Staff witnesses ignored the potential risks and cost increases that could result from rejecting the Grid Investment Plan and delaying T&D investments. 
Based on the foregoing, the Commission finds and concludes that as in the 2019 Rate Case, Georgia Power has demonstrated the need for the investments outlined in the Grid Investment Plan and the Grid Investment Plan is in the public interest. The Commission finds that the Company’s approach represents a reasonable and cost-effective approach to managing grid-related risks during a period of transformation. 
Commission Determination Regarding DERMS / Section G Proposals
The Company requested $100 million to invest in a Distributed Energy Resource Management System (DERMS) to integrate Distributed Energy Resources (DER) with greater visibility, predictability and control. PIA Staff proposes to reject DERMS spending until the penetration level of DER on the grid increases. PIA Staff claims that existing systems and processes can manage DER. The Company persuasively argued that DERMS will allow the Company to consider demand side resources alongside supply side resources in future IRPs, as ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. Further, the Commission supports the Company’s implementation of DERMS at this point will also allow for maturation of the DERMS solution as thousands or tens of thousands of additional DER are connected to the grid in the coming years. As such, the Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s investment and requested funding for DERMS is reasonable and should be approved in order to optimize the integration of DER on the Georgia Power system. 
Commission Determination Regarding Stock-Based Compensation and Payroll Taxes.
The Company included expenses related to stock-based compensation in its 2022 Rate Case filing. The Company explained that the total compensation plan, including stock-based at-risk compensation is market competitive and appropriately balances operational with financial focus for both short-term and longer term to drive employee behavior in ways that balance the interests of customers and shareholders alike. According to the Company, moving at-risk pay to base pay would not be in the best interest of customers because it would encourage employees to focus solely on short-term operational performance with no balance or accountability to long-term operational costs and efficiencies. Therefore, the Company believes that stock-based compensation should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates.

The Company testified that its budgeted costs to attract, retain and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and appropriately should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates. The Company maintains that customers benefit from a healthy company, which should be measured over the short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions made by the Company are optimized for both short- and long-term benefits. In addition, the Company proposed to include the payroll tax expenses associated with stock-based incentive compensation, which the Company also contends represent prudently incurred costs.

PIA Staff proposed disallowances for the Company’s stock-based compensation program expenses. Specifically, Staff removed the Performance Unit Shares (“PSU”) and the Restricted Stock Units (“RSU”) expense from retail jurisdictional operating expenses establishing the Company’s revenue requirement. PIA Staff also recommended reducing the Company’s Performance Pay Program (“PPP”) expenses by two-thirds to only allow recovery from customers for operational goals that benefit Georgia Power customers. 

According to PIA Staff, the stock-based incentive compensation and two-thirds of the PPP expenses for these programs should not be included in the revenue requirement as these costs are incurred to improve the Southern Company financial performance for the benefit of shareholders, not to improve customer service or meet other regulated utility service requirements. In addition, PIA Staff recommended that the Commission exclude the payroll tax expenses associated with the stock-based compensation. 

In its rebuttal testimony, Georgia Power argued that these proposed disallowances are inappropriate and should be rejected by the Commission. Company witnesses testified that PIA Staff did not base these recommendations on supporting data or market analysis that the Company’s expenses are excessive or that the programs are not competitive with the external market. Georgia Power also argued its position is consistent with the 2019 Rate Case Order. 

The Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable and appropriate for the Company to include stock-based compensation and the associated payroll taxes in its revenue requirement. The Company’s long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and competitive with the utility industry. The Company’s long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and competitive with the utility industry. The Company’s budgeted costs for stock-based compensation to attract, retain and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and appropriately should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates. 
Commission Determination regarding Amortization of Retired Unit Remaining Net Book Values. 
The Company has proposed to amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Wansley Units 1-2, Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1, which were retired by August 31, 2022 in accordance with the Company’s 2022 IRP, over their remaining useful lives as approved in Docket No. 42516. These proposed amortization periods are consistent with similar regulatory assets and PIA Staff did not object to the Company’s proposal. 

In addition, the Company requested to defer depreciation and proposed to amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 over a nine-year period beginning January 1, 2023. Based on the depreciation study filed in this case, Plant Bowen Units 1-2 are scheduled to be depreciated through 2027 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 are scheduled to be depreciated through 2028 in accordance with the retirement dates proposed in the 2022 IRP. More specifically, the Company requests to defer the difference each year between the amount of depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are ultimately retired, the deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at the retirement date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending December 31, 2031. The Company’s request was unchallenged in this case. The Commission should approve the deferral and amortization of Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3 as requested by the Company without modification. 

The Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s proposed amortization of the remaining net book value of units retired in the 2022 IRP, which is consistent with the Company’s treatment of other regulatory assets and liabilities is reasonable and appropriate. The Commission also finds and concludes that it is reasonable to approve the depreciation deferral and nine-year amortization for the associated regulatory assets for Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3 as requested by the Company.
E. Cost Allocation
Commission Determination Regarding Cost-of-Service Study Methodology. 
The Company filed its 2022 Cost of Service Study (“COSS”) consistent with the methodology used in prior Georgia Power rate cases. Georgia Power advocated for the continued use of the 12 Coincident Peak (“12-CP”) methodology for allocating costs. Further, the Company states that its COSS accurately reflects cost causation and can be used to judge the adequacy of rates with respect to the costs to serve and be used for rate design purposes. 

Instead of the 12-CP production cost methodology, PIA Staff proposes to use the Base Intermediate Peak and Probability of Dispatch methodologies. PIA Staff argues that the shortcoming of the 12-CP method is that accurate load data is required by class throughout the year and that once a system to record such data is in place, the administration and maintenance of such a system is not cumbersome. PIA Staff Witness Watkins stated at hearing that the Company had an abundance of more than adequate data. Thus, the only shortcoming of the 12-CP method identified by PIA Staff does not hold true for Georgia Power. 

GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar and GAM propose the Company use either a 4-CP or 6-CP production cost allocation methodology arguing that as a summer peaking utility, the four summer months June through September drive the Company’s peak demand and should be the basis for allocating the Company’s production costs. However, in rebuttal the Company identified that its system is driven and influenced by non- summer periods in addition to summer peaks, and it utilizes Expected Unserved Energy in its determination of the optimal reserve margin for both summer and winter seasons. The Company argues that this renders a pure summer peak or 4-CP allocator is an oversimplification of the Company’s generation planning process.

Based on the evidence presented, the Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s cost of service study is reflective of cost causation and is objective, fair, and equitable to the different groups of Georgia Power customers. The Company’s cost of service study methodology provides a reasonable basis to functionalize, classify and allocate costs to customer classes, is reflective of the Company’s system needs, and is consistent with the methodology employed and approved it its previous rate cases before the Commission. The Commission approves the Company’s COSS as filed by Company Witness Evans.
