Commission Determination on Basic Service Charge.
Georgia Power proposes to increase the Basic Service Charge (“BSC”) for the Domestic Group by approximately $2.27 per month. The Company explained that it is proposing to increase all traditional base rates on an equal percentage basis, including rate elements, which therefore includes the basic service charge. 

PIA Staff Witness Watkins disagrees with the Company’s recommendation to increase the basic service charge on an equal percentage basis. Witness Watkins testifies that the basic service charge should only reflect those direct costs required to connect and maintain a customer’s account. Therefore, PIA Staff recommends this Commission deny the Company’s requested increase in the basic service charge. PIA Staff recommends in the event that this Commission grants the Company’s requested increase, that the income qualified senior discount be increased by an amount equal to the increase in the basic service charge. 

GIPL Witness Barnes also recommends the Commission deny the Company’s requested basic service charge increase and leave the current residential basic service charge in place. Witness Barnes testifies that that the cost-of-service study erroneously classifies a portion of the costs associated with the shared distribution system as customer-related based on its Minimum Distribution System (“MDS”) analysis. 

In rebuttal, the Company proposed to increase the income qualified senior discount by the amount the basic service charge is ultimately increased.

After considering the parties’ testimony and all of the evidence presented, the Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s proposed BSC increase is just and reasonable and strikes an appropriate balance to provide rates that more clearly reflect actual cost causation, minimize subsidization, and provide proper price signals to customers in the rate class, while also moderating the impact of such increase on low-usage customers. As in prior rate cases, the parties in the present case filed testimony advocating various methodologies for cost allocation and various rate designs favorable to each party’s interests. In light of the reasonable allocation of costs and rate design contemplated, the Commission finds that the allocation of costs and rate design contemplated and specified by Georgia Power are reasonable and in the best interests of all customer groups. 
F. Rate Design 
Company Witness Larry Legg provided testimony regarding the Company’s existing tariffs and its proposed changes to rate design. The Company testified that its portfolio of rates proposed in the 2022 Rate Case filing offer optionality for customers while supporting a gradual move toward more modern rates. PIA Staff and several Intervenors provided testimony on various rate design issues in this proceeding, as discussed below. Having considered the testimony and exhibits of each witness and the entire record in this proceeding, the Commission makes its findings and conclusions on each of the issues set forth below. 
Residential Rate Design.
The Company proposes several modifications to its residential rate design offerings. Consistent with its request in the 2019 base rate case, the Company proposes to close the Residential Service (R) tariff (“the R Rate”) to new residential customers on January 1, 2023. The default residential rate for new premises would continue to be Time of Use – Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”), and the Company proposes closing Time of Use – Residential Energy Only (“TOU-REO”) to new residential customers.
a.Modifications to the R Rate. 
PIA Staff proposed several modifications to the ‘R’ Rate. Staff contends that the Company’s retention of a declining winter block rate on the ‘R’ rate incents wintertime usage in conflict with the Company’s winter seasonal planning posture and winter system cost structure. PIA Staff recommend the elimination of the declining-block rates during the non-summer months to a flat rate for all kWh usage due to increasingly higher winter peaks. 
The Company responds in rebuttal that the R Rate is antiquated and sends poor price signals to customers regarding their energy usage. The Company argues that it makes little sense to fundamentally alter the R Rate’s design in light of its other more modern offerings. The Company recommends closing the R Rate to new residential premises.
b.Closing the Residential Service (R) Rate to New Premises.
Georgia Power requests to begin transitioning from the more antiquated, volumetric option of the R Rate to move toward more modern rates by first closing the R Rate to new residential premises. The Company argues this will allow it to establish new residential accounts that better align with the cost to serve customers. If approved, customers establishing service at new premises will continue to be enrolled in the TOU-RD rate as a default rate, as established in the 2019 Rate Case Order. Customers will still have the ability to select from four of the most modern rate options. After the initial year of service at those new premises, customers will be eligible for two additional rates. The R Rate would remain available to existing residential premises on the Georgia Power system that established service prior to January 2023. 
PIA Staff opposed the Company’s recommendation to close the R Rate to new premises. PIA Staff argued that while it supports marketing and education to grow customer adoption of modern rates, Staff does not support the Company eliminating customer choice in rates. GIPL Witness Barnes also opposed the Company’s proposal to close the R Rate. Based on concerns with time of use rates in general and specifically the default TOU-RD, both PIA Staff and Witness Barnes recommend the Company re-designate the R Rate as the default residential rate. 
c.Time of Use – Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”)
To transition to more modern rate designs and to provide customers with price signals that more accurately reflect the cost of service, the Company proposes maintaining TOU-RD as the default rate for new residential premises. The Company argues that this a logical step in modernizing its rate design offerings. 
PIA Staff and Certain Intervenors object to the Company’s TOU-RD proposal. PIA Staff assert that the TOU-RD rate would be a poor default rate for residential customers because customers lack sufficient information or knowledge on these issues to appropriately minimize their bills on such a rate. PIA Staff, the Coalition, and GIPL Witness Barnes further argued that from analysis of Company data, TOU-RD has been significantly more expensive than the R Rate or TOU-REO.
The Company testified in rebuttal that TOU-RD is designed to be revenue neutral for the average residential customer and to provide customers with better price signals to enable them to make more informed decisions to reduce their energy use and electric bills. The Company stated that the bill comparisons that PIA Staff highlight in testimony are heavily skewed by extremely low usage and fail to tell a complete story because they represent premises that are new to the Company’s system. The Company testifies that the average monthly usage for those premises is far below the level at which the usage will ultimately mature.
The Company testifies that customer surveys reflect TOU-RD customers are more satisfied with their rate and feel more knowledgeable regarding the behaviors that can help them capitalize on their rate plan and reduce their energy bills.
d.Time of Use – Residential Energy Only (TOU-REO)

The Company requests closing TOU-REO to new customers to avoid confusion associated with multiple residential TOU rates. PIA Staff and certain Intervenors recommend that TOU-REO continue to be offered to all residential customers because this rate provides a viable option for customers to shift their load and usage patterns from on-peak hours to off-peak hours. PIA Staff recommends if the Commission close the R Rate to new premises, TOU-REO is a more appropriate default time of use rate for customers than TOU-RD.

The Company testified in rebuttal that TOU-REO is much more volatile than TOU-RD and, moreover, that TOU-RD sends better price signals and more appropriately reflects the cost of a customer’s electric service. The Company reiterated that TOU-RD provides the most appropriate pricing signals to customers to enable to them modify their usage patterns and reduce their electric bills. 

e.FlatBill

Staff approves of the Company’s request to have a $50 minimum bill for FlatBill customers. Staff recommends the Company to expand customer awareness of its budget billing offerings.


Commission Decision on Residential Rate Design Issues.

In light of the parties’ testimony and all of the evidence presented, the Commission approves Georgia Power’s request to close the ‘R’ Rate to new residential premises and supports the Company’s gradual movement toward more modern rates. Closing the R rate to new premises does not affect existing customers on the ‘R’ rate unless they move to a residence that has never received service from Georgia Power before. In beginning the move toward more modern rates, the Commission finds PIA Staff and Intervenor arguments to otherwise modify the ‘R’ Rate by adjusting the declining winter block rate and usage tiers to be unnecessary. 
The Commission also approves the closure of TOU-REO and the continuation of TOU-RD as the default residential rate for new premises. PIA Staff and Intervenors have not provided sufficient justification for the need for the Company to offer residential customers more than two time-of-use rates where TOU-RD provides the most modern and appropriate pricing signals. 
The Commission approves the Company’s proposed adjustments to FlatBill, TOU-RD, and each of the remaining residential tariffs as filed by the Company.
Commission Determination Regarding Commercial and Industrial Rate Design.
a.Charge-It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider 

The Company requests the Commission approve its new CIEV rider. The CIEV rider will modify the monthly billing demands by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors. The Company proposes CIEV as a temporary rider to expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load factor for the account reaches 15% or greater. 

PIA Staff recommend the Commission approve the CIEV rider as filed in the Company’s Errata-Supplemental filing with quarterly reporting regarding the location, peak demand, usage, and revenue received from CIEV customers. EVgo Witness Murchie and ChargePoint Witness Wilson also supports the CIEV rider. Witness Wilson and Murchie recommend the Company allow any qualifying customers, not just new customers, take service under CIEV. AACE Witnesses Akers, Nelson, and Selvidge argue that the CIEV rider discriminates between EV charging providers. 

The Company explains in its rebuttal that the CIEV rider is a time limited offering to encourage the growth of electric vehicles (“EVs”) in Georgia and therefore argues it is inappropriate to expand participation to existing customers, which would eliminate the incentive for adoption of EV charging by new premises. 

b.Embedding ECCR costs in Marginal Rates. 

PIA Staff recommends that customers on marginal rates be assessed the fully embedded cost of the ECCR tariff. PIA Staff and GIPL Witness Barnes argue that ECCR costs should be recovered based on a customer’s kWH energy usage basis.  

The Company disagrees, arguing that by including fully embedded costs like ECCR in marginal rates is inappropriate because such a change is contrary with how marginal rates like RTP are designed to operate. The Company explains that fully embedded costs are recovered from the Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the rates. The Company also states that the ECCR costs include CCR ARO compliance costs, which are fully embedded costs incurred by the Company independent of a customer’s kWh usage. As such, counter to recommendations by GIPL Witness Barnes and PIA Staff, ECCR costs are appropriately recovered through the CBL portion of the rate. The Company asserts that if ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to the marginal portion of RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million overall. 

Given that the RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that are considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia and that a cost shift of this magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers, the Commission rejects PIA Staff and GIPL’s proposed ECCR modifications to marginal rates. 

c. Proposed Modifications to Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) Reduction Criteria. 

DOD Witness Blank  recommends modifying the Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install behind the meter generation. The Company disagreed with this proposal because customer generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does not justify the reduction of a customer’s CBL. According to the Company, CBLs may be reduced when load originally represented in the CBL has been permanently removed. Mr. Blank did not provide sufficient persuasive evidence to merit a change in the CBL criteria. Therefore, the Commission denies Witness Blank’s recommendation. 

DOD Witness Blank also criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction assessment based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under the RTP tariff for two years. In contrast, the Company supports the requirement that a customer remain off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because short term changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of embedded load into marginal load on the RTP rate. The RTP tariffs include provisions to revise the CBL as a result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy efficiency improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to temporary factors such as economic downturns. As previously held by this Commission, requiring customers who have not removed load to stay off RTP for 24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the integrity of the RTP program and protects other customers from potential cost shifts that might result from premature revision of the CBL. The Commission denies Witness Blank’s recommendation. 

d. Fixed Price Alternative and Price Protection Product offerings 

PIA Staff proposed to modify the terms and conditions of the Fixed Price Alternative (“FPA”) and Price Protection Product (“PPP”) offerings to remove Company discretion over when to offer these rates. The Company maintains that removing its discretion regarding when to offer these products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by other customers, since delays in acting during volatile markets could result in the development of product offerings that do not properly reflect pricing impacts. The Company explained that it considers multiple factors when deciding whether to offer the FPA rate and PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not limited to, uncertainty around environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors that could impact the pricing of these offerings. The Company stresses that it is to maintain flexibility to act quickly and update PPP offerings to best reflect market conditions, especially during times of fuel market volatility as have been experienced for the last year. 

Commission Decision on Commercial and Industrial Rate Design Issues.

The Commission finds and concludes that the CIEV tariff is reasonable and appropriately designed to encourage new EV charging service adoption. As such, the CIEV tariff is approved without modification. The Commission further finds and concludes that PIA Staff and Intervenor proposed changes to the Company’s commercial and industrial tariffs are not justified and should be denied. This Commission supports the balance the Company maintains in meeting customer demands for more beneficial rates with greater marginal load components. Further, the Commission rejects PIA Staff and UMS’ request to expand the TOU-FD eligibility. The Company’s commercial and industrial rates as filed should be approved without modification. 
Community Solar Program
Georgia Power’s Community Solar tariff offers qualifying customers the opportunity to support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing to a portion of the output from local solar facilities. (Tr. 888, 2184.) Every month, participating customers pay a fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities, and in return receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production from those facilities. (Id.) Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are retired on the customer’s behalf. (Tr. 888, 2184-85.)

In this case, the Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block and proposed that the pricing for General Service (“GS”) Community Solar participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt block. (Tr. 888, 929.) Georgia Power states that the Community Solar subscription price is designed to ensure that participating customers do not shift costs to other customers when kilowatt hours reduce the monthly bills of participating customers. (Tr. 939.) The Company argues that based on the approved structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per kW block of energy as proposed by several parties would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift cost recovery to non-participants. (Tr. 914.) The Company states that the Community Solar tariff is not intended to create bill savings for participating customers, but rather, is targeted toward customers who are interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. The
 
PIA Staff proposed two alternatives regarding the appropriate pricing for the Community Solar Program offers two alternatives and is premised on a Commission policy determination as to the purpose of the program. PIA Staff’s recommendation depends on whether the Community Solar Program is found by the Commission to offer bill savings opportunities for participating customers. As proposed by PIA Staff, if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy and realize bill savings, Staff recommends a pricing reduction to $20 and $22 pricing for residential and commercial customers. If the purpose of the program is to support clean energy but not the opportunity for bill savings, Staff supports the Company’s proposed $27.99 and 29.99 pricing for residential and commercial customers, respectively. (Tr.2449-50.) 

The Coalition recommended the Commission reject the Company’s proposed increase to the subscription price for the Community Solar Program. To increase participation in the program and provide a viable option for those who cannot host or afford rooftop solar, the Coalition recommends two alternatives. First, the Commission could maintain the current community solar fixed price per kW block structure but reduce the price to $16 per kW block for residential customers to exclude lost revenues from fuel, environmental, and nuclear cost recovery riders. Second, the Commission could permit customers to lock in the current $24.99 per kW block rate for the duration of the customer’s participation in the program. 

Although PIA Staff may believe the purpose of the Community Solar program is an open policy decision for the Commission, the Commission has already determined that the purpose of the program is not for bill savings. In the Commission’s June 7, 2017 Order approving the Community Solar Program tariff, the Commission clearly stated that the Community Solar Program “is not designed to produce credits in excess of a customer’s usage and a customer should anticipate the [Community Solar] program charges will exceed the value of any bill credits received.” Order approving Community Solar Program and CS-1 Tariff, Georgia Public Service Commission, Docket Nos. 40161 and 16573 (June 7, 2017) at 2. Rather, as confirmed by the Company, the Community Solar Program is targeted toward customers who are interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. While the Company agrees that increased participation in the Community Solar program should be encouraged, increased participation should not be at the expense of non-participating customers. The inclusion of commercial customers in the program and the adoption of the income qualified community solar pilot should help increase customer participation. With regard to the pricing for the Community Solar program, the Commission approves the Company’s requested block price increase for the Community Solar program to $27.99 per kW block for residential customers and $29.99 per residential block for commercial customers on the general service rate.
G. Commission Action on the Monthly Netting Pilot.
The Commission-approved a Monthly Netting Pilot (“Pilot”) in its 2019 Rate Case. The Pilot directed the Company to offer monthly netting on a pilot basis for the first 5,000 subscribers or until newly installed capacity reached 32 MW. The Pilot provided enrolled customers with full retail credit for excess generation, netted monthly. The Pilot was designed to provide the Company with data to evaluate the effect of monthly netting on the Company’s cost of service and customer class cost shifting.
The Company recommends this Commission: (1) discontinue the subsidization of monthly netting customers at the expense of other customers by using the monthly netting methodology combined with volumetric rate structures; (2) require all customers who enroll in the RNR tariff on or after January 1, 2023, to take service under a three-part rate; (3) develop a formal framework for the Commission’s referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading business practices.
1.Expansion of Monthly Netting Pilot.
The Company does not support expanding the monthly netting pilot. The Company asserts that a customer participating in the monthly netting pilot shifts an additional $287 of costs onto non-participating customers on an annual basis. (Tr. 5031.) The Company asserts this represents a cumulative shift of approximately $1.4 million annually for the 5,000 pilot customers. The Company requests recovery of this $1.4 million in this case. The Company also testifies that if left unaddressed and as participation in monthly netting increases, this cost-shift will increase. Therefore, the Company also seeks this Commission’s approval to close the monthly netting pilot.  
PIA Staff Witnesses Barber, Cook and Watkins do not make a specific recommendation regarding the expansion of the monthly netting pilot but offer the Commission several alternatives, which includes a range of possible compensation rates the Company could use to compensate RNR customers for excess energy pushed back to the grid. This range varies from approximately 6.99 cents to over 13 cents per kWh. 

Intervenors supported a continuation of the monthly netting pilot with various caveats. Coalition Witness Hill recommends the Commission continue the Pilot through the next ARP period and recommends a cap of no less 75,000 customers, if one is required. GIPL Witness Barnes recommends the Commission reinstate monthly netting with a cap set at 3% of the Company’s 2021 peak demand, to be reassessed once reached, and to include a minimum monthly bill of $22.43. Witness Barnes also recommends the Commission deny the Company’s requested 1.4 million monthly netting revenue erosion adjustment. 

GA Solar Witness Sercy recommends the Commission lift both the customer and capacity limits on monthly netting. GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar Witnesses Lucas, Young and Giese recommend the Commission expand the Pilot with no participation cap until the Company adopts a more rigorous cost-based approach to identify and address the cost shift. Witnesses Lucas, Young and Giese propose the Commission implement a $20 minimum monthly bill that is inclusive of the customer charge. 

SACE/Southface Witness Brown recommends the Commission reinstate the monthly netting pilot, without a participation cap, for three additional years. Witness Brown recommends the Company conduct an additional cost-of-service study to account for additional benefits of distributed solar that the cost shift did not take into consideration. 

On rebuttal, the Company responded that compensating BTM solar resources at the high levels proposed by PIA Staff and Intervenors, which are well above avoided cost and close to the retail rate, will further increase the cost shift to non-participating customers under the RNR tariff, as these values are not credibly tied to any quantification of actual benefit provided by the BTM resource. The Company argues that all parties ignored the Commission’s existing and established RCB Framework, which was jointly developed, applied, and approved to compensate BTM generators for excess energy. The Company defends its cost-shifting analysis and argues that neither PIA Staff nor Intervenors were able to credibly rebut it. 
2.Three Part Rates. 
The Company requests that the monthly netting pilot remain capped at its currently approved 5,000 customers to prevent additional cost shifting and revenue erosion. The Company recommends all new and existing customers participating under the RNR tariff be required to enroll in a three-part rate like TOU-RD beginning January 1, 2023 to better account for and address the cost shift caused by RNR customers, especially those participating in the monthly netting pilot.
PIA Staff disagrees with the Company’s recommendation to transition monthly netting customers to TOU-RD, arguing that it is “not well-suited for RNR customers due to high demand charge and extremely low off peak energy price.” Coalition Witness Hill, GIPL Witness Barnes, Georgia Solar Witness Sercy, GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar Witnesses Lucas, Young and Giese, and Southface Witness Brown all also oppose the Company’s proposal to require new customers enroll in a three-part rate. Witness Sercy recommends customers be allowed to select their rate schedule, and Witness Barnes testifies that TOU-RD is misaligned with cost-causation and is punitive to customers with infrequent high demand. 

The Company reiterates in rebuttal that it proposes transition netting customers to three-part rates to reduce the cost shift and testifies that continuing to allow RNR customers to take service under the R rate will only intensify the cost shift. The Company argues that Intervenors fail to demonstrate that the TOU-RD tariff does not appropriately recover costs and instead that Intervenors contentions are reactive to the to the potential for the TOU-RD rate to mitigate the current subsidy available to monthly netting customers on the R rate.

3.Increased Consumer Protection.
All parties support the Company’s recommendation to develop a more formal framework for the Commission’s referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading business practices.
Commission Decision on Monthly Netting. 
The Commission finds and concludes that the monthly netting pilot resulted in a cost shift to non-participating customers and should not be expanded. In order to address the cost shift associated with BTM generation, the Commission directs that new customers that enroll in the RNR tariff must take part in one of the Company’s three-part rates beginning January 1, 2023. RNR instantaneous shall continue to be made available to interested customers. RNR instantaneous customers shall continue to be paid for excess energy pushed back onto the grid at the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy Cost rate, consistent with the RCB Framework, and as filed annually with this Commission in Docket No. 16573. 
Within 90 days of this Order, the Company and Commission Staff shall initiate discussions and work with the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to formalize a consumer complaint process to better address consumer complaints related to bad actors in the rooftop solar industry.

III.ORDERING PARAGRAPHS

WHEREFORE, IT IS ORDERED, that the Company’s Alternate Rate Plan shall be adopted as proposed and as amended by the Company’s Supplemental Filing and Errata and the rates pursuant to the Alternate Rate Plan are just and reasonable, and effective for service rendered from and after January 1, 2023.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company is awarded a 11.0% ROE and 3.53% cost of debt, using 56% common equity in the capital structure. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the earnings band shall be 9.50-12.00%, with earnings above the top of the band shared 80/20 with customers.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company will recover CCR ARO compliance costs ratably over a three-year period in base rates as proposed by the Company. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company may include in base rates the projected capital and O&M costs for the Make Ready and Community Charging programs and the associated electric transportation activities such as the net investment of all the existing community electric vehicle charging facilities, whether located on and off Company property and the associated revenues and expenses, the commercial and residential rebates for installing EV chargers, and an amount for EV education and awareness.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company appropriately included stock-based compensation expenses in its revenue requirement in the 2022 Rate Case filing. Georgia Power’s long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and competitive with the utility industry. Furthermore, the Company’s budgeted costs for stock-based compensation to attract, retain and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and appropriately should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s Grid Investment Plan and projected spend for 2022 through 2025 is approved as filed, without modification. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s DERMS request is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to recover $48.9 million over a five-year amortization period ending December 31, 2027, for software and cloud computing costs is just and reasonable and is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to implement a new Customer Information System is approved as filed.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s inclusion of $94 million for incremental inflation in the Company’s revenue requirement is approved as filed.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company shall amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Wansley Units 1-2, Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 over their remaining useful lives as approved in Docket No. 42516. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that beginning January 1, 2023, for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3, the Company shall defer the difference each year between the amount of depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are retired, the deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at the retirement date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending December 31, 2031. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s adjustment to the depreciation rates for Wansley 1-2, Bowen 1-2, and Scherer 1-3, is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the depreciable life for Plant Hatch is extended from 60 years to 80 years. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request for a three-year amortization period ending December 31, 2025, to recover COVID-19 pandemic response costs is just and reasonable and is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to recover a regulatory asset balance of $786,000 as of December 31, 2022, over a three-year amortization period ending December 31, 2025, for costs associated with providing customers hourly usage data is just and reasonable and is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company is authorized to opt out of the IRS tax normalization for the purposes of recognizing eligible Investment Tax Credits for battery storage facilities in accordance with the Inflation Reduction Act.

ORDERED FURTHER, the Company’s COSS as filed in the testimony of Lee Evans is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s plan to modernize rates by making new residential premises ineligible for the ‘R’ Rate is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to streamline the Company’s tariff portfolio and avoid customer confusion by closing TOU-REO to new residential customers is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the minimum monthly offer for new FlatBill customers on both the FLAT and FLAT-GS tariffs from $25 to $50 is just and reasonable and is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to implement several changes to the RLG tariff to (1) clarify the existing fixture options, (2) expand and clarify the available pole options, and (3) close one fixture to future customer enrollments is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed changes CS tariff to update the pricing for the existing Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per kW block for residential customers and set the price for customers on the General Service (“GS”) tariff to be $29.99 per kW block is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed updates to the Simple Solar (“SS”)  tariff from the 2022 IRP are approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s CIEV rider is approved as a temporary rider to expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load factor for the account reaches 15% or greater.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to include a new pricing option within the Time of Use – Supplier Choice (“TOU-SC”) tariff is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission specifically approves the Company’s proposed changes to the TOU-RD, REO, DPEC Rider, RTP, RNR, RLG, CS, and SS tariffs.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed one-time interconnection fee of $200 for customers seeking to interconnect a customer generating facility to the Company’s system is just and reasonable and is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission approves the Company’s request to eliminate the PLH and SP tariffs. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the basic service charge to each traditional base rate, except as noted in testimony, on an equal percentage basis is approved. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company shall update the senior citizen low income discount to match the increase in the basic service charge.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission approves the modification of the Company’s payment and services policies in section F of the Company’s Rules and Regulations. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to include new language in the customer generation section of the Company’s Rules and Regulations to clarify when a customer-owned generator is required to undergo witness testing and would be responsible for the appropriate fees associated with this testing is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the charge for underground residential distribution service found in section C10 of the Rules and Regulations is approved.

ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed changes to Sections F and G of the Rules and Regulations is approved.
ORDERED FURTHER, the monthly netting pilot resulted in a cost shift to non-participating customers and should not be expanded. 
ORDERED FURTHER, that new customers that enroll in the RNR tariff beginning January 1, 2023 must take service in one of the Company’s three-part rates. 
ORDERED FURTHER, that RNR instantaneous customers shall continue to be paid for excess energy pushed back onto the grid at the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy Cost rate, consistent with the RCB Framework, and as filed annually with this Commission in Docket No. 16573. 
ORDERED FURTHER, within 90 days of this Order, the Company and Commission Staff shall initiate discussions and work with the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to formalize a consumer complaint process to better address consumer complaints related to bad actors in the rooftop solar industry.
ORDERED FURTHER, that all changes to tariffs, riders, rules and regulations filed by the Company should be approved as filed unless ordered otherwise in this Order.

ORDERED FURTHER, that Georgia Power shall file compliance tariffs within 60 days of the issuance of this Order, reflecting rates to implement the rate increases ordered herein. These tariffs shall reflect the rate allocations adopted in this Order, and shall be subject to the Commission’s review for final approval. 

ORDERED FURTHER, that all findings, conclusions and decisions contained within the preceding sections of this Order are adopted as findings of fact, conclusions of law, and decisions of regulatory policy of this Commission.

ORDERED FURTHER, that jurisdiction over this proceeding is expressly retained for the purpose of entering such further order or orders as this Commission may deem proper.

ORDERED FURTHER, any motion for reconsideration, rehearing, or oral argument shall not stay the effectiveness of this order unless expressly ordered by the Commission.

The above by action of the Commission in Administrative Session on the ___ day of December, 2022.

              POST HEARING BRIEF OF GEORGIA POWER COMPANY

                                     I.      INTRODUCTION

       Georgia Power Company (“Georgia Power” or the “Company”) submits this brief in
support of its proposed Alternate Rate Plan (“ARP”) as amended by the Company’s August 22,
2022 Supplemental Filing, September 28, 2022 Errata, and November 21, 2022 Errata filed in this
proceeding. The Company’s 2022 Rate Case was filed pursuant to the terms and conditions of the
Georgia Public Service Commission’s (the “Commission”) February 6, 2020 Order Adopting
Settlement Agreement in Georgia Power’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516 (“2019 Rate Case
Final Order”). As explained herein, based on the evidence before it, the Commission should
approve the Company’s application for a rate increase.

                         II.       SUMMARY OF THE ARGUMENT

       Georgia Power recommends that the Commission maintain the constructive regulatory
environment, effectuated through the Company’s ARP, that has benefitted Georgia Power’s
customers for decades. This Commission’s constructive regulatory policies have created a
sustained period of high customer satisfaction, high reliability, and rates below the national
average, and have contributed to Georgia consistently being ranked as the best state in the nation
in which to do business. This Commission has a well-deserved national reputation for maintaining
a consistent and balanced regulatory environment that benefits all stakeholders.
       Many of the recommendations and positions taken by the Commission’s Public Interest
Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff and Intervenors appear to ignore what has changed over the past three

                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 1 of 77

years, as well as what has remained constant since the 2019 rate case order. Changes over the past
three years include: (1) the emergence of a global pandemic; (2) rampant and rapid inflation that
has increased costs for businesses and consumers alike; (3) growing pressure on supply chain and
labor markets; (4) significant increases in the federal funds rate and cost of borrowing; and
(5) evolving customer needs, demands and requests for increased distributed energy resources and
carbon-free energy. Notwithstanding these changes, several things have remained constant: (a) this
Commission’s continued constructive and balanced approach that ensures customers continue to
receive clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electricity, even during a global pandemic; (b) the
importance of financial integrity and credit quality, which is never more evident than during times
of global uncertainty and financial market volatility, such as that being experienced today;
(c) customers remain at the center of everything the Company does, as evidenced by Georgia
Power’s continued excellent customer service and reliability performance with rates below the
national average; and (d) that the state of Georgia continues to be ranked as one of the best places
for business, with Georgia’s economy continuing to grow and with economic development
reaching all-time highs.
       When considering the past three years, many of the recommendations made by parties in
this case would harm customers by upsetting the well-designed and balanced regulatory structure
currently in place, and ultimately undermine the financial health and integrity of Georgia Power.
These recommendations are unacceptable to the Company.
       PIA Staff and Intervenors have also proposed modifications to the ARP that are
fundamentally inconsistent with the concept of an ARP that incentivizes the Company to innovate
and achieve operational efficiencies that benefit customers. Therefore, Georgia Power does not
support the recommendations to narrow the earnings band, set the top end of the earnings band at
an artificially low level, or eliminate the sharing mechanism for earnings above the top end of the
band. The recommended alterations to the Company’s proposed authorized earnings band would
remove revenue sharing incentives and effectively create an earnings cap, potential rate instability,
and be counterproductive to the goal of incentivizing the Company to drive efficiencies in the
business for the benefit of customers. In addition, the recommendations by Staff and other
intervenors to revise the content and estimates supporting the Company’s annual compliance


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 2 of 77

filings edges ever closer to having abbreviated rate cases every year, which encourages less
predictability in rates.
        The Company opposes the return on equity (“ROE”) setpoint and capital structure
recommendations put forth by PIA Staff and several Intervenors. If adopted, PIA Staff’s
recommended 105 basis point ROE reduction from the Company’s current setpoint would
represent the largest single ROE reduction for Georgia Power in decades. Many of these same
parties also propose to reduce the equity portion of the Company’s capital structure, the most
drastic of which would reduce the Company’s equity ratio from 56% to 51% in the second and
third years of the ARP. The Company’s current capital structure was approved by this Commission
in response to the 2017 Tax Cut and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) and modified in the Company’s last rate
case. Passage of the TCJA was the impetus for the initial increase in equity several years ago, and
the impact of the TCJA remains unchanged. With this in mind, PIA Staff’s suggested 500-basis
point equity ratio reduction from the current Commission-approved 56% based on the anticipated
completion of Plant Vogtle Units 3 and 4 has no merit. Such severe changes to the Company’s
ROE and capital structure would significantly impair the Company’s financial integrity and its
ability to raise capital at reasonable cost and upon reasonable terms for the benefit of customers.
In contrast, the ARP, together with the Company’s proposed 11.00% ROE and 56% equity level,
will support the Company’s financial integrity and ensure the Company continues to access capital
markets at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. At these levels, the Company’s Funds from
Operations (“FFO”) to Debt ratio, a key metric used by the ratings agencies to analyze a company’s
financial risk, will meet the thresholds identified by the credit rating agencies as necessary to
maintain the Company’s current credit quality.
        PIA Staff wrongly asserts that the Company can arbitrarily reduce its Operations and
Maintenance (“O&M”) expense based on the past three years, as if major outage or maintenance
work occurs at the same level every year and based on the erroneous assumption that operating
through the recent pandemic was indicative of Georgia Power’s cost to serve customers during a
normal operating environment. The Company’s O&M requests in this case were thoughtfully
designed to support the next three years of critical function and operation of Georgia Power to
ensure that the Company can continue to provide clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electric
service with exceptional customer service.


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 3 of 77

       PIA Staff has also proposed to substantially reduce or eliminate transmission and
distribution investments within the Grid Investment Plan, which are required to support the long-
term reliability and resiliency of our electric system, seemingly without considering the long-term
adverse impacts such reductions would cause to the electric system. The Company cannot delay
or take shortcuts in implementing these grid investments as they are necessary to address aging
transmission infrastructure and improve reliability performance for nearly 1.4 million customers
throughout the state who are served by lower performing feeders.
       In the aggregate, the modifications proposed by PIA Staff and several Intervenors undercut
core aspects of the ARP and are unacceptable. The Company cannot agree to an ARP that would
impair its financial integrity, threaten its current credit ratings, fundamentally alter the framework
of the ARP and strip it of its core benefits, or jeopardize the Company’s ability to provide
customers with safe and reliable electric service. The Commission should approve the Company’s
proposed ARP, without modification, as a fair and reasonable resolution of this proceeding that
appropriately balances the interests of customers, the Company, and investors. Approval of the
Company’s three-year ARP will continue the Commission’s long-standing tradition of
constructive regulation and provide stability and certainty for customers and the Company.
       The Company understands this Commission must consider the cost of electric service when
evaluating Georgia Power’s cost to serve its 2.7 million customers. Georgia Power has
thoughtfully and appropriately balanced the request in this proceeding with both the financial
impacts to, and needs of, our customers. Georgia Power operates a capital-intensive business and
has the privilege and obligation to serve its customers in this rapidly changing world. Adopting
the Company’s proposed ARP will ensure the Company remains financially healthy and able to
provide clean, safe, reliable, and affordable service to its customers now and into the future.
Therefore, and for the reasons discussed further herein, the Commission should approve the
Company’s application to adjust rates as filed, including the Company’s proposed ARP.

                                      IV.     ARGUMENT

A.     Approval of the Company’s Application as Filed Will Continue the Constructive
       Regulatory Environment that Customers, the Commission, the Company, and
       Investors Rely Upon.

       Approval of the Company’s Application, including the Company’s recommended ARP,
supports the constructive regulation in Georgia that has benefitted customers for decades. The
Company’s filing in this case is consistent with the Commission’s policies that have cultivated a
sustained period of high customer satisfaction, high reliability, rates below the national average,
and contributed to Georgia consistently being ranked as the best state in the nation in which to do
business. The Commission’s regulatory structure has created an environment in which: (i) the
Company can provide the clean, safe, and reliable service that our customers depend on to meet
their evolving energy needs; (ii) the Company can access capital on favorable terms to meet the
current and future needs of our customers; and (iii) the Company’s investors have an opportunity


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 6 of 77

to receive the returns appropriate for a well-run and efficient utility that meets the needs of its
customers. This Commission’s ability to balance strong and consistent policy while also adapting
to rapidly evolving technology and implementing gradual changes when appropriate, has
facilitated a consistent regulatory environment viewed favorably by customers and investors alike.
Approval of the Company’s filing will continue this trend.
       The ARP filed by the Company satisfies the core responsibilities of the Commission as
stated by the Supreme Court of Georgia:

       It is the responsibility of the commission to require a regulated utility to provide a
       level of service within its service area this year, next year, and in the foreseeable
       future; consonant with this responsibility the commission must approve utility rates
       that will produce enough revenue not only for operating expenses but also for the
       capital costs of the business. Capital for the business includes that required for
       current and future construction, and its costs include interest and dividends that
       must be paid to reasonably attract such capital.

Georgia Power Co. v. Georgia Public Service Commission, 231 Ga. 339, 344 (1973).

       Given the Company’s performance under the Commission’s consistent regulation within
the context of a global pandemic, rampant inflation, supply chain and labor market pressures,
increasing cost of debt, and evolving customer needs, the modifications and changes proposed by
PIA Staff and Intervenors are inappropriate and punitive. There is no support under Georgia law,
the Commission’s regulatory precedent, or the record in this proceeding, for the proposition
implicit in PIA Staff and certain Intervenor’s recommendations that rates should be set as low as
possible according to calculations that clearly threaten the Company’s ability to maintain, let alone
improve, its credit metrics. Moreover, the parties advocating for these modifications disregard the
impact their proposals would have on the Company’s ability to access capital markets on
reasonable terms and attract the substantial capital necessary to continue providing clean, safe, and
reliable service at the industry-leading level of customer service that the Company’s customers
deserve and expect.
       Rating agencies look for consistent application of sound economic regulatory principles by
utility regulators. (See Tr. 795-96.) In fact, the most recent opinions from each of the three credit
rating agencies reference their assessment that Georgia Power’s multiyear rate plan has been


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 7 of 77

“supportive of GPC’s credit quality,”1 that “the overall regulatory framework in Georgia will
remain credit supportive,”2 and that they “expect a constructive resolution to Georgia Power’s
pending rate case filing, which proposes a three-year rate plan.”3 (Tr. 3949.) In contrast, if a
regulatory body allowed a utility the opportunity to earn a fair return on utility assets and
subsequently applied cost recovery principles in a manner inconsistent with the regulatory
compact, investors’ interest in providing funds to that utility would be altered, the utility’s risk
profile would deteriorate, and the utility’s access to capital at reasonable terms would suffer – all
to the detriment of customers. (See Tr. 794.)
        The combined effect of the recommendations by PIA Staff and some Intervenors to alter
the Company’s ARP proposal would result in a negative reaction from investors and could lead to
negative rating actions to the detriment of the Company and customers. (Tr. 3950, 3957, 4167,
4171, 4184.) The Company encourages this Commission to continue fostering regulatory
consistency and avoid drastic action that could disturb the balance this Commission has created,
which has been proven to benefit customers.

B.      The Commission Should Adopt the Company’s Proposed ROE, Cost of Capital, and
        Capital Structure, and Reject PIA Staff’s ROE, Cost of Capital, and Capital
        Structure to Avoid Jeopardizing Georgia Power’s Financial Integrity.

        1.      The Commission should adopt the Company’s proposed capital structure and ROE
                as calculated by Mr. Coyne.

        The Commission should adopt Mr. Coyne’s 11% ROE recommendation and the
Company’s proposed 56% equity capital structure to ensure the Company can continue to access
capital markets at reasonable rates and terms. Georgia Power proposes that its capital structure
containing approximately 56% common equity and 44% debt be used for ratemaking purposes in
this proceeding. (Tr. 72, 202, 631, 672.) Mr. Coyne analyzed the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”)
model, Capital Asset Pricing Model (“CAPM”), Bond Yield Risk Premium model, and the
Expected Earnings models for a comparable utility group and estimated the cost of equity to run
from 8.99% to 13.55%, with a four model average of 10.92%. (Tr. 631, 652, 667, 673.) Based on


1
     S&P Summary Georgia Power, November 18, 2021.
2
     Moody’s Credit Opinion, October 26, 2022.
3
     Fitch Georgia Power Company, October 4, 2022.

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 8 of 77

these analyses and including an adjustment for flotation costs, Mr. Coyne recommends an allowed
ROE equal to 11.00% for Georgia Power in this proceeding. (Tr. 631, 673.) Mr. Coyne’s
recommendation also considers the general economic and capital market environment and the
influence capital market conditions exert over the results of the DCF, CAPM, and Risk Premium
models. (Tr. 632.) An 11.00% ROE is a fair, forward-looking rate of return on equity that provides
the Company with an opportunity to earn a return on its investment over the period in which rates
are in effect commensurate with returns that investors expect to earn on other investments of
similar risk. (Tr. 678.)
        The Commission should also consider that, relative to the 2019 rate case, the three key
quantitative components impacting the Company’s weighted cost of capital (approved capital
structure, projected cost of debt, and allowed cost of equity) in this request results in a net decrease
of $18 million to our requested revenue requirement. (Tr. 214.) Over the past three years the
Company has taken advantage of a historically low interest rate environment and relatively flat
yield curve and is passing those savings to customers with the projected embedded cost of debt.
(Tr. 215.) Although presently in a rising interest rate environment, the Company will continue to
seek, as it has in the past, financing structures that minimize the impact of cost to our customers.

        2.      PIA Staff and Intervenors’ recommended ROE reductions are drastic,
                unprecedented, and punitive.

        The positions taken by PIA Staff and several Intervenors would undermine Georgia’s well-
established and balanced regulatory structure by drastically reducing the Company’s ROE. PIA
Staff’s recommended 105 basis point ROE reduction would represent the largest single ROE
reduction for Georgia Power in decades and would be particularly egregious in light of the
Company’s superior performance over the last three years, including during the COVID-19
pandemic. (Tr. 3946.) A 105-basis point reduction in ROE is not consistent with utility
performance that exceeds customer and investor expectations. (See Tr. 4009.) Since 2019, the
Company has performed well, delivering clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electricity to
customers while striving to find efficiencies and foster innovation. The ROE reductions proposed
by Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis would signify a dramatic departure from
the gradual approach employed by this Commission over the past several rate cases and would
undo the steps the Commission took in 2018 to address the Tax Reform Act and protect the

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 9 of 77

Company’s credit quality from the stresses placed on it by tax reform. (Tr. 3957, 4025, 4108, 4122,
4178-79.) The capital markets would surely view such a reduction negatively as it would represent
a radical and dramatic departure from the consistent and historically constructive regulation for
which this Commission has a reputation – one where excellence is recognized and rewarded, not
punished. (Id.)
       PIA Staff and several Intervenors reference the industry average authorized ROEs over
various time frames, whether to note that they have been declining since 2009 (Tr. 3480) or have
averaged within the range of 9.35% to 10.42% for years 2010 through 2022 to date. (Tr. 2460-62,
2709-11, 2849, 3517-18.) However, Georgia Power is not an average utility and Georgia is not an
average state. (Tr. 574.) The Commission has never approved Georgia Power’s ROEs based upon
industry average and has, in fact, historically established Georgia Power’s ROE set point above
the national average. (Tr. 575.) Indeed, the Commission has historically approved, through the
three-year ARPs, ROEs approximately 100 basis points above the industry average, taking into
consideration the Company’s superior performance, ability to manage operating risk and
agreement not to seek rate relief for three years except in limited circumstances.
       The Company’s proposed ROE of 11.00% is thoroughly supported by Mr. Coyne. While
PIA Staff Witness Gorman, GIPL Witness Ellis, GAM Witness LaConte, and DOD Witness Reno
questioned Mr. Coyne’s results by taking positions that can differ among professional experts, Mr.
Coyne appropriately defended his positions based on his considerable and extensive knowledge
and expertise. (Tr. 2752, 3495-96, 3652-53.) For example, several parties questioned Mr. Coyne’s
use of market capital structure adjustment to correct for financial risk differences between the
proxy group’s market capital structure and the book regulatory capital structure proposed for
Georgia Power. Mr. Coyne defended this adjustment as correct and necessary to provide an overall
cost of capital commensurate with market expectations.
       PIA Staff and Intervenor recommendations do not represent current market conditions and
would not result in full recovery of the Company’s cost of equity. (Tr. 3957, 4116, 4120-21, 4179.)
As shown in Table 1 below, three of the parties who recommended ROE adjustments in this case
who also made ROE recommendations in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case, recommended higher
adjustments in the 2022 Rate Case than they did in the 2019 Rate Case. (Tr. 1295, 1471, 2716,
3441, 3264.) Comparing these parties’ prior ROE recommendations with current


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 10 of 77

recommendations inherently acknowledges that the cost of capital has increased in the last three
years.

                                               Table 1

          Party                        2019 Recommended ROE             2022 Recommended ROE
          Georgia Power                10.9%                            11.00%
          Department of Defense        9.1%                             9.47%
          PIA Staff                    9.2%                             9.45%

         Despite implicitly acknowledging that the cost of capital has increased since 2019, each
party recommending an ROE setpoint proposed a value lower than the Company’s current ROE.
These parties provide no justification for recommending such severe reductions in the Company’s
cost of equity since the last base rate case. Mr. Ellis’ 5.54% ROE recommendation is almost 500
basis points below Georgia Power’s current ROE, nearly 400 basis points below the 9.53% average
authorized ROE for vertically integrated electric utilities in 2021 (Tr. 4122, 4178), and 300 basis
points below any return authorized for any electric utility in at least 40 years. (Tr. 4117, 4178.) In
fact, Mr. Ellis’ recommendation is so low as to be confiscatory and severely beyond the zone of
reasonableness for a vertically integrated investor-owned utility.
         For these reasons, Mr. Gorman, Ms. LaConte, Ms. Reno, and Mr. Ellis’ recommendations
are not only unsupported but also are unduly punitive in light of Georgia Power’s record of strong
performance. (Tr. 3957.) The ROE reductions proposed by PIA Staff and Intervenors are a
dramatic departure from the Commission’s constructive and supportive regulatory environment
over the past several rate cases (Tr. 3957, 4025, 4108, 4122, 4178-79), and would surely be viewed
negatively by the capital markets, as Mr. Fetter unequivocally testified. (Tr. 4184.)

         3. PIA Staff’s and Intervenors’ proposed equity reductions would destroy the necessary
            balance agreed to following the Tax Reform Settlement and affirmed in the 2019 Rate
            Case and would threaten the Company’s financial integrity.

         Many of the same parties proposing drastic ROE cuts also propose to reduce the equity
portion of the Company’s capital structure, including PIA Staff’s recommended 500 basis point
reduction from the level deemed appropriate by this Commission just three years ago. Such
unprecedented and severe changes would significantly impair the Company’s financial integrity,


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 11 of 77

which is necessary to ensure its ability to raise capital at reasonable cost and upon reasonable terms
for the benefit of customers.
        Although Mr. Gorman acknowledges that the Company’s authorized equity ratio was
previously increased due to the negative effects of the 2017 TCJA, he completely ignores that
these negative effects continue today. (Tr. 3538.) Instead, he proposes that the Company’s equity
ratio should be reduced to 51% once Vogtle Unit 4 is placed in service (Tr. 3515), wrongly
assuming that the construction of Vogtle Units 3 and 4, rather than the TCJA, was the catalyst to
increase the Company’s equity ratio to 56%. (Tr. 3540, 3542, 3545.) As such, the placement of
Vogtle Units 3 and 4 in service should not be an advance trigger for a reduction in the Company’s
equity ratio. The Commission should adhere to its prior policy findings and maintain the
Company’s equity ratio due to the continued risk and negative impacts from the TCJA.
        The Settlement Agreement approved in the 2019 Rate Case left it to the Commission to
determine the Company’s appropriate capital structure. Based on the evidence presented in that
case, the Commission found the appropriate capital structure to be 56% equity and 44% debt. (Tr.
3440-41.) As noted in the Commission’s Short Order Adopting Settlement as Modified in the
Company’s 2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516, the Company’s equity ratio was adjusted from
51% to 55% as a part of the TCJA Settlement in Docket No. 36989 (“Tax Reform Settlement”) to
address the negative implications of tax reform and provide support for maintaining the
Company’s credit profile. (Tr. 3537-38.) The Commission further determined that the “proposed
capital structure of 56% common equity level is just and reasonable considering all of the evidence
presented.”4 There have not been, nor are there anticipated to be, any changes to the TCJA or its
negative impacts on the Company since the Commission approved the 56% equity, 44% debt
capital structure in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case that would justify lowering the Company’s
equity ratio. (Tr. 3956.)
        GAM Witness LaConte recommends the Company’s equity ratio should be more in line
with the average equity ratio for the companies included in Witness Coyne’s proxy group that have
similar credit ratings as Georgia Power or should not exceed 53%. (See Tr. 2713.) Ms. LaConte’s
proposal of 53% equity is not based on a careful and accurate analysis of the credit rating


4
    Short Order Adopting Settlement Agreement as Modified, Georgia Public Service Commission, Docket No.
    42516 (December 17, 2019) at 8.

                                Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 12 of 77

implications of her recommendation. If a 53% equity level were adopted by the Commission, that
decrease would cause over 200 basis points of deterioration in FFO to debt, harming the
Company’s financial integrity. (Tr. 3956-57.) Additionally, as mentioned earlier, the 56% equity
level set in the 2019 rate case appropriately mitigates the negative credit impacts of the TCJA. (Tr.
3957.) The impacts of the TCJA still exist; therefore, the Company must maintain its 56% equity
ratio as carefully reviewed and approved by the Commission in the 2019 rate case. None of the
capital structure recommendations proposed by PIA Staff or Intervenors adequately reflect
Georgia Power’s need for continued access to capital on reasonable terms and the importance of
maintaining its credit quality. (Tr. 4182.) Therefore, the Commission should reject the proposed
capital structure changes proffered by PIA Staff and Intervenors.

       4.      Errors in PIA Staff Witness Gorman’s FFO to debt calculations result in an
               incorrect conclusion that the Company’s future cash flows are sufficient to yield an
               appropriate credit rating going forward.

       The Company’s request in this case carefully sought to ensure the Company could continue
to access capital markets to meet the future needs of customers, continue to provide high levels of
service and preserve the opportunity to earn a fair return on investments necessary to support a
well-run and efficient utility. To evaluate how its recommendations would impact Georgia Power’s
credit ratings, the Company calculated its FFO to debt ratio, which is a financial metric used to
assess risk based on the Company’s ability to pay off debt using net operating income alone. (Tr.
799-800.) Mr. Gorman acknowledges the importance of maintaining the Company’s FFO to debt
(financial risk metrics) above the credit rating agencies’ general benchmarks and tries to present
his analysis with that in mind. (Tr. 3531-33.) However, due to an error in calculating the FFO to
debt ratio, his resulting proposition would undermine the Company’s ability to meet the rating
agencies’ expectations of restored credit metrics to levels supported by Witness Fetter.
       Debt imputation is the act of adding debt-like fixed obligations to a utility’s “traditional”
debt balance when calculating FFO to debt. (Tr. 3953.) For many years, S&P has viewed certain
components on the balance sheet, including pension obligations, lease liabilities and asset
retirement obligations, as similar to debt and therefore adds them to the debt balance when
calculating credit metrics. (Id.) S&P uses the information included on the Company’s publicly
available balance sheet and accompanying financial disclosures to obtain the necessary

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 13 of 77

information for the proper debt imputation calculation. (Id.) Georgia Power has no off-balance
sheet debt, or any other off-balance sheet components, that the rating agencies use when
calculating adjusted debt. (Tr. 3954.)
       Mr. Gorman and the Company agree that these debt-like obligations are the type of debt
imputations that are appropriate to include in estimating S&P’s FFO to debt calculation. (Tr. 3669,
3954.) However, due to the confusion around where these debt-like components are identified,
these components were omitted from Mr. Gorman’s calculations. (Tr. 3671-72; 3678.) Mr.
Gorman should have included the debt imputations related to these debt-like components in his
estimated recalculation of S&P credit metrics, as he did in the 2019 rate case. (Tr. 3954.) By
excluding more than $5.5 billion of debt imputations, Mr. Gorman’s credit metric expectation is
significantly overstated and fails to illustrate the harmful effects of his recommended ROE and
equity ratio. (Tr. 3952.) The magnitude of this error casts substantial doubt on the reliability of
Witness Gorman’s statements supporting how his recommendations affect the Company’s credit
quality.
       In his recalculation of one of S&P’s most important credit metrics, FFO to debt, Mr.
Gorman omitted the important debt imputation methodology S&P has directed in previously issued
authoritative publications. (See Tr. 3678, 3952.) These debt imputations are essential adjustments
that must be made to arrive at a properly recalculated S&P credit metric. (Tr. 3676-78, 3953.) Had
Mr. Gorman’s recalculation included the proper S&P debt imputations, it would have shown that
the Company’s metrics, assuming Staff’s adjustments were accepted by the Commission, are
insufficient to maintain sufficient access to capital at reasonable rates. Due to this error and the
assumptions serving as the basis for Mr. Gorman’s recommendations, the Commission should not
adopt PIA Staff’s proposed ROE and capital structure, which will be more deleterious to Georgia
Power’s financial integrity than even PIA Staff intended.

       5.      The cumulative effect of PIA Staff’s recommendations is extremely negative to the
               Company’s financial integrity and the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s ROE
               and capital structure recommendations.

       The cumulative negative impact of Mr. Gorman’s recommended 9.45% ROE set point and
capital structure of 51% equity, beginning in year 2024, coupled with the recommendations of
Messrs. Smith and Trokey, would be dramatic and harmful to the Company’s financial integrity.

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 14 of 77

(Tr. 3950.) As stated above, if one were to recalculate Georgia Power’s FFO to debt assuming Mr.
Gorman’s recommended ROE of 9.45% and an equity ratio of 51% in 2024-2025, the results are
extremely negative to the financial integrity of Georgia Power. (Tr. 3950.) Further, incorporating
the detrimental effects of Messrs. Smith and Trokey’s recommendations, the 2023-2025 average
FFO to debt would deteriorate by roughly 500 basis points. (Tr. 3951.) The resulting Georgia
Power credit profile, assuming the collective recommendations by PIA Staff, would place the
Company’s financial integrity at or below the lowest acceptable level recommended by Mr. Fetter.
(Id.) A key component of this rate case is to maintain the credit quality Georgia Power has today,
not diminish it.
       Based on the latest Moody’s Credit Opinion issued October 26, 2022, Moody’s states:

               Georgia Power operates within a credit supportive Georgia regulatory
               framework which generally provides timely recovery of prudent costs and
               investments and allows for the company to maintain a typically stable
               financial profile.

       Moody’s goes on to state:

               Georgia Power is currently in a pending rate case and will look to address
               the deferred fuel costs in a filing next February. Depending on resolution of
               these regulatory filings, we expect Georgia Power’s financial profile to
               improve including a ratio of CFO pre-W/C to debt of about 20% in 2023.
               Once the new nuclear units are in service and depending on the ultimate
               investment recovery allowed by regulators as result of the cost prudency
               review, the majority of which will be completed when Unit 4 is in service,
               we expect a substantial increase in Georgia Power’s cash flow which should
               translate into considerable improvement in credit metrics.

       (Tr. 3951-52.)

       The punitive adjustments that Mr. Gorman and Messrs. Smith and Trokey recommend
would limit the FFO to debt metric to approximately 20% for the foreseeable future without the
opportunity for improvement. Such limitation runs counter to the rating agencies’ expectation that,
beginning in 2024, Georgia Power will remedy its credit profile such that it assumes a position of
improved cash flow and adequate credit quality. (Tr. 118, 125, 132, 3952.) PIA Staff’s
unreasonable recommendations would harm the Company’s credit profile, impair the Company’s


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 15 of 77

ability to improve its credit metrics, disturb the constructive regulatory environment that has
benefitted Georgia Power’s customers, and harm customers by restricting the Company’s access
to debt capital at reasonable terms and costs. (Tr. 3951.)

       6.       The Company’s ability to manage its business to meet investor expectations ensures
                that Georgia Power is in a financially healthy position to meet customer
                expectations as it delivers clean, safe, reliable, and affordable electric service.

       The changes PIA Staff and many of the intervening parties propose are ill-advised and fail
to consider the impact of their proposals on the Company’s ability to access capital markets on
reasonable terms to attract the substantial capital necessary to continue providing customers with
the clean, safe, and reliable electric service they deserve and expect. (Tr. 4012.) Customers benefit
when the Company balances the interests of all stakeholders because a financially healthy utility
can access and attract the necessary capital (both debt and equity) to meet the needs of its
customers and is more likely to achieve high levels of reliability and customer satisfaction. (Tr.
3950, 3977.) Customers also benefit from the Company’s operating efficiencies in its efforts to
meet investor expectations. (Tr. 3950.) The efficiencies gained and the corresponding cost
reductions that result from those efforts are provided to customers through savings in rates. (Tr.
264-65, 575.)
       In addition to accessing capital markets on a timely basis at reasonable rates, a utility with
strong credit ratings shares the benefit of those attractive interest rate levels with customers since
the cost of capital is factored into utility rates. (Tr. 797-98, 806.) Conversely, the lower a regulated
utility’s credit rating, the more that utility must pay to raise funds from debt and equity investors
to carry out its capital-intensive operations. (Tr. 798.) In turn, the ratemaking process factors the
cost of capital for both debt and equity into customer rates. (Tr.790-91.) This is especially true for
a company like Georgia Power, which needs to attract significant levels of capital in the near term
for continued transmission and distribution investments, environmental compliance, and the
construction of new nuclear units, all while continuing to safely and reliably serve customers. (Tr.
794, 799, 806.) Therefore, the Commission should approve the rate adjustment and capital
structure Georgia Power proposes in this case so that the Company remains positioned to continue
meeting the energy needs of our customers and the communities we serve.
