                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 16 of 77

C.     The Company Cannot Accept an ARP as Proposed by PIA Staff and Some
       Intervenors.

       Under Georgia law, the Company’s agreement is required to enter into an ARP. Without
an agreement, this case must be resolved under traditional ratemaking standards, which involve a
single test year analysis and would eliminate the positive attributes of a three-year predictable plan
such as the institution of earnings ranges and earnings sharing mechanisms. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-25;
46-2-26.1. Without an ARP, more frequent, successive rate cases would be required over the next
three years to accomplish what a three-year ARP can accomplish now. (Tr. 3958.) However,
successive annual cases do not possess the positive attributes of rate certainty, rate stability, or the
earnings sharing mechanism present in an ARP. (Id.) PIA Staff and several Intervenors have
proposed radical and harmful modifications to the ARP that should be rejected. PIA Staff goes so
far as to suggest that the Commission impose an earnings cap (see Tr. 1379), which is
fundamentally inconsistent with the concept of the ARP sharing mechanism that benefits both
customers and the Company. (Tr. 3945-46.) These proposed modifications undercut core aspects
of the ARP, are unacceptable, and the Company would not agree to such an ARP.

       1.      Continuation of the Company’s current earnings band will retain many of the
               positive structural features of the ARP for customers’ benefit.

       The Company requested to maintain the current approved earnings band of 9.50% to
12.00% within an ARP construct that provides for the Company to share 80% of any earnings
above the band with customers. (Tr. 208.) Under this arrangement, all stakeholders win as
demonstrated by the Company’s consistent delivery of exceptional service while keeping rates low
in relation to other utilities. Maintaining the earnings band in its current form affords the Company
the opportunity to continue the innovation and efficiencies in operations that the Company was
able to identify since the last rate case. Customers have benefitted under this structure and will
continue to benefit under the Company’s proposed ARP and earnings band if approved by the
Commission.


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 17 of 77

       2.      PIA Staff and Intervenor-proposed changes to the earnings band fail to incentivize
               the Company to innovate and seek operational efficiencies that provide customer
               benefits.

               a. The earnings sharing mechanism should not be modified and PIA Staff’s
                  proposed earnings cap should be rejected.

       PIA Staff has proposed an earnings band ranging from 8.45% to 10.45% and recommended
that 100% of earnings above the band be applied to deferred costs rather than split between
customers and the Company. (Tr. 1379.) PIA Staff’s proposal amounts to an earnings cap by
eliminating any retained revenues earned above the earnings band. (Id.) If an earnings cap was
approved in this manner, the Company would not be appropriately incentivized to innovate and
seek operational efficiencies during the ARP period, which is not in the best interests of customers.
In addition, the top end of the earnings band proposed by PIA Staff is set artificially low, below
even the Company’s cost of equity. PIA Staff’s elimination of the sharing mechanism and
imposition of the earnings cap is unreasonable and should be rejected.

               b. The Company will not accept a narrowed earnings band as recommended by
                  DOD Witness Blank.

       It appears that Mr. Blank is only recommending an ROE earnings band of 100 basis points
above the ROE if the Commission authorizes an ROE below the Company’s requested 11%. (Tr.
3413.) This change would result in the top of the earnings band being lower than the 12% proposed
by the Company. Otherwise, Mr. Blank reiterates his recommendation from the 2019 rate case that
the earnings band be narrowed to +/- 50 basis points above and below the target ROE based on the
assumption that it would reduce the risk to the Company. (Tr. 3412.) However, narrowing the
earnings band may increase risks and uncertainty to customers, altering the fundamental nature of
the ARPs that have been in place for the last 25 years. (Tr. 3962.) Georgia Power has rightly
proposed to maintain the current approved earnings band of 9.50% to12.00%, consistent with the
structure of the earnings bands previously approved by the Commission for the last several
decades, and recommendations to the contrary should be rejected. (Tr. 208, 3962.)


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 18 of 77

               c. The Commission should reject Commercial Group’s recommendation to adjust
                  the earnings band commensurate with any adjustment to the requested 11%
                  ROE set point.

       The Commission should reject the Commercial Group Witness Chriss’ recommendation to
adjust the earnings band commensurate with any adjustment to the requested 11% ROE set point.
(Tr. 2453.) If the Commission were to authorize a set point ROE less than the Company’s requested
11%, the bottom end of the earnings band should remain at 9.50% with the top end of the earnings
band set at 12.00%, which is consistent with the structure of the earnings bands previously
approved by the Commission.

       3.      Customers benefit from the Commission’s adoption of an ARP.

       As a result of the ARP currently in place, the Company returned approximately $297
million to customers through the earnings sharing arrangement approved by the Commission in
the 2013 base rate case, without jeopardizing superior customer satisfaction or reliability. (Tr.
3203, 3961.) In the absence of an ARP, there would be no earnings sharing mechanism or incentive
for the Company to share earnings with customers. (Tr. 3945.) In addition, under the ARP, the
amount customers pay through rates does not change when the Company’s earnings are within, or
exceed, the band because rates are established at the ROE set point. (Tr. 3961.) As previously
mentioned, the sharing mechanism is a critical component of the ARP and appropriately
encourages the Company to look for efficiencies and process improvements. (Tr. 3961.) This
incentive provides the opportunity for increased earnings that benefit both customers and the
Company.
       Three-year ARPs also avoid the potential for more frequent base rate cases, provide rate
stability and predictability to customers, and signal the type of regulatory consistency viewed
favorably by credit rating agencies. Further, because the Company surrenders its right to file a full
rate case during the three-year term when the ROE is within the authorized earnings band, the
Company assumes the risks of changing conditions during the term of the ARP. (Tr. 3958.) For
example, the level of inflation may increase more than projected, the cost of debt or equity might
increase, sales may be less than forecasted, or the Company’s overall cost of service may be more
than forecasted. (Id.) A three-year rate plan also holds the Company accountable for inefficiencies
or cost overruns due to rates being set at a predetermined level for the duration of the three-year

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 19 of 77

period. (Id.) The Commission’s history of adopting three-year ARPs benefits customers and the
Company alike. As such, the Commission should adopt the structure of the Company’s proposed
ARP and reject proposals that would alter that structure.

       4.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommended data revisions to the
               annual compliance filing process as an unnecessary erosion of the ARP process.

       The data elements currently updated in the annual Compliance Filings established in the
Settlement Agreement approved in the Company’s 2019 Rate Case provide benefits to customers
and equity to all parties. The additional elements that Staff proposes to incorporate into the annual
Compliance Filings risk transforming this established, efficient, and effective process into a
burdensome, mini rate case proceeding. (See Tr. 1373.) More importantly, as the number of data
elements to be re-estimated and readdressed on an annual basis via the Compliance Filings
increases, the more the benefits and value of the three-year ARP are undermined. (Tr. 3980; 4008.)

D.     The Commission Should Reject Most of PIA Staff and Intervenors’ Revenue
       Requirement Adjustments.

       1.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to use the test period
               revenue requirement to set rates in the first year of the three-year ARP.

       The Company has included in its filing both the test period revenue requirement and the
revenue requirements for each plan year 2023 through 2025. (Tr. 226.) However, the Company
supports setting the rates in the first year of the proposed ARP based on the revenue requirement
for 2023. The revenue deficiency calculated for the calendar year ending December 31, 2023,
rather than the deficiency for the split test year, reflects a more accurate depiction of the costs the
Company will incur in its first year under a new ARP when the new rates are effective.
Determining the revenue deficiency using the calendar year reduces regulatory lag. In the
Company’s Errata Filing, the projected revenue deficiency based on the traditional test period and
the 2023 calendar year is $459.8 million and $612.3 million, respectively, resulting in a difference
of $152.5 million. This difference illustrates that basing the year one rate increase on the split test
period’s revenue deficiency would create a revenue shortfall of $152.5 million in the very first
year of the three-year period. Setting rates based on the test period would not permit the Company
to timely recover its prudently incurred costs in serving customers. Therefore, the Commission


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 20 of 77

should reject PIA Staff recommendations to use the test period revenue requirement to set first
year rates in 2023.

       2.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to use any revenue
               sufficiency to accelerate recognition of deferred costs.

       Georgia Power’s proposed case does not have a projected revenue sufficiency in 2023 to
apply to deferred costs as proposed by PIA Staff. (Tr. 3959.) As stated previously, the Company’s
revenue deficiency should be based on the calendar year 2023 revenue requirement rather than the
test period, if the Company’s ARP is adopted. As such, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s
recommendations to apply any revenue sufficiency to accelerate recognition of deferred costs or
to amortize a portion of CCR ARO compliance costs. Nevertheless, as stated in the Company’s
rebuttal testimony, the Company generally agrees with PIA Staff’s concept of applying revenue
sufficiency, if any, to certain regulatory assets to maintain rate stability for the customers. (Id.)

       3.      In the interests of greater rate stability, the Commission should adopt the
               Company’s proposed levelized rate increase rather than the step increase proposed
               by PIA Staff.

       As part of its recommended ARP, the Company proposed a levelized approach to balance
the benefit to customers from three years of stable and predictable rates with the Company’s need
to timely recover its projected revenue deficiency. (Tr. 73.) Rate stability provides customers with
a beneficial tool when budgeting for their utility costs and provides a predictable revenue stream
for the Company. A levelized approach to rate increases shifts economic risks to the Company by
insulating customers from the impacts of a possible downturn in the sales forecast or cost increases.
(Id.) This approach makes practical sense as the Company is better equipped to manage negative
economic factors that impact the cost of running the business, as opposed to asking customers to
shoulder the impacts, which would occur with increased rates under step increases. In addition,
customers are fairly compensated for the projected over-collection in the first two years of the
levelization. For the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company proposes
to defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving customers credit
on the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted average cost of capital. This


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 21 of 77

proposed deferral reduces the levelized revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully
amortizes the advancement by the end of 2025. (Tr. 3960-61.)
       In contrast, the step increase approach would increase customer rates by an increasing
amount during each of the next three years. Although an annual increase would result in a lower
than requested projected revenue requirement for years 2023 and 2024, the increase for 2025
would be greater than the levelized amount, and rates would increase more overall during the ARP.
The levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to customers than a step increase
approach over the three-year ARP. (Tr. 3960, 4017.) The fluctuation in projected costs year-to-
year under a step approach creates uncertainty for customers. (Id.) Although the Company shares
PIA Staff’s interest in rate stability, levelizing the proposed increases over the three-year ARP is
the more appropriate approach for customers.

       4.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s O&M reductions that are based solely
               on prior historical levels of O&M expense.

       The Company’s O&M expenses are complex and driven by many factors, such as timing
and scope of outages, scheduled maintenance cycles, repair work, medical claims, the labor
market, and contractor rates. The conditions in the historical period are not identical to the test
period and the Company and its business units consider all these factors in the development of
their respective budgets for the upcoming three-year period. (Tr. 3967.) In addition, the historical
years used by PIA Staff Witness Mugrace includes years 2020 and 2021, which were greatly
impacted by the COVID pandemic and are not representative of a “normal” year that should be
relied upon as reflective of costs to be incurred in the forecasted periods. (Id.) Simply put, while
historical expenses may be an input into the Company’s budgeting process and considered in
forecasting O&M expenses, it would be inappropriate to rely only on historical levels of O&M
expense as the sole indicator of future expenses in the coming three years.
       Annually, the Company utilizes input from all areas of the business to plan and budget the
resources needed in support of expected workload for the upcoming five-year forecasted period.
The Company considers planned outage schedules, expected maintenance activities, asset and
vegetation management programs, customer service needs, technology, fleet, materials, staffing
levels, contractor rates, and expected inflation when developing the budget. Budget assumptions
are vetted and approved to ensure consistency across business units. During this process, Georgia

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 22 of 77

Power reviews expenses and adjusts budgeted amounts based on known increases or decreases,
accordingly. (Id.) Even with known cost increases, budgets may fluctuate over a given period of
time due to multiple factors, including unit outages, changes in workforce needs for both Company
labor and contract labor, Company benefit costs, and Commission-approved items such as storm
recovery and demand side management (“DSM”) expenses. The Company does not create its
budget by simply applying an inflation factor to all the actual or historic year expenses, in part
because the Company’s O&M costs are not static over time. (Id.)
       Moreover, the Company is constantly focused on managing its O&M expenditures,
emphasizing cost containment as part of its corporate culture, as reasonable and necessary cost
containment is in the best interest of both the Company and its customers. (Tr. 3968.) The
Company has identified efficiencies in nearly every part of its business while still providing
superior customer satisfaction and reliable service to customers. If historical expenses alone were
considered in setting the appropriate O&M expenses for the ARP period, they would not
adequately capture these efficiencies and cost savings for customers. The Commission should
reject each of PIA Staff’s recommendations to replace the Company’s carefully budgeted O&M
expense estimates with simple historical averages.

       5.      The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs
               associated with the innovation center in retail rates.

       PIA Staff Witness Mugrace argues that the Company’s costs incurred related to the Energy
Innovation Center (“EIC”) should be removed from retail rate base because such costs are “not
known and measurable” and “may not achieve any objective with respect to reliability,
affordability, sustainability or customer engagement.” (Tr. 2027.) Mr. Mugrace likens these
expenses to research and development costs and claims that there is no evidence of benefit to
customers. (Id.) The Company disagrees with PIA Staff’s characterization of the EIC and the
allegations that it provides no known or measurable benefit to customers. The EIC is related to but
distinct from Research and Development in that the EIC is focused on education and exchanging
ideas and information with all customer segments and stakeholders. (Tr. 3969.) The Company
engages directly with customers through the EIC platform to share with customers general
educational materials on innovative technologies, discuss end-use applications that will be
beneficial for customers, bring stakeholders together, and establish a foundation for future

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 23 of 77

partnerships between the utility and customers. Establishing a forum to circulate these ideas around
technology innovation, and their market milestones, is fundamentally beneficial to Georgia
Power’s customers because it has led to project activities that deliver cost saving efficiency and
sustainability outcomes, with the potential to deliver grid-level benefits that improve reliability.
(Id.)
        For example, as explained by Mr. Robinson in the Company’s rebuttal hearing, the
Company’s transition to using unmanned aerial systems to inspect transmission lines was an idea
brought forth through the EIC. (Tr. 4039.) These types of efforts lead to direct customer
engagement and benefits that are tangible, known, and measurable. The EIC is a resource for
customers and stakeholders to learn about new energy technologies and how they may participate
in their deployment, where applicable and appropriate. The Commission has previously approved
the recovery of these costs in retail rates and should do so again here.

        6.     The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs
               associated with service awards and recreation costs in retail rates.

        Contrary to the recommendations of Mr. Mugrace, the Company’s costs incurred related
to service awards and recreation costs are not premature and are known and measurable. The
Company’s service award program is a part of the overall compensation package offered to all
full-time and part-time active employees, which helps motivate and retain the talent needed to
efficiently operate the Company’s business. It demonstrates recognition and appreciation to
employees for Company service at specific service intervals, which are estimable and measurable.
Further, recognizing valuable and dedicated employees is important to the Company and valuable
in providing consistent and top-notch service to Georgia Power’s customers. These costs are
appropriately recovered in retail rates. The Commission has previously approved the recovery of
these costs in retail rates and should do so again here.

        7.     The Commission should approve the Company’s request to recover the costs
               associated with association dues, community service, and experimental costs in
               retail rates.

        Witness Mugrace argues that costs associated with association dues, community service,
and experimental costs do not benefit ratepayers in the provision of safe and adequate service. (Tr.


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 24 of 77

2038.) However, these costs are necessary to operating the business and our customers benefit
from such activities. Through these efforts, the Company develops technology efficiencies that
reduce O&M expenses and equipment improvements that improve the reliability of the grid. As a
member of certain associations, the Company and its employees learn, grow, and contribute to the
sustainability of the electric industry. For example, as members of the Institute of Electronic and
Electronics Engineers, the Company has subject matter experts who sit on standards committees,
which directly benefits the Company’s customers. (Tr. 4042.) Costs associated with community
organizations, community development, economic development, and other business issues also
benefit the communities we serve. Investments in these areas help the Company engage with
customers and communities, which allows for growth and sustainability across the state. Again,
the Commission has previously approved the recovery of these costs in retail rates and should do
so again here.

       8.        To appropriately account for the impact of rising inflation on the Company’s cost
                 of service, the Commission should approve the Company’s calculation of
                 incremental inflation in its revenue requirement.

       The Commission should reject Mr. Mugrace’s proposed inflation reductions as
unnecessary and inappropriate. Mr. Mugrace argues that because inflation may not stay elevated
during the three-year rate plan, the Company’s inflation adjustment forecast is too high resulting
in rates being set too high for utility service. (Tr. 2043.) As stated in the Company’s rebuttal
hearing, inflation has continued to increase since the Company’s filing in June 2022 (Tr. 4014)
and is likely already underestimated rather than overestimated in the Company’s test year and each
ARP year. Therefore, no reduction to the Company’s estimated incremental inflation is justified.
       In addition, Mr. Mugrace errs in calculating his inflation adjustment. As explained in the
Company’s rebuttal testimony, the Company began to see significant price increases across all
aspects of its business consistent with the rising inflation factors found in the Employment Cost
Index (“ECI”) and Gross Domestic Product (“GDP”) deflator. (Tr. 3971.) In response to these
signals, the Company adjusted its projected O&M expenses to account for these cost increases in
this filing by identifying labor and non-labor costs that typically would fluctuate based on inflation
factors and applying the incremental increase in ECI and GDP inflation rates to the appropriate
cost types. Although Mr. Mugrace states that the ECI is an appropriate source of inflation rates for

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 25 of 77

labor costs (Tr. 2042), he ultimately used only the lower GDP index to escalate total non-
Production O&M expenses, including labor and non-labor costs, to calculate his incremental
inflation adjustment. (Tr. 2042-43.) However, to appropriately calculate an inflation adjustment,
the O&M expenses need to be separated into labor and non-labor charges and the ECI and GDP
rates need to be applied to the respective costs, as the Company did in performing its calculation
of the incremental inflation adjustment. (Tr. 3972.) Mr. Mugrace’s adjustments underestimate and
fail to capture the impact of inflation on the Company’s revenue requirement. Based on the
foregoing error and the lack of need to adjust the incremental inflation in the Company’s proposal,
the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s inflation adjustment.

       9.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposals that eliminate cost recovery
               for costs associated with the Company’s employee compensation programs.

       To provide its award-winning service to customers, Georgia Power must attract and retain
a high level of skilled and professional employees, who are paid competitively and appropriately
based on their role. Georgia Power’s employee compensation program is designed to provide
competitive compensation at levels targeting the 50th percentile of the appropriate markets. The
Company’s well-balanced and market competitive compensation program provides different types
of compensation that are commonly known and recognized by compensation professionals to be
market appropriate to retain and attract talent, which includes (i) fixed based compensation, (ii)
variable short-term compensation, and (iii) long-term stock-based compensation. The goal of
providing different types of compensation is to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that
employees are providing the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation
as a whole allows Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently
serve its customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational
and financial performance, thus aligning the compensation programs with Georgia Power’s need
to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce.

               a. The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposed disallowances related to
                  the structure of the Company’s total compensation plan.

       According to the testimony of PIA Staff Witnesses Smith, Trokey, and Mugrace, certain
portions of the Company’s total compensation program – the long-term incentive stock-based


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 26 of 77

compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to financial goals – do not benefit
customers and should therefore be borne by shareholders instead of customers. (Tr. 1364-65, 2047-
48.) The Company strongly disagrees with this opinion. The long-term incentive-based at-risk
compensation program incentivizes employees to make decisions that are in the long-term best
interests of customers and the Company. (Tr. 3977.) Customers benefit from a financially healthy
company, which should be measured over the short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions
made by the Company are optimized for both short- and long-term benefits. This is especially true
in the utility industry, where decisions related to infrastructure and other major capital projects
may have long-lasting financial consequences to all stakeholders, including customers. (Id.)
Customers would be harmed if the Company were to drive its employees to sacrifice long-term
financial health for short-term benefits. By designing the at-risk portion of the total compensation
plan to include longer-term goals, the Company achieves an appropriate incentive balance that
motivates employees to deliver safe, clean, and reliable electric service to our customers in a
manner that is economically efficient both now and in the years that follow. (Id.)
       The Company’s existing compensation policy is necessary to attract, motivate, and retain
a highly skilled, dedicated workforce. If “at-risk” components of the employee compensation
program, which includes the long-term incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts
of the PPP attributed to financial goals, were eliminated or significantly reduced, the Company
would have to commensurately increase other components of its total compensation package –
such as direct salary costs – to maintain its competitiveness as an employer within the industry and
to continue to attract a skilled workforce. (Tr. 3975-76.) It would result in higher fixed costs and
poor alignment of all stakeholders’ interests, including customers. (Tr. 3976)
       PIA Staff’s proposed disallowance of these portions of the employee compensation
program are not based upon any supporting data or market analysis that the Company’s expenses
are excessive or that the programs are not competitive with the external market. There is also no
basis for Mr. Mugrace’s assertion that, “[t]hese types of incentive costs benefit the Shareholders
of the Company, and of SCS and SNC, and do not directly benefit the ratepayers in the area of safe
and reliable utility service.” (Tr. 2332.) In fact, the opposite is true. Georgia Power could not
efficiently operate its business or provide customers with clean, safe, and reliable utility service
without its dedicated workforce. The budgeted costs to attract, retain, and engage employees are


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 27 of 77

expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and should be included in
the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates. Moreover, the payroll tax
expenses associated with stock-based compensation are a prudently incurred cost of doing business
and, therefore, should also be recovered from customers. The Commission should reject PIA
Staff’s recommendations to remove the “at risk” portions and stock-based compensation from
rates.

                b. Georgia Power’s SERP, non-SERP, and retention expenses are necessary to
                   retain highly skilled professional employees and are appropriately recovered
                   in retail rates.

         The Company’s costs related to Supplemental Executive Retirement Plan (“SERP”) and
non-SERP benefit costs as well as employee retention are necessary expenses to ensure the
attraction and retention of our highly skilled management team of leaders. The SERP and non-
SERP programs are used to attract and retain senior management level employees across the
various business units of the Company who have key roles, experiences, and skills in managing
employees to ensure Georgia Power continues to provide reliable customer service and maintain
our standards of safety. (Tr. 3973.) These types of programs are common for other companies who
provide qualified pension plan benefits and are regularly evaluated to ensure that the total
compensation for our employees, including these pension benefit programs, is market competitive.
(Tr. 3973-74.) Furthermore, these non-qualified pension programs are not funded and therefore,
are not covered by corporate-owned life insurance benefits as stated by Mr. Mugrace in his
testimony. (Tr. 2036.)
         In addition, employee retention agreements are utilized as targeted, strategic compensation
tools to retain high-performing employees in critical roles throughout the Company who offer deep
experience, knowledge, and expertise that is highly sought after and crucial to retain. These
agreements are entered into in direct response to specifically identified market pressures. Such
pressures often include recruitment by Company competitors and peers, within and outside of the
Company’s industry, as well as within and outside of the Company’s geographic footprint.
Employee retention costs allow the Company to continue providing excellent service for our
customer base and further maintain the resiliency of our infrastructure. (Tr. 3974.) Further, these
costs offset the potential future expenses that would be incurred to recruit, develop, and train

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 28 of 77

substitute employees for these key roles. As noted above, the Company could not offer superior
customer service and clean, safe, and reliable service in the absence of a strong internal team.
Therefore, these costs are operating expenses of the business and appropriate for inclusion in retail
rates.

               c. Limiting the Company’s Performance Pay Program (“PPP”) compensation to
                  just operational goals under incentivizes the Company’s employees to innovate
                  and find better ways to serve customers.

         PIA Staff Witness Mugrace recommends disallowing two thirds of the Company’s PPP
from recovery in retail rates. (Tr. 2047-48.) This recommendation undermines the strength of the
Company’s compensation structure and will negatively impact every single employee of Georgia
Power. Mr. Mugrace bases his recommendation on the assumption that compensation related to
financial goals and earnings per share only benefit shareholders and exclude benefits for
customers. (Id.) However, Mr. Mugrace offers no evidence or supporting testimony to validate
such an assumption. In contrast, Company Witnesses clearly explained that without the long-term
incentive stock-based compensation plan and the amounts of the PPP attributed to the Company’s
financial goals, the Company would have to commensurately increase other components of its
compensation package, resulting in higher fixed costs. (Tr. 3950, 3977-78.) Goals based on
financial metrics – when included in an incentive compensation plan designed to balance financial
and operational goals – provide benefits to customers by reducing expenses, creating greater
efficiencies in operations, ensuring a financially strong company, and allowing access to the capital
markets at a low cost. (Tr. 3978.)
         Georgia Power seeks to create a balanced incentive system to ensure that employees
provide the best service to customers at the least cost. Evaluating compensation as a whole allows
Georgia Power to maximize the effective use of resources essential to efficiently serving its
customers by linking a portion of the employees’ compensation package to operational and
financial performance and allows for the alignment of the compensation programs with Georgia
Power’s need to attract, engage, retain, and motivate its highly skilled workforce. A well-designed
total compensation program using sound compensation practices and principles provides a balance
between operational focus and financial focus for both the short term and longer term to drive
employee behavior in ways that balance the interests of customers and shareholders alike. A

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 29 of 77

compensation plan that contains only operational goals might inappropriately drive employees to
use more financial resources than necessary to achieve operational success, while a plan that
contains only financial goals might inappropriately drive employees to make decisions that
adversely impact operational success. (Tr. 3978.) A balanced program of incentive compensation
is a powerful tool that drives employees to put the customer at the center of all we do while
sustaining the financial integrity of the Company.
       Disallowing recovery of a financial component for a level of standard market pay forces a
utility to consider modifying the entire incentive design to be based on operational measures alone
(which are recoverable) and eliminate financial components of the incentive program design.
However, making this change to an operational-based incentive program design would not be in
the best interests of customers, as it would encourage focusing solely on operational performance
without regard to or accountability for operational costs. (Id.) This imbalance is not in the best
interests of customers, as the failure to appropriately consider operational cost impacts could lead
to higher rate increases for customers in the future. As such, the Commission should reject PIA
Staff’s proposed disallowance and limitation of PPP compensation to only operational goals.

       10.     The Commission should approve the Company’s proposed treatment of the
               remaining net book value for units retired in the 2022 IRP as well as Bowen Units
               1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3.

       The Company has proposed to amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Wansley Units 1-2,
Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1, which were retired by August 31, 2022 in
accordance with the Company’s 2022 IRP, over their remaining useful lives as approved in Docket
No. 42516. These proposed amortization periods are consistent with similar regulatory assets and
PIA Staff did not object to the Company’s proposal. As such, the Commission should approve the
Company’s proposed recovery and regulatory treatment for these assets. In addition, the
Commission should approve the Company’s requested depreciation deferral and proposed nine-
year amortization for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 beginning January 1,
2023. Based on the depreciation study filed in this case, Plant Bowen Units 1-2 are scheduled to
be depreciated through 2027 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 are scheduled to be depreciated through
2028 in accordance with the retirement dates proposed in the 2022 IRP. For these units, the
Company requests the Commission to defer the difference each year between the amount of

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 30 of 77

depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation
study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a
regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are retired, the
deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at the retirement
date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending December
31, 2031. The Company’s request is reasonable and was unchallenged in this case. The
Commission should approve the deferral and amortization of Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units
1-3 as requested by the Company without modification.

       11.     The Commission should reject GAM’s proposal to prohibit the Company from
               earning an ROE on unrecovered CCR ARO compliance costs until securitization is
               authorized.

       As Witness Pollock admitted upon cross examination, securitization is not currently an
option in Georgia and would require a change in state law to implement. (Tr. 2606.) As such,
denying the Company the opportunity to recover all of its financing costs on any unrecovered CCR
ARO compliance costs based on an option that is currently unlawful amounts to a disallowance
and inappropriately and prematurely assumes that such costs were not prudently incurred. It would
be inappropriate to deny the Company recovery of currently incurred financing costs based solely
on the potential for future securitization legislation, over which neither the Company nor the
Commission have control, and which might never come to fruition. Securitization is not needed as
this Commission already has all the tools necessary to appropriately balance the needs of our
customers and the Company. (Tr. 2740.)
       The Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs are required to comply with federal and state
environmental regulations and are appropriately included in rate base. The Commission has
approved the Company’s Environmental Compliance Strategy, which included the Company’s ash
pond closure plans and CCR strategy, as part of the 2022 IRP in Docket No. 44160. (Tr. 1285.)
No party in this case filed testimony or presented evidence challenging the appropriateness of the
Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs sought to be recovered in this case. Further, there have
been no changes in the CCR permits issued to Georgia Power or in federal or state CCR regulations


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 31 of 77

since the 2022 IRP that change the Company’s compliance strategy.5 As such, the Company should
be able to timely recover these prudently incurred compliance costs and have the opportunity to
recover all of its financing costs, including equity. Witness Pollock’s recommendation prevents
the Company’s ability to recover its actual costs of financing these required expenditures. In
addition, Witness Pollock’s proposed ratemaking treatment contradicts past treatment of ARO
compliance costs and such departure from past precedent is unjustified. The Commission should
not unduly restrict the Company’s ability to timely recover its prudently incurred CCR ARO
compliance costs pursuant to the current and unchallenged recovery methodology and reject
Witness Pollock’s deferral for securitization proposal.

        12.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s alternative revenue forecast.

        PIA Staff Witnesses Baron and Wellborn offered testimony proposing nothing more than
a higher, alternative revenue forecast for the Commission’s consideration to lower the Company’s
revenue requirement in this case. While the Company appreciates that differences of opinion may
exist in the intricacies of forecast modeling, the Company believes the projected revenue forecast
included in its filing continues to be the most reasonable and appropriate method to determine the
Company’s revenue requirement at present rates. Further, PIA Staff agreed that the Company’s
method was reasonable and did not identify any errors or corrections in the Company’s filing. (Tr.
1535.) With no shortcomings in the Company’s revenue forecast, there is no action for the
Commission to take and no weight should be placed on the recommendations of Witnesses Baron
and Wellborn.

        13.      Other than the proposed extension of the depreciable life of Plant Hatch, the
                 Commission should reject PIA Staff’s depreciation recommendations.

        PIA Staff Witness Kaufman removes contingency from plant dismantlement cost estimates
for coal and gas production facilities, concluding that contingency represents “unexpected


5
    While intervenors questioned Company witnesses during Rebuttal Hearings on an Environmental Protection
    Agency (“EPA”) determination for Plant Gavin in Ohio finalized on November 18, 2022, the EPA determination
    does not apply to Georgia Power CCR units. Further, the deficiencies noted by EPA in the Plant Gavin

                                  Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                                Docket No. 44280
                                                  Page 32 of 77

outcomes” and therefore, should be removed from the depreciation expense calculations for the
Company’s coal and gas plants. However, when developing cost estimates, contingency is a
fundamental element of a comprehensive and transparent estimate. Contingency is necessary to
acknowledge and mitigate the risk of inherent cost uncertainties that accompany capital-intensive
projects such as dismantlement of a coal or gas fired electric generation facility.
       Mr. Kaufman’s depreciation adjustments fail to consider factors that may influence current
and future plant dismantlements that have not impacted historic plant dismantlements. For
example, prior to the retirement of Plant Hammond in 2019, historic retired facilities did not have
air environmental controls (e.g., baghouses, scrubbers, Selective Catalytic Reduction Systems).
These environmental controls significantly increase the footprint and dismantlement cost of the
remaining coal facilities. The Company’s historic dismantlement cost estimates, including
contingency, have been included in retail rates and the Commission should continue to approve
such cost recovery here.
       In addition, PIA Staff Witness Kaufman presented depreciation rates that included
calculation errors for Production, Transmission, Distribution, and General functions. When
adjusting the lives for these functions, Mr. Kaufman simply extended the useful lives. He failed to
consider the impact to other fundamental inputs such as interim retirements and theoretical
reserves. By omitting the full impact of the proposed life extensions, Mr. Kauffman proposed
depreciation rates that do not properly allocate the capital cost of the Company’s assets over their
average service lives.
       Further, when applying Mr. Kauffman’s proposed adjustments to easements, PIA Staff
failed to apply the proposed rates to the proper plant in service balances. Rather than applying the
proposed rate for easements to the plant in service balance for easements, PIA Staff applied the
functional composite rate, which includes easements, to the functional plant in service balance that
does not include easements. The combination of these errors, along with the omission of
contingency as described above, resulted in understated depreciation expense. Therefore, the
Commission should reject all of Mr. Kaufman’s depreciation adjustments apart from extending the
assumed life of Plant Hatch, with which the Company agrees.

E.     Electric Vehicle (“EV”) Charging Facilities Benefit Georgia Power Customers and
       the State and the Commission Should Reject PIA Staff’s Recommendation to Remove
       Georgia Power’s Electric Transportation Initiatives from Retail Rates.

       1.      The Company plays a critical role in supporting the growth of the electric
               transportation ecosystem in Georgia and its investment provides benefits to Georgia
               Power’s customers.

       The Company’s continued investments in a statewide EV charging infrastructure have
enhanced Georgia’s electric transportation capabilities, realized economic savings, and provided
environmental benefits while fostering the growth of statewide EV usage. As customer demand
for EVs and access to fast charging continues to grow, Georgia Power proposes corresponding
expansion of its investments in its customers. (Tr. 462, 464.)
       Georgia Power seeks to expand its investment following the successes of those approved
in the previous rate case. The 2019 Rate Case approved an $18 million capital investment in the
Make Ready program, allowing the Company to partner with communities to deploy EV chargers
to a variety of customers, ranging from local businesses to schools and churches. (Tr. 425-26.) The
program has since been fully subscribed and now has a waiting list for future participants. (Tr.
426.) Similarly, multimillion dollar investments in the Community Charging program have
allowed the Company to expand statewide access to EVs. The Company seeks to transition the
program to offer and serve charging stations of last resort in underserved communities that lack a
competitive charging market. (Tr. 370, 429, 3964.)
       With two major EV manufacturing announcements this year alone, the demand for EVs
continues to increase, requiring further expansion of the state’s charging infrastructure to meet the
energy needs of Georgia Power’s customers. (Tr. 78, 461-62; 3964.) The Company’s proposed
investments will continue to benefit all Georgians through environmental impacts and downward
pressure on energy rates. (Tr. 377.)

       2.      The Commission should approve the Company’s requested investment in Make
               Ready infrastructure, which appropriately fosters greater adoption of EV charging
               infrastructure by commercial and industrial customers.

       In light of the Make Ready program’s benefits and the success of the program since its
approval in the 2019 Rate Case, the Commission should approve the Company’s request to
continue investing in Georgia’s EV infrastructure by expanding the Make Ready program. (Tr.

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 34 of 77

141.) To satisfy its obligation of meeting the growing energy needs of its customers, the Company
proposes investments of approximately $27 million in each of 2023, 2024, and 2025 in the Make
Ready program. (Tr. 386, 3965.) Through the program, the Company will work with customers to
build out the infrastructure behind the meter for qualifying commercial and industrial customers
to facilitate the installation of EV charging stations. (Tr. 140, 373, 3964-65.)
         All ratepayers will experience benefits from the expansion of the Make Ready program.
(Tr. 462-63.) EV owners and commercial partners will experience the firsthand benefits of electric
transportation infrastructure upgrades. In addition, the Company’s Rate Impact Measure (“RIM”)
positive analyses evidence downward pressure on rates for all ratepayers. (Tr. 369-70, 377, 388;
3966.)

         3.     The Commission should approve the Company’s requested Community Charging
                funding in support of Georgia Power’s service as a provider of last resort in rural
                and underserved communities.

         The Commission should also approve the Company’s requested Community Charging
funding because it supports expansion of the state’s EV charging infrastructure, helping make EV
ownership and usage more accessible to Georgians. With only approximately 3,000 EV charging
plugs across Georgia, large swaths of the state’s rural and economically challenged communities
do not have sufficient access to EV charging infrastructure, leaving countless Georgians without
the ability to adopt EVs as a primary means of transportation. (Tr. 950.) The Company’s proposed
Community Charging investment will allow it to reach underserved communities by installing
chargers of last resort along statewide travel corridors without impacting existing competitive
charging markets. (Tr. 370, 428-29, 460; 3965.) Presently, Georgia Power owns 59 EV charging
locations through the Community Charging program, representing only 3% of Georgia’s EV
charging infrastructure. (Tr. 951.) The Company’s proposed investments in the Community


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 35 of 77

Charging program will support areas of the state without sufficient access to EV chargers by
deploying an additional 33 locations over the next three years. (Tr. 967; 4088.)

F.     The Commission Should Reject PIA Staff’s Proposal to Gut the Grid Investment Plan
       by Removing Capital Dollars for the Transmission Plan and Removing 85% of the
       Investments for the Distribution Plan.

       Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan, approved by the Commission in the 2019 base rate
case, is a 12-year program that focuses on improving distribution reliability and replacing aging
transmission assets. (Tr. 244.) The Commission previously approved the Company’s request to
invest $1.3 billion for the 2020-2022 period. (Tr. 244.) In the current case, the Company requested
approval of dollars associated with the next three-year period of the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.)
The Grid Investment Plan continues to focus on distribution investments that will improve
reliability for customers and transmission investments to replace aging system assets. (Tr. 245.)
Ignoring the need for the critical investments addressed by the Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff
asks that this Commission reverse course and (1) reject all transmission investments planned for
the next three years, and (2) remove 85% of the planned distribution investments. (Tr. 1558, 1582,
1584.) The Commission should reject this extreme recommendation, which is unreasonable,
unsupported by the record in this case, and contrary to the best interests of customers.

       1.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendations concerning the
               transmission portion of Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan.

       As outlined in the 2019 Rate Case and again in the current docket, the transmission portion
of the Grid Investment Plan focuses on replacing aging assets that have or will exceed their
Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. (Tr. 337, 3983.) Company
Witness Robinson provided robust testimony explaining that Georgia Power’s transmission
equipment installed during the 1950s, 1960s, and 1970s, is now approaching 50, 60, and 70 year
lives. (Tr. 342-43, 4069-70.) Due to the large percentage of transmission equipment that is beyond
or nearing its Company expected life, Georgia Power carefully designed the Grid Investment Plan
to target the replacement of these assets to prevent transmission system reliability deterioration
across the electric system. (Tr. 3983.) Based on the Company’s extensive operational experience,
the likelihood of failure for the transmission assets included in the plan will exponentially increase


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 36 of 77

as the assets age beyond their expected life. (Id.) If Georgia Power does not continue making these
necessary investments in its transmission system, the reliability of the electric system will be
negatively impacted due to increased failures related to aged transmission equipment. (Id.) Such
failures can be catastrophic and simultaneous in nature. (Id.) Replacing these aged assets will
(1) reduce the number of emergency failures, (2) mitigate system operation reliability concerns,
and (3) avoid higher maintenance and replacement costs. Further, these investments will help
ensure the safety of the general public and the Company’s employees. (Tr. 3983.)

               a. The Company is not “prematurely replacing” transmission equipment in the
                  Grid Investment Plan.

       PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens mischaracterize the Company’s Grid
Investment Plan strategy for the replacement of aged transmission assets as “premature equipment
replacement.” (Tr. 1558.) Importantly, the Company relied on its extensive operational and
maintenance experience, outage and failure history, and ability to extend the expected life of its
transmission assets to determine the age at which it should replace the transmission assets within
the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3986.) The Company also reviewed, analyzed, and compared its
replacement assumptions against industry expected life ages for the transmission equipment
identified for replacement in the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.)
       As the Company testified during direct hearings, the Company used the bathtub curve to
explain equipment failure rate variation over time. (Tr. 333-34.) Witness Alvarez and Stephens’
testimony presented a bathtub curve based only upon the estimated useful life of a hypothetical
piece of equipment. (Tr. 1572.) In contrast, Georgia Power based its bathtub curve on the
Company’s expected life for specific Georgia Power transmission assets. (Tr. 3982.) The life
expectancy of Company equipment is supported by internal maintenance procedures, past
experiences, equipment specifications, and equipment monitoring. (Tr. 3986.) Importantly,
estimated useful life and expected life are two very different terms. (Id.)
       Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens misrepresent the point at which the Company is replacing
transmission equipment as part of the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3987.) They assume the Company
is replacing equipment in the middle of its estimated useful life bathtub curve, whereas the
Company is actually replacing equipment in the “end-of-life period” where failures occur at an
exponential rate due to age-related degradation of equipment. (Id.) To be clear, the Company is

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 37 of 77

strategically targeting transmission projects within this “end-of-life period” to prevent customer
outages, avoid unplanned and catastrophic equipment failure, and better manage replacement costs
for the benefit of customers. (Id.) The result, from both a reliability and cost perspective, would
be worse if the Company waited until this aging equipment failed to replace it. Replacing
transmission equipment after catastrophic failure costs 2 to 13 times more than strategically
replacing this equipment through the Company’s Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 338, 3987.) The
Company has presented the next three years of transmission projects within the Grid Investment
Plan to strategically replace equipment that will have exceeded its Company expected life by the
end of 2031 in the most coordinated and cost-effective manner, which is in the best interests of
customers. (Tr. 3983.) Therefore, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s mischaracterization
of the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan and approve the Company’s plan as
presented.

               b. The Company employed objective approaches for identifying aging
                  transmission assets that warrant replacement as part of the Grid Investment
                  Plan as recommended by PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana.

       PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens assert that the transmission portion of the Grid
Investment Plan should be based on objective approaches and diagnostic testing rather than simply
on asset age. (Tr. 1558.) However, they ignore that the Company used objective measures and
relied on its long history of robust maintenance practices, internal experience, and extensive
operational knowledge to identify which transmission equipment to replace as part of the Grid
Investment Plan, including preventive and diagnostic testing. (Tr. 3982.) To that end, Georgia
Power incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace transmission equipment
that will be beyond the Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. (Id.)
Notably, through its comprehensive maintenance practices, the Company has already extended the
life of this transmission equipment beyond its industry expected life. (Id.) For example, while the
industry expected life for transformers and breakers is 35 years and 25 years, respectively, the
Company’s expected life for transformers and breakers is 50 years and 35 years. (Id.) Accordingly,
the Company has already adopted and implemented the objective approaches that PIA Staff
recommends in identifying the proposed transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan.
       Further, the Commission should ignore Witness Stephens’ isolated example of a single 99-

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 38 of 77

year-old transformer on Georgia Power’s system. (Tr. 1735, 3984.) This isolated example should
not be used as a basis for assuming that the Company’s entire transformer fleet can or should
operate that far beyond Company expected life. As shown in the Company’s rebuttal testimony,
the percentage of transmission and distribution substation equipment within 10 years of the oldest
vintages for such equipment on Georgia Power’s system represents only approximately 2% of each
equipment category, with power transformers being less than 0.2%. (Tr. 3985.) Georgia Power
acknowledges that a small portion of substation equipment can operate well past Company
expected life. (Id.) However, this is the exception to the rule and the overwhelming majority of
Georgia Power’s transmission and distribution assets should not be expected to operate safely and
reliably for this long. Over the last 10 years, Georgia Power substation transformers have failed at
an average age of 43 years, which is already past the industry expected life of 35 years, but earlier
than the age of 50 years at which this equipment will be replaced within the Grid Investment Plan.
(Tr. 3983.) Given the large number of transmission assets that will exceed their Company expected
life in the near future, replacement of these assets through the Grid Investment Plan is the
appropriate solution, and better for customers and the transmission system than simply operating
these aged assets for an unknown period of time until they fail. (Tr. 3985-86.)
       Finally, contrary to Staff Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana’s overreliance on
inspection and performance testing for determining when equipment should be replaced, routine
diagnostic testing and inspections cannot always accurately predict when equipment will fail. (Tr.
3982-83.) As Witness Robinson testified, of the 196 substation transformers that failed in-service
over the last ten years, 108 (55% of these transformers) failed after passing such diagnostic tests
and inspections. (Tr. 3983, 4058, 4060.) The Company’s extensive operational experience
informed its recommendation to replace transmission assets that have or will exceed their
Company expected life by 2031 through the Grid Investment Plan rather than solely relying on the
results of the Company’s routine diagnostic tests and inspections. Therefore, the Commission
should give little weight to the arguments of PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens in their
attempt to discredit the Company’s strategic and thoughtful plan for transmission asset
replacement.


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 39 of 77

               c. A cost benefit analysis for the transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan
                  is not practical or necessary for the Commission to approve the Company’s
                  request in this case.

       Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens also argue that the Commission should not approve any
transmission Grid Investment Plan funding in the absence of a cost benefit analysis. (Tr. 1587,
1746.) As explained repeatedly by the Company in this case (and in the 2019 Rate Case), it is
impractical to perform a cost-benefit analysis for transmission investments included in the Grid
Investment Plan due to the dynamic and networked nature of the transmission system. (Tr. 3984,
4067-68.) Several factors impact transmission system operation and could change suddenly in any
such cost benefit study, including system loading and flows, facility and generation outages, and
weather. (Tr. 3984.) The ability to conduct a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit analysis of
transmission investments in the Grid Investment Plan is further complicated by the type of failure
that could occur (controlled or catastrophic), impacts to other capital projects, lead times for
replacement equipment, and the cost of generation redispatch due to loss of transmission elements.
(Id.) The constant change in these variables would produce a cost-benefit analysis with unreliable
conclusions that could be rendered obsolete or invalid at any given time. The results of such
analysis would be questionable, at best. (Id.) For these reasons, the Company used asset age to
determine capital investment in each transmission facility to mitigate the risk of outages associated
with a significant and growing number of assets that are approaching or have exceeded their
Company expected lives. (Id.) Thus, even if the Company were to prepare a cost benefit analysis,
it would not provide the Commission meaningful information on which to rely. Therefore, yet
again, the Commission should dismiss PIA Staff’s attempt to undermine the Company’s
Transmission Grid Investment Plan request.

       2.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to gut the distribution
               portion of the Grid Investment Plan by removing the undergrounding and basic
               insulation level investment packages.

       The Company’s proposed undergrounding and Basic Insulation Level (“BIL”) investment
packages should not be removed from the Grid Investment Plan. If it were to eliminate these
investment packages, the Commission would eliminate 85% of the planned distribution
investments included in the Grid Investment Plan—essentially forestalling the purpose of the


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 40 of 77

distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, which focuses on ensuring lasting reliability
improvements while minimizing the total cost to customers. (Tr. 3989.) In addition, the Company
disagrees with PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ assertion that “there is no pending
reliability emergency.” (Tr. 1565, 1567, 3989.) Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue
to worsen every year that investments are not made. Between 2013 and 2021, the Company’s
system average interruption duration index (“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately 44% and
system average interruption frequency index (“SAIFI”) worsened by approximately 10%. (Tr.
3989.) For a subset of feeders (also referred to as circuits) in the system, and the customers on
those feeders, the decline in reliability is even more pronounced. This worsening reliability has
significant negative impacts on customer experience due to more frequent and longer outages, as
well as increases in the economic cost incurred by customers. (Tr. 3989-90.)
       Removing the undergrounding and BIL investment packages from the Grid Investment
Plan would result in significantly longer and more frequent outages across the system, as these two
packages are the most effective way to permanently address the root causes of outages on the
nearly 400 feeders selected for these investment packages. (Tr. 3990.) Without these investments,
the Company would achieve an end-state SAIDI of approximately 127 by 2031, essentially equal
to the program starting point SAIDI in 2019, compared to the Grid Investment Plan’s current
projected end-state of approximately 80. (Id.) The results of PIA Staff’s recommendations would
maintain the system-level SAIDI at third quartile levels and stall reliability improvement
opportunities. (Id.) As such, the Commission should reject PIA Staff’s proposal to remove the
undergrounding and BIL packages from the Company’s Distribution Grid Investment Plan.

               a. The Grid Investment Plan is a dynamic multiyear effort that the Company
                  should appropriately revise as lessons are learned, not abandon simply because
                  circumstances change and early results reveal more efficient future options.

       Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens criticize the Company’s revised SAIDI target from 65 as
filed in the 2019 Rate Case to 80 in the 2022 Rate Case. (Tr. 1563.) They claim that 23.1%
deterioration in target system SAIDI after only three years “calls into question the Company’s
projection of the actual performance improvement customers will receive for very large capital
investment made by Georgia Power.” (Id.) In reality, the revision to the end-state SAIDI targeted
in the Company’s Distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan is a result of lessons learned

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 41 of 77

from the first three years of implementation and not a reflection of changed reliability expectations.
(Tr. 3990.) In fact, the Company has seen better than expected reliability improvements from the
set of investments that have completed construction. (Id.) Initial results indicate an improvement
of approximately 65% in both SAIDI and SAIFI for Grid Investment Plan projects that have been
completed for over a year and cycled through both a winter and summer peak since completion.
(Tr. 3990-91.) The investments are working as intended and are delivering improved reliability for
Georgia Power’s customers. (Tr. 3991.)
       Based on insights and lessons learned in the first three years of the program, Georgia Power
confirmed the following: (i) that the overall strategy and economic rationale supporting the Grid
Investment Plan is optimal for customers; (ii) the total cost required to deliver individual
investments is higher than originally projected because of increases in scope and cost of work (e.g.,
inflation, COVID shortages); and (iii) while undergrounding remains the most effective solution
for improving customer reliability, the use of undergrounding should be carefully evaluated to
ensure it is used only where it is the most cost efficient solution. (Tr. 3991.) With these learnings,
the Company appropriately reassessed and refreshed the distribution portion of the Grid
Investment Plan, employing the same methodology used in the 2019 Rate Case, to ensure that the
investments being made continue to result in the lowest total cost to customers. (Id.) As a result of
this evaluation, the Company determined that the set of distribution investments in the best
interests of customers are projected to deliver an 80 end-state SAIDI as compared to the 65 end-
state SAIDI included in the 2019 Rate Case plan, which are still first quartile results compared to
industry peers. (Id.) The Company considers this intermittent refinement of the 10-year Grid
Investment Plan essential for ensuring that customer capital is managed responsibly. (Id.) The
outcome of these refinements is a distribution plan that continues to deliver, where it is
economically beneficial, strong reliability benefits for customers.

               b. The distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan appropriately addresses
                  feeder level issues through system level optimization.

       The Company’s distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan optimizes benefits at the
system level rather than the feeder level because the cost of system reliability is borne by all
customers served by Georgia Power. (Tr. 3992.) In other words, the cost for capital investment
and ongoing O&M expense for the grid is paid for by all customers, regardless of each customer’s

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 42 of 77

reliability experience and regardless of whether the customer’s individual feeder is included within
the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.) The Company intentionally chose to optimize benefits across the
system to ensure the investments made were in the best interest of all customers instead of just the
subset of customers served by an individual feeder. (Id.)
       Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend that the Company conduct a root cause
analysis for every feeder on the Company’s system (Tr. 1608), which implies that the Company
has not done such analysis. That is a misconception. Each and every distribution investment within
the Grid Investment Plan is evaluated on a feeder-by-feeder basis with a root cause analysis. (Tr.
3992.) The Company does exactly the level of analysis suggested by Staff Witnesses Alvarez and
Stephens. The initial ‘hurdle’ for any feeder to qualify for investment is that the feeder has had
sustained poor performance over the past five years. (Tr. 3993.) The Company then undertakes a
second evaluation to determine the root causes of outages on each feeder to determine if the
outages were a result of vegetation, vehicles, animals, or weather and how that compares to the
rest of the system. (Id.) An evaluated feeder qualifies for a distribution investment package that
would permanently resolve the outage cause(s) only if it experiences a higher percentage of
outages driven by the particular cause(s) than the system average. (Id.) In this way, the Company
deploys standard, repeatable criteria systematically across all feeders to identify outliers in each
category of outage cause.
       Therefore, prior to optimizing the plan, the Company has completed a feeder-by-feeder,
root cause analysis on the entire distribution system. (Tr. 3993.) Through this analysis, the
Company determines whether a feeder should be considered for any investment and, if so, which
of the six possible investment options specifically would address the root causes of reliability
issues on that feeder. (Id.) For each of the possible investment packages identified during the root
cause analysis, the Company projects the costs and the reliability benefits specific to deploying
qualified investment package(s) on each feeder. (Id.) The Company then evaluates the impact of
all possible combinations of feeder level investments to determine the final investment plan that
provides the best reliability and the lowest total cost to customers. (Tr. 3994.) As a result, the
Company considers the direct cost of investments compared to the costs customers bear from
increased outages if no investment was made. (Id.) Contrary to the allegations raised by PIA Staff,
the Company appropriately considers feeder level costs and benefits prior to optimizing its


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 43 of 77

proposed distribution investments.

                c. The Company’s undergrounding and BIL investment packages offer cost
                   effective and permanent solutions to systemic feeder level problems that cannot
                   be addressed by greater vegetation management as proposed by PIA Staff.

        PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens wrongly claim that increased vegetation
management is an effective alternative to the undergrounding and BIL Grid Investment Plan
packages. (Tr. 1602.) PIA Staff offered no independent analysis to validate their assertion. (Tr.
3995.) Though vegetation management has an important place in managing overall system
reliability, it is a temporary solution that cannot address a variety of other outage drivers, such as
vehicles, wildlife, and lightning. (Id.)
        PIA Staff could not explain how vegetation management could generate the same expected
level of reliability improvement as the dedicated investment packages included in the Distribution
Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3995.) During cross examination, Mr. Stephens confirmed that no
analysis had been done to determine how much it would cost to actually improve reliability through
vegetation management. (Tr. 1874.) Mr. Stephens himself also acknowledged that he had merely
made an assumption about vegetation management as a cost-effective alternative. (Tr. 1872-73.)
Many activities fall within the category of ‘vegetation management,’ and Mr. Stephens did not
complete any analysis to determine which activities specifically would need to be added or
increased to obtain reliability improvements through vegetation management. (Tr. 1875.) Finally,
Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens proposed a legislated expansion of utility rights of way as a
potential approach to improving reliability. (Tr. 1605, 1877.) Mr. Alvarez confirmed at the hearing
that he had not undertaken any analysis of how long it would take or how much it would cost to
pass such legislation. (Tr. 1877-78.) As such, PIA Staff failed to demonstrate how its proposed
alternatives to the Grid Investment Plan were either less costly or more beneficial than the
Company’s proposal.
        In addition, Staff’s assertions that the undergrounding and BIL packages are not cost-
effective are unfounded. PIA Staff systematically underestimated investment benefits by not
accurately representing the cost to customers of foregoing undergrounding and BIL. (Tr. 3994.)
First, PIA Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens did not account for the continued reliability
degradation that customers would experience if feeders selected for undergrounding or BIL are

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 44 of 77

denied investment, underestimating the economic cost to customers of the increased outages. (Id.)
Second, PIA Staff did not quantify the outage repair-related and vegetation-related O&M expenses
that will be incurred if these feeders are removed from the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.) In contrast,
the Company has taken extreme care to account for all costs and benefits that will be experienced
by customers during the formulation of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company is confident that
the distribution investments that are part of the Grid Investment Plan are in the best interests of
customers, resulting in the greatest reliability benefits for the lowest total cost to customers.

               d. The Company’s Grid Investment Plan already encompasses the qualities of a
                  Worst Performing Feeder Program as recommended by PIA Staff.

       In searching for an alternative to the Company’s Distribution Grid Investment Plan, PIA
Staff proposes that the Company simply invest in a more robust “Worst Performing Feeder”
Program. (Tr. 1620.) Such a program would include (1) clear and repeatable criteria to identify
poor-performing feeders, (2) a dedicated budget and associated personnel to administer the
program, and (3) standard tracking and reporting on the program’s progress. (See Tr. 1608.) To be
clear, the distribution portion of the Company’s Grid Investment Plan already encompasses the
