key qualities that Staff recommends as part of its proposed ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program.
       First, as is presented in the Customer Benefit Analysis, the Company has clear and specific
criteria for each distribution investment package in the Grid Investment Plan. (Tr. 3997.) This
includes reliability-specific criteria (e.g., SAIDI, SAIFI), physical attribute-related criteria (e.g.,
overhead vs. underground), and criteria that identify the root causes of outages (e.g., outages due
to vegetation or vehicles, feeder load). Where the root cause is unclear, not addressable, or cost
prohibitive, an investment is not made. (Id.)
       Second, the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan has a designated budget and
designated personnel similar to what PIA Staff recommends. (Tr. 3998.) In the 2019 Rate Case,
the budget for the first three years of the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan was
approved by the Commission. Moreover, since 2019, the Company has established a team solely
devoted to the Grid Investment Plan – from program and project managers, to engineers, supply
chain personnel, and construction workers on the ground.
       Third, Staff Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend “standards for tracking spending,
projects, and results.” (Tr. 1608.) The Company rigorously tracks all spending for the Grid

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 45 of 77

Investment Plan (at both the project and program level), as well as the reliability performance of
each Distribution feeder – before, during, and after investment. (Tr. 3998.) This fact is evidenced
by the adjustments made by the Company in the overall plan and optimal range for investment.
The Company also reports on the Grid Investment Plan performance during the Company’s rate
case and makes itself available to respond to any data requests on the Grid Investment Plan. (Id.)
       Finally, Staff failed to conduct the analysis required to validate its recommendation that
the Company should focus only on feeders that are performing 2.5 times worse than average as
part of its ‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program. (Tr. 3996.) This lack of analysis is inconsistent
with Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens’ own assertions that grid investments should only be made
if they are cost effective at the feeder level. PIA Staff did not consider the type of investment that
would be required to address the root cause of outages on feeders that are performing 2.5 times
worse than average. (Id.) For many of these feeders, undergrounding and BIL would be the best
solution to address root causes – yet both are investment packages within the Grid Investment Plan
that Staff recommends removing. The distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan
encompasses the very qualities that PIA Staff recommends should be part of the Company’s
‘Worst Performing Feeder’ program and will provide key reliability benefits in a cost-effective
manner for the benefit of customers. Therefore, the Commission should approve the distribution
portion of the Grid Investment Plan as proposed by the Company, without modification.

G.     To Appropriately Integrate Additional Distributed Energy Resources (“DER”) to the
       System, the Commission Should Approve the Company’s Requests for DERMS and
       Improved Communication and Visibility Requirements.

       1.      The Company must invest in DERMS to optimize the benefits from increased DER
               penetration on the system.

       PIA Staff proposes to reject DERMS spending until the level of DER on the grid increases
and claims that existing systems and processes can manage DER. (Tr. 1625-26.) While current
software systems allow for the monitoring of power flows and equipment status, they lack the
enhanced monitoring and operational capabilities of DERMS that are necessary to allow for the
optimization of an electric system that takes full advantage of the proliferation of DER. (Tr. 212,
259-60, 3999.) The systems currently in place cannot manage the substantial number of DER
expected to connect to the electric grid in the coming years. As more DER are added to the system,

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 46 of 77

it is essential that the Company have visibility, predictability, and control (where applicable) of
these resources. Failure to do so could compromise the Company’s ability to accurately forecast
and optimize the full array of generation resources and could negatively impact power quality and
reliability. (Tr. 3999.) Further, current software systems do not allow a system operator to process
the significant amount of data coming in from DER or make informed decisions quickly enough
to optimize grid operation. (Id.)
       In contrast, a DERMS that is fully integrated with the Company’s other grid control
systems will allow the Company to continually optimize larger portions of the grid and address a
variety of constraints, such as economic dispatch, Volt/VAR optimization, and reliability. (Id.)
Additionally, DERMS will allow the Company to consider demand side resources alongside
supply side resources in future IRPs, as ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. The
Company’s implementation of DERMS at this point will also allow for maturation of the DERMS
solution as thousands or tens of thousands of additional DER are connected to the grid. If DER
growth continues unrestricted without the installation of DERMS, the Company risks a loss of grid
control, which would compromise grid reliability and stability. (Id.) A DERMS solution is a
natural, logical, and necessary step for the Company to prepare for the growth and integration of
variable and distributed energy resources.
       To reiterate, there are significant risks to grid stability if the Company were to delay
implementing DERMS. (Tr. 4000.) Beginning implementation of a DERMS solution now allows
for the full DERMS capabilities to be developed in parallel with increasing DER penetration across
the electric grid. Further, the complexity of integrating DERMS with the Company’s existing
critical software systems will require a significant amount of time. Undertaking DERMS over the
next three years reduces the risk of restricting DER interconnections in the future because of a lack
of DER forecasting, control, and optimization capability. (Id.) The Commission should approve
the Company’s requested funding for DERMS in this case to optimize the integration of DER on
the Georgia Power system.

       2.      Improved communications interfaces with customer generation systems will enable
               the Company to safely and reliably integrate DER operating in parallel with the
               system.

       Contrary to the arguments posed by PIA Staff Witnesses Barber, Deitchman, and Watkins

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 47 of 77

(Tr. 2195), the Company did not propose edits to Section G of its Rules and Regulations to require
unconditional access and control of customer generators. Rather, the Company proposed requiring
that the customer generation facilities interconnecting to the Company’s electric system include a
communications interface, installed and maintained by the customer, that supports information
exchange with, and control by, Georgia Power so that the Company may continue to operate the
electric system in a safe, reliable, and efficient manner. (Tr. 4000.)
       The proposed requirement to allow for communication and increased DER visibility is
critical to maintaining the system’s safety and reliability. Without visibility, it is impossible for a
utility to ascertain the status, availability, real time actual output, and forecast availability of a
resource. Without control, it is impossible for a utility to update functional settings such as active
power setpoint, frequency response, and reactive power modes in response to real time grid
conditions. (Tr. 4001.) These functions enable real time economic optimization of the electric grid.
The Company is requesting that all existing and future customer generation facilities be equipped
with a local communications interface so that these facilities will be available to the Company’s
eventual DERMS solution. Without visibility and control capability on existing customer
generation facilities, the Company cannot be fully aware of the interaction of those facilities with
other generation facilities and the rest of the electric grid. (Id.) As the number of distributed
generators grows, the ability to communicate and potentially interact with customer generation
will allow for additional growth that might not otherwise be possible. Therefore, the Commission
should approve the Company’s proposed modifications to Section G of its rules and regulations.

H.     The Company’s Residential Rate Design Recommendations are in the Best Interests
       of Customers and Should be Approved by the Commission.

       1.      PIA Staff’s proposal to eliminate the declining winter rate structure is unnecessary.

       The Company originally designed the residential rate to reflect the seasonality of costs to
serve residential customers. (Tr. 4236.) The increasing price blocks in June through September
reflect the fact that the Company’s costs are higher in the summer months. (Id.) The increasing
price structure provides incentives for energy efficiency and conservation. The Company is
projected to remain a summer peaking utility and the inclining rate in the summer months and the
declining block structure in the non-summer months supports that. (Id.) Further, the declining


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 48 of 77

block structure in the non-summer months recognizes that off-peak costs are lower and provides
price signals to reflect this reality. (Id.) The declining block structure provides an economical
option for customers choosing to use electric-based heating. The prices are set to more than cover
the Company’s marginal costs and resulting revenues place downward pressure on rates for all
customers. (Id.) The Commission should continue to reject requests to change the declining block
rate structure as it has in the last two rate cases.

        2.      Closing the ‘R’ Rate to new residential premises is an appropriate and gradual first
                step toward modernizing the Company’s rate offerings.

        The Company’s proposal to close the R rate for new residential premises effective January
1, 2023, should be approved. This proposal is a logical step in the transition to more modern rate
structures with price signals that more accurately reflect the cost of electric service for customers.
(Tr. 4237.) To be clear, if the R rate is closed to new premises, customers will still be able to
choose from among any of the Company’s residential rate options for which they qualify, including
Time of Use – Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”); Time of Use – Plug-in Electric Vehicle (“TOU-
PEV”); or PrePay. (Tr. Id.) After the initial year of service at those new premises, customers will
be eligible for two additional rates, FlatBill and Pay by Day. (Id.)

        3.      TOU-RD is an appropriate default rate for new premises.

                a.      Customers can understand the TOU-RD rate.

        PIA Staff wrongly asserts that customers cannot understand the TOU-RD rate. (See Tr.
2179.) Customers can understand that if pricing is higher during certain hours, they can shift their
energy usage to hours during which prices are lower to save money. (Tr. 2893.) Customers are
also fully capable of understanding that under a demand rate they can save money by not running
major appliances at the same time. As analogized by GIPL Witness Barnes, rooftop solar
customers can understand that to save money they can run their higher demand appliances during
daylight hours when their behind the meter (“BTM”) systems are generating power in order to
offset their own usage demand rather than purchasing that energy from the Company at a higher
price. (Tr. 4647.) Similarly, rooftop solar and non-rooftop solar customers alike can understand
the savings recognized by shifting their energy use to times of the day when energy costs less and


                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 49 of 77

avoiding running major appliances at the same time.
        Additionally, customers who have enrolled on the TOU-RD rate have remained on the rate
for several years, which indicates that they understand and are satisfied with the rate. In fact,
customer surveys reflect that TOU-RD customers are more satisfied and feel more knowledgeable
about behavioral changes they can make to reduce their energy bills than customers on the R rate.
(Tr. 4237-38.) Further, if customers have questions about the Company’s rates, Georgia Power’s
team at the customer care center is available to help them understand how each rate option works,
and the Company provides information directly to TOU-RD customers, in addition to making
available helpful videos on the Company’s website. (Tr. 4261-62.)

                 b. The TOU-RD Rate is not more costly for customers than the R rate.

        The TOU-RD rate is not designed to cost customers more than the R rate; rather, the TOU-
RD rate is designed to be revenue neutral for the average customer – meaning that the average
customer would pay approximately the same annual bill amount on the TOU-RD rate as they
would on the R rate. (Tr. 4238.) In fact, because TOU-RD provides better price signals to
customers, they can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric
bills by shifting usage to off peak periods and staggering the use of key appliances. (Id.) As a
result, customers receive more efficient economic signals regarding their usage and energy
choices. (Id.)
        As new premises are added to Georgia Power’s electric system, it can take anywhere from
one to two years for the premises’ monthly usage to normalize. (Id.) This ramp-up period could be
due to numerous factors, including the timing of home construction, installation of appliances, or
occupancy. At least one intervening witness commented during their direct testimony that the
TOU-RD rate is an effective rate for customers that demonstrate a “good load factor.” (Tr. 4788.)
By allowing new premises to mature towards a normal usage level, customers at these new
premises will greatly improve their load factors and have a better bill experience when comparing
TOU-RD to the R rate. Because the bill comparisons that PIA Staff highlights in testimony
represent premises that are new to our system, the average monthly usage for those premises is far
below the level at which the usage will ultimately mature. (Tr. 4238.) Therefore, the bill
comparisons used by PIA Staff are heavily skewed by extremely low usage and fail to tell a


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 50 of 77

complete story.

               c.      TOU-RD is less volatile than the TOU-REO rate.

       As a default rate for residential customers, the Company recommends using the
comparatively less volatile rate, TOU-RD. The TOU-RD rate is much more stable than either
TOU-REO or the ‘R’ Rate when comparing the difference between minimum and maximum bills
over a given year, as shown in Figure 2 of the rebuttal testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg.
(Tr. 4240.) Recognizing customer interest in bill stability, the Commission should adopt the
Company’s recommendation to use TOU-RD as the new default residential rate if closing the ‘R’
Rate to new premises is adopted.

               d.      TOU-RD is more cost reflective than the R rate.

       TOU-RD is a modern, three-part rate that sends price signals that more accurately and
appropriately reflect the cost of electric service. Costs to serve customers are divided into three
components: (1) customer-related costs; (2) demand-related costs; and (3) energy-related costs. To
ensure our rates most accurately reflect the cost to serve our customers, all three cost components
should be accounted for within the design of a rate. (Tr. 4239.) As such, three-part rates, which
include customer, demand, and energy-related components, are the most cost-reflective rates
because they appropriately account for each of the three cost categories. (Id.) Conversely, when a
rate, such as the R rate, lacks a demand component, the demand-related costs of electric service
must be recovered through the rate’s energy component, which does not appropriately match the
collection of revenues with how the Company incurs costs. The result is a less efficient price signal
that leads to less efficient use of the electric system by customers and higher prices overall. (Tr.
4240.) Based on this reasoning, the Company supports customer movement to three-part rates such
as TOU-RD. Because the TOU-RD rate provides better price signals than the R rate, customers
can make more informed decisions about their energy use and save on their electric bills by shifting
usage or staggering the use of key appliances. (Tr. 4233.)

I.     The Company’s Commercial Rate Design Recommendations are in the Best Interests
       of Customers and Should be Approved by the Commission.

       The Company’s commercial and industrial rates continue to be appropriately structured,

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 51 of 77

and the Company does not agree with PIA Staff and Intervenor proposed changes to these rates.

       1.      The Commission should deny PIA Staff’s recommendation that customers on
               marginal rates be assessed the fully embedded cost of the ECCR tariff because such
               a change is contrary with how marginal rates like RTP are designed to operate.

       Fully embedded costs are recovered from the Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of
the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs are recovered from the marginal portion of the
rates. (Tr. 4233.) To include embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal portion of rates like
RTP, as suggested by PIA Staff (Tr. 2148), would undermine the rate structure and significantly
decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers.
       PIA Staff’s recommendation is fundamentally inconsistent with how marginal rates like
RTP are designed to operate. (Tr. 4241.) For RTP as designed, the Company’s fully embedded
costs are recovered from the CBL portion of the rate, while marginal costs are recovered from the
marginal portion of the rate. (Id.) To include fully embedded costs such as ECCR in the marginal
portion of the RTP rate would not only corrupt the rate structure, but also prevent these rates from
functioning as marginal rates because the hourly prices would no longer reflect marginal costs or
real-time prices. (Tr. 4241-42.) Moreover, the ECCR costs include coal combustion residual asset
retirement obligations (“CCR ARO”), which are fully embedded costs incurred by the Company
independent of a customer’s kWh usage. (Tr. 4241.) As such, counter to recommendations by
GIPL Witness Barnes (Tr. 2976) and PIA Staff (Tr. 2148), ECCR costs are appropriately recovered
through the CBL portion of the rate.
       If ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to the marginal portion of
RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million overall. (Tr. 4242.) A cost
shift of this magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future
customers. The RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that are
considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia. (Id.) PIA Staff’s proposal
undermines the principles of marginal rate design by dramatically altering the allocation of ECCR
costs within the RTP rate and would have a significant negative impact on the Company’s ability
to help attract economic development opportunities to the state. (Id.) PIA Staff acknowledges the
drastic nature of its proposal and offers to mitigate its own proposal by only shifting 50% of
embedded costs to the marginal portion of the RTP rate. (Tr. 2148.) However, even an incremental

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 52 of 77

move of 50% would have a dramatic and negative impact on RTP customers by fundamentally
changing the rate and undermining the core principles of a marginal rate structure.

       2.      The Commission should deny DOD Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify
               the Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install behind
               the meter generation.

       The Commission should reject DOD Witness Blank’s recommendation to modify the
Company’s criteria for the reduction of CBLs for customers who install BTM generation because
customer generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does not justify the
reduction of a customer’s CBL. CBLs may be reduced when load originally represented in the
CBL has been permanently removed. (Tr. 4242.) Generation does not permanently remove load,
and therefore does not justify a reduction of the customer’s CBL. (Tr. 4242-43.) Further, Mr. Blank
misunderstands how the Company’s back up service rate and RTP rates work. He incorrectly
assumes that an RTP customer can also take service under the Company’s Back Up Service rate
simply because it has BTM generation on site. (Tr. 3404.) They cannot. (Tr. 4243.) This
misunderstanding should not be used as a basis to make changes to how CBL reductions for RTP
customers are calculated. The Commission should reject this recommendation.
       Second, Mr. Blank criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction assessment
based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under the RTP
tariff for two years. (Tr. 3404-5.) The Company supports the requirement that a customer remain
off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because short term
changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of embedded load
into marginal load on the RTP rate. (Tr. 4243.) The RTP tariffs include provisions to revise the
CBL as the result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy efficiency
improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to temporary factors such as
economic downturns. (Id.) Requiring customers who have not removed load to stay off RTP for
24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the integrity of the RTP program and protects other
customers from potential cost shifts that might result from premature revision of the CBL. (Id.)
       RTP customers can still benefit from installing onsite generation even in the absence of
Witness Blank’s recommendations. RTP customers that install and use generation receive the
benefit of the Company’s marginal price for that hour. (Tr. 4243.) This is true when the customer

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 53 of 77

avoids paying the marginal price while operating above their CBL level, or when the customer
receives a credit at the marginal price when operating below their CBL level. (Id.)

       3.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff’s recommendation to modify the terms
               and conditions of the Fixed Price Alternative (“FPA”) and Price Protection Product
               (“PPP”) offerings to remove Company discretion over when to offer these rates.

       The Company considers multiple factors when deciding whether to offer the FPA rate and
PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not limited to, uncertainty around
environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors that could impact the pricing of
these offerings. Additionally, it is important for the Company to maintain flexibility to act quickly
and update PPP offerings to best reflect market conditions, especially during times of fuel market
volatility as have been experienced for the last year. As a result, removing the Company’s
discretion regarding when to offer these products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by
other customers, since delays in acting during volatile markets could result in the development of
product offerings that do not properly reflect pricing impacts.

J.     The Commission Should Approve the Company’s Requested Block Prices for the
       Community Solar Program.

       The Community Solar tariff offers qualifying customers the opportunity to support
renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing to a portion of
the output from local solar facilities. (Tr. 888, 2184.) Every month, participating customers pay a
fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities, and in return
receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production from those facilities.
(Id.) Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are retired on the customer’s
behalf. (Tr. 888, 2184-85.)
       The Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential Community Solar
Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block to ensure that participants are not subsidized
by non-participants. (Tr. 888, 928-29, 2185, 2299.) In addition, following the Commission’s
approval to expand the opportunity to participate in Community Solar to customers on the General
Service (“GS”) tariff, the Company has proposed that the pricing for GS Community Solar
participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt block. (Tr. 888, 929.) The Community Solar


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 54 of 77

subscription price is designed to ensure that participating customers do not shift costs to other
customers when kilowatt hours reduce the monthly bills of participating customers. (Tr. 939.) As
such, the Company has requested an increase in its subscription rates for residential and
commercial customers participating in Community Solar. (Tr. 914, 929.) Based on the approved
structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per kW block of energy as
proposed by several parties would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift cost recovery to
non-participants. (Tr. 914.)
         PIA Staff’s recommendation regarding the appropriate pricing for the Community Solar
Program offers two alternatives and is premised on a Commission policy determination as to the
purpose of the program: (i) if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy and realize bill
savings, Staff recommends its $20 and $22 pricing for residential and commercial customers, or
(ii) if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy but not the opportunity for bill savings,
Staff supports the Company’s proposed $27.99 and 29.99 pricing for residential and commercial
customers, respectively. (Tr.2449-50.) PIA Staff’s recommendations assume that this decision has
not yet been determined. However, in the Commission’s June 7, 2017 Order approving the
Community Solar Program tariff, the Commission clearly stated that the Community Solar
Program “is not designed to produce credits in excess of a customer’s usage and a customer should
anticipate the [Community Solar] program charges will exceed the value of any bill credits
received.”6 Although PIA Staff may believe the purpose of the program is an open policy decision
for the Commission, the Commission has already determined that the purpose of the program is
not for bill savings. Rather, the Community Solar Program is targeted toward customers who are
interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their
premises. As such, the Commission should approve the Company’s requested block price increase
for the Community Solar program.

K.       The Charge It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider is an Appropriately Structured, Time
         Limited Offering that Will Encourage EV Adoption and Should be Approved by the
         Commission Without Modification.

         Georgia Power is supporting the rapidly expanding EV market with a rider that is tailored

6
     Order approving Community Solar Program and CS-1 Tariff, Georgia Public Service Commission, Docket
     Nos. 40161 and 16573 (June 7, 2017) at 2.

                                Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 55 of 77

to provide affordably priced electric service and to encourage the growth of EVs in our state. (Tr.
407, 462, 2418.) The CIEV rider will modify how the Power & Light tariffs price commercial EV
charging loads. (Tr. 886.) Specifically, the new CIEV rider will modify the monthly billing
demands by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors as specified in the tariff. CIEV is a
temporary rider that will expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load
factor for the account reaches 15% or greater – whichever comes first. Participation is available to
new, separately metered charging loads that are served on Georgia Power’s system after January
1, 2023. (Tr. 930.)
       The Commission should not open the CIEV rider to existing locations as recommended by
AACE, ChargePoint, and EVgo. (Tr. 2310-11; Tr. 2418-19; Tr. 2521-22.) First, the CIEV rider
was designed to incentivize the installation of new EV charging stations and opening this rate to
existing customers would defeat the purpose of incentivizing new installations. Second, by limiting
this tariff to new, separately metered premises, the Company designed the CIEV rider to avoid a
cost shift among existing customers. If existing customers were permitted to participate on the
CIEV rider, the reduced billing demand allocation in each year would necessarily shift demand
costs to other customers. The Company does not support this cost shift and advocates that the
CIEV rider should be limited to new, separately metered premises in order to appropriately
incentivize new EV charging installations without doing harm to existing customers.

L.     The Company’s Cost of Service Study Reasonably Allocates the Company’s Revenue
       Requirement Across Retail Rates Based on Principles of Cost Causation and Should
       be Approved Without Modification.

       1.      The 12-CP method is the most appropriate way to allocate the Company’s
               production costs.

       A 12-CP demand is defined as the sum of the highest kW load for the Company in each
month of the 12-month period divided by twelve. (Tr. 892.) At the jurisdictional, class, or rate
group level, 12-CP is the respective coincident demands at the time of the monthly system peaks.
(Id.) Using this allocator ensures that the coincident load burden placed on the system by a
jurisdiction, class, or rate group is allocated an appropriate share of the costs of the system. (Id.)
       The Company’s adoption of seasonal planning reinforces the appropriateness of the 12-CP
approach. (Id.) The Company must plan to have sufficient generation to serve its customers in all

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 56 of 77

twelve months of the year and the 12-CP method appropriately captures the summer and winter
peaks targeted through seasonal planning. (Id.) The summer and winter reliability planning of the
Company further underscores that no one month or season is adequate at representing the
coincident impact of customers on system capacity. (Id.) Georgia Power forecasts to remain a
summer peaking utility. (Tr. 1061.) While the IRP identified winter reliability concerns, the
Company’s rate designs remain appropriate to address the forecasted peak loads and higher
marginal costs that occur in summer months. (Id.)
         In addition, as stated in Mr. Evans’ Direct Testimony, the 12-CP allocation methodology
has been used in every COSS study filed by the Company with this Commission since 1989. (Tr.
893.) The 12-CP method has been one of FERC’s preferred allocation techniques for determining
wholesale jurisdictional obligations, and it has been used by the Company in its filings before
FERC. (Id.) It has proven to be a common and popular allocation technique employed in numerous
retail filings before many state commissions throughout the United States. (Id.) It is a
straightforward methodology, has a strong cost causative relationship, and has a history of
providing stable and sound results. (Id.) The Commission should approve the Company’s use of
the 12-CP methodology for allocating production costs.

         2.     The Commission should reject PIA Staff and Intervenor proposals to adopt
                alternative production cost allocators.

         PIA Staff argues that the shortcoming of the 12-CP method is that accurate load data is
required by class throughout the year. (Tr 2109.) PIA Staff Witness Watkins admitted at the
hearing, however, that the Company has an abundance of necessary data. (Tr. 2248.) Thus, the
only shortcoming identified by PIA Staff to the Company’s method does not hold true for Georgia
Power.
         In lieu of the 12-CP methodology, PIA Staff proposes to use the Base Intermediate Peak
and Probability of Dispatch methodologies. (Tr. 2243.) However, adoption of these methodologies
not only impacts the allocation of costs in base rate cases, but also may impact the allocation of
fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery docket. The trade-off between the level of Production fixed
investment and the variable cost of producing a unit of output is one primarily made between base
capital costs and fuel costs. Altering the allocation of Production capital costs with such a complex
calculation would necessitate a similar alteration to the allocation in the Fuel Cost Recovery

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 57 of 77

docket. Recognizing the current, approved allocation of fuel costs in the Fuel Cost Recovery
docket, the Company continues to support the 12-CP methodology as the most appropriate
Production allocator. It has been employed by Georgia Power for allocating production capacity-
related costs since 1989, and as such, has therefore influenced present rates. (Tr. 893.)
       GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar and GAM propose the Company use either a 4-CP or 6-CP
production cost allocation methodology, arguing that as a summer peaking utility, the four summer
months June through September drive the Company’s peak demand and should be the basis for
allocating the Company’s production costs. (Tr. 3215; Tr. 3217; Tr. 3230.) In reality, the
generation planning for Georgia Power’s system is driven and influenced by non-summer periods
in addition to summer peaks. Georgia Power utilizes Expected Unserved Energy (“EUE”) in its
determination of the optimal reserve margin for both summer and winter seasons. A pure summer
peak or 4-CP allocator is an oversimplification of the Company’s generation planning process.
Using a pure summer peak allocator could create unstable, unbalanced, and inequitable cost
allocation in which some rate groups receive little if any Production demand cost allocation
whatsoever. An example of this could be if the pure summer peak allocator for that year occurred
in daytime hours such that there was no subsequent cost allocation to outdoor lighting. The ongoing
capacity needs of Georgia Power are heavily driven by winter needs and to not recognize or
allocate costs based on winter loads would, therefore, be inappropriate. As such, the Commission
should reject Intervenor proposals to modify the production cost allocation methodology and
approve the Company’s continued use of a 12-CP methodology.

M.     The Commission Should Not Expand the Monthly Netting Pilot and Should Revise
       the RNR Tariff to Address the Negative Impacts to Non-Participating Customers.

       The Commission should reject Intervenor arguments in favor of expanding the monthly
netting pilot, even as a short-term interim measure to increase BTM solar penetration rates. As
explained by Company Witnesses Evans and Legg, the Company’s analysis identifies that the
monthly netting pilot results in a substantial additional cost shift to non-participating customers
above the baseline cost shift incurred by instantaneous netting under the RNR tariff. (Tr. 3719.)
This additional cost-shift—while currently contained to only a subset of 5,000 pilot customers—
will increase in both scope and severity were the Commission to adopt Intervenor
recommendations to expand the monthly netting pilot. Further, PIA Staff and Intervenor proposals

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 58 of 77

for valuing excess energy “pushed back” onto the grid by BTM resources significantly exceed the
value established by the Commission in the Renewable Cost Benefit (“RCB”) Framework, which
is appropriately based upon the Company’s avoided cost.
       In considering whether to expand the monthly netting pilot, the Commission should note
that many other legislatures and commissions have found monthly netting to be a deeply flawed
accounting construct that excessively compensates BTM solar resources. This excess
compensation—while a boon to the solar industry—results in real and quantifiable harm to other
customers. This harm increases exponentially at scale because as the amount of monthly netting
customers grow, all other customers must shoulder an ever-increasing burden of the costs avoided
by monthly netting customers. Conversely, instantaneous netting coupled with appropriate rate
structures, such as three-part rates, will ensure that RNR customers receive appropriate price
signals and compensation levels that encourage BTM solar development, while also preventing an
inevitable and progressively significant cost shift that occurs under monthly netting.
       While the Company’s data suggests that the monthly netting pilot increased the rate of
BTM solar adoption on the Company’s system, this increase in BTM adoption resulted from an
excessive subsidy that creates considerable additional expense for non-participating customers.
Furthermore, since the closure of the monthly netting pilot to new customers, the Company has
continued to receive new BTM applications at a rate close to that under the monthly netting pilot.
With this trajectory in mind, and given the boost to the BTM solar market provided by the recently-
enacted federal Inflation Reduction Act (“IRA”) and other market trends decreasing the costs to
install BTM resources, further subsidization of the BTM market through expansion of the monthly
netting pilot is unnecessary and inappropriate.

       1.      The Company’s analysis confirms that monthly netting creates a disproportionate
               cost shift that increases rates for non-participating customers.

       Monthly netting does not adhere to fundamental rate design and cost-causation principles
and presents two substantial flaws: (1) it compensates BTM customers at the Company’s retail rate
– which is much higher than the price the Company otherwise would pay for energy; and (2) it
enables BTM customers to avoid paying their share of the costs to construct, operate and maintain
the Company’s electric system. This excess payment structure results in significant cross-subsidies
that shift costs to non-participating customers.

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 59 of 77

       As demonstrated by Company Witnesses Evans and Legg, a customer participating in the
monthly netting pilot shifts an additional $287 of costs onto non-participating customers on an
annual basis. (Tr. 5031.) When limited to the current subset of 5,000 pilot customers, the
incremental aggregated cost shift associated with monthly netting equals approximately $1.4
million annually. If expanded to between 30,000-75,000 customers, as recommended by
Intervenors, this cost shift would increase to approximately $8.6 million to $21.5 million per year.
Moreover, including the $826 annual cost shift that already results from RNR customers receiving
service under the Company’s volumetric-based R tariff, this aggregate cost shift increases even
further to approximately $24.8 million to $61.9 million per year. As the number of monthly netting
customers grow, the costs that non-participating customers must pay to subsidize them will also
grow. This increase compounds over time and has caused many states with aggressive monthly
netting policies to reconsider and rebalance the interests of participating and non-participating
customers in the interests of fairness underlying fundamental rate design and cost causation
principles.

               a. Monthly netting customers rely upon and utilize the Company’s system while
                  avoiding cost responsibility.

       By netting an RNR customer’s consumption of energy against the excess energy they
export to the grid, monthly netting enables an RNR customer to receive a zero kilowatt-hour
(“kWh”) bill during a billing period. On a simple volumetric rate such as the Company’s R rate,
an RNR customer with a net zero kWh bill pays only the Company’s basic service charge and
applicable riders. This is notwithstanding that a substantial portion of an RNR customer’s energy
was supplied and delivered exclusively by the Company’s system during the billing period. In this
way, monthly netting allows RNR customers to fully use and rely upon the Company’s electric
system while avoiding contributing to the costs of the system.
       As conceded by PIA Staff Witness Watkins during the hearing, RNR customers do not
actually reduce their electricity consumption. (Tr. 4413.) RNR customers merely supplement
Company-supplied energy with their own self-supplied generation during certain times of the day.
An RNR customer’s peak demand remains the same, with or without the BTM solar generation.
Because the Company is obligated to serve the peak demand of all of its customers, including RNR


                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 60 of 77

customers, the Company must plan for and be prepared to serve the RNR customer at all times,
including when their BTM generation is intermittent.
        Despite PIA Staff and Intervenor attempts to downplay this issue, the prevalence of net
zero bills for RNR customers is material. Indeed, PIA Staff’s Supplemental Direct Testimony
Table 3 reveals that nearly 11% of monthly netting bills in 2021 resulted in net zero bills. (Tr.
4362, 4419.) Likewise, Table 4 of PIA Staff’s Supplemental Direct Testimony similarly
demonstrates that nearly 40% of RNR customers experience at least one month with a net zero
bill. (Tr. 4363, 4419-20.) PIA Staff Witness Watkins counters that the excess energy contributed
by the BTM solar generation fully compensates for the energy consumed by the RNR customer
during the billing period. (Tr. 4415.) PIA Staff’s argument, however, relies upon the assumption
that the value of the customer’s excess energy and the value of energy delivered by the Company’s
system are equal, which they are not. As testified by Company Witnesses Evans, Legg and Gattie
(Tr. 3727-28, 3845), these values are not equal, and this false equivalence between imported and
exported energy under monthly netting creates the cost shift revealed in the Company’s analysis
and acknowledged in other jurisdictions.

                b. The Company’s retail rate includes components that are not related to the cost
                   of energy.

        By netting their consumption against excess generation, RNR customers receive
compensation for their excess energy at the full volumetric portion of the Company’s retail rate.
The Company’s Commission-approved retail rate, however, recovers costs in addition to energy
costs, including costs for transmission, distribution and other services included in the Company’s
cost of service. (Tr. 3726.) As evidenced by the multiple witnesses testifying on all of the cost of
service issues in this proceeding, the Company’s retail rates are inclusive of its total cost of service.
This includes costs associated with the Company’s other reasonable and cost-eligible expenses
and investments required to provide safe and reliable electric service.
        By contrast, the excess energy pushed back by RNR customers does not provide all these
services, yet under monthly netting these customers receive compensation at the full volumetric
portion of the retail rate. Unlike the Company’s electric service, which provides all the services
required to generate, transmit, and distribute energy to customers, BTM solar generators provide
only marginal energy, which is why under the Commission-approved RCB Framework, this

                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 61 of 77

energy is appropriately valued at the utility’s avoided costs—the monetary value the Company
saves by not having to generate this energy. Monthly netting is not part of the Company’s revenue
requirement calculation but is rather part of cost allocation and rate design. As such, it must be
evaluated in accordance with cost causation and rate design principles, which inform how the
Company’s full revenue requirement should be recovered from customers. Multi-part rate designs
are intended to ensure that RNR customers take service on a rate that is more reflective of their
actual cost of service, thereby mitigating the cost shift to non-participants. Further, although PIA
Staff argues that the Commission should ignore any cost shift because current rate design is not at
parity, this does not provide a reasonable basis for further subsidizing BTM customers at the
expense of all other customers. (Tr. 4358.) The goal of rate design should always be to reflect
actual cost causation as closely as possible.
       In lieu of recommending expanding the monthly netting pilot, PIA Staff lays out a range
of possible compensation rates to be applied to the Company’s current instantaneous netting
customers. This range varies from approximately 6.99 cents to over 13 cents. Compensating BTM
solar resources at such high levels, which are well beyond the Company’s avoided costs, will
further increase the cost shift to non-participating customers under the RNR tariff. As far as the
Company is aware, these values provided by PIA Staff are not credibly tied to any quantification
of actual benefit provided by the BTM resource, but rather represent a range of figures adopted
from policy decisions made in other jurisdictions. This range of compensation rates is too arbitrary
a set of variables to appropriately inform the level of compensation for BTM resources. The
Company, PIA Staff, and several Intervenors have already undergone this analysis, in integrating
BTM resources into the Company’s RCB Framework. Rather than selecting an arbitrary
compensation rate with the primary goal of incentivizing BTM resources, the Commission should
adopt a default RNR tariff rate design structure which better reflects the cost to serve customers,
such as the Company’s TOU-RD rate. This rate is justifiable, cost-based, and is an appropriate
step toward addressing the disproportionate cost shift associated with net metering and adopting a
BTM solar program that fairly compensates BTM customers while protecting non-participating
customers.


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 62 of 77

        2.     The Commission should not deviate from its deliberative and measured approach
               towards incentivizing renewable energy resources.

        PIA Staff and Intervenors point narrowly to Georgia’s BTM solar penetration rate as an
indictment of Georgia’s renewable energy policy, asserting that Georgia’s BTM solar penetration
ranking alone infers a need for this Commission to act and expand the monthly netting pilot. (Tr.
3876-3877, 4349-4350, 4383.) Relying on BTM penetration rates as the primary measure of
success, however, discounts Georgia’s considerable achievement in cost-effectively expanding
renewable energy within Georgia without jeopardizing affordability for customers. As testified by
Company Witness Gattie, this Commission’s deliberate and measured renewable energy policy
has enabled the state to deploy considerable amounts of solar generation while simultaneously
retaining residential rates below the national average. (Tr. 3836-3839, 3847.) Through its
thoughtful energy policy, this Commission has produced meaningful results for customers. The
Commission should consider this record of accomplishment and continue the successful path it
has created here in Georgia.
        By holding BTM penetration rates as the sole measure of success, PIA Staff and
Intervenors fail to consider that many states with higher BTM solar penetration rates have
prioritized the development of BTM resources to the detriment of important considerations such
as customer cost and system reliability. As explained by Company Witness Gattie, many states
with high penetration rates of BTM solar have experienced significant negative impacts such as
reduced system reliability, increased cost, and ratepayer fairness issues. Likewise, as noted
previously, given overall market trends, Georgia is likely to see expanded BTM solar penetration,
without additional subsidies or Commission action.
        First, notwithstanding that the monthly netting pilot reaching its full 5,000 customer limit,
the Company continues to receive only slightly fewer BTM solar applications each month in 2022
than received each month in 2020 before the pilot was launched. (Tr. 3716.) This level of growth
post-pilot,   without   expanded     monthly     netting    incentives,      supports   the   Company’s
recommendations that this Commission need not introduce an additional subsidy for BTM
resources. PIA Staff and Interveners have not provided credible evidence that the lack of monthly
netting will significantly hamper growth rates. Such arguments also falsely cite to the existence of
monthly netting as the sole variable limiting prior growth in BTM solar under the Company’s


                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 63 of 77

instantaneous netting program. These arguments ignore the significant reduction in the availability
and cost for BTM solar facilities in the last few years and the growth of a robust solar installation
industry—all of which have increased the availability and cost-competitiveness of BTM solar.
        According to public information made available by SEIA, “[t]he cost to install solar has
dropped by more than 60% over the last decade . . . [and] . . . has experienced an average annual
growth rate of 33% . . .[t]hanks to strong federal policies like the solar Investment Tax Credit,
rapidly declining costs, and increasing demand across the private and public sector for clean
electricity . . .”7 Also according to SEIA, and as noted in the below chart that identifies solar
penetrations by state, Georgia’s level of solar deployment is on pace with other states such as New

        Second, the recently passed federal IRA will significantly increase development in

downplay the benefits of the IRA. (Tr. 4403-4404, 4848-4853.) Despite characterizing as
speculative and negligible Company Witness Gattie’s conclusions regarding the wide-ranging

benefits of the IRA and the IRA’s likely impact on the solar market, GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar

          By GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar’s own account, the IRA will have a monumental impact

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company

arguments that the IRA will not be enough incentive for Georgia’s BTM solar market to grow is

suggest that the IRA will significantly increase BTM solar development in Georgia. Expansion of
the monthly netting pilot risks providing a too generous and unneeded subsidy to BTM solar at the

       Finally, SACE Witness Hill proposed the strawman argument that Georgia could miss out
on IRA tax benefits if the Commission did not expand monthly netting. (Tr. 4448, 4484-85.) This

monthly netting before availing themselves of the tax breaks under the IRA, and SACE has
provided no evidence supporting this statement. Not a single Georgia customer will miss out on
any tax credits under the IRA if they choose to install BTM solar. Georgia will have the same
access to funds under the IRA as every other state. The IRA exists to provide financial incentives
to private investment to achieve a specific policy goal and exists without regard to the availability
of monthly netting.

       3.      The Commission should reject PIA Staff and Intervenor recommendations to
               recognize benefits not included in the Company’s Renewable Cost Benefit
               Framework.

       The Company has provided an exhaustive cost of service study in this case that includes
the costs to serve all existing customers, including customers receiving service on the RNR tariff.
Contrary to PIA Staff and Intervenor assertions, the Company considered all relevant, credible and
quantifiable benefits provided by BTM solar in its analysis.
       The Company has determined that approximately $21 in system benefits per customer
accrue per year because of a customer’s BTM solar generating facility. Despite the Company’s
quantification of these benefits, PIA Staff and Intervenors recommend the Commission recognize
and include additional benefits not included in the Company’s analysis, such as economic
development, environmental and other externalities that are not included in the Company’s cost of

                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 66 of 77

service. For instance, SACE Witness Brown testifies that the Company should compensate RNR
customers not only for excess energy, but for things such as lowering reliance on fossil fuels and
expanded employment opportunities. (Tr. 4892.) As this Commission has recognized many times
before, compensation for export energy should not include externalities that are not otherwise
included in the Company’s costs of service.
        From as early as 1923 with the Bluefield decision, the United States Supreme Court
defined the guidelines by which a regulator sets rates for a private utility. Bluefield Water Works
& Improvement Co. v. Public Service Commission of West Virginia, 262 U.S. 679 (1923). At its
most basic, rates must be set to recover the utility’s reasonable costs and earn a reasonable return.
In other words, establishing rates relies on the utility’s quantifiable amount of prudent investment
and reasonable costs. Consistent with 100 years of law governing cost of service ratemaking, the
Commission sets rates based on quantifiable costs associated with electric service and not indirect
externalities.
        Lawmakers and regulators can and do make policy decisions based on potential public
benefits. But these policy decisions should not undermine the fundamental cost of service
framework underpinning the way rates are established. Nor should these policy decisions create
negative impacts for the Company’s other customers. If the Commission chooses to further
incentivize the penetration of BTM solar because of its intangible environmental and economic
benefits, that decision must be grounded in a knowledge of the costs such a choice imposes on the
Company and on the non-participating customers that will ultimately bear those costs.

        4.       Other states are rejecting monthly netting due to significant cost shifts and non-
                 participating customer impacts.

        Many legislatures and commissions that have recognized the negative externalities
associated with monthly netting are now returning to avoided cost based methodologies as a
replacement for monthly netting. As detailed by Company Witness Gattie, over the past several
years, many states have reconsidered their net metering policies, primarily because monthly retail
net metering overcompensates BTM solar resources at the expense of non-participating customers.
(Tr. 3822-46.) Whether on the legislative or regulatory level, these states have targeted the very
cost shift that the Company has highlighted in this case and changed their policies to mitigate the
negative impacts caused by monthly netting. During the hearing, PIA Staff Witness Watkins

                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 67 of 77

acknowledged this point, agreeing that the “. . . cost shift was certainly an element in each of those
discussions . . .” (Tr. 4407.) While these states have employed different ways of describing this
cost shift, they generally agree that it arises directly from compensating monthly netting customers
at a rate equal to the retail rate for electricity, which overstates the actual value these resources
provide to the grid and pushes fixed cost recovery onto other customers.
        A poignant recent example can be found in California, where the California Public Service
Commission (“CPUC”) is reconsidering its existing net metering tariff in Docket No. R.20-08-020
(“NEM 3.0 Proceeding”).9 Much like the monthly netting pilot in Georgia, California’s existing
net metering policy provides customer-generators full retail rate credits for energy exported to the
grid and requires them to pay a few charges that align NEM customer costs more closely with non-
NEM customer costs. On November 10, 2022, the presiding administrative law judge issued a
proposed decision recommending a move away from retail net metering. (Georgia Power Exhibit
43.) In doing so, the decision relies heavily on evidence that full retail net metering imposes a
markedly unjust cost shift that requires remediation. As the CPUC notes, “[t]his cost shift is created
by the ability of distributed generation customers to avoid fixed costs, including grid costs and
public purpose program costs, which then become the responsibility of non-participating
ratepayers, including low-income customers.” (Id. at 4.) Moreover, the decision notes that this cost
shift ultimately undermines the clean energy goals, including electrification: “[b]ut a cost shift
exists, and continuation of the cost shift feeds into higher electricity rates, which discourage
electrification.” (Id. at 90.) It thus concludes, “[a]ccordingly, the successor tariff should address
the cost shift not only to ensure equity but also to encourage electrification to ensure California
can meet its climate and clean energy objectives.” (Id.)
        In its decision, the CPUC was especially concerned with the effect that such cost shifts had
on low-income non-participating customers:

        The Commission agrees that the look-back study indicates that NEM 2.0
        disproportionately harms low-income customers not participating in the net energy
        metering tariff. Findings in the look-back study show that NEM 2.0 and, thus, NEM
        1.0 disproportionally benefited non-CARE residential net energy metering
        customers while all customers, including those with lower incomes, must bear the

9
    See California Public Utilities Commission, Net Energy Metering, NEM Overview,
    https://www.cpuc.ca.gov/industries-and-topics/electrical-energy/demand-side-management/net-energy-metering

                                Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                              Docket No. 44280
                                                Page 68 of 77

         addition of the 82 to 91 percent of the cost of service bypass by net energy metering
         customers. The Commission finds the look-back study indicates that NEM 2.0
         disproportionately harms low-income customers not for participating in the net
         energy meeting tariff.

         Indeed, when presented with this evidence from California during his November 29, 2022
hearing testimony, Intervenor Georgia Coalition for Local Governments Witness Hill, while
distinguishing the level of solar penetration in California from Georgia, conceded that cost-shifting
was an issue which Georgia would eventually have to address concerning its net metering program.
(Tr. 4508.) (“This -- the level of cost shifting and dealing with monthly net metering at some point,
I don’t dispute that that’s a good idea.”). Moreover, he sensibly admitted that Georgia could and
should draw lessons from the policy decisions of other jurisdictions, such as California:

         Q And I assume, Dr. Hill, that you don’t oppose learning from lessons learned in
         some other jurisdictions; correct?

         A No. Absolutely.

         (Tr. 4511.)

         While California is perhaps the most notable and recent state to reverse course on monthly
netting, California is not the only state coming to this conclusion about the cost shifts inherent in
retail net metering. In January 2017, in Decision No. 75859, the Arizona Corporation Commission
(“ACC”) eliminated retail net metering within the state.10 In so doing, it similarly relied on
demonstrable evidence that retail net metering inflicted costs on non-participating customers. The
Commission noted, “[w]hile we refrain from commenting on the appropriateness of any particular
rate design as part of this proceeding, the Commission is committed to modifying residential rate
design in a manner that mitigates the recognized cost shift caused by rooftop solar customers’ self-
consumption.”11 Similarly, the Louisiana Public Service Commission, in its Order dispensing with
retail net metering, endorsed its staff’s analysis that, “[t]o the extent that the solar customers are


10
     Opinion and Order, In the Matter of the Commission’s Investigation of Value and Cost of Distributed
     Generation, Arizona Corporation Commission, Docket No. E-00000J-14-0023 (Jan. 3, 2017).
11
     Id. at 175.

                                  Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                                Docket No. 44280
                                                  Page 69 of 77

paid any more than avoided costs for the energy they produce and sell to the utility, all of the non-
solar customers must make up the difference.”12
         To remedy this cost shift, many of these states have elected to replace net metering with a
compensation regime based on avoided-cost methodologies. In Indiana, Gov. Eric Holcomb signed
a bill (SA309) in 2017 that put the state on a path to phasing out retail net metering by 2047.13
Before that deadline, the bill allowed utilities to pay solar customers a new, lower rate – known as
the “excess distribution generation” (“EDG”) rate – when solar penetration reached 1.5% of their
summer peak load, or by July 2022.
         CenterPoint (previously known as Vectren), one of Indiana’s utilities, was the first to reach
this 1.5% benchmark and in 2021 filed a request to institute the EDG rate. CenterPoint also
proposed switching from monthly net metering to instantaneous netting, in which customers pay
the full retail rate for all power used from the grid, and all solar power sent back to the grid is paid
at the much lower EDG rate. The Indiana Utility Regulatory Commission (“IURC”) approved
CenterPoint’s full request in the spring of 2021. Indiana’s other predominant utilities, including
NIPSCO, AES, Indiana Michigan (“I&M”) Power, and Duke Energy, have followed suit in
requesting the same instantaneous netting arrangement, and the IURC has approved their requests.
         In the 2020 South Carolina Public Service Commission (“South Carolina PSC”)
proceeding in which Duke Energy Carolinas proposed a successor tariff to its monthly netting
program, Duke estimated a cost shift of $35-$40 per month per monthly netting solar customer.
The customer participation rate for monthly netting in South Carolina was 1.4% in 2021 when the
South Carolina PSC approved a settlement agreement (to which Vote Solar was a signatory) to
reform the NEM tariff and to greatly reduce the cost shift.14

12
     See also In Re: Review of Policies Related to Customer-Owned Solar Generation and Possible Modification of
     the Commission’s Current Net Metering Rules, Louisiana Public Service Commission, Docket No. R-33929
     (Sept. 11, 2019).

     See Ind. Code §§ 8-1-40-1 et. seq. (2022).
