     (transitioning to permanent net metering tariffs using strategic time of use rates, a monthly grid access fee, a $30
     minimum monthly bill, and monthly netting with payments at the avoided cost rate); See also, N.C. Gen. Stat. §
     62-126.4(b) (2022) (requiring the North Carolina Utilities Commission to establish net metering rates under all
     tariff designs that ensures that the net metering retail customers pays its full fixed cost of service, which may
     include fixed monthly energy and demand charges).


                                    Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                                  Docket No. 44280
                                                    Page 70 of 77

        Arizona has adopted a similar path. Since the aforementioned ACC decision eliminating
the state’s retail net metering program, the state has implemented net billing, under which utilities
purchase excess solar at an “excess generation credit rate” that is worth less than the retail value
of electricity. As with Indiana, this program employs instantaneous netting as well. Further, the
ACC determined that rooftop solar customers are partial requirements customers who export
power to the grid and may be separated into their own rate class for the purposes of assessing
additional customer charges for net metering.
        Intervenors have offered testimony noting that many states have not eliminated retail net
metering, or they have adopted a variety of export compensation structures that retain elements of
monthly netting. (Tr. 4350-4351, 4383-4384, 4397-4398.) But this testimony misses the point.
Other states may have other programs, but all have acknowledged monthly netting is an
inappropriate structure and the least-optimal option. Monthly netting was primarily enacted
decades ago as a crude and easily administrable accounting method to compensate distributed solar
resources. Notably, monthly netting was not based on any economic foundation as to how these
resources should be fairly compensated. This has resulted in many states re-evaluating long
standing net metering policies and attempting to quantify the “value” such resources provide. In
considering whether to expand monthly netting, the Commission should take the lessons learned
in other states and prevent similar problems from occurring here in Georgia.

        5.     The Cogen Act prohibits a compensation rate for BTM resources in excess of
               avoided cost.

        Notwithstanding the cost shift and customer fairness issues associated with monthly netting
described above, Georgia law prohibits the Commission from adopting a compensation rate for
RNR customers that exceeds the Company’s avoided cost. Unlike other jurisdictions referenced
above, Georgia has not legislatively mandated or approved monthly netting. The Georgia
Cogeneration and Distributed Generation Act of 2001 (“Cogen Act”), which governs the rates and
structure to be adopted under the RNR tariff, plainly states that the Company is not required to
purchase BTM generation at a rate that exceeds the Company’s avoided cost. Indeed, while the
Cogen Act clearly establishes an avoided cost cap to BTM compensation, the Cogen Act does not
mandate or require the Company to credit BTM generation at the Company’s retail rate for
electricity.

                             Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                           Docket No. 44280
                                             Page 71 of 77

       The Cogen Act requires that the Company purchase excess energy from customers with
BTM generation, like those served under the RNR tariff, until the aggregate demand of customer-
owned generation exceeds 0.2 percent of the Company’s annual peak demand. The Cogen Act
provides that—for instances where a customer’s generation exceeds the customer’s demand—the
Company must pay for excess kilowatt-hours at an “agreed to rate” and may levy certain fixed
customer charges to recover costs associated with serving BTM generation. As stated in the Cogen
Act:

               (1)(C) When electricity generated by the customer’s distributed generation
               system exceeds the electricity supplied by the electric service provider, the
               customer generator:

                       (i)     Shall be billed for the appropriate customer charges for that
                               billing period; and

                       (ii)    Shall be credited for the excess kilowatt-hours generated
                               during the billing period at an agreed to rate as filed with
                               the commission, with this kilowatt-hour credit appearing on
                               the bill for the billing period; or

               O.C.G.A. § 46-3-55(1)(C) (emphasis added.)

       First, the Cogen Act’s reference to an “agreed to rate approved by the Commission” makes
clear the legislature intended for the Commission to establish an RNR-specific rate different from
the Company’s retail rate. Had the Cogen Act simply intended for the Company to compensate
BTM generation at the retail rate, the Cogen Act would not refer to this additional “agreed to rate”
for BTM resources. Second, the Cogen Act further states that the “agreed to rate” the Company
must pay to BTM generation shall not exceed the avoided cost rate, unless such generation is
subscribed under a renewable energy program. Even in this limited exception, however, the Cogen
Act only requires the Company to pay a rate equal to the cost of energy:

               (a) An electric service provider will only be required to purchase energy as
               specified in Code Section 46-3-55 from an eligible customer generator on a
               first-come, first-served basis until the cumulative generating capacity of all
               renewable energy sources equals 0.2 percent of the utility’s annual peak
               demand in the previous year; provided, however, that no electric service
               provider will be required to purchase such energy at a price above avoided


                              Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                            Docket No. 44280
                                              Page 72 of 77

                 energy cost unless that amount of energy has been subscribed under any
                 renewable energy program.

                 (b) Once the capacity is subscribed, an electric service provider may
                 purchase energy from an eligible customer generator at a cost of energy as
                 defined for a utility by the commission, in the case of an electric utility, or
                 by the appropriate governing body, in the case of any other electric service
                 provider or electric supplier.

                 O.C.G.A. § 46-3-56 (emphasis added.)

The Company’s “cost of energy” for BTM solar is equal to the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy
Cost rate—the rate currently provided to BTM instantaneous netting customers under the RNR
tariff.15 Neither the Cogen Act, nor Georgia public utility law generally, define a “renewable
energy program” nor do they lay out the necessary steps for such energy to be “subscribed.” A
reasonable interpretation is that the phrase “unless that amount of energy has been subscribed
under any renewable energy program” means that the Commission can establish a program
whereby one or more customers who desire to purchase BTM-generated energy at a higher rate
can do so—“subscribe”—with the purpose of increasing compensation to BTM generators. Such
a program, properly designed, would further comport with the text of the Cogen Act because it
would not shift the costs of the higher compensation rate to non-participating customers. But even
with this generous interpretation, the Company still would not be required under the Cogen Act to
purchase such energy from BTM sources at a rate that exceeds the cost of energy.
         The Cogen Act’s deliberate use of the term “energy” makes clear an intent to capture only
the energy portion of the customer’s electric bill and exclude compensation for transmission and
distribution and other services—all of which are components of the Company’s retail electric rate.

As the testimony of Company Witnesses Legg and Evans demonstrates, the Company’s limited
implementation of monthly netting results in significant cost shifts between monthly netting
customers and non-participants. Rather than expanding monthly netting, the Cogen Act requires
the Company to compensate customer generation at the avoided cost rate (absent Company
agreement or a subscription program). To be clear, this is precisely the Company’s existing


15
     Per the BTM Joint Recommendation filed at and approved by the Commission in June 2017, the Renewable Cost
     Benefit Framework is applied to the Solar Avoided Energy Cost value.

                                 Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                               Docket No. 44280
                                                 Page 73 of 77

instantaneous netting structure under the RNR tariff, first established by the Commission shortly
after the legislature first enacted the Cogen Act over twenty years ago, which should remain the
structure moving forward.

        6.      The Commission should adopt the Company’s RNR tariff recommendations to
                address the cost shift and consumer protection concerns.

                a. The Commission should implement a default three part rate for RNR customers
                   to mitigate the ongoing cost shift and incorrect price signals.

        Due to the disproportionate cost shift presented under the Company’s current RNR tariff,
the Company proposes requiring all customers who enroll in RNR on or after January 1, 2023 to
take service under a three-part rate, such as TOU-RD. Three part rates, connect cost drivers with
cost recovery and ensure that RNR customers take service on a rate that is closer to their actual
cost of service, thereby lowering the disproportionate cost shift to non-participating customers.
Adopting a rate design structure that better reflects the cost to serve RNR customers is the first
step on the path toward addressing the cost shift and adopting a BTM solar program protective of
non-participating customers.
        Notably, monthly netting undermines fundamental principles of cost causation and rate
design by artificially elevating the value of excess energy to the level of the Company’s total cost
of service to generate electricity, deliver electricity across its system, and provide the infrastructure
for a safe and reliable system. These are sunk costs, which are recovered over time and reflect
substantial investments that provide a real value to customers. The Company cannot simply reduce
its distribution and transmission costs that are necessary to serve its customers and they are
appropriately recovered in rates as a component of the cost of electric service. If a rate structure
requires the Company to over-compensate RNR customers for their excess energy, this subsidy
necessarily shifts the responsibility for paying these customers’ share of system costs to non-
participating customers. In summary, the current RNR tariff’s cost shift, which all parties agree
exists, , is caused by RNR customers’ ability to bypass virtually all volumetric rates. This cost shift
is mitigated with a three-part rate that is more reflective of the Company’s actual cost to serve
RNR customers.
        PIA Staff and Intervenors primary concerns with the TOU-RD rate is that the rate lowers
compensation for BTM solar. PIA Staff and Intervenors concerns are related to what compensation

                               Post Hearing Brief of Georgia Power Company
                                             Docket No. 44280
                                               Page 74 of 77

levels BTM solar customers receive and not whether the rate is fair, cost-based and non-harmful
to non-participating customers. As pointed out during the hearing, Commissioner Shaw identified
that without a time varying or demand-based rate structure, a monthly netting customer is under
no incentive to consume self-generated power at peak times, or to install energy storage or modify
behavior to maximize the alleged benefits of self-generated power. (Tr. 4854.) Under the
Company’s proposal to adopt the TOU-RD rate for RNR customers, this issue would be addressed,
as the TOU-RD rate sends the appropriate price signals to customers. With monthly netting, this
price signal is not sent, because excess energy if fully compensated at the retail rate.

               b. Continued growth in the solar BTM market requires additional consumer
                  protections.

       Another unfortunate externality of expanding BTM solar resources has been an increasing
number of unethical business practices employed by some solar marketers, lead generators, solar
installers, and solar loan providers offering services to Georgia Power’s BTM customers.
Increasing the incentives for BTM resources without additional oversight of BTM service
providers invites potentially more unqualified actors and additional customer complaints.
Therefore, in addition to its recommendations that the Commission reject expansion of the monthly
netting pilot, the Company proposes the development of a formal framework for the Commission’s
referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney
General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading business practices in the
marketplace for BTM generation resources.

                                      V.      CONCLUSION

       For all the above reasons, the Commission should approve Georgia Power Company’s
2022 Rate Case as filed by the Company.


       Respectfully submitted, this 8th day of December 2022.

BY THE COMMISSION:

I.     GEORGIA POWER COMPANY’S 2022 RATE CASE

        On June 24, 2022, Georgia Power Company (the “Company” or “Georgia Power”) filed
this rate case pursuant to the terms and conditions of the Georgia Public Service Commission’s
(the “Commission”) February 6, 2020 Order Adopting Settlement Agreement in Georgia Power’s
2019 Rate Case in Docket No. 42516 (“2019 Rate Case Final Order”). In the 2019 Rate Case Final

                                            Page 3

Order, the Commission provided Georgia Power with specific instructions for its next filing as
follows:


               The Commission finds that a three-year term for the Settlement Agreement
               as Modified ending December 31, 2022 is reasonable. By July 1, 2022, the
               Company shall file testimony and exhibits required in a general rate case
               along with supporting schedules required by the Commission to support a
               “traditional” rate case. The test period utilized by the Company in its rate
               case filing shall be from August 1, 2022 to July 31, 2023. The Company
               may propose to continue, modify or discontinue this Alternate Rate Plan.
               The Company shall also file projected revenue requirements for calendar
               years 2023, 2024, and 2025.


Docket No. 42516, Order Adopting Settlement Agreement.

        The Commission has general ratemaking jurisdiction over Georgia Power Company under
O.C.G.A. Ch. 2, T. 46. The Georgia Public Service Commission has general supervision over
electric light and power companies. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-20(a) and 46-2-21. The Commission has
“exclusive power to determine what are just and reasonable rates and charges to be made by any
person, firm, or corporation subject to its jurisdiction.” O.C.G.A. § 26-2-23; see also O.C.G.A.
§§ 46-1-1(5), 46-2-24, 46-2-25, 46-2-26.1, and 46-2-26.2.

         The Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing was made in compliance with the April 7, 2022
Procedural and Scheduling Order, as subsequently amended by the Commission’s July 26, 2022
Amended PSO, August 5, 2022 Second Amended PSO, and November 8, 2022 Third Amended
PSO (collectively as amended, the “PSO”). The PSO for the Company’s 2022 Rate Case identified
the procedures to be followed in this docket along with corresponding dates on which designated
events were set to occur with respect to the Company’s filing. In the body of the PSO, the
Commission, pursuant to O.C.G.A. § 46-2-25, suspended the subject matter of Georgia Power’s
filing for a period of five months ending January 1, 2023. In addition, the Commission determined
that the proceedings on the Company’s filing constituted complex litigation, as that term is defined
in O.C.G.A. § 9-11-33. The Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing included information responsive to
the Commission’s rule regarding Minimum Filing Requirements (“MFRs”); exhibits reflecting
Georgia Power’s cost of service study, sales and revenue forecast, depreciation rates, and cash
working capital; and the testimony and exhibits of Christopher C. Womack, the panel of Aaron P.
Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson, James M. Coyne, Lee
Evans, Steven M. Fetter, and Larry T. Legg. On August 22, 2022, Georgia Power filed a
supplemental filing to reflect the revenue requirement impacts from various items included in the
Final Order approved by the Commission in the Company’s 2022 Integrated Resource Plan
(“IRP”) and demand side management (“DSM”) Certification proceedings in Docket Nos. 44160
and 44161 (“Supplemental Filing”). On September 28, 2022, Georgia Power filed an errata to its
filing correcting the projected test period revenues for the Fixed Price Alternative (“FPA”) rate,
and on November 21, 2022, Georgia Power filed an errata to its supplemental direct testimony
correcting a formulaic error in its analysis of the cost shift associated with monthly netting
(together, the “Errata”).
                                               Page 4

        Georgia Power’s 2022 Rate Case filing, as amended by the Supplemental Filing and the
Errata, requests approval to continue the three-year alternate rate plan (“ARP”) structure and
requests rate increases of $852, $107 and $453 million to be effective January 1, 2023, 2024 and
2025, respectively. These proposed rate adjustment increases include a levelized adjustment for
traditional base rates, a levelized decrease of $1 million for the traditional costs of the ECCR tariff,
and annual increases to recover compliance costs associated with coal ash pond closure
requirements as well as the DSM costs approved in Docket No. 44161 and municipal franchise fee
(“MFF”) tariffs. Hearings on Georgia Power’s direct case in support of its filing were held
September 37, 2022, through September 29, 2022.

        In addition to the Commission’s Public Interest Advocacy (“PIA”) Staff, which has a
statutory right to participate in this proceeding, interventions were filed by a number of interested
parties. These interested parties included the Americans for Affordable Clean Energy (“AACE”);
ChargePoint, Inc.; the Commercial Group; Concerned Ratepayers of Georgia; Cypress Creek
Renewables, LLC; EVgo Services, LLC; Georgia Association of Manufacturers (“GAM”);
Georgia Coalition of Local Governments (the “Coalition”); Georgia Conservation Voters; Georgia
Interfaith Power & Light, Inc. (“GIPL”), the Georgia Solar Energy Association, Inc. (“GA Solar”);
Georgia Solar Energy Industries Association (“GSEIA”), Solar Energy Industries Association
(“SEIA”), and Vote Solar; Georgia Watch; Interstate Gas Supply, Inc.; The Kroger Company
(“Kroger”); Metropolitan Atlanta Rapid Transit Authority (“MARTA”); Resource Supply
Management (“RSM”); the Sierra Club; Southern Alliance for Clean Energy (“SACE”) and
Southface Energy Institute, Inc. (“Southface”); Southern Renewable Energy Association
(“SREA”); the U.S. Department of Defense (on behalf of all Other Federal Executive Agencies)
(“DOD”); and Utility Management Services.

        Thereafter, on October 20, 2022, PIA Staff and Intervenors filed testimony and exhibits
presenting their respective direct cases. With the exception of Concerned Ratepayers of Georgia,
Cypress Creek Renewables, LLC, Georgia Conservation Voters, GA Solar, Georgia Watch,
Interstate Gas Supply, Inc., Kroger, RSM, Sierra Club, and SREA, all other parties to this case
filed direct testimony in this proceeding. Also on October 20, 2022, and in accordance with the
PSO, Georgia Power filed the supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of Dr. David Gattie and
the panel of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Hearings on PIA Staff and Intervenors’ direct cases were
held November 8, 2022 through November 10, 2022, and hearings regarding the Company’s
supplemental direct testimony were held on November 10, 2022.

        On November 18, 2022, the Company filed the rebuttal testimony of the panel of Aaron P.
Abramovitz, Sarah P. Adams, Adam D. Houston, and Michael B. Robinson, James M. Coyne,
Steven M. Fetter, and the panel of Lee Evans and Larry T. Legg in response to the positions
advocated by PIA Staff and various Intervenors. Also on November 18, 2022, PIA Staff filed the
supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of the panel of Jamie Barber, Timothy Cook, and Glenn
A. Watkins; the Coalition filed the direct testimony David G. Hill, Ph.D.; GIPL filed supplemental
direct testimony of Justin Barnes; GA Solar filed the supplemental direct testimony of Kenneth
Sercy; GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar filed supplemental direct testimony and exhibits of the panel
of Kevin Lucas, Thatcher Young, and Will Giese; and SACE and Southface filed the direct
testimony and exhibits of Dr. Marilyn A. Brown and Alden M. Hathaway. On November 23, 2022,
Georgia Power filed the supplemental rebuttal testimony of Lee Evans and Larry Legg. Hearings
regarding the Company’s rebuttal testimony, PIA Staff and Intervenor supplemental direct, and
                                                Page 5

the Company’s supplemental rebuttal testimony were held November 29, 2022 through November
30, 2022.

       At each phase of the evidentiary hearings in this case, the Commission also heard from
numerous public witnesses who expressed their views on the Company’s application, either
individually or on behalf of specific groups.


II.    FINDINGS OF FACT AND CONCLUSIONS OF LAW


A.     ROE, Capital Structure, Cost of Debt, and Financial Integrity

        To ensure that the competing interests of all parties were properly considered, the Commission
has carefully analyzed all evidence of record including the testimony given and the various exhibits
entered by all the parties. As set forth hereinafter, the Commission makes findings of fact and
conclusions of law based on the evidentiary record created.

       1.      Commission Determination Regarding Return on Equity (“ROE”).

       The difference between the respective ROE recommendations of Georgia Power and PIA
Staff were significant. Georgia Power requested an ROE of 11.0%; whereas PIA Staff recommended
an ROE of 9.45%, GAM recommended an ROE of no higher than 9.83%, GIPL recommended an
ROE of 5.54%, and DOD recommended an ROE of 9.47%.

        Georgia Power Witness Coyne relies on the following widely accepted models to develop his
ROE determination: the Discounted Cash Flow (“DCF”) model, Capital Asset Pricing Model
(“CAPM”), Bond Yield Risk Premium model, and the Expected Earnings models for a comparable
utility group. Witness Coyne also considers the general economic and capital market
environments, and the influence that capital market conditions exert over the results of the DCF,
CAPM, and Risk Premium models in arriving at his recommended ROE. Based on that analysis.
Witness Coyne estimates the cost of equity to run from 8.99% to 13.55%, with a four-model
average of 10.92%. After including an adjustment for flotation costs, Witness Coyne arrives at his
recommended allowed ROE of 11.00% in this proceeding.

        DOD Witness Reno recommends an ROE of 9.47% with a rate of return of 6.85%, an
embedded cost of long-term debt of 3.53%, and an assumed capital structure comprised of 44.18%
long-term debt and 53.00% common equity. Ms. Reno bases her ROE recommendation on the
higher end of an 8.81-9.47% range derived from her mean estimates of market-based cost of equity
methodologies using a proxy group of comparable risk utilities. Witness Reno develops her ROE
recommendation using the variants of the Constant-Growth DCF model, the CAPM and Empirical
CAPM, and the Comparable Earnings Model (“CEM”).

      PIA Staff Witness Gorman uses the DCF Model with constant growth, sustainable growth,
and multi-stage growth rate estimates, the Risk-Premium Model, and the CAPM to estimate the
Company’s recommended ROE at 9.45%. Witness Gorman recommends that the Commission reject
Witness Coyne’s recommended ROE of 11.0% as excessive.

                                                Page 6

      GAM Witness LaConte recommends a ROE of 9.83%. Witness LaConte bases her
recommendation on Witness Coyne’s analysis, adjusted to remove flotation costs, include the 180-
day DCF analysis, and to consider historical market risk premiums in CAPM.

      GIPL Witness Ellis recommends a ROE of 5.54%. Witness Ellis uses the DCF and CAPM
models, however, he uses drastically different input models that have not been adopted by this
Commission and to do so now would be unprecedented.

        As in every rate case, the Commission understands its obligation to provide a fair rate of return
to the regulated utility. A fair rate of return is normally interpreted to mean that an efficient and
economically managed utility will be able to maintain its financial integrity, attract capital, and have
an opportunity to earn comparable returns to those of similar risk investments. These concepts are
derived from economic and financial theory and are generally implemented using financial models
and economic concepts. In determining a fair rate of return in the present case, the Commission is
mindful of the standards established by the United States Supreme Court in Bluefield Water Works
and Improvement Co. v. Public Serv. Comm’n of West Virginia, 262 U.S. 679 (1923), and Federal
Power Comm’n v. Hope Natural Gas Co., 320 U.S. 591 (1942).

        Consistent with the Bluefield and Hope decisions, the Commission has considered
comparable earnings, financial integrity, and capital attraction in reaching the decision that the 11.0%
ROE recommended by Witness Coyne is just and reasonable. An 11.0 % ROE is a fair, forward
looking rate of return on equity that provides the Company with an opportunity to earn a return on
its investment over the period in which rates are in effect commensurate with returns that investors
expect to earn on other investments of similar risk. Accordingly, the Commission finds and
concludes that the ROE proposed by Witness Coyne’s recommendation will allow the Company to
continue accessing capital markets at competitive rates and on reasonable terms.

        Witness Coyne’s testimony in this case supported his quantification and application of equity
flotation costs and a financial risk adjustment. PIA Staff Witness Gorman, DOD Witness Reno, and
GAM Witness LaConte presented no credible contrary analysis. Notwithstanding arguments against
including flotation costs, Witness Coyne calculates flotation costs based upon economic evidence.

        Witness Coyne’s use of market capital structure adjusted to correct for financial risk
differences between the proxy group’s market capital structure and the book regulatory capital
structure proposed for Georgia Power is appropriate. Witness Coyne’s adjustment is correct and
necessary to provide an overall cost of capital commensurate with market expectations. Neither
Witness Gorman nor Witness Reno contested his testimony that his financial risk adjustment reflected
universally accepted financial economic theory underlying estimations of the of the cost of capital.

        The recommendations of Witnesses Reno, Ellis, LaConte, and Gorman are unreasonably low
under the governing Hope and Bluefield standards, as was shown by the prospective comparable
earnings analysis presented by Witness Reno and Witness Coyne. Although every methodology for
estimating the cost of equity has its limitations, no witness presented a sufficient justification for the
Commission to depart from its long history of affording due weight to a prospective comparable
earnings assessment using the expected performance of a reasonable and representative proxy group
                                                 Page 7

of utilities. The Commission considered the fact that PIA Staff Witness Gorman ignored the
comparable earnings analysis, which has been one of the traditional cost of equity analyses employed
by prior PIA Staff witnesses and accepted by this Commission. Although Witness Reno conducted
a comparable earnings analysis, she provided no justification for ignoring the results.

         After careful consideration of the evidence presented by the parties, this Commission
concludes that a 9.45% or 9.83% ROE is unjustifiably low and could impede the Company’s ability
to attract and access capital at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. The corrected calculation of
the cash flows available to the Company after debt service further confirm that a ROE below 9.83%
on the existing capital structure is unreasonably low and likely would result in financial harm
detrimental to the interests of customers and the public. Through the ROE it awards, the Commission
has historically acknowledged the Company’s long-standing history of excellent performance and
customer service as being above industry average. Witnesses Gorman, Ellis, LaConte, and Reno
disregard this Commission’s well-established precedent and policy without explanation. In light of
the increased risk the Company assumes by agreeing to be bound by an ARP, this Commission has
included additional basis points in its awarded ROE. PIA Staff’s recommendations would result in at
least a 105 basis point reduction in ROE from the Company’s current authorized ROE, which ignores
and contradicts this Commission’s policy of gradualism. Therefore, the Commission approves a
11.0% ROE, as supported by Company Witness Coyne.

       2.      Commission Determination Regarding Capital Structure.

        Georgia Power proposed that a capital structure containing approximately 56% common
equity and 44% debt be used for ratemaking purposes in this proceeding. The Company maintains
that the proposed 11.0% ROE and 56% equity ratio will ensure that Georgia Power has access to
capital whenever necessary, provides the opportunity to earn a fair return for its investors, and does
so at rates favorable to customers. Witness Reno accepted the Company’s proposed capital
structure.

        Staff Witness Gorman agrees with the request 56% common equity structure for 2023.
Witness Gorman recommends that the Commission lower the Company’s equity ratio to 51%
common equity for 2024 and 2025 after Vogtle Units 3 and 4 are placed in-service, consistent with
the ratio approved in the 2013 Rate Case. According to Witness Gorman, his recommendations are
consistent with ratemaking capital structures for other similarly situated regulated utility companies
and are adequate to support the Company’s bond rating, financial integrity, and credit standing, but
at much lower cost to customers. Witness Gorman contends that Witness Coyne’s 56% common
equity ratio is excessive for a low-risk electric regulated utility and compared to the electric utility
industry average.

        GAM Witness LaConte testifies that the Company’s 56% common equity ratio is excessive
and unnecessary to maintain financial integrity. Witness LaConte testified that the average book
common equity ratio of Witness Coyne’s proxy group, adjusted to remove utilities credit ratings lower
than the Company’s, is 53% and recommends the Company’s common equity ratio be authorized at
a level no greater 53% to be more in line with the average of other vertically integrated electric
investor-owned utilities.


                                                Page 8

       GIPL Witness Ellis recommends an increase in the Company’s proposed common equity ratio
to 61.7%, which he justifies due to his drastically low recommended ROE of 5.54%. Witness Ellis
does not provide any examples of investor-owned utilities operating under a similar recommended
ROE and common-equity ratio structures.

        The Commission finds and concludes that adoption of PIA Staff and Intervenors’ capital
structure positions would contradict this Commission’s history of consistent and predictable
regulation and harm the Company’s financial integrity. The Commission understands that its
constructive regulation is a component of the credit rating agencies’ assessment of Georgia Power’s
credit profile because it determines the Company’s rate levels necessary to recover expenses such as
depreciation, O&M, storm damage cost recovery, and return on investment as well as the terms and
conditions of electric service. This Commission reviewed the Company’s equity ratio in light of the
Tax Cuts and Jobs Act (“TCJA”) less than five years ago and determined that an increase in equity
capitalization was necessary to counterbalance the negative effects of tax reform on the Company’s
cash flows. The impacts of the TCJA still exist; therefore, the Company argues it must maintain its
56% equity ratio as carefully reviewed and approved by the Commission in the 2019 Rate Case.
None of the capital structure recommendations proposed by PIA Staff or Intervenors adequately
reflect Georgia Power’s need for continued access to capital on reasonable terms and the
importance of maintaining its credit quality. Based on the evidence presented, the Commission
finds and concludes that the recommended capital structure of 56% common equity level is just and
reasonable to all parties considering all the evidence presented and is necessary to avoid a credit
rating downgrade.

        3.      Commission Determination Regarding Cost of Debt.

        Georgia Power recommended a 3.53% cost of debt be used for ratemaking purposes in this
proceeding. Only DOD Witness Reno challenges the Company’s proposed cost of long-term debt.
Witness Reno testifies that market and economic indicators do not support the increasing cost of debt
in the ARP. Instead, Witness Reno recommends that for purposes of the ARP, the Commission
require that the Company use the cost of long-term debt for the test year (3.53 %).

       The Commission finds that the Company’s proposed cost of debt is a fair and reasonable
approximation of the cost of debt during the three-year ARP period and should be approved.

        4.      Commission Determination Regarding Georgia Power Financial Integrity.

        In addition to setting rates that permit the Company to continue to provide clean, safe, reliable
and affordable electric service, the Commission is tasked with ensuring the financial integrity of the
Company in order for it to access capital at reasonable rates and on reasonable terms. The Company
presented testimony on the impacts of its proposed cost of capital and capital structure as well as the
impacts of PIA Staff’s proposals on the Company’s financial risk metrics used by the credit rating
agencies to determine credit quality. Although PIA Staff Witness Gorman calculated the FFO to Debt
for S&P, he erred in his calculations and underestimated the effect of his recommendations on the
Company’s financial health. When corrected, these errors reveal that PIA Staff’s proposals would
reduce the Company’s credit metrics to levels well below the very clearly communicated
expectations of the credit rating agencies, and that would likely trigger negative ratings actions
(Tr. 3950, 4184.) This degradation in financial health and credit quality would harm the
                                                 Page 9

Company’s customers. Notably, PIA Staff counsel did not question the Company’s calculations
or resulting impact of Witness Gorman’s errors during the hearings on the Company’s rebuttal
testimony.

        To evaluate how its recommendations would impact Georgia Power’s credit ratings, the
Company calculated its FFO to debt ratio, which is a financial metric used to assess risk based on the
Company’s ability to pay off debt using net operating income alone. (Tr. 799-800.) Mr. Gorman
acknowledges the importance of maintaining the Company’s FFO to debt (financial risk metrics)
above the credit rating agencies’ general benchmarks, and attempted to present his analysis with that
in mind. (Tr. 3531-33.) However, as the Company pointed out on rebuttal, Mr. Gorman’s FFO to debt
calculation did not impute debt in accordance with S&P’s credit metric methodology. This error
stemmed from confusion between the PIA Staff and the Company as to whether Georgia Power had
debt-like obligations including pension obligations, lease liabilities, and asset retirement obligations
and where such obligations are recorded. By excluding more than $5.5 billion of debt imputations,
Mr. Gorman’s credit metric expectation is overstated and fails to illustrate the harmful effects of his
recommended ROE and equity ratio. Had Mr. Gorman’s recalculation included the proper S&P debt
imputations, it would have shown that the Company’s metrics, assuming Staff’s adjustments were
accepted by the Commission, are insufficient to maintain sufficient access to capital at reasonable
rates. Due to this error and the assumptions serving as the basis for Mr. Gorman’s recommendations,
the Commission does not adopt PIA Staff’s proposed ROE and capital structure. The cumulative
adjustments recommended by PIA Staff would limit the FFO to debt metric to approximately 20%
for the foreseeable future without the opportunity for improvement.

         The Commission’s action in this proceeding should not impair or worsen the Company’s
credit quality or restrict the Company’s access to capital at reasonable terms and costs. The
Commission agrees with the Company that by setting rates based on a 11.0% ROE, 56% common
equity ratio, and 3.53% cost of debt is fair and reasonable and will not threaten the Company with a
credit rating downgrade.

B.     Alternate Rate Plan and Alternate Rate Plan Earnings Band

        Under Georgia law, the Commission may not “order” the Company to enter into an ARP
unless Georgia Power agrees to such an ARP. Without an agreement, the Commission must resolve
this case under traditional ratemaking standards, which involve a single test year analysis and
would eliminate the positive attributes of a three-year predictable plan such as the institution of
earnings ranges and earnings sharing mechanisms. O.C.G.A. §§ 46-2-25; 46-2-26.1. However, for
several rate case cycles, this Commission has approved, and the Company has agreed to, three-
year ARPs in lieu of traditional test year ratemaking. The Company recommends continuing the
current ARP structure approved by the Commission in the 2019 Rate Case. This would continue
the earnings sharing mechanism for earnings above the band and maintain the Interim Cost
Recovery mechanism for earnings projected to fall below the band. The Company proposed to
keep the current earnings band of 9.50% to 12.00% with the approved ROE as an approximate
midpoint.

       PIA Staff proposed an earnings band ranging from 8.45-10.45% with the 9.45% ROE set
point centered in the middle of the 200-point band. PIA Staff also proposed modifications to the

                                                Page 10

Company’s ARP to apply all earnings above the band to deferred costs rather than shared between
customers and the Company.

        Regarding the earnings band, DOD Witness Larry Blank recommends an ROE earnings
band of 100 basis points above the ROE if the Commission authorizes an ROE below the
Company’s requested 11%. In the alternative, Witness Blank reiterates his recommendation from
the 2019 Rate Case that the earnings band be narrowed to +/- 50 basis points above and below the
target ROE based on the assumption that it would reduce the risk to the Company. GSEIA
Witnesses Lucas and Young recommended the Commission reduce the approved band to a 100-
point band (+/- 50 basis points), with the ROE at the center, but testify that at minimum it should
be set at a 200-point band. Commercial Group Witness Chriss supports the Company’s band and
recommends that the Commission maintain the basis point spread around the band of the final
approved ROE.

       The Commission has resolved prior Georgia Power rate case proceedings through three-
year accounting orders as opposed to traditional rate case proceedings. Under this arrangement, all
stakeholders win as demonstrated by the Company’s consistent delivery of exceptional service
while keeping rates low in relation to other utilities. Maintaining the earnings band incentivizes
the Company to continue to innovate and find efficiencies in operations beyond those the Company
was able to identify since the last rate case. Customers have benefitted under this structure and the
Commission approves the continuation of these benefits for customers under the Company’s
proposed ARP. PIA Staff’s recommendation is effectively an earnings cap with the top end of the
band set artificially low at a level below even the Company’s cost of equity.

         Witness Blank’s recommendation to narrow the ROE earnings band to +/- 50 basis points
fails to recognize that narrowing the earnings band necessarily increases risk to customers.
Increasing the risk to customers alters the fundamental nature of the ARPs that have been in place
for the last 25 years. Georgia Power has proposed an earnings band around the proposed ROE that
is consistent with the structure of the earnings band previously approved by the Commission.

       Therefore, the Commission finds and concludes that since customers benefit under this
ARP structure, the Company’s three-year proposed ARP is fair and reasonable in light of all the
evidence presented. The Commission finds that it is reasonable to adopt the Company’s proposed
ARP and approve the current earnings band of 9.50% to 12.00%.

C.     Commission Determination on Recovery of CCR ARO Compliance Costs

        The Company and PIA Staff both agree there should be a continuation of the CCR ARO
cost recovery methodology approved in the 2019 Rate Case. GAM Witness Pollock recommends
the Company not be allowed to earn a return on the CCR ARO regulatory asset balance until
securitization is authorized.

        The Company’s CCR ARO compliance costs are required to comply with federal and state
environmental regulations and are appropriately included in rate base. Further, this Commission
approved the Company’s Environmental Compliance Strategy, which included the Company’s ash
pond closure plans and CCR strategy, as part of the 2022 IRP in Docket No. 44160. No party in
this case filed testimony or presented evidence challenging the appropriateness of the Company’s
                                               Page 11

CCR ARO compliance costs sought to be recovered in this case. Witness Pollock’s
recommendation is not currently possible and would require a change in state law to implement.
It would be inappropriate for this Commission to deny the Company recovery of its currently
incurred financing costs based solely on the potential for future securitization legislation.

        This Commission has carefully considered the evidence and testimony presented on these
issues and finds that it is just and reasonable for Georgia Power to continue to recover CCR ARO
compliance costs over a three-year period in base rates as proposed by the Company because this
provides the best balance between the impact on customer’s rates and the total cost. No Commission
action on securitization of CCR ARO compliance costs is warranted at this time. GAM Witness
Pollock’s recommendation is denied.

D.     Revenue Requirement Adjustments

       1.      Commission Determination Regarding Levelized or Step Rate Adjustment.

        Within its recommended ARP, the Company proposed to levelized its requested rate
increase to balance the benefit to customers from three years of stable and predictable rates with
the Company’s need to timely recover its projected revenue deficiency. The Company argues that
customers are fairly compensated for the projected over-collection in the first two years of the
levelization. For the projected advancement of revenues in 2023 and 2024, the Company would
defer the amount as a regulatory liability, reducing retail rate base and giving customers credit on
the advanced amount based on the Company’s full weighted average cost of capital. This proposed
deferral reduces the levelized revenue requirement requested by the Company and fully amortizes
the advancement by the end of 2025. The Company argues a levelized approach to rate increases
shifts economic risks to the Company by insulating customers from the impacts of a possible
downturn in the sales forecast or cost increases. The Company states that it is better equipped to
manage negative economic factors that impact the cost of running the business, as opposed to
asking customers to shoulder the impacts, which would occur with increased rates under step
increases. The Company claims a levelized approach results in $43 million less being billed to
customers than a step increase approach over the three-year ARP.

        In contrast, PIA Staff advocates for a step increase approach, which would increase
customer rates by an increasing amount during each of the next three years and better align with
the Company’s demonstrated revenue deficiencies in 2024 and 2025. Under PIA Staff’s approach,
current rates would be maintained through 2023 and no rate increase would take place until 2024.
This results in lower than projected revenue requirements for years 2023 and 2024.

        The Commission finds and concludes that in light of all the facts of the case that levelizing
the proposed increases over the three-year ARP is the more appropriate approach for customers.
All parties support rate stability and the Commission holds that the fluctuation in projected costs
year-to-year under a step approach creates greater uncertainty for customers than under a levelized
approach. Therefore, PIA Staff’s recommended rate adjustment structure with a step increase
across the three-year ARP is denied.


                                               Page 12

       2.      Commission Determination Regarding Electric Vehicle Charging Facilities.

        Since 2014, the Company has deployed electric vehicle (“EV”) charging infrastructure
throughout the state to bring greater awareness to the benefits of driving electric vehicles, including
both economic savings and environmental impact, as well as enabling the growth of EVs in
Georgia. The EV chargers were installed to help the Company respond to the evolving needs and
expectations of its customers, including expanded access points for customers to charge electric
vehicles. Georgia Power’s 2022 Rate Case filing reflects (i) $27 million per year for each year of
the ARP for infrastructure upgrades to implement the Make Ready program and (ii) $3 million per
year to maintain the development and deployment of the Community Charging program, both of
which connect communities across the state through corridor chargers and serves the infrastructure
needs of existing drivers in high-adoption regions. The Company proposed to use the Community
Charging program to expand critical charging infrastructure to alleviate charging deserts that are
found in rural and underserved communities and would not impact existing competitive charging
markets.

        The Company argued that because the Commission has previously approved the recovery
of certain EV-related costs from both the Make Ready and Community Charging programs and
because of their proven success, the Commission can and should approve the Company’s full EV-
related request in this proceeding. Nevertheless, Georgia Power acknowledged that the actual
amount to be recovered is a policy decision by the Commission.

        PIA Staff Witnesses Trokey and Smith excluded in full both Make Ready and Community
Charing program EV costs and revenues from rate base in their recommended adjustments to the
Company’s 2022 Rate Case filing. According to PIA Staff, Make Ready program costs should not
be included in the Company’s base rates because the program obligates all customers to subsidize
an unregulated service, from which they will not directly benefit. Staff argues that the Company’s
implementation of Make Ready to date has exceeded the scope of uses specified in the 2019 rate
case stipulation. Staff further argues that the Make Ready Program creates an uneven playing field
for EV Charging Service providers. Regarding the Community Charging program, Staff argues
the program does not generate enough revenue to cover its costs and that it is inappropriate to
allow the Company to expand its market share of retail chargers at the expense of customers in
light of market competition and increased state and federal fundings.

        The AACE Witness panel of Witnesses Akers, Nelson, and Selvidge support the concept
of the Make Ready program but recommend that it include targeted goals, objectives, guardrails,
and be subject to Commission review. The panel argued that the Company’s current community
chargers are not serving charging deserts. The AACE panel recommended the Community
Charging program be limited to only provide level 2 charging, and proposed that the Company be
directed to develop public guidelines through which the need for community charger investment
can be identified.

       EVgo Witness Murchie recommended the Commission approve the Company’s proposed
expansion of the Make Ready program. Witness Murchie recommended the Commission direct
the Company to develop and publish its program rules and requirements and expand eligibility to
include third-party charging station owner-operators. Witness Murchie also recommended the
Commission approve the Community Charging program with additional specifications. He
                                               Page 13

recommended the Company use objective and publicly available siting criteria to determine the
location of its Community Charging stations and that it file a pricing proposal with the Commission
based on a market analysis to allow stakeholder and additional Commission review.

        ChargePoint Witness Wilson testified in support of both the Make Ready and Community
Charging programs, and Coalition Witnesses Deffley and Nifong provided testimony in support
of the Make Ready program and recommended its eligibility expand to include sites with less than
five charging stations.

        The Commission finds and concludes that the $30 million annual capital investment for
projected capital and O&M costs associated with the Company’s two electric transportation
programs should be included in the Company’s retail rates. As previously stated by this
Commission, whether EV charging facility costs warrant inclusion in the Company’s cost of
service is a matter of Commission policy. This Commission finds that EV charging facility costs
must not be examined in isolation: EV charging stations serve as much more than an additional
source of revenue for the Company or any other business for that matter. Electric transportation
investments also help attract economic growth, as evidenced by the major EV manufacturing
announcements, and EV charging stations provide public benefits above and beyond dollars and
cents. For example, the overall grid benefits from EV drivers who routinely shift their load and
place a positive benefit onto the grid. In addition, evidence presented by the Company indicates
that the Make Ready program has positive Rate Impact Measure values, which benefits all
customers by putting downward pressure on rates. Accordingly, the Commission finds that
allowing EV charging station costs into rate base strikes the right balance for the monetary and
non-monetary benefits associated with EV infrastructure deployment.

       3.      Commission Determination Regarding the Company’s Grid Investment Plan

        Georgia Power’s Grid Investment Plan is a 12-year strategic effort that focuses on (1)
improving distribution reliability and (2) replacing aging transmission assets. The Commission
approved the first three years of projected capital expenditures for this program in the 2019 Rate
Case. As part of its current filing, the Company requests that the Commission approve dollars
associated with the next three-year period of the Grid Investment Plan (2022-2025). Company
Witness Robinson testified regarding the Company’s Grid Investment Plan request in this docket.
He explained that while the Grid Investment Plan objectives remain unchanged, the Company
refined certain program components and assumptions based on the positive insights, learnings, and
data gathered during the initial three-year implementation period of the Grid Investment Plan. The
Company has requested $2.2 billion for the transmission and distribution portions of the Grid
Investment Plan for projects to be initiated during the three year ARP window from 2022 through
2025.

       PIA Staff witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana oppose the Grid Investment Plan and
recommend that the Commission: (1) reject all transmission investments planned for the next three
years, and (2) remove 85% of the planned distribution investments. Through these
recommendations, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens ask that the Commission reverse course and
disregard the evidentiary record from the 2019 Rate Case and the present docket supporting the
Company’s investment in the Grid Investment Plan. Regarding the transmission portion of the
Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff contends that the Company is prematurely replacing equipment
                                              Page 14

based solely on asset age and recommends that Georgia Power be required to employ more
objective testing and inspection practices for identifying appropriate transmission investments.
Regarding the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan, PIA Staff recommends that this
Commission eliminate 85% of the planned distribution investments included in the Grid
Investment Plan through denial of the undergrounding and Basic Insulation Level investments. In
addition, Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens recommend that all Grid Investment Plan costs be
accompanied by corresponding reliability improvements outweighing such cost. Given that the
Company’s revised program components based on learnings from initial implementation,
Witnesses Alvarez and Stephens question all estimate and benefit projections underpinning the
Grid Investment Plan. PIA Staff suggests that no pending “reliability emergency” that justifies
dramatic increases transmission and distribution expenditures as proposed in the Grid Investment
Plan.

        The Company filed rebuttal testimony responding to the issues raised by Witnesses
Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana. The Company fundamentally disagrees with Staff’s Grid Investment
Plan-related recommendations and overall characterization of the rationale and justification for
these investments. The Company rebutted the notion that the Grid Investment Plan involves the
“premature replacement” of aging transmission assets. The Company explained in the 2019 Rate
Case and in the current docket that asset age drives the transmission portion of the Grid Investment
Plan. According to the Company, Georgia Power employed objective approaches to determine
which equipment should be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan as proposed by PIA Staff.
Witness Robinson pointed out that the Company relied on its long history of robust maintenance
practices, internal experience, and historical operational knowledge to determine which
transmission equipment will be replaced as part of the Grid Investment Plan. The Company
incorporated this experience into its recommendation to replace transmission equipment that will
be greater than the Company expected life by the end of the Grid Investment Plan period. Georgia
Power explained that transmission equipment installed during the 1950s, 1960s, and 1970s, is now
approaching 50, 60, and 70 year lives. The transmission portion of the Grid Investment Plan
reasonably targets the replacement of aging assets to preserve and improve system reliability and
help ensure the safety of the general public and Company employees.

        The Company also responded to arguments that the Commission should require cost-
benefit analyses for each transmission investment included in the Grid investment Plan. The
Company maintains that such analyses are impractical and not useful given the dynamic and
networked nature of the transmission system. Witness Robinson identifies several factors that
complicate the development a meaningful and accurate cost-benefit analysis of these investments,
including failure type (controlled or catastrophic), impacts on other capital projects, lead times for
replacement equipment, and redispatch generation-related costs.

         Georgia Power also countered PIA Staff’s distribution-related recommendations. Witness
Robinson explained that eliminating 85% of the planned distribution investments as proposed by
Witness Alvarez and Stephens, eliminates the overarching purpose and rationale supporting the
Grid Investment Plan. The Company disagreed with PIA Staff regarding the existence of a pending
reliability emergency and maintains that Georgia Power’s system reliability will continue
worsening each year that the Company does not make these grid investments. The Company
presented data showing that from 2013—2016, its system average interruption duration index
(“SAIDI”) worsened by approximately 44% and system average interruption frequency index
                                               Page 15

(“SAIFI”) worsened by approximately 10%. The Company highlights that it carefully and
strategically developed the distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan to ensure lasting
reliability improvements while minimizing the total cost to the customer. Witness Robinson also
rebutted arguments that it reduced reliability expectations by revising the end-state SAIDI for the
distribution portion of the Grid Investment Plan. Witness Robinson highlights that Georgia Power
appropriately acted in the best interest of customers by reassessing and refreshing the distribution
portion of the Grid Investment Plan—these efforts helped ensure that the investments will continue
producing positive results in the lowest total cost to customers. The Company explains that is
intermittent refinement a multi-year investment plan is essential to ensure that customer capital is
managed responsibly. The Company maintains that the Grid Investment Plan encompasses the
qualities of the alternative programs recommended by PIA Staff—it accomplishes these goals
strategically and appropriately addresses feeder level issues through system level optimization.

        Considering all the evidence, the Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable and
appropriate for the Company to continue pursuing investments in the Grid Investment Plan as
proposed. Specifically, the Commission approves dollars associated with the next three-year
period of the Grid Investment Plan: $2.2B for the 2023-2025 period.1 The Commission notes that
Company witness Robinson presented detailed and persuasive testimony in support of the Grid
Investment Plan. In light of the Commission’s assessment of the credibility of witnesses opining
on the facts and legal considerations at issue with the Grid Investment Plan, the Commission relies
heavily on Witness Robinson’s testimony.2 Witnesses Alvarez, Stephens, and Rana did not present
sufficient evidence challenging the reasonableness and justification for the Grid Investment Plan-
related work. The recommendations and testimony presented by these Staff witnesses ignored the
potential risks and cost increases that could result from rejecting the Grid Investment Plan and
delaying T&D investments.

        Based on the foregoing, the Commission finds and concludes that as in the 2019 Rate Case,
Georgia Power has demonstrated the need for the investments outlined in the Grid Investment Plan
and the Grid Investment Plan is in the public interest. The Commission finds that the Company’s
approach represents a reasonable and cost-effective approach to managing grid-related risks during
a period of transformation.

       4.      Commission Determination Regarding DERMS / Section G Proposals

         The Company requested $100 million to invest in a Distributed Energy Resource
Management System (DERMS) to integrate Distributed Energy Resources (DER) with greater
visibility, predictability and control. PIA Staff proposes to reject DERMS spending until the
penetration level of DER on the grid increases. PIA Staff claims that existing systems and
processes can manage DER. The Company persuasively argued that DERMS will allow the
Company to consider demand side resources alongside supply side resources in future IRPs, as
ordered by the Commission in the 2022 IRP. Further, the Commission supports the Company’s
implementation of DERMS at this point will also allow for maturation of the DERMS solution as
thousands or tens of thousands of additional DER are connected to the grid in the coming years.

1
 Exhibit ___(APA/SPA/ADH/MBR-7) contains the distribution packages for the 2023-2025 period and
Exhibit___(APA/SPA/ADH/MBR-8) contains the transmission packages for the 2023-2025 period.

                                              Page 16

As such, the Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s investment and requested
funding for DERMS is reasonable and should be approved in order to optimize the integration of
DER on the Georgia Power system.

       5.      Commission Determination Regarding Stock-Based Compensation and Payroll
               Taxes.

        The Company included expenses related to stock-based compensation in its 2022 Rate
Case filing. The Company explained that the total compensation plan, including stock-based at-
risk compensation is market competitive and appropriately balances operational with financial
focus for both short-term and longer term to drive employee behavior in ways that balance the
interests of customers and shareholders alike. According to the Company, moving at-risk pay to
base pay would not be in the best interest of customers because it would encourage employees to
focus solely on short-term operational performance with no balance or accountability to long-term
operational costs and efficiencies. Therefore, the Company believes that stock-based
compensation should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail
rates.

        The Company testified that its budgeted costs to attract, retain and engage employees are
expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and appropriately should be
included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail rates. The Company
maintains that customers benefit from a healthy company, which should be measured over the
short- and long-term to ensure that the decisions made by the Company are optimized for both
short- and long-term benefits. In addition, the Company proposed to include the payroll tax
expenses associated with stock-based incentive compensation, which the Company also contends
represent prudently incurred costs.

       PIA Staff proposed disallowances for the Company’s stock-based compensation program
expenses. Specifically, Staff removed the Performance Unit Shares (“PSU”) and the Restricted
Stock Units (“RSU”) expense from retail jurisdictional operating expenses establishing the
Company’s revenue requirement. PIA Staff also recommended reducing the Company’s
Performance Pay Program (“PPP”) expenses by two-thirds to only allow recovery from customers
for operational goals that benefit Georgia Power customers.

        According to PIA Staff, the stock-based incentive compensation and two-thirds of the PPP
expenses for these programs should not be included in the revenue requirement as these costs are
incurred to improve the Southern Company financial performance for the benefit of shareholders,
not to improve customer service or meet other regulated utility service requirements. In addition,
PIA Staff recommended that the Commission exclude the payroll tax expenses associated with the
stock-based compensation.

       In its rebuttal testimony, Georgia Power argued that these proposed disallowances are
inappropriate and should be rejected by the Commission. Company witnesses testified that PIA
Staff did not base these recommendations on supporting data or market analysis that the
Company’s expenses are excessive or that the programs are not competitive with the external
market. Georgia Power also argued its position is consistent with the 2019 Rate Case Order.

                                             Page 17

         The Commission finds and concludes that it is reasonable and appropriate for the Company
to include stock-based compensation and the associated payroll taxes in its revenue requirement.
The Company’s long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and competitive with
the utility industry. The Company’s long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and
competitive with the utility industry. The Company’s budgeted costs for stock-based compensation
to attract, retain and engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the
Company’s business and appropriately should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements
and recovered in retail rates.

       6.      Commission Determination regarding Amortization of Retired Unit Remaining Net
               Book Values.

       The Company has proposed to amortize the regulatory assets for Plant Wansley Units 1-2,
Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1, which were retired by August 31, 2022 in
accordance with the Company’s 2022 IRP, over their remaining useful lives as approved in Docket
No. 42516. These proposed amortization periods are consistent with similar regulatory assets and
PIA Staff did not object to the Company’s proposal.

         In addition, the Company requested to defer depreciation and proposed to amortize the
regulatory assets for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 over a nine-year period
beginning January 1, 2023. Based on the depreciation study filed in this case, Plant Bowen Units
1-2 are scheduled to be depreciated through 2027 and Plant Scherer Units 1-3 are scheduled to be
depreciated through 2028 in accordance with the retirement dates proposed in the 2022 IRP. More
specifically, the Company requests to defer the difference each year between the amount of
depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation
study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a
regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are ultimately
retired, the deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at
the retirement date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending
December 31, 2031. The Company’s request was unchallenged in this case. The Commission
should approve the deferral and amortization of Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3 as
requested by the Company without modification.

        The Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s proposed amortization of the
remaining net book value of units retired in the 2022 IRP, which is consistent with the Company’s
treatment of other regulatory assets and liabilities is reasonable and appropriate. The Commission
also finds and concludes that it is reasonable to approve the depreciation deferral and nine-year
amortization for the associated regulatory assets for Bowen Units 1-2 and Scherer Units 1-3 as
requested by the Company.

E.     Cost Allocation

       1.      Commission Determination Regarding Cost-of-Service Study Methodology.

        The Company filed its 2022 Cost of Service Study (“COSS”) consistent with the
methodology used in prior Georgia Power rate cases. Georgia Power advocated for the continued
use of the 12 Coincident Peak (“12-CP”) methodology for allocating costs. Further, the Company

                                             Page 18

states that its COSS accurately reflects cost causation and can be used to judge the adequacy of
rates with respect to the costs to serve and be used for rate design purposes.

        Instead of the 12-CP production cost methodology, PIA Staff proposes to use the Base
Intermediate Peak and Probability of Dispatch methodologies. PIA Staff argues that the
shortcoming of the 12-CP method is that accurate load data is required by class throughout the
year and that once a system to record such data is in place, the administration and maintenance of
such a system is not cumbersome. PIA Staff Witness Watkins stated at hearing that the Company
had an abundance of more than adequate data. Thus, the only shortcoming of the 12-CP method
identified by PIA Staff does not hold true for Georgia Power.

        GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar and GAM propose the Company use either a 4-CP or 6-CP
production cost allocation methodology arguing that as a summer peaking utility, the four summer
months June through September drive the Company’s peak demand and should be the basis for
allocating the Company’s production costs. However, in rebuttal the Company identified that its
system is driven and influenced by non- summer periods in addition to summer peaks, and it
utilizes Expected Unserved Energy in its determination of the optimal reserve margin for both
summer and winter seasons. The Company argues that this renders a pure summer peak or 4-CP
allocator is an oversimplification of the Company’s generation planning process.

         Based on the evidence presented, the Commission finds and concludes that the Company’s
cost of service study is reflective of cost causation and is objective, fair, and equitable to the
different groups of Georgia Power customers. The Company’s cost of service study methodology
provides a reasonable basis to functionalize, classify and allocate costs to customer classes, is
reflective of the Company’s system needs, and is consistent with the methodology employed and
approved it its previous rate cases before the Commission. The Commission approves the Company’s
COSS as filed by Company Witness Evans.

       2.      Commission Determination on Basic Service Charge.

        Georgia Power proposes to increase the Basic Service Charge (“BSC”) for the Domestic
Group by approximately $2.27 per month. The Company explained that it is proposing to increase
all traditional base rates on an equal percentage basis, including rate elements, which therefore
includes the basic service charge.

        PIA Staff Witness Watkins disagrees with the Company’s recommendation to increase the
basic service charge on an equal percentage basis. Witness Watkins testifies that the basic service
charge should only reflect those direct costs required to connect and maintain a customer’s
account. Therefore, PIA Staff recommends this Commission deny the Company’s requested
increase in the basic service charge. PIA Staff recommends in the event that this Commission
grants the Company’s requested increase, that the income qualified senior discount be increased
by an amount equal to the increase in the basic service charge.

       GIPL Witness Barnes also recommends the Commission deny the Company’s requested
basic service charge increase and leave the current residential basic service charge in place.
Witness Barnes testifies that that the cost-of-service study erroneously classifies a portion of the

                                              Page 19

costs associated with the shared distribution system as customer-related based on its Minimum
Distribution System (“MDS”) analysis.

      In rebuttal, the Company proposed to increase the income qualified senior discount by the
amount the basic service charge is ultimately increased.

        After considering the parties’ testimony and all of the evidence presented, the Commission
finds and concludes that the Company’s proposed BSC increase is just and reasonable and strikes an
appropriate balance to provide rates that more clearly reflect actual cost causation, minimize
subsidization, and provide proper price signals to customers in the rate class, while also moderating
the impact of such increase on low-usage customers. As in prior rate cases, the parties in the present
case filed testimony advocating various methodologies for cost allocation and various rate designs
favorable to each party’s interests. In light of the reasonable allocation of costs and rate design
contemplated, the Commission finds that the allocation of costs and rate design contemplated and
specified by Georgia Power are reasonable and in the best interests of all customer groups.

F.     Rate Design

        Company Witness Larry Legg provided testimony regarding the Company’s existing tariffs
and its proposed changes to rate design. The Company testified that its portfolio of rates proposed
in the 2022 Rate Case filing offer optionality for customers while supporting a gradual move
toward more modern rates. PIA Staff and several Intervenors provided testimony on various rate
design issues in this proceeding, as discussed below. Having considered the testimony and exhibits
of each witness and the entire record in this proceeding, the Commission makes its findings and
conclusions on each of the issues set forth below.

       1.      Residential Rate Design.

        The Company proposes several modifications to its residential rate design offerings.
Consistent with its request in the 2019 base rate case, the Company proposes to close the
Residential Service (R) tariff (“the R Rate”) to new residential customers on January 1, 2023. The
default residential rate for new premises would continue to be Time of Use – Residential Demand
(“TOU-RD”), and the Company proposes closing Time of Use – Residential Energy Only (“TOU-
REO”) to new residential customers.

               a.      Modifications to the R Rate.

         PIA Staff proposed several modifications to the ‘R’ Rate. Staff contends that the
Company’s retention of a declining winter block rate on the ‘R’ rate incents wintertime usage in
conflict with the Company’s winter seasonal planning posture and winter system cost structure.
PIA Staff recommend the elimination of the declining-block rates during the non-summer months
to a flat rate for all kWh usage due to increasingly higher winter peaks.

       The Company responds in rebuttal that the R Rate is antiquated and sends poor price signals
to customers regarding their energy usage. The Company argues that it makes little sense to
fundamentally alter the R Rate’s design in light of its other more modern offerings. The Company
recommends closing the R Rate to new residential premises.

                                               Page 20

               b.      Closing the Residential Service (R) Rate to New Premises.

       Georgia Power requests to begin transitioning from the more antiquated, volumetric option
of the R Rate to move toward more modern rates by first closing the R Rate to new residential
premises. The Company argues this will allow it to establish new residential accounts that better
align with the cost to serve customers. If approved, customers establishing service at new premises
will continue to be enrolled in the TOU-RD rate as a default rate, as established in the 2019 Rate
Case Order. Customers will still have the ability to select from four of the most modern rate
options. After the initial year of service at those new premises, customers will be eligible for two
additional rates. The R Rate would remain available to existing residential premises on the Georgia
Power system that established service prior to January 2023.

       PIA Staff opposed the Company’s recommendation to close the R Rate to new premises.
PIA Staff argued that while it supports marketing and education to grow customer adoption of
modern rates, Staff does not support the Company eliminating customer choice in rates. GIPL
Witness Barnes also opposed the Company’s proposal to close the R Rate. Based on concerns with
time of use rates in general and specifically the default TOU-RD, both PIA Staff and Witness
Barnes recommend the Company re-designate the R Rate as the default residential rate.

               c.      Time of Use – Residential Demand (“TOU-RD”)

       To transition to more modern rate designs and to provide customers with price signals that
more accurately reflect the cost of service, the Company proposes maintaining TOU-RD as the
default rate for new residential premises. The Company argues that this a logical step in
modernizing its rate design offerings.

        PIA Staff and Certain Intervenors object to the Company’s TOU-RD proposal. PIA Staff
assert that the TOU-RD rate would be a poor default rate for residential customers because
customers lack sufficient information or knowledge on these issues to appropriately minimize their
bills on such a rate. PIA Staff, the Coalition, and GIPL Witness Barnes further argued that from
analysis of Company data, TOU-RD has been significantly more expensive than the R Rate or
TOU-REO.

        The Company testified in rebuttal that TOU-RD is designed to be revenue neutral for the
average residential customer and to provide customers with better price signals to enable them to
make more informed decisions to reduce their energy use and electric bills. The Company stated
that the bill comparisons that PIA Staff highlight in testimony are heavily skewed by extremely
low usage and fail to tell a complete story because they represent premises that are new to the
Company’s system. The Company testifies that the average monthly usage for those premises is
far below the level at which the usage will ultimately mature.

        The Company testifies that customer surveys reflect TOU-RD customers are more satisfied
with their rate and feel more knowledgeable regarding the behaviors that can help them capitalize
on their rate plan and reduce their energy bills.


                                              Page 21

               d.      Time of Use – Residential Energy Only (TOU-REO)

        The Company requests closing TOU-REO to new customers to avoid confusion associated
with multiple residential TOU rates. PIA Staff and certain Intervenors recommend that TOU-REO
continue to be offered to all residential customers because this rate provides a viable option for
customers to shift their load and usage patterns from on-peak hours to off-peak hours. PIA Staff
recommends if the Commission close the R Rate to new premises, TOU-REO is a more appropriate
default time of use rate for customers than TOU-RD.

        The Company testified in rebuttal that TOU-REO is much more volatile than TOU-RD
