and, moreover, that TOU-RD sends better price signals and more appropriately reflects the cost of
a customer’s electric service. The Company reiterated that TOU-RD provides the most appropriate
pricing signals to customers to enable to them modify their usage patterns and reduce their electric
bills.

               e.      FlatBill

        Staff approves of the Company’s request to have a $50 minimum bill for FlatBill
customers. Staff recommends the Company to expand customer awareness of its budget billing
offerings.


       Commission Decision on Residential Rate Design Issues.

        In light of the parties’ testimony and all of the evidence presented, the Commission approves
Georgia Power’s request to close the ‘R’ Rate to new residential premises and supports the
Company’s gradual movement toward more modern rates. Closing the R rate to new premises does
not affect existing customers on the ‘R’ rate unless they move to a residence that has never received
service from Georgia Power before. In beginning the move toward more modern rates, the
Commission finds PIA Staff and Intervenor arguments to otherwise modify the ‘R’ Rate by adjusting
the declining winter block rate and usage tiers to be unnecessary.

         The Commission also approves the closure of TOU-REO and the continuation of TOU-RD
as the default residential rate for new premises. PIA Staff and Intervenors have not provided sufficient
justification for the need for the Company to offer residential customers more than two time-of-use
rates where TOU-RD provides the most modern and appropriate pricing signals.

        The Commission approves the Company’s proposed adjustments to FlatBill, TOU-RD, and
each of the remaining residential tariffs as filed by the Company.

       2.      Commission Determination Regarding Commercial and Industrial Rate Design.

               a.      Charge-It Electric Vehicle (“CIEV”) Rider

      The Company requests the Commission approve its new CIEV rider. The CIEV rider will
modify the monthly billing demands by using Billing Demand Adjustment Factors. The Company


                                                Page 22

proposes CIEV as a temporary rider to expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling
12-month load factor for the account reaches 15% or greater.

       PIA Staff recommend the Commission approve the CIEV rider as filed in the Company’s
Errata-Supplemental filing with quarterly reporting regarding the location, peak demand, usage,
and revenue received from CIEV customers. EVgo Witness Murchie and ChargePoint Witness
Wilson also supports the CIEV rider. Witness Wilson and Murchie recommend the Company
allow any qualifying customers, not just new customers, take service under CIEV. AACE
Witnesses Akers, Nelson, and Selvidge argue that the CIEV rider discriminates between EV
charging providers.

       The Company explains in its rebuttal that the CIEV rider is a time limited offering to
encourage the growth of electric vehicles (“EVs”) in Georgia and therefore argues it is
inappropriate to expand participation to existing customers, which would eliminate the incentive
for adoption of EV charging by new premises.

               b.      Embedding ECCR costs in Marginal Rates.

        PIA Staff recommends that customers on marginal rates be assessed the fully embedded
cost of the ECCR tariff. PIA Staff and GIPL Witness Barnes argue that ECCR costs should be
recovered based on a customer’s kWH energy usage basis.

        The Company disagrees, arguing that by including fully embedded costs like ECCR in
marginal rates is inappropriate because such a change is contrary with how marginal rates like
RTP are designed to operate. The Company explains that fully embedded costs are recovered from
the Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) portion of the marginally priced rates, while marginal costs
are recovered from the marginal portion of the rates. The Company also states that the ECCR costs
include CCR ARO compliance costs, which are fully embedded costs incurred by the Company
independent of a customer’s kWh usage. As such, counter to recommendations by GIPL Witness
Barnes and PIA Staff, ECCR costs are appropriately recovered through the CBL portion of the
rate. The Company asserts that if ECCR costs, or other fully embedded costs, were allocated to
the marginal portion of RTP rates, RTP costs would increase by approximately $150 million
overall.

       Given that the RTP rate is a critical rate for large, economic development customers that
are considering locating, relocating, or expanding operations in Georgia and that a cost shift of this
magnitude would significantly decrease the appeal of the rate to current and future customers, the
Commission rejects PIA Staff and GIPL’s proposed ECCR modifications to marginal rates.

               c.      Proposed Modifications to Customer Baseline Load (“CBL”) Reduction
                       Criteria.

        DOD Witness Blank recommends modifying the Company’s criteria for the reduction of
CBLs for customers who install behind the meter generation. The Company disagreed with this
proposal because customer generation does not permanently remove the customer’s load and does
not justify the reduction of a customer’s CBL. According to the Company, CBLs may be reduced

                                               Page 23

when load originally represented in the CBL has been permanently removed. Mr. Blank did not
provide sufficient persuasive evidence to merit a change in the CBL criteria. Therefore, the
Commission denies Witness Blank’s recommendation.

        DOD Witness Blank also criticizes the Company’s requirement that a CBL reduction
assessment based on historical load is not permitted unless the customer stops taking service under
the RTP tariff for two years. In contrast, the Company supports the requirement that a customer
remain off the RTP rate for two years before returning or requesting a CBL reduction, because
short term changes to a customer’s usage should not result in the permanent conversion of
embedded load into marginal load on the RTP rate. The RTP tariffs include provisions to revise
the CBL as a result of actions like permanent equipment removal and energy efficiency
improvements, but the tariffs specifically prohibit CBL revisions due to temporary factors such as
economic downturns. As previously held by this Commission, requiring customers who have not
removed load to stay off RTP for 24 months in order to set a new CBL preserves the integrity of
the RTP program and protects other customers from potential cost shifts that might result from
premature revision of the CBL. The Commission denies Witness Blank’s recommendation.

               d.     Fixed Price Alternative and Price Protection Product offerings

        PIA Staff proposed to modify the terms and conditions of the Fixed Price Alternative
(“FPA”) and Price Protection Product (“PPP”) offerings to remove Company discretion over when
to offer these rates. The Company maintains that removing its discretion regarding when to offer
these products adds unnecessary risk that could be borne by other customers, since delays in acting
during volatile markets could result in the development of product offerings that do not properly
reflect pricing impacts. The Company explained that it considers multiple factors when deciding
whether to offer the FPA rate and PPP offerings to customers. These factors include, but are not
limited to, uncertainty around environmental regulation, fuel markets, and other potential factors
that could impact the pricing of these offerings. The Company stresses that it is to maintain
flexibility to act quickly and update PPP offerings to best reflect market conditions, especially
during times of fuel market volatility as have been experienced for the last year.

       Commission Decision on Commercial and Industrial Rate Design Issues.

       The Commission finds and concludes that the CIEV tariff is reasonable and appropriately
designed to encourage new EV charging service adoption. As such, the CIEV tariff is approved
without modification. The Commission further finds and concludes that PIA Staff and Intervenor
proposed changes to the Company’s commercial and industrial tariffs are not justified and should
be denied. This Commission supports the balance the Company maintains in meeting customer
demands for more beneficial rates with greater marginal load components. Further, the
Commission rejects PIA Staff and UMS’ request to expand the TOU-FD eligibility. The
Company’s commercial and industrial rates as filed should be approved without modification.

       3.      Community Solar Program

       Georgia Power’s Community Solar tariff offers qualifying customers the opportunity to
support renewable energy and the development of solar power in Georgia by subscribing to a
portion of the output from local solar facilities. (Tr. 888, 2184.) Every month, participating
                                              Page 24

customers pay a fee for a subscription block of the energy produced by Community Solar facilities,
and in return receive energy credits on their monthly bill that are based on the production from
those facilities. (Id.) Additionally, the equivalent renewable energy credits (“RECs”) are retired
on the customer’s behalf. (Tr. 888, 2184-85.)

        In this case, the Company proposes to update the pricing for the existing residential
Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per one kilowatt block and proposed that the
pricing for General Service (“GS”) Community Solar participants be set at $29.99 per one kilowatt
block. (Tr. 888, 929.) Georgia Power states that the Community Solar subscription price is
designed to ensure that participating customers do not shift costs to other customers when kilowatt
hours reduce the monthly bills of participating customers. (Tr. 939.) The Company argues that
based on the approved structure of the Community Solar program, a reduction in the price per kW
block of energy as proposed by several parties would leave costs unrecovered and, therefore, shift
cost recovery to non-participants. (Tr. 914.) The Company states that the Community Solar tariff
is not intended to create bill savings for participating customers, but rather, is targeted toward
customers who are interested in supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install
solar panels at their premises. The

        PIA Staff proposed two alternatives regarding the appropriate pricing for the Community
Solar Program offers two alternatives and is premised on a Commission policy determination as
to the purpose of the program. PIA Staff’s recommendation depends on whether the Community
Solar Program is found by the Commission to offer bill savings opportunities for participating
customers. As proposed by PIA Staff, if the purpose of the program is to support clean energy and
realize bill savings, Staff recommends a pricing reduction to $20 and $22 pricing for residential
and commercial customers. If the purpose of the program is to support clean energy but not the
opportunity for bill savings, Staff supports the Company’s proposed $27.99 and 29.99 pricing for
residential and commercial customers, respectively. (Tr.2449-50.)

        The Coalition recommended the Commission reject the Company’s proposed increase to
the subscription price for the Community Solar Program. To increase participation in the program
and provide a viable option for those who cannot host or afford rooftop solar, the Coalition
recommends two alternatives. First, the Commission could maintain the current community solar
fixed price per kW block structure but reduce the price to $16 per kW block for residential
customers to exclude lost revenues from fuel, environmental, and nuclear cost recovery riders.
Second, the Commission could permit customers to lock in the current $24.99 per kW block rate
for the duration of the customer’s participation in the program.

        Although PIA Staff may believe the purpose of the Community Solar program is an open
policy decision for the Commission, the Commission has already determined that the purpose of
the program is not for bill savings. In the Commission’s June 7, 2017 Order approving the
Community Solar Program tariff, the Commission clearly stated that the Community Solar
Program “is not designed to produce credits in excess of a customer’s usage and a customer should
anticipate the [Community Solar] program charges will exceed the value of any bill credits
received.” Order approving Community Solar Program and CS-1 Tariff, Georgia Public Service
Commission, Docket Nos. 40161 and 16573 (June 7, 2017) at 2. Rather, as confirmed by the
Company, the Community Solar Program is targeted toward customers who are interested in

                                              Page 25

supporting solar but either do not want or are unable to install solar panels at their premises. While
the Company agrees that increased participation in the Community Solar program should be
encouraged, increased participation should not be at the expense of non-participating customers.
The inclusion of commercial customers in the program and the adoption of the income qualified
community solar pilot should help increase customer participation. With regard to the pricing for
the Community Solar program, the Commission approves the Company’s requested block price
increase for the Community Solar program to $27.99 per kW block for residential customers and
$29.99 per residential block for commercial customers on the general service rate.

G.     Commission Action on the Monthly Netting Pilot.

         The Commission-approved a Monthly Netting Pilot (“Pilot”) in its 2019 Rate Case. The
Pilot directed the Company to offer monthly netting on a pilot basis for the first 5,000 subscribers
or until newly installed capacity reached 32 MW. The Pilot provided enrolled customers with full
retail credit for excess generation, netted monthly. The Pilot was designed to provide the Company
with data to evaluate the effect of monthly netting on the Company’s cost of service and customer
class cost shifting.

        The Company recommends this Commission: (1) discontinue the subsidization of monthly
netting customers at the expense of other customers by using the monthly netting methodology
combined with volumetric rate structures; (2) require all customers who enroll in the RNR tariff
on or after January 1, 2023, to take service under a three-part rate; (3) develop a formal framework
for the Commission’s referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the
Georgia Attorney General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading
business practices.

       1.      Expansion of Monthly Netting Pilot.

        The Company does not support expanding the monthly netting pilot. The Company asserts
that a customer participating in the monthly netting pilot shifts an additional $287 of costs onto
non-participating customers on an annual basis. (Tr. 5031.) The Company asserts this represents a
cumulative shift of approximately $1.4 million annually for the 5,000 pilot customers. The
Company requests recovery of this $1.4 million in this case. The Company also testifies that if left
unaddressed and as participation in monthly netting increases, this cost-shift will increase.
Therefore, the Company also seeks this Commission’s approval to close the monthly netting pilot.

        PIA Staff Witnesses Barber, Cook and Watkins do not make a specific recommendation
regarding the expansion of the monthly netting pilot but offer the Commission several alternatives,
which includes a range of possible compensation rates the Company could use to compensate RNR
customers for excess energy pushed back to the grid. This range varies from approximately 6.99
cents to over 13 cents per kWh.

        Intervenors supported a continuation of the monthly netting pilot with various caveats.
Coalition Witness Hill recommends the Commission continue the Pilot through the next ARP
period and recommends a cap of no less 75,000 customers, if one is required. GIPL Witness Barnes
recommends the Commission reinstate monthly netting with a cap set at 3% of the Company’s
2021 peak demand, to be reassessed once reached, and to include a minimum monthly bill of

                                               Page 26

$22.43. Witness Barnes also recommends the Commission deny the Company’s requested 1.4
million monthly netting revenue erosion adjustment.

        GA Solar Witness Sercy recommends the Commission lift both the customer and capacity
limits on monthly netting. GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar Witnesses Lucas, Young and Giese
recommend the Commission expand the Pilot with no participation cap until the Company adopts
a more rigorous cost-based approach to identify and address the cost shift. Witnesses Lucas, Young
and Giese propose the Commission implement a $20 minimum monthly bill that is inclusive of
the customer charge.

        SACE/Southface Witness Brown recommends the Commission reinstate the monthly
netting pilot, without a participation cap, for three additional years. Witness Brown recommends
the Company conduct an additional cost-of-service study to account for additional benefits of
distributed solar that the cost shift did not take into consideration.

         On rebuttal, the Company responded that compensating BTM solar resources at the high
levels proposed by PIA Staff and Intervenors, which are well above avoided cost and close to the
retail rate, will further increase the cost shift to non-participating customers under the RNR tariff,
as these values are not credibly tied to any quantification of actual benefit provided by the BTM
resource. The Company argues that all parties ignored the Commission’s existing and established
RCB Framework, which was jointly developed, applied, and approved to compensate BTM
generators for excess energy. The Company defends its cost-shifting analysis and argues that
neither PIA Staff nor Intervenors were able to credibly rebut it.

       2.      Three Part Rates.

        The Company requests that the monthly netting pilot remain capped at its currently
approved 5,000 customers to prevent additional cost shifting and revenue erosion. The Company
recommends all new and existing customers participating under the RNR tariff be required to
enroll in a three-part rate like TOU-RD beginning January 1, 2023 to better account for and address
the cost shift caused by RNR customers, especially those participating in the monthly netting pilot.

        PIA Staff disagrees with the Company’s recommendation to transition monthly netting
customers to TOU-RD, arguing that it is “not well-suited for RNR customers due to high demand
charge and extremely low off peak energy price.” Coalition Witness Hill, GIPL Witness Barnes,
Georgia Solar Witness Sercy, GSEIA, SEIA, and Vote Solar Witnesses Lucas, Young and Giese,
and Southface Witness Brown all also oppose the Company’s proposal to require new customers
enroll in a three-part rate. Witness Sercy recommends customers be allowed to select their rate
schedule, and Witness Barnes testifies that TOU-RD is misaligned with cost-causation and is
punitive to customers with infrequent high demand.

         The Company reiterates in rebuttal that it proposes transition netting customers to three-
part rates to reduce the cost shift and testifies that continuing to allow RNR customers to take
service under the R rate will only intensify the cost shift. The Company argues that Intervenors
fail to demonstrate that the TOU-RD tariff does not appropriately recover costs and instead that
Intervenors contentions are reactive to the to the potential for the TOU-RD rate to mitigate the
current subsidy available to monthly netting customers on the R rate.
                                               Page 27

       3.      Increased Consumer Protection.

        All parties support the Company’s recommendation to develop a more formal framework
for the Commission’s referral of consumer complaints to the Consumer Protection Division of the
Georgia Attorney General’s Office to help protect customers from dishonest and misleading
business practices.

       Commission Decision on Monthly Netting.

        The Commission finds and concludes that the monthly netting pilot resulted in a cost shift
to non-participating customers and should not be expanded. In order to address the cost shift
associated with BTM generation, the Commission directs that new customers that enroll in the
RNR tariff must take part in one of the Company’s three-part rates beginning January 1, 2023.
RNR instantaneous shall continue to be made available to interested customers. RNR
instantaneous customers shall continue to be paid for excess energy pushed back onto the grid at
the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy Cost rate, consistent with the RCB Framework, and as filed
annually with this Commission in Docket No. 16573.

        Within 90 days of this Order, the Company and Commission Staff shall initiate discussions
and work with the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia Attorney General’s Office to
formalize a consumer complaint process to better address consumer complaints related to bad
actors in the rooftop solar industry.


III.   ORDERING PARAGRAPHS

        WHEREFORE, IT IS ORDERED, that the Company’s Alternate Rate Plan shall be
adopted as proposed and as amended by the Company’s Supplemental Filing and Errata and the
rates pursuant to the Alternate Rate Plan are just and reasonable, and effective for service rendered
from and after January 1, 2023.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company is awarded a 11.0% ROE and 3.53% cost of
debt, using 56% common equity in the capital structure.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the earnings band shall be 9.50-12.00%, with earnings
above the top of the band shared 80/20 with customers.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company will recover CCR ARO compliance costs
ratably over a three-year period in base rates as proposed by the Company.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company may include in base rates the projected capital
and O&M costs for the Make Ready and Community Charging programs and the associated
electric transportation activities such as the net investment of all the existing community electric
vehicle charging facilities, whether located on and off Company property and the associated
revenues and expenses, the commercial and residential rebates for installing EV chargers, and an
amount for EV education and awareness.

                                               Page 28

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company appropriately included stock-based
compensation expenses in its revenue requirement in the 2022 Rate Case filing. Georgia Power’s
long-term at-risk compensation program is comparable to and competitive with the utility industry.
Furthermore, the Company’s budgeted costs for stock-based compensation to attract, retain and
engage employees are expenses that arise in the ordinary course of the Company’s business and
appropriately should be included in the Company’s revenue requirements and recovered in retail
rates.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s Grid Investment Plan and projected spend
for 2022 through 2025 is approved as filed, without modification.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s DERMS request is approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to recover $48.9 million over a five-
year amortization period ending December 31, 2027, for software and cloud computing costs is
just and reasonable and is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to implement a new Customer
Information System is approved as filed.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s inclusion of $94 million for incremental
inflation in the Company’s revenue requirement is approved as filed.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company shall amortize the regulatory assets for Plant
Wansley Units 1-2, Plant Wansley Unit 5A, and Plant Boulevard Unit 1 over their remaining useful
lives as approved in Docket No. 42516.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that beginning January 1, 2023, for Plant Bowen Units 1-2 and
Plant Scherer Units 1-3, the Company shall defer the difference each year between the amount of
depreciation expense recommended under the rates provided in the Company’s new depreciation
study and the amount depreciation expense would be assuming a nine-year remaining life into a
regulatory asset account beginning January 1, 2023. Once the generating units are retired, the
deferred regulatory asset balance, including any remaining undepreciated balance at the retirement
date, would be amortized over the remainder of the nine-year recovery period ending December
31, 2031.

      ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s adjustment to the depreciation rates for
Wansley 1-2, Bowen 1-2, and Scherer 1-3, is approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the depreciable life for Plant Hatch is extended from 60
years to 80 years.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request for a three-year amortization period
ending December 31, 2025, to recover COVID-19 pandemic response costs is just and reasonable
and is approved.

                                             Page 29

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s request to recover a regulatory asset balance
of $786,000 as of December 31, 2022, over a three-year amortization period ending December 31,
2025, for costs associated with providing customers hourly usage data is just and reasonable and
is approved.

         ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company is authorized to opt out of the IRS tax
normalization for the purposes of recognizing eligible Investment Tax Credits for battery storage
facilities in accordance with the Inflation Reduction Act.

       ORDERED FURTHER, the Company’s COSS as filed in the testimony of Lee Evans is
approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s plan to modernize rates by making new
residential premises ineligible for the ‘R’ Rate is approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to streamline the Company’s tariff
portfolio and avoid customer confusion by closing TOU-REO to new residential customers is
approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the minimum monthly
offer for new FlatBill customers on both the FLAT and FLAT-GS tariffs from $25 to $50 is just
and reasonable and is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to implement several changes to
the RLG tariff to (1) clarify the existing fixture options, (2) expand and clarify the available pole
options, and (3) close one fixture to future customer enrollments is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed changes CS tariff to update the
pricing for the existing Community Solar Charge from $24.99 to $27.99 per kW block for
residential customers and set the price for customers on the General Service (“GS”) tariff to be
$29.99 per kW block is approved.

         ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed updates to the Simple Solar (“SS”)
tariff from the 2022 IRP are approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s CIEV rider is approved as a temporary rider
to expire either at the end of 48 months or when the rolling 12-month load factor for the account
reaches 15% or greater.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to include a new pricing option
within the Time of Use – Supplier Choice (“TOU-SC”) tariff is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission specifically approves the Company’s
proposed changes to the TOU-RD, REO, DPEC Rider, RTP, RNR, RLG, CS, and SS tariffs.


                                               Page 30

         ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed one-time interconnection fee of
$200 for customers seeking to interconnect a customer generating facility to the Company’s system
is just and reasonable and is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission approves the Company’s request to
eliminate the PLH and SP tariffs.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the basic service charge
to each traditional base rate, except as noted in testimony, on an equal percentage basis is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company shall update the senior citizen low income
discount to match the increase in the basic service charge.

     ORDERED FURTHER, that the Commission approves the modification of the
Company’s payment and services policies in section F of the Company’s Rules and Regulations.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to include new language in the
customer generation section of the Company’s Rules and Regulations to clarify when a customer-
owned generator is required to undergo witness testing and would be responsible for the
appropriate fees associated with this testing is approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposal to increase the charge for
underground residential distribution service found in section C10 of the Rules and Regulations is
approved.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that the Company’s proposed changes to Sections F and G of
the Rules and Regulations is approved.

        ORDERED FURTHER, the monthly netting pilot resulted in a cost shift to non-
participating customers and should not be expanded.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that new customers that enroll in the RNR tariff beginning
January 1, 2023 must take service in one of the Company’s three-part rates.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that RNR instantaneous customers shall continue to be paid for
excess energy pushed back onto the grid at the Company’s Solar Avoided Energy Cost rate,
consistent with the RCB Framework, and as filed annually with this Commission in Docket No.
16573.

        ORDERED FURTHER, within 90 days of this Order, the Company and Commission
Staff shall initiate discussions and work with the Consumer Protection Division of the Georgia
Attorney General’s Office to formalize a consumer complaint process to better address consumer
complaints related to bad actors in the rooftop solar industry.

      ORDERED FURTHER, that all changes to tariffs, riders, rules and regulations filed by
the Company should be approved as filed unless ordered otherwise in this Order.


                                               Page 31

         ORDERED FURTHER, that Georgia Power shall file compliance tariffs within 60 days
of the issuance of this Order, reflecting rates to implement the rate increases ordered herein. These
tariffs shall reflect the rate allocations adopted in this Order, and shall be subject to the
Commission’s review for final approval.

        ORDERED FURTHER, that all findings, conclusions and decisions contained within the
preceding sections of this Order are adopted as findings of fact, conclusions of law, and decisions
of regulatory policy of this Commission.

       ORDERED FURTHER, that jurisdiction over this proceeding is expressly retained for
the purpose of entering such further order or orders as this Commission may deem proper.

        ORDERED FURTHER, any motion for reconsideration, rehearing, or oral argument shall
not stay the effectiveness of this order unless expressly ordered by the Commission.

December, 2022.
